Nine fourths

A guided walk through the OpenAI preprint “The matrix multiplication exponent is nine fourths” (24 November 2026, 111 pages), for a reader who knows graduate mathematics and saw Strassen’s algorithm some years ago.

The preprint claims that the exponent of square matrix multiplication is $\omega_F = 9/4 = 2.25$ over every field $F$. The upper bound $\omega_F\le 9/4$ is earlier work. The new part is the lower bound. This page walks the argument section by section, states every numbered result with its page, expands the steps the paper compresses, and supplies the background that the paper assumes.

How to read the labels. paper a statement or step taken from the preprint. imported a result the preprint takes from another paper; it is stated here and not proved. filled here a derivation or background account written for this tutorial; it is not in the preprint in this form. not verified an assertion that this tutorial could not reconstruct. p. 25 a page of paper.pdf.

0. Status of the claim

Read this section before the mathematics. It states what kind of document the source is and what this tutorial did with it.

ItemFact
Source“The matrix multiplication exponent is nine fourths”, author line “OpenAI”, dated 24 November 2026, 111 pages, in the public repository openai/math. This tutorial was written on 25 November 2026, one day after that date.
ReviewThe source is a preprint. No referee report or independent verification is cited in it, and none was available to this project.
Upper bound$\omega_F\le 9/4$ is prior work, cited by the preprint: [41] OpenAI, 2 October 2026 (over $\mathbb C$); [39] Lü (arbitrary characteristic); [5] Amanov, van den Berg and Zuiddam (every field); [48] Shpilka (exposition and explicit algorithm); [55] Uznański (rectangular case). These papers were not read for this tutorial.
Lower boundNew in the preprint. It depends on two companion OpenAI preprints with the same date: [42] “Euclidean geometry in the asymptotic spectrum of tensors” and [43] “Circuit lower bounds for Hamiltonian cycle and the permanent”. The lower bound for list distinctness (Theorem 2.2) rests on results of both. This tutorial treats [42] and [43] as black boxes: each imported statement is marked imported and is not checked.
Formal proofThe repository README states that a Lean development proves the square exponent $9/4$ for division-free circuits. This project did not open the Lean files.
Public reactionRyan Williams wrote on X on 25 November 2026: “Apparently Strassen was right: the matrix mult exponent is (exactly) 2.25”. The word “Apparently” is his.
What “settled” would require. not verified The phrase “settles the question once and for all” is stronger than the evidence available on 25 November 2026. The proof is 111 pages, it is one day old, it has no referee, and its lower bound imports results from two other unrefereed preprints of the same date. This tutorial explains the argument as the preprint gives it. It does not certify the argument. The audit section lists what was checked.

One remark on the sentence “Strassen was right”. The preprint itself reports (p. 10) that its result refutes a conjecture of Strassen: for tight tensors, [53, Conjecture 5.3] predicts asymptotic rank $n^2$ for the matrix multiplication tensor, and Corollary 1.2 gives $n^{9/4}$. §2.4 gives the details.

A. Background: from Strassen’s identity to tensors and spectral points

Purpose. This section rebuilds, in order, the five ideas that the preprint assumes on its first page: recursion on blocks, bilinear algorithms as tensor decompositions, border rank, asymptotic rank, and Strassen’s asymptotic spectrum. Everything in this section is standard and is written for this tutorial. filled here

A.1 The question

The product $C=AB$ of two $n\times n$ matrices has $n^2$ entries $C_{ik}=\sum_j A_{ij}B_{jk}$. Computing each entry separately uses $n^3$ multiplications. The exponent of matrix multiplication over a field $F$ is $$\omega_F=\inf\{\,s\in\mathbb R:\ \text{two } n\times n \text{ matrices over } F \text{ can be multiplied with } O(n^{s}) \text{ arithmetic operations}\,\}.$$ Reading the input already costs $n^2$, so $2\le\omega_F\le 3$. The conjecture “$\omega=2$” says that the product costs $O(n^{2+\varepsilon})$ for every $\varepsilon\gt 0$. The preprint claims $\omega_F=9/4$: the exponent exceeds the exponent of the output size by exactly $1/4$.

A.2 Strassen’s seven products and the recursion

Write $2\times2$ block matrices $A=(A_{ij})$, $B=(B_{ij})$. Strassen (1969) [50] found seven products

$$\begin{aligned} M_1&=(A_{11}+A_{22})(B_{11}+B_{22}), & M_2&=(A_{21}+A_{22})B_{11}, & M_3&=A_{11}(B_{12}-B_{22}),\\ M_4&=A_{22}(B_{21}-B_{11}), & M_5&=(A_{11}+A_{12})B_{22}, & M_6&=(A_{21}-A_{11})(B_{11}+B_{12}),\\ M_7&=(A_{12}-A_{22})(B_{21}+B_{22}), \end{aligned}$$

from which the four blocks of the product are linear combinations:

$$C_{11}=M_1+M_4-M_5+M_7,\quad C_{12}=M_3+M_5,\quad C_{21}=M_2+M_4,\quad C_{22}=M_1-M_2+M_3+M_6 .$$

The identities never use commutativity of the entries. They therefore hold when the entries are themselves matrices, and the scheme can be applied to blocks of size $n/2$, then $n/4$, and so on.

Background — the recursion gives an exponent. filled here Suppose $b\times b$ block matrices can be multiplied with $a$ block products and a fixed number of block additions. The operation count $T(n)$ for side $n=b^j$ satisfies $T(n)=a\,T(n/b)+c\,n^2$. Unrolling, $T(b^j)=a^j T(1)+c\sum_{i\lt j}a^i (b^{j-i})^2$. If $a\gt b^2$ the sum is a geometric series dominated by its last terms and $T(n)=O(a^j)=O(n^{\log_b a})$. For other $n$, pad with zeros to the next power of $b$; this changes $n$ by a factor less than $b$ and the count by a constant factor. Hence $$\omega_F\le\log_b a .$$ The schoolbook method has $(b,a)=(2,8)$ and gives $3$. Strassen’s identity has $(b,a)=(2,7)$ and gives $\log_2 7\approx2.807$.

Widget 1 — block size, number of products, exponent

The last button shows what the preprint’s Corollary 1.2 implies at one size: an exact bilinear algorithm at side $n$ needs at least $\lceil n^{9/4}\rceil$ products, so at side $4$ no algorithm with fewer than $\lceil 4^{9/4}\rceil=23$ products exists if the preprint is correct. The widget does not claim that an algorithm with 23 products exists at side 4.

A.3 Bilinear algorithms are tensor decompositions

A bilinear algorithm with $r$ products computes $r$ numbers $m_\ell=\alpha_\ell(A)\,\beta_\ell(B)$, where $\alpha_\ell$ is a linear form in the entries of $A$ and $\beta_\ell$ a linear form in the entries of $B$, and then outputs every $C_{ik}$ as a linear combination of the $m_\ell$. Strassen’s scheme is one with $r=7$ at side $2$. The recursion of A.2 only needs such a scheme, so the least possible $r$ at each size is the quantity to study.

Definition A.1 (tensor rank, restriction, the tensor $T_n$). filled here Let $U,V,W$ be finite-dimensional $F$-vector spaces. A tensor $T\in U\otimes V\otimes W$ is simple if $T=u\otimes v\otimes w$. The rank $\operatorname{rank}_F(T)$ is the least $r$ with $T=\sum_{\ell=1}^r u_\ell\otimes v_\ell\otimes w_\ell$. A tensor $T$ is a restriction of $T'\in U'\otimes V'\otimes W'$, written $T\le T'$, if $T=(f\otimes g\otimes h)(T')$ for linear maps $f:U'\to U$, $g:V'\to V$, $h:W'\to W$. With the unit tensor (diagonal) $\langle r\rangle=\sum_{\ell=1}^r e_\ell\otimes e_\ell\otimes e_\ell$, one has $\operatorname{rank}(T)\le r$ exactly when $T\le\langle r\rangle$: the maps send $e_\ell$ to $u_\ell$, $v_\ell$, $w_\ell$. The matrix multiplication tensor of side $n$ is $$T_n=\sum_{i,j,k=1}^n e_{ij}\otimes e_{jk}\otimes e_{ki}\ \in\ F^{n\times n}\otimes F^{n\times n}\otimes F^{n\times n}.$$

The third factor $e_{ki}$ is dual to the output entry $(i,k)$: pairing $T_n$ with $A$ in the first factor and $B$ in the second gives $\sum_{i,k}(AB)_{ik}\,e_{ki}$. A decomposition $T_n=\sum_\ell u_\ell\otimes v_\ell\otimes w_\ell$ is the same thing as a bilinear algorithm with products $m_\ell=\langle u_\ell,A\rangle\langle v_\ell,B\rangle$ and output coefficients read from $w_\ell$. So the least number of products of a bilinear algorithm at side $n$ equals $\operatorname{rank}(T_n)$.

Two structural facts make one size control all sizes. First, the tensor (Kronecker) product of two tensors is formed factor by factor, $(T\otimes T')\in(U\otimes U')\otimes(V\otimes V')\otimes(W\otimes W')$, and rank is submultiplicative: $\operatorname{rank}(T\otimes T')\le\operatorname{rank}(T)\operatorname{rank}(T')$. Second, grouping index pairs gives $$T_n\otimes T_m\cong T_{nm},\qquad\text{hence}\qquad T_n^{\otimes j}\cong T_{n^j}.$$ Indeed $e_{ij}\otimes e_{i'j'}$ is the matrix unit $e_{(i,i'),(j,j')}$ of side $nm$. Consequently $\operatorname{rank}(T_{n^j})\le\operatorname{rank}(T_n)^j$, which is the tensor form of “apply the scheme recursively”, and $\omega_F\le\log_n\operatorname{rank}_F(T_n)$ for every $n\ge2$. The converse direction, from an arbitrary arithmetic circuit to a bilinear algorithm with at most twice as many products, is Lemma 2.4 of the preprint (§2.3).

A.4 Border rank and asymptotic rank

Rank is not closed under limits. The standard example is $W=e_1\otimes e_1\otimes e_2+e_1\otimes e_2\otimes e_1+e_2\otimes e_1\otimes e_1$, which has rank $3$, while $$\frac1\varepsilon\Bigl((e_1+\varepsilon e_2)^{\otimes3}-e_1^{\otimes 3}\Bigr)=W+O(\varepsilon)$$ exhibits $W$ as a limit of tensors of rank $2$. The border rank of $T$ is the least $r$ such that $T$ lies in the closure of the set of tensors of rank at most $r$. The preprint uses the geometric version: closure in the Zariski topology over the algebraic closure $\overline F$, which makes sense over every field. Bini (1980) [10] showed how to turn an approximate identity with error terms in $\varepsilon$ into an exact one by polynomial interpolation in $\varepsilon$, at the cost of a factor polynomial in the degree. After taking tensor powers and roots that factor disappears, so border rank also bounds the exponent: $\omega_F\le\log_n\underline{\operatorname{rank}}(T_n)$.

The asymptotic rank removes every such sub-exponential factor at once: $$\widetilde{\operatorname{rank}}_F(T)=\lim_{j\to\infty}\operatorname{rank}_F\bigl(T^{\otimes j}\bigr)^{1/j}.$$ The limit exists by submultiplicativity (Fekete’s lemma applied to $\log\operatorname{rank}(T^{\otimes j})$). Because $T_n^{\otimes j}\cong T_{n^j}$, $$\omega_F=\log_n\widetilde{\operatorname{rank}}_F(T_n)\qquad\text{for every } n\ge2 .$$ In this language the preprint’s claim is $\widetilde{\operatorname{rank}}_F(T_n)=n^{9/4}$ (Corollary 1.2).

A.5 The history in numbers

After Strassen, upper bounds improved by working with several products at once. Schönhage’s asymptotic sum inequality (1981) [47] converts a border-rank bound for a direct sum of rectangular products into a bound on $\omega$: if $\underline{\operatorname{rank}}\bigl(\bigoplus_i T_{a_i,b_i,c_i}\bigr)\le r$ then $\sum_i (a_ib_ic_i)^{\omega/3}\le r$. Strassen’s laser method (1987) [51] and the Coppersmith–Winograd tensor (1990) [24] extract many independent products from powers of one auxiliary tensor; the refinements of that method occupy the following thirty-five years. None of this machinery is used in the lower-bound proof. Fig. 1 places the numbers.

On the lower-bound side the preprint (p. 5) quotes the earlier state: Bläser’s $\operatorname{rank}(T_n)\ge\frac52n^2-3n$ over every field [11], Landsberg’s $3n^2-o(n^2)$ over $\mathbb C$ [35], and the Landsberg–Michałek border-rank bound $2n^2-\lceil\log_2 n\rceil-1$ [36]. Each is a constant times $n^2$. A constant factor disappears on taking $j$-th roots along $T_n^{\otimes j}\cong T_{n^j}$, so none of them gives $\omega\gt2$.

A.6 Strassen’s asymptotic spectrum

The lower-bound proof is written in the language of Strassen’s asymptotic spectrum [52]. Fix a field and consider all 3-tensors. A spectral point is a map $\varphi$ from tensors to $\mathbb R_{\ge0}$ that is

  • monotone under restriction: $T\le T'\Rightarrow\varphi(T)\le\varphi(T')$;
  • additive on direct sums: $\varphi(T\oplus T')=\varphi(T)+\varphi(T')$;
  • multiplicative on tensor products: $\varphi(T\otimes T')=\varphi(T)\varphi(T')$;
  • normalized: $\varphi(\langle1\rangle)=1$, hence $\varphi(\langle r\rangle)=r$.

If $\operatorname{rank}(T^{\otimes j})\le r$ then $T^{\otimes j}\le\langle r\rangle$ and $\varphi(T)^j\le r$. So every spectral point is a lower bound for asymptotic rank: $\varphi(T)\le\widetilde{\operatorname{rank}}(T)$. Strassen’s theorem says that these bounds are jointly sharp.

Imported — Strassen’s duality [52]. imported The set $X$ of spectral points is compact in the topology of pointwise convergence, and for every tensor $T$ $$\widetilde{\operatorname{rank}}(T)=\max_{\varphi\in X}\varphi(T).$$ The preprint uses this formula on p. 5 and p. 43.

Examples make the definition concrete. Flatten $T\in U\otimes V\otimes W$ to a matrix $U\to V\otimes W$. Its matrix rank is monotone, additive and multiplicative, so each of the three flattening ranks is a spectral point. On $T_n$ each flattening has rank $n^2$. This is the classical reason for $\omega\ge2$ and, by the duality, any proof of $\omega\gt2$ must use spectral points other than flattening ranks. The preprint’s proof does not exhibit such a point directly. It reasons about the geometry that the whole family of spectral points induces on the sites of a tensor network (§3).

Background — tensors on more than three sites. filled here From §2 on, the preprint works with tensors that have one vector space at each element of a finite set $S$ of sites, with $|S|\ge2$; a 3-tensor is the case $|S|=3$. Restriction applies one linear map per site. The tensor product groups the factors site by site. The direct sum takes the direct sum of the spaces at every site. The unit is the tensor $1$ in one-dimensional spaces. Spectral points are defined by the same four axioms. The diagonal on a subset $A\subseteq S$ with $q$ values is $\Delta_A(q)=\sum_{j=1}^q\bigotimes_{i\in A}e_j$, with one-dimensional spaces at the sites outside $A$. A diagonal on two sites is called a wire: it is the identity matrix of size $q$, viewed as a tensor.

M. Map of the proof

Purpose. The lower bound is a proof by contradiction with one hypothesis, $\omega_k\lt9/4$ over a finite field $k$, which the preprint calls a strict saving. Fig. 2 shows which statement uses which. Every box is a link. Boxes with a dashed violet border are imported from other papers.

The logic in one paragraph. Suppose $\omega_F\lt9/4$ for some field. Lemma 2.5 moves the hypothesis to a finite field $k$. Under the hypothesis, Part I shows that a certain family of tensors (sphere tensors) has prices given by an explicit barycenter formula. Parts II–IV convert this price information into an operation on tensor networks: any network can be replaced by a network for a tensor power that is smaller, per copy, by a power of $\log n$ (Theorem 2.1). Applying this operation a fixed number of times to a sorting network that tests whether the words in a list are distinct gives a network that is smaller than a lower bound proved by other means (Theorem 2.2). The contradiction shows $\omega_k\ge9/4$.

1. The exponent of matrix multiplication pp. 1–5

Role in the chain. Section 1 of the preprint states the theorem and its corollaries and gives a four-page overview. This tutorial section states the results in full, then expands the overview’s central picture (§1.3), because that picture explains where the number $9/4$ comes from before any machinery is built.

1.0 The statements

Definition (formal arithmetic circuit). p. 1 paper The inputs are independent variables (the $2n^2$ matrix entries). The constants lie in $F$. A gate adds, subtracts, multiplies, or divides by a nonzero rational function. Each output must equal the corresponding entry of the product as an identity of rational functions. The same convention is used over finite fields. $\omega_F$ is the infimum of the real $s$ for which such circuits of size $O_{F,s}(n^s)$ exist.

The convention matters over a finite field, where a polynomial can vanish at every point without being the zero polynomial. The circuit must compute the product as a formal identity, not only as a function on $F^{2n^2}$.

Theorem 1.1. p. 1 paper Over every field $F$, the exponent of square matrix multiplication is $\omega_F=9/4$.
Corollary 1.2. p. 2 paper For every $n\ge2$ and every field $F$, the geometric border rank of $T_n$ is at least $n^{9/4}$, and the asymptotic rank of $T_n$ over $F$ equals $n^{9/4}$. Every formal arithmetic circuit over $F$ for this product has at least $\lceil n^{9/4}/2\rceil$ nonscalar multiplication or division gates.

In particular an exact bilinear algorithm at side $n$ uses at least $\lceil n^{9/4}\rceil$ products. For $n=2$ this gives $\lceil 4.757\rceil=5$, weaker than the known optimal value $7$; the statement is a uniform lower bound, not the exact rank at each size.

Corollary 1.3. p. 2 paper Write $T_{a,b,c}$ for the tensor of multiplying an $a\times b$ matrix by a $b\times c$ matrix. For all positive integers $a,b,c$, the geometric border rank of $T_{a,b,c}$ and its asymptotic rank over $F$ are at least $$\max\{ab,\ bc,\ ca,\ (abc)^{3/4}\}.$$ Let $\alpha_F$ be the supremum of the $\beta\ge0$ for which multiplying an $n\times\lfloor n^\beta\rfloor$ matrix by an $\lfloor n^\beta\rfloor\times n$ matrix has exponent two in $n$. Over every field, $1/2\le\alpha_F\le2/3$.

The proofs of both corollaries are in §2.4.

1.2 Why a faster matrix product would test lists too cheaply pp. 2–3

The proof does not attack $T_n$ directly. It compares two estimates for a different computation over a finite field: given $2^w$ words of $2w$ bits each, output $1$ if the words are pairwise distinct and $0$ otherwise. Cost is measured by the size of a tensor network with at most three bits at each vertex (defined in §2.1). One network may test $t$ lists at once and may share internal wiring between lists.

  • Lower estimate (Theorem 2.2). Even with sharing, the volume per input bit exceeds $w^\sigma$ for a fixed $\sigma\gt0$, uniformly for $1\le t\le 2^w$.
  • Upper estimate (Theorem 2.1, under the strict saving). Any trivalent network can be replaced by a network for a tensor power of its table whose size per copy is smaller by a power of the logarithm, plus a charge of an arbitrarily small power of the logarithm per exterior bit.

Starting from a sorting network and compressing a fixed number of times brings the cost per input bit down to $O(w^\eta)$ for any chosen $0\lt\eta\lt\sigma$. That contradicts the lower estimate. §2.2 carries out the arithmetic.

Preview of AHA 5. The lower bound for matrix multiplication is proved through a different problem. No property of $T_n$ is bounded directly. A hypothetical fast matrix product is used as a tool that would make list-distinctness tests cheaper than a separate lower bound allows.

1.3 Three wires and a shared label pp. 3–4

This subsection is the conceptual centre of the preprint’s overview. It uses three notions that §3 defines precisely; here is the short form. Take a spectral point $\varphi$ on tensors with three sites $A,B,C$ and fix a scale $D\gt0$. The price of a tensor is $H(T)=D\log\varphi(T)$. Prices add under tensor products and can only decrease under restriction. A wire with $q$ values between two sites has a price proportional to $\log q$; the constant of proportionality is called the distance $d(\cdot,\cdot)$ between the two sites. A label with $q$ values shared by all three sites, $\Delta_{ABC}(q)=\langle q\rangle$, has price $D\log q$; $D$ is the common-label rate.

Gap fill — the perimeter inequality, with every step. filled here

Step 1: $T_n$ is a product of three wires. The index $j$ occurs in the factor at $A$ (as the column index of $e_{ij}$) and at $B$ (as the row index of $e_{jk}$) and nowhere else. Likewise $k$ is shared by $B$ and $C$, and $i$ by $C$ and $A$. Hence, after regrouping the basis at each site, $$T_n\ \cong\ \Delta_{AB}(n)\otimes\Delta_{BC}(n)\otimes\Delta_{CA}(n).$$ Prices add on tensor products, and a wire with $n$ values between two sites at distance $d$ has price $d\log n$. Therefore $$H(T_n)=\bigl(d(A,B)+d(B,C)+d(C,A)\bigr)\log n .$$

Step 2: a rank decomposition is a restriction of a shared label. If $T_n=\sum_{\ell=1}^r u_\ell\otimes v_\ell\otimes w_\ell$, the linear maps $e_\ell\mapsto u_\ell$ at $A$, $e_\ell\mapsto v_\ell$ at $B$, $e_\ell\mapsto w_\ell$ at $C$ send $\Delta_{ABC}(r)$ to $T_n$. So $T_n\le\Delta_{ABC}(r)$ and, by monotonicity, $H(T_n)\le H(\Delta_{ABC}(r))=D\log r$.

Step 3: combine. $$\bigl(\text{sum of the three wire lengths}\bigr)\log n\ \le\ D\log r,\qquad\text{so}\qquad r\ \ge\ n^{\,\text{perimeter}/D}.$$

The inequality holds for every spectral point, at every placement of the three sites in the geometry that the point defines. Each such placement gives a lower bound on rank whose exponent is the ratio of a triangle’s perimeter to its common-label rate.

AHA 1. The exponent is a ratio of two geometric quantities: $\omega\ \ge$ (perimeter of the triangle of wires) $/$ (rate of a label shared by its three corners). A lower bound on $\omega$ is a triangle with a large perimeter and a cheap shared label.

What ratios occur? A flattening rank across the cut $A\mid BC$ is a spectral point with $d(A,B)=d(A,C)=1$, $d(B,C)=0$ and $D=1$ at scale one. Its ratio is $2/1=2$, the classical bound. The preprint states that all prices coming from Euclidean flats give ratio two, citing the companion paper [42] (p. 3); this tutorial did not check that statement. To exceed two, a geometry that is not flat is needed.

The critical configuration is a tripod: three arms of lengths $a_1,a_2,a_3$ glued at one end $O$, with the three sites at the free ends. The distance between tips $i$ and $j$ is $a_i+a_j$ (the path goes through $O$), so the perimeter is $2(a_1+a_2+a_3)$. The preprint’s Proposition 8.1 (§8) says that the common-label rate on any three sites at these distances is at least $$D_*(a)=a_1+a_2+a_3-\Bigl(\frac1{a_1}+\frac1{a_2}+\frac1{a_3}\Bigr)^{-1}.$$ With three unit arms, $D_*=3-\tfrac13=\tfrac83$ and the perimeter is $6$. If a spectral point realizes this tripod with rate exactly $8/3$, then $$\omega\ \ge\ \frac{6}{8/3}=\frac94 .$$

Widget 2 — the tripod calculator

Read the output as follows. $D_*$ is the least possible common-label rate at these distances (Proposition 8.1), so perimeter$/D_*$ is the largest exponent that a triangle of this shape can certify. It lies in $[2,\,9/4]$ and equals $9/4$ only for equal arms (the arithmetic–harmonic mean inequality, §11.3). Whether a spectral point attains $D=D_*$ on a tripod is the content of §7–§8.

AHA 2. Flat geometry gives $2$. A tree-like tripod gives $6/(8/3)=9/4$. The gap between $2$ and $9/4$ is a statement about curvature: three sites that are joined through a common centre, as in a tree, share a label more cheaply ($8/3$) than three sites at the same distances in a flat geometry would (rate $3$ at ratio two).

How the tripod enters the proof (p. 4) is indirect, and this point is easy to misread. Under the strict-saving hypothesis $\omega\lt9/4$, a tripod with rate $8/3$ cannot occur, because it would give $\omega\ge9/4$. Part I exploits the exclusion. If varying the parameters of a tensor moved its preferred site within a certain family of prices, the motion would produce exactly such a tripod (Theorem 7.1). So under the hypothesis nothing moves, and one extension of the sites prices a whole family of tensors at once. This synchronization (Theorem 12.1) is the geometric input to the compression theorem.

1.4 From prices to a smaller network pp. 4–5

The family that is priced consists of sphere tensors (§13): tensors whose entries are determined by marked points on the projective line. When marked points collide, the tensor expands into contractions through legs of controlled dimension. Under the hypothesis, the largest prices of these tensors are determined (§14): for prescribed leg widths (logarithms of dimensions), the price is asymptotically the cost of preparing the tensor at the cheapest common site and transporting each leg to its destination.

The preprint’s Figure 2 (p. 4) shows the same structure as the lower half of Fig. 2 above: Part I feeds Part II (stable sorting and batched table lookup) and Part III (concentration of the incident bits), and both feed Part IV (compression of tensor powers). The compression must pay for each table of a network and for the records that say how tables are connected. Direct lookup in a table of $u$ incident bits can cost $2^u$. In a large batch, sorting queries together with table entries amortizes that cost. For $2^u$ records of width $O(u)$ the sort of Part II costs $O(u^p)$ per record for some $p\lt2$.

The strict inequality $p\lt2$ is needed for a specific reason (p. 4): the quantity that collisions control is proportional to the square of a leg width. §27 shows where the exponent $p-1\lt1$ closes the bookkeeping.

1.5 Earlier bounds p. 5

The preprint’s history paragraph is summarized in A.5. One sentence from it is used later: the upper bound $9/4$ “comes from polynomial multiplication”. The proof in [41] tracks how spectral points value the tensor of polynomial multiplication as the two input degrees grow. The same tensor is the measuring device in §9–§11.

2. The obstruction from list distinctness pp. 7–10

Role in the chain. This section is the whole proof of Theorem 1.1, given two theorems that are proved later: Theorem 2.1 (compression, proved in §27) and Theorem 2.2 (list distinctness, proved in §4). It also contains the two lemmas that pass between arbitrary fields and finite fields, and the proofs of the corollaries.

2.1 Two estimates for networks on bits p. 7

Definition (bit tensor, tensor network, size, volume). p. 7 paper Fix a finite field $k$. A bit tensor of arity $u$ is a function $\{0,1\}^u\to k$. In a tensor network each vertex carries such a table. A wire pairs two argument positions, and each unpaired position is a named exterior bit. For a fixed setting of the exterior bits, multiply the vertex entries and sum over all settings of the internal wires; this defines the tensor represented by the network. A network is trivalent if each vertex has arity at most three. Its size counts the vertices and exterior bits. Its volume counts the vertices and all wires, including the exterior wires. For networks of unrestricted arity the count is vertices plus their total arity.
Background — why this is a model of computation. filled here A Boolean circuit becomes a tensor network as follows. Replace each gate by its $0/1$ table, a function of its inputs and its output that is $1$ exactly on consistent triples. Replace each fan-out by an equality table $\mathbf 1\{x=y=z\}$. For a fixed input, exactly one assignment of the internal wires makes all tables equal to $1$, so the sum over internal wires equals $1$ if the circuit’s output wire carries the pinned value and $0$ otherwise. Tensor networks are more general than circuits: the tables take arbitrary values in $k$, there is no direction of time, and the sum over internal wires can produce cancellation. A lower bound on network volume is therefore stronger than a circuit lower bound for the same function. In a trivalent network every vertex meets at most three wires, so $\text{wires}\le\frac32\cdot\text{vertices}+\text{exterior bits}$ and size and volume agree up to an absolute factor.
Theorem 2.1 (Compression of tensor powers). p. 7 paper Suppose $\omega_k\lt9/4$. For every $\eta\gt0$ there is a $\gamma\gt0$ such that the following holds for all sufficiently large $n$. Write $h=\lceil\log_2n\rceil$. If a trivalent $k$-network of size $m\le n^{1.05}$ with $e\ge n$ exterior bits represents $V$, then for some integer $d\ge1$ its power $V^{\otimes d}$ has a trivalent $k$-network with $de$ distinct exterior bits and size at most $$d\bigl(mh^{-\gamma}+eh^{\eta}\bigr).\tag{2.1}$$ Here $d\le n^{o(1)}$ uniformly in the input network for fixed $k,\eta$.

Per copy, the first term saves a factor $h^{\gamma}$ on the old size; the second term charges $h^\eta$ for each exterior bit. The output is again a trivalent network whose exterior bits are individually accessible, so the theorem can be applied to its own output.

Definition (the list-distinctness tensor $D_w$). p. 7 paper For $w\ge1$, $D_w$ is the zero-one tensor for a list of $2^w$ words of $2w$ bits each. Its value is one exactly when the words are pairwise distinct. It has $n_w=2w\,2^w$ exterior bits. The tensor $D_w^{\otimes t}$ tests distinctness separately within each of $t$ independently specified lists. $\operatorname{vol}_k$ denotes minimum trivalent volume over $k$.
Theorem 2.2 (Volume of list distinctness). p. 7 paper There is a constant $\sigma\gt0$, depending only on $k$, such that for all sufficiently large $w$ and every integer $1\le t\le2^w$, $$\operatorname{vol}_k\bigl(D_w^{\otimes t}\bigr)\gt t\,n_w\,w^{\sigma}.\tag{2.2}$$

The uniformity in $t$ is used in an essential way: the replication factor $d$ in Theorem 2.1 is chosen by the compression, not by the user, so the lower bound must hold for whatever number of lists comes out.

2.2 Comparison at a fixed input length p. 8

Proposition 2.3. p. 8 paper Over every finite field $k$, $\omega_k\ge9/4$.

Step 1: a first network for $D_w$. A sorting network on $2^w$ records uses $O(2^ww^2)$ comparisons [8]. Comparing and conditionally exchanging two $2w$-bit words takes $O(w)$ Boolean gates. After sorting, equal words are adjacent, so the conjunction of the strict inequalities between adjacent words computes $D_w$. Convert the circuit to a tensor network as in §2.1 (gate tables, equality tables at fan-outs, output pinned to one). This gives a trivalent network over $k$ of size $$m_0=O(2^ww^2\cdot w)=O(n_ww^2),\qquad n_w=2w\,2^w .$$

Step 2: choose the constants. Suppose $\omega_k\lt9/4$. Let $\sigma$ be the constant of Theorem 2.2. Choose $0\lt\eta\lt\sigma$, take $\gamma$ from Theorem 2.1 for this $\eta$, and fix an integer $J\gt2/\gamma$. Set $n=n_w$ and $h=\lceil\log_2n\rceil=w+O(\log w)$. These parameters stay fixed through all $J$ applications of Theorem 2.1.

Step 3: the recurrence. At step $j$ write $t_j$ for the number of lists, $m_j$ for the network size and $x_j=m_j/(t_jn)$ for the size per exterior bit. Initially $t_0=1$ and $x_0=O(w^2)$. Applying (2.1) with replication factor $d_j$ to a network with $e=t_jn$ exterior bits gives $m_{j+1}\le d_j(m_jh^{-\gamma}+t_jn\,h^\eta)$, that is, $$t_{j+1}=d_jt_j,\qquad x_{j+1}\le h^{-\gamma}x_j+h^{\eta}.$$

Step 4: the hypotheses of Theorem 2.1 hold at each step. The network has $t_jn\ge n$ exterior bits. Since the bound $d_j\le n^{o(1)}$ is uniform and $J$ is fixed, $t_j=n^{o(1)}$. The recurrence gives $x_j=O(w^2+h^\eta)$, so $m_j=t_jn\,x_j\le n^{1.05}$ for large $w$.

Step 5: solve. $$x_J\le h^{-J\gamma}x_0+h^{\eta}\sum_{r=0}^{J-1}h^{-r\gamma}=O(w^{\eta}).$$ The first term is $O(h^{-J\gamma}w^2)=O(1)$ because $J\gamma\gt2$ and $h\ge w$. The geometric sum is at most $(1-h^{-\gamma})^{-1}=O(1)$.

Step 6: contradiction. Since $\log_2t_J=o(w)$, we have $t_J\le2^w$ for large $w$ and Theorem 2.2 applies to these $t_J$ lists. Their network has volume $O(t_Jn\,w^{\eta})$ by trivalency. Theorem 2.2 demands volume $\gt t_Jn\,w^{\sigma}$. As $\eta\lt\sigma$, this fails for sufficiently large $w$.∎

Background — Batcher’s sorting network. filled here A comparator takes two records and outputs them in sorted order. A sorting network is a fixed sequence of comparators, chosen in advance and independent of the data, that sorts every input. Batcher (1968) [8] built one recursively: sort the two halves, then merge them with an odd–even merging network, which itself recurses on the odd-indexed and even-indexed subsequences and finishes with one layer of comparators. Merging $N$ records uses $O(N\log N)$ comparators, so sorting uses $S(N)=2S(N/2)+O(N\log N)=O(N\log^2N)$. With $N=2^w$ this is the $O(2^ww^2)$ in Step 1. A network is used, and not a faster adaptive algorithm, because a fixed comparator sequence translates gate by gate into a fixed tensor network.

Widget 3 — the compression recurrence $x_{j+1}=h^{-\gamma}x_j+h^{\eta}$

AHA 5. The compression theorem is applied to its own output $J\gt2/\gamma$ times. Each round removes a factor $h^{\gamma}$ from the part of the cost that is above the floor $h^{\eta}$. The initial excess is $w^2$, so a fixed number of rounds reaches the floor, and the floor $w^{\eta}$ is below the lower bound $w^{\sigma}$. The argument needs three things together: the saving is a positive power of $\log n$, the charge exponent $\eta$ can be chosen after $\sigma$ is known, and the lower bound is uniform in the number of lists.

2.3 Passing from finite fields to arbitrary fields pp. 8–9

Proposition 2.3 concerns finite fields. Two lemmas transfer the conclusion to every field. The first extracts a bilinear identity from an arbitrary circuit; the second specializes that identity to a finite field.

Lemma 2.4 (Bilinearization of a formal circuit). p. 8 paper If a formal circuit for multiplying two $s$-by-$s$ matrices over $F$ uses $q$ nonscalar multiplication or division gates, then $$\operatorname{rank}_{\overline F}(T_s)\le2q.\tag{2.3}$$ Here $q$ counts a multiplication when both inputs are nonconstant and a division when its divisor is nonconstant.

Note on the source: the rank in (2.3) is over the algebraic closure $\overline F$. The text layer of paper.pdf loses the overline; the LaTeX source obstruction.tex has \operatorname{rank}_{\overline F}.

Step 1: expand around a regular point. The field $\overline F$ is infinite. Each gate computes a rational function of the $2s^2$ inputs, and there are finitely many gates, so there are matrices $A,B$ over $\overline F$ at which every intermediate rational function is regular and every divisor is nonzero. (A finite set of nonzero polynomials has a common non-root over an infinite field.)

Step 2: dual numbers. Evaluate the circuit at $A+uX$, $B+vY$, where $X,Y$ are matrices of new variables and $u,v$ are formal symbols with $u^2=v^2=0$. Every gate value has a unique expansion $$f=f_{00}+u\,f_{10}+v\,f_{01}+uv\,f_{11},$$ where $f_{00}\in\overline F$ is a scalar (the value at $(A,B)$), $f_{10}$ is a linear form in $X$, $f_{01}$ is a linear form in $Y$, and $f_{11}$ is bilinear in $(X,Y)$. This is the Taylor expansion to first order in each of the two matrix arguments separately.

Step 3: a product gate $f=ab$. Multiplying the two expansions and using $u^2=v^2=0$, $$f_{11}=a_{00}b_{11}+a_{11}b_{00}+a_{10}b_{01}+a_{01}b_{10}.$$ The first two terms are scalar multiples of bilinear forms already computed. Only $a_{10}b_{01}$ and $a_{01}b_{10}$ are new products of an $X$-linear form with a $Y$-linear form.

Step 4: a quotient gate $f=a/b$. Apply Step 3 to $bf=a$ and solve, using $b_{00}\ne0$: $$b_{00}f_{11}=a_{11}-b_{11}f_{00}-b_{10}f_{01}-b_{01}f_{10}.$$ Again at most two new products of linear forms appear, $b_{10}f_{01}$ and $b_{01}f_{10}$.

Step 5: collect. Additions, subtractions, and multiplications or divisions by a scalar contribute no new product. The outputs are the entries of $(A+uX)(B+vY)=AB+u\,XB+v\,AY+uv\,XY$, so the mixed coefficients of the outputs are the entries of $XY$. By induction over the gates, every $f_{11}$ lies in the span of at most $2q$ products $\lambda_m(X)\mu_m(Y)$ of linear forms. Writing each $(XY)_{ik}$ in terms of these products is a bilinear algorithm with at most $2q$ products, that is, $\operatorname{rank}_{\overline F}(T_s)\le2q$.∎

AHA 3. The factor $2$ in $\lceil n^{9/4}/2\rceil$ is the dual-number product rule. One general multiplication or division gate contributes at most two new products of linear forms to the bilinear part.
Lemma 2.5 (Finite specialization). p. 9 paper If $\omega_F\lt9/4$ over a field $F$, then $\omega_k\lt9/4$ over some finite field $k$.

Step 1: one identity with a strict saving. The circuit bound has the form $O(s^{9/4-\epsilon})$ for a fixed $\epsilon\gt0$. By Lemma 2.4, at a sufficiently large side $s\ge2$ there is a rank decomposition $T_s=\sum_{\ell=1}^ru_\ell\otimes v_\ell\otimes w_\ell$ over $\overline F$ with $r\lt s^{9/4}$ terms.

Step 2: a finitely generated ring. Let $R\subseteq\overline F$ be the ring generated over $\mathbb Z$ by the $3rs^2$ coordinates of the vectors $u_\ell,v_\ell,w_\ell$. The decomposition is a system of $s^6$ polynomial equations in these coordinates, one for each coordinate of $T_s$, with right-hand sides $0$ or $1$.

Step 3: reduce modulo a maximal ideal. Choose a maximal ideal $\mathfrak m$ of $R$ and put $k=R/\mathfrak m$. The ring homomorphism $R\to k$ maps the identity of Step 2 to the same identity over $k$, so $\operatorname{rank}_k(T_s)\le r$, provided $k$ is a finite field. That is Step 4.

Step 4: $k$ is finite. The field $k$ is finitely generated as a $\mathbb Z$-algebra. In characteristic $p$, it is a finitely generated $\mathbb F_p$-algebra that is a field, hence a finite extension of $\mathbb F_p$ by Zariski’s lemma. In characteristic zero, $k\supseteq\mathbb Q$ would be a finitely generated $\mathbb Q$-algebra that is a field, hence a number field. Multiply the finitely many algebra generators by a common positive integer $M$ so that they become algebraic integers. Then the ring they generate over $\mathbb Z$ lies in $\mathcal O_k[1/M]$. This ring does not contain $1/\ell$ for a prime $\ell\nmid M$, but the field $k$ contains $1/\ell$. So characteristic zero is impossible.

Step 5: power the algorithm. Take the $j$-th tensor power of the fixed decomposition over $k$. It is a bilinear algorithm for $T_{s^j}$ with $r^j$ products. Each of the three linear maps is a $j$-fold tensor power of a fixed matrix and can be applied one tensor coordinate at a time. Since $s^2\le r$ (flattening rank), every intermediate array has at most $r^j$ entries. The linear work and the $r^j$ scalar products cost $O_{r,s}(j\,r^j)$ operations at side $s^j$. Padding to the next power of $s$ gives $\omega_k\le\log_sr\lt9/4$.∎

Background — Zariski’s lemma, and why reduction works. filled here Zariski’s lemma: if a field $L$ is finitely generated as an algebra over a field $K$, then $L$ is a finite extension of $K$. It is the algebraic core of Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz. The argument in Step 4 shows a stronger standard fact: a field that is finitely generated as a ring over $\mathbb Z$ is finite. The phenomenon behind Lemma 2.5 is general: a statement that asserts the existence of a solution to finitely many polynomial equations with integer coefficients, if true over some field, is true over some finite field. A rank decomposition is such a statement. A rank lower bound is not, which is why the transfer goes from “$\omega_F\lt9/4$” to “$\omega_k\lt9/4$” and the contradiction is then derived over $k$.
AHA 4. One bilinear identity is a finite system of polynomial equations with integer right-hand sides. It survives reduction to a finite field. So a counterexample to the theorem over any field produces a counterexample over a finite field, and the proof only has to exclude finite fields.
(of Theorem 1.1, p. 9) Proposition 2.3 and Lemma 2.5 give $\omega_F\ge9/4$ over every field. The upper bound over every field is due to Amanov, van den Berg and Zuiddam [5].∎

2.4 Rank bounds from the exponent pp. 9–10

(of Corollary 1.2)

Rank and gates. Fix $n\ge2$. A rank-$r$ decomposition of $T_{n^j}$ over any field $K$ gives $\omega_K\le\log_{n^j}r$ by the recursion and padding of Lemma 2.5. Theorem 1.1 therefore gives $\operatorname{rank}_K(T_{n^j})\ge n^{9j/4}$ at each $j$, and taking $j$-th roots gives $\widetilde{\operatorname{rank}}\ge n^{9/4}$. At $K=\overline F$ and $j=1$, Lemma 2.4 gives $2q\ge\operatorname{rank}_{\overline F}(T_n)\ge n^{9/4}$, hence $q\ge\lceil n^{9/4}/2\rceil$.

Geometric border rank. Work in the tensor space $V$ over $\overline F$ containing $T_n$, and suppose $T_n$ lies in the Zariski closure of the set $X_r$ of tensors of rank at most $r$. Let $W_j\subseteq V^{\otimes j}$ be the linear span of $\{x^{\otimes j}:x\in X_r\}$. The set $\{x\in V:x^{\otimes j}\in W_j\}$ is defined by polynomial equations in $x$ (linear conditions on $x^{\otimes j}$) and contains $X_r$, so it contains the closure of $X_r$ and in particular $T_n$. Hence $T_n^{\otimes j}\in W_j$. The elements $x^{\otimes j}$ lie in the symmetric power $\operatorname{Sym}^jV$, whose dimension $\binom{j+\dim V-1}{j}=O_n(j^{\dim V-1})$ bounds $\dim W_j$. So $T_n^{\otimes j}$ is a linear combination of $O_n(j^{\dim V-1})$ tensors $x^{\otimes j}$, each of rank at most $r^j$, and $$n^{9j/4}\le\operatorname{rank}(T_{n^j})\le O_n(j^{\dim V-1})\,r^j .$$ Taking $j$-th roots and letting $j\to\infty$ gives $r\ge n^{9/4}$.

Asymptotic rank, upper bound over the original field. Fix $\varepsilon\gt0$. At side $n^j$ the known circuit upper bound and Lemma 2.4 give a decomposition of rank $r_j=O_{F,\varepsilon}(n^{j(9/4+\varepsilon)})$ over $\overline F$. Its coefficients lie in a finite extension $E_j/F$ of degree $d_j$. Take the $t$-th power of this decomposition, expand each of the three vectors of each simple tensor in an $F$-basis of $E_j$ containing $1$, and project the products of basis elements onto the coordinate of $1$. Because $T_n^{\otimes jt}$ is defined over $F$, this gives $\operatorname{rank}_F(T_n^{\otimes jt})\le d_j^3r_j^t$. Take $jt$-th roots, let $t\to\infty$ with $j$ fixed, then $j\to\infty$ and $\varepsilon\downarrow0$. The result is $\widetilde{\operatorname{rank}}_F(T_n)\le n^{9/4}$.∎

Two side remarks of the preprint (p. 10). First, Corollary 1.2 conflicts with the dyadic border-rank estimate proposed in the first version of Sun’s preprint [54]: at $n=2^8$ the proposed upper bound is $2^{17}$ and the lower bound $n^{9/4}$ is $2^{18}$. Second, the support of $T_n$ is tight: with $B\gt n$, assign weights $i-Bj$, $Bj-B^2k$, $B^2k-i$ to $e_{ij},e_{jk},e_{ki}$; each assignment is injective on its factor and the three weights sum to zero on every support triple. Each one-factor flattening has rank $n^2$. Strassen’s conjecture for tight tensors [53, Conjecture 5.3] predicts asymptotic rank $n^2$ in this case. Corollary 1.2 gives $n^{9/4}$, so the preprint’s result contradicts that conjecture.

(of Corollary 1.3)

The three one-factor flattenings of $T_{a,b,c}$ have ranks $ab,bc,ca$. They bound geometric border rank from below because matrix-rank conditions are closed, and asymptotic rank because flattening ranks are multiplicative. For the fourth bound, group corresponding factors: $$T_{a,b,c}\otimes T_{b,c,a}\otimes T_{c,a,b}\cong T_{abc}.$$ Each of the three indices becomes a tuple with $abc$ possibilities. Both rank notions are invariant under cyclic relabeling of the factors and submultiplicative, so the cube of either rank of $T_{a,b,c}$ is at least $(abc)^{9/4}$ by Corollary 1.2.

For $a=c=n$, $b=\lfloor n^\beta\rfloor$ the bound is $\Omega(n^{3(2+\beta)/4})$. Lemma 2.4 applies entrywise to rectangular products. An exponent of two therefore requires $3(2+\beta)/4\le2$, that is $\beta\le2/3$. The known algorithm at $\beta=1/2$ [5] gives $\alpha_F\ge1/2$.∎

3. Tensor prices and wire distances pp. 10–12

Role in the chain. This section fixes the language of the whole proof: a spectral point is turned into a price, and prices of wires define a metric on the sites of a tensor. It then lists the properties of that metric which the preprint imports from the companion paper [42]. Every later section uses these definitions; §5–§14 use the imports.

3.1 From tensors to distances p. 11

Definition (spectral point on a set of sites, price). p. 11 paper Fix a field and a finite set $S$ of at least two sites. Each tensor has one finite-dimensional vector space at each site. $T$ is a restriction of $U$ if one linear map at each site takes $U$ to $T$. A spectral point $\varphi$ is a restriction-monotone map to $\mathbb R_{\ge0}$ that preserves zero and the unit and satisfies $$\varphi(T\oplus U)=\varphi(T)+\varphi(U),\qquad\varphi(T\otimes U)=\varphi(T)\varphi(U).$$ Every nonzero tensor restricts to the unit $\mathbf 1$, so its value is at least one. The price at scale $D\gt0$ is $H(T)=D\log\varphi(T)$, with $H(0)=-\infty$. In §3 and Part I logarithms are natural.

In price language the axioms read: $H$ is nonincreasing under restriction; $H(T\otimes U)=H(T)+H(U)$; $H(T\oplus U)=D\log\bigl(e^{H(T)/D}+e^{H(U)/D}\bigr)$; $H(\mathbf 1)=0$. The scale $D$ is a free normalization. It is chosen so that the label shared by all sites has rate $D$ (below), and it lets one rescale prices and distances together.

Definition (common-label rate, wire distance). p. 11 paper For nonempty $A\subseteq S$, the diagonal $\Delta_A(q)=\sum_{j=1}^q\bigotimes_{i\in A}e_j$ shares a $q$-valued index among the sites of $A$; its other legs are one-dimensional. Then $$H(\Delta_A(q))=\delta(A)\log q\qquad(q\ge2)$$ for a number $\delta(A)$, the common-label rate on $A$. In particular $\delta(S)=D$. For $a\ne b$ put $d(a,b)=\delta(\{a,b\})$ and $d(a,a)=0$.
Gap fill — why the price of a diagonal is linear in $\log q$. filled here Put $f(q)=H(\Delta_A(q))$. Two facts: (i) $\Delta_A(q)^{\otimes m}=\Delta_A(q^m)$, because a tuple of $m$ shared labels is one shared label with $q^m$ values, so $f(q^m)=mf(q)$; (ii) $\Delta_A(q')\le\Delta_A(q)$ for $q'\le q$, by projecting away labels at one site, so $f$ is nondecreasing. Given $q\ge2$ and $m\ge1$, choose $k$ with $2^k\le q^m\lt2^{k+1}$. Then $kf(2)\le mf(q)\le(k+1)f(2)$, and $k/m\to\log_2q$ as $m\to\infty$. So $f(q)=f(2)\log_2q$, and $\delta(A)=f(2)/\log2$. For $A=S$, $\Delta_S(q)$ is the direct sum of $q$ units, so $\varphi=q$ and $f(q)=D\log q$.

Example (p. 11). Matrix rank across a fixed cut of $S$ is a spectral point. At scale one its wire distance is one for sites on opposite sides of the cut and zero for sites on the same side.

Gap fill — the triangle inequality and the transport bound. filled here

Triangle inequality. Take wires $\Delta_{ab}(q)$ and $\Delta_{bc}(q)$. Their tensor product has, at site $b$, the space $F^q\otimes F^q$. The linear functional $e_i\otimes e_j\mapsto\delta_{ij}$ at $b$ (and identities at $a$, $c$) maps the product to $\sum_ie_i\otimes e_i=\Delta_{ac}(q)$. So $\Delta_{ac}(q)\le\Delta_{ab}(q)\otimes\Delta_{bc}(q)$, and monotonicity with additivity gives $d(a,c)\log q\le d(a,b)\log q+d(b,c)\log q$. Symmetry is clear, so $d$ is a pseudometric on $S$. Sites at distance zero are identified.

Transport. Let $T\ne0$ have legs of dimensions $q_i$, placed at sites $x=(x_i)$; several legs may be at one site. Tensor $T$ with one wire $\Delta_{x_iy_i}(q_i)$ per leg and contract, at each $x_i$, the $i$-th leg of $T$ against the end of its wire. The result is the same tensor with leg $i$ now at $y_i$. Hence $H(T;y)\le H(T;x)+\sum_id(x_i,y_i)\log q_i$. Exchanging $x$ and $y$ gives the transport bound $$|H(T;x)-H(T;y)|\le\sum_id(x_i,y_i)\log q_i.\tag{3.1}$$ The quantity $\log q_i$ is called the width of leg $i$. Moving a leg of width $w$ through distance $d$ costs at most $d\cdot w$. When all legs are at a single site, any nonzero tensor is a vector there, is equivalent to the unit under local maps, and has price zero.

The reader now has the dictionary used in §1.3: $H(T_n)=(\text{perimeter})\log n$ because $T_n$ is a product of three wires, and $H(T_n)\le\delta(\{A,B,C\})\log r$ when $\operatorname{rank}(T_n)\le r$.

3.2 Geometric extension and spectral comparison pp. 11–12

Definition (spectral space). p. 11 paper A spectral space is a metric space with compatible prices for all finite placements of tensor legs at its points, whose wire rates are the given distances. Compatibility allows trivial legs to be added and legs at coincident sites to be grouped. On a finite set of positive diameter the scale is its common-label rate, which may vary with the set. Identifying sites at zero wire distance turns a spectral price on finitely many sites into a spectral space.

So far the “geometry” is a finite metric space. The step that makes geometric arguments possible is an embedding theorem from the companion paper.

Imported — [42, Sections 2 and 2.3, Theorem 4.3 and Proposition 3.3]. p. 12 imported
  1. Every spectral space embeds, with all its prices, in a complete CAT(0) spectral space. In the extension every pair $a,b$ has a unique midpoint $m$, and every point $o$ satisfies $$d(o,m)^2\le\tfrac12d(o,a)^2+\tfrac12d(o,b)^2-\tfrac14d(a,b)^2.$$
  2. Prices are jointly convex as their legs move along geodesics.
  3. Distances and prices may be rescaled together.
  4. In a pointed metric ultralimit of such spaces, the price of a fixed tensor on a bounded sequence of placements is the ultralimit of its prices.
Imported — [42, Section 2] and [42, Lemma 2.2]. p. 12 imported
  1. Over an algebraically closed field, price sublevel sets in a fixed tensor format are Zariski closed.
  2. Under a field extension, the possible joint values of spectral points on tensors defined over the original field are preserved.
  3. Strict spectral comparison. If two nonzero tensors on the same sites satisfy $\varphi(T)\lt\varphi(U)$ for every spectral point, there is a fixed integer $C$ such that, for every $j\ge1$, $T^{\otimes j}$ is a restriction of the direct sum of $C$ copies of $U^{\otimes j}$.

The preprint also uses standard facts about CAT(0) spaces from [12]: convexity of the distance function, nonexpansiveness of the nearest-point projection onto a nonempty closed convex set, and the existence of a unique barycenter of a finitely supported probability measure, with the variance inequality stated below.

Background — CAT(0) spaces in one page. filled here

A geodesic metric space is one where any two points $a,b$ are joined by a path of length $d(a,b)$. It is CAT(0) (a Hadamard space when complete) if every geodesic triangle is at least as thin as the Euclidean triangle with the same side lengths. An equivalent form is the midpoint inequality of item 1 above (the Bruhat–Tits or CN inequality). In the Euclidean plane it holds with equality: it is the parallelogram law for the median. In a CAT(0) space the median is at most as long as in the plane.

Examples. Euclidean and Hilbert spaces (equality). Metric trees, where every triangle is a tripod (strict inequality in general: for the tripod with unit arms and $o$ the third tip, the left side is $1$ and the right side is $\frac12\cdot4+\frac12\cdot4-\frac14\cdot4=3$). Products of CAT(0) spaces with the $\ell^2$ metric. Symmetric spaces of noncompact type and Euclidean buildings.

Consequences used in the preprint. (a) Geodesics are unique. (b) For geodesics $\gamma,\gamma'$ parametrized proportionally to arc length, $t\mapsto d(\gamma(t),\gamma'(t))$ is convex. (c) For a closed convex set $C$ there is a unique nearest point $\pi_C(x)$, and $d(\pi_C(x),\pi_C(y))\le d(x,y)$. (d) For points $x_i$ with weights $\mu_i\gt0$, $\sum\mu_i=1$, the function $z\mapsto\sum_i\mu_id(z,x_i)^2$ has a unique minimizer $b$, the barycenter, and $\sum_i\mu_id(z,x_i)^2\ge\sum_i\mu_id(b,x_i)^2+d(z,b)^2$ for every $z$. (e) A bounded group of isometries has a fixed point: the circumcenter of an orbit (the centre of the unique smallest closed ball containing it) is fixed by the group. Fact (e) is used in §7.1.

Background — ultralimits, and why they cost effectivity. filled here

Fix a nonprincipal ultrafilter $\mathcal U$ on $\mathbb N$. Every bounded sequence of reals $(t_n)$ has a unique limit $\lim_{\mathcal U}t_n$ along $\mathcal U$; it is a limit point of the sequence, chosen consistently so that $\lim_{\mathcal U}$ is linear and monotone. Given pointed metric spaces $(X_n,d_n,o_n)$, consider sequences $(x_n)$ with $d_n(x_n,o_n)$ bounded, declare $d\bigl((x_n),(y_n)\bigr)=\lim_{\mathcal U}d_n(x_n,y_n)$, and identify sequences at distance zero. The result is the ultralimit, a complete metric space. An ultralimit of CAT(0) spaces is CAT(0), because the midpoint inequality passes to limits.

The preprint uses ultralimits in two ways. With rescaling $d_n=\lambda_nd$ and $\lambda_n\to\infty$ it produces tangent cones: the geometry seen at infinitesimal scale near a point (§7.2, §8.2). And it uses them for compactness: a family of spaces with no uniform bound of some kind has an ultralimit that violates the corresponding statement outright. Arguments of the second kind prove that a constant exists and give no value for it. This is the source of the non-effective constants in Parts II and IV.

Not verified — everything imported from [42] in this section. not verified The CAT(0) embedding with joint convexity of prices is the foundation of all geometric arguments in Part I. It is a theorem of the companion preprint [42], which this project did not read. The same holds for Zariski-closedness of price sublevel sets and for strict spectral comparison.

4. The cost of testing many lists pp. 12–15

Role in the chain. This section proves Theorem 2.2, the lower bound that the compression theorem will contradict. It does not use the hypothesis $\omega\lt9/4$. It reduces list distinctness to routing tagged records, then splits into two cases according to a property of the field called ports. Each case ends by citing a theorem of a companion paper.

4.1 Checking a tagged permutation pp. 12–13

Definition (tagged-permutation tensor). p. 12 paper Write $h=w-1$, $N=2^h$, and index records by the $h$-bit words. The tensor $\operatorname{Tag}(h,h)$ of [42, Definition 8.1] takes $N$ records $(j_i,x_i)$ and an ordered list of $N$ payloads $y_j$, with $h$ bits per tag $j_i$ and per payload. Its value is one when $i\mapsto j_i$ is a permutation and $x_i=y_{j_i}$ for every $i$. Its other entries are zero.

$\operatorname{Tag}$ checks that every record $(j_i,x_i)$ has been delivered to the address named by its tag. The preprint shows that two distinctness tests suffice to compute it.

First test: the tags form a permutation. Encode each tag $j_i$ as the word $(0,j_i,0^{h+1})$ and fill the remaining $N$ positions with the words $(1,j,0^{h+1})$, one for each $h$-bit word $j$. All $2N=2^w$ words have length $1+h+(h+1)=2w$. The filler words are pairwise distinct and differ from every encoded tag in the first bit. So $D_w$ accepts exactly when the $j_i$ are pairwise distinct, that is, when $i\mapsto j_i$ is a permutation $\pi$ of the $N$ addresses.

Second test: the payloads match. The first $N$ words are $(j_i,0,x_i,0)$, of length $h+1+h+1=2w$. At the position for address $j$ in the second half, sum over two choices: the reserve word $(j,1,0^h,0)$ with weight $+1$, and the comparison word $(j,0,y_j,0)$ with weight $-1$. Assume the tags form $\pi$. Words in the first half are distinct, words in the second half are distinct, and a first-half word can equal a second-half word only at the address $j=j_i$, only if the comparison word was chosen there, and only if $x_i=y_j$. For each address the two choices therefore contribute $1$ (reserve: no collision) and $-\mathbf 1\{x_{\pi^{-1}(j)}\ne y_j\}$ (comparison: accepted only if there is no collision). The signed sum of $D_w$ over all $2^N$ choice patterns factors as $$\prod_j\bigl(1-\mathbf 1_{\{x_{\pi^{-1}(j)}\ne y_j\}}\bigr)=\prod_j\mathbf 1_{\{x_{\pi^{-1}(j)}=y_j\}}.$$ This identity holds in every characteristic.

Cost. In the network, a summed selector bit for each address controls its word and its weight. Copying the tags, selecting the words and pinning the constants costs $O(Nh)$ trivalent volume per list. Hence, if $D_w^{\otimes t}$ has a realization of volume $V$, two uses of it give a network for $\operatorname{Tag}(h,h)^{\otimes t}$ of volume at most $2V+O(tNh)$, also when the realization shares work among lists.∎

4.2 Fields without ports pp. 13–14

Definition (ports). p. 13 paper A class of Hadamard spaces has ports if it admits regular graphs of unbounded order and common bounded degree with the following property: for every placement of their vertices into any space of the class, the mean squared distance of two independent uniform vertices is at most a fixed constant times the mean squared edge length. A field has ports when its Hadamard spectral spaces have this property [42, Definition 11.1].
Background — what the definition says. filled here For maps into a Hilbert space the stated inequality is the Poincaré inequality $\mathbb E_{u,v}\|f(u)-f(v)\|^2\le C\,\mathbb E_{u\sim v}\|f(u)-f(v)\|^2$, which holds with a uniform $C$ for a family of bounded-degree graphs exactly when the family is a family of expanders. “Ports” asks for graphs that are expanders with respect to every Hadamard spectral space of the field. If the field has ports, such graphs provide cheap long-range connections. If it has none, every bounded-degree graph can be placed with short edges and typical vertex pairs far apart, and this is what the next lemma exploits. The preprint does not decide which case holds for a given field; it proves Theorem 2.2 in both.
Lemma 4.1 (Uniform destinations). p. 13 paper Let the field have no ports. Consider a fixed trivalent topology with $r$ input bits and $r$ output bits. Suppose that for every permutation in the support of a distribution, some choice of vertex tables realizes the $r$ independent wires joining each input to its assigned output. If each input has a uniform destination on a set of $q\ge2$ outputs, the topology has size $\Omega(r\sqrt{\log q})$. The destination sets may depend on the input.

Step 1: a price inequality for any placement. Give each exterior bit its own equality leaf and connect the components in a path by adding edges; this raises the maximum degree by at most two and gives a graph with $m$ edges, $m$ at most a constant times the network size. Let $\alpha$ be the law of a uniform oriented edge and $\mu$ the joint law of a uniform input and its random destination. For any placement of the vertices in a spectral space with distance $d$, $$r\,\mathbb E_\mu d\le m\,\mathbb E_\alpha d.$$ Reason: for a fixed permutation $\pi$ the network, with its tables, is a restriction of the tensor product of its internal bit wires (each vertex table is a linear map at that vertex’s site), and it represents the product of the $r$ wires from each input to its destination. Monotonicity and additivity of prices give $\sum_id(\text{in}_i,\text{out}_{\pi(i)})\log2\le\sum_{\text{edges}}(\text{length})\log2$. Average over $\pi$.

Step 2: a placement where destinations are far. The class of Hadamard spectral spaces is closed under isometry, rescaling, closed convex subspaces and pointed ultralimits (§3.2). The product theorem [43, Theorem 6.1] gives a subclass with the same properties and no ports, closed also under finite $\ell^2$ products. Let $\mathcal K$ be the set of squared-distance tables $\mathsf D=d^2$ on the vertices that are realized in this subclass with $\mathbb E_\alpha\mathsf D\le1$. Products of rescaled placements realize convex combinations of tables, connectivity bounds every entry, and ultralimits realize coordinatewise limits. So $\mathcal K$ is convex and compact.

Step 3: entropy forces distance. For a probability law $\nu\le2\mu$ on ordered pairs let $\beta_\nu=\max_{\mathsf D\in\mathcal K}\mathbb E_\nu\mathsf D$. Write $X,Y$ for the endpoints under $\nu$. The graph entropy bound [43, Corollary 5.2] and bounded degree give $H_\nu(Y\mid X)\le C(1+\beta_\nu)$, with entropy in bits. Every pair has $\nu$-mass at most $2/(rq)$, so $$H_\nu(Y\mid X)=H_\nu(X,Y)-H_\nu(X)\ge\log_2(rq/2)-\log_2r=\log_2(q/2).$$ Hence $\beta_\nu\ge c\log q$ for all sufficiently large $q$, uniformly in $\nu$.

Step 4: one table for all laws. The function $(\mathsf D,\nu)\mapsto\mathbb E_\nu\mathsf D$ is bilinear on the product of two compact convex sets, so the minimax theorem gives a single $\mathsf D=d^2\in\mathcal K$ with $\mathbb E_\nu\mathsf D\ge c\log q$ for every law $\nu\le2\mu$. If the pairs with $\mathsf D\lt c\log q$ had $\mu$-mass at least $1/2$, the law $\mu$ conditioned on them would be a law $\nu\le2\mu$ violating this. So at least half the $\mu$-mass has $d\ge\sqrt{c\log q}$ and $\mathbb E_\mu d\ge\frac12\sqrt{c\log q}$, while $\mathbb E_\alpha d\le(\mathbb E_\alpha d^2)^{1/2}\le1$. Step 1 gives $m\ge\frac12r\sqrt{c\log q}$. For bounded $q$, counting exterior bits suffices.∎

Imported — [43, Theorem 6.1] (product theorem) and [43, Corollary 5.2] (graph entropy bound). imported Used in Steps 2 and 3 exactly as quoted there. This tutorial does not know their precise statements in [43].

Conclusion of the case without ports (p. 14). In a network for $\operatorname{Tag}(h,h)^{\otimes t}$, pin each list’s tags to a uniform random permutation. The topology then realizes a permutation of $r=tNh$ payload bits, and each input bit has $q=N$ equally likely destinations (its own bit position in its own list). Lemma 4.1 gives $2V+O(tNh)\ge c\,tNh\sqrt h$. Since $n_w=2w\,2^w=4N(h+1)$, this is $V\ge c'\,t\,n_w\sqrt w$ and proves (2.2) for any fixed $0\lt\sigma\lt1/2$, uniformly in $t$.

4.3 Finite fields with ports pp. 14–15

Fix a finite field with ports. The argument here is a small-scale copy of the main proof: cheap distinctness tests would compress a family of tensors below a known lower bound.

Imported — [43, Theorem 12.1] (exact batch lower bound). p. 14 imported Let $U^*_\ell$ be the signed counting tensor on $\ell$ exterior bits from [43]: its entries count the signed computation paths of a polynomially clocked universal machine, reduced in the field. One copy has an exact trivalent network of size $\ell^{O(1)}$. There are $\delta,\xi\gt0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $\ell$, $$\operatorname{vol}_k\bigl((U^*_\ell)^{\otimes j}\bigr)\gt j\,\ell^{1+\delta}\qquad(1\le j\le2^{\ell^\xi}).$$
Imported — [42, Proposition 14.1] (descent with tagged permutations). p. 14 imported There is a constant $a\gt0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $h$, given a trivalent network of size $s$ in the range $N\le s\le N^4$ with $e$ exterior bits, one can construct a network for $d$ copies of its tensor using trivalent gates and $\operatorname{Tag}(h,h)$ tests. Its size $S$, charging every incident bit including those at tests, satisfies $$S\le Cd\bigl(sh^{-a}+e(\log h)^C\bigr),\qquad\log_2d=O\bigl((\log h)^2\bigr).$$ The bounds are uniform in the network.

Constants. Choose $b\gt\max\{2,2/\xi\}$ and $0\lt\sigma\lt\min\{a/4,\,b\delta/4,\,1/4\}$. Suppose, for contradiction, that for arbitrarily large $w=h+1$ some $1\le t\le2N$ admits a realization of $D_w^{\otimes t}$ of volume at most $t\,n_ww^\sigma$. By §4.1 this gives a network for $t$ tagged-permutation tests of size $O(tNh^{1+\sigma})$.

Substitution. In a descent network, group the $\operatorname{Tag}$ tests into batches of $t$ and substitute the cheap realization for each batch. In the last batch, pin unused tests to the identity permutation with zero payloads, where each has value one. The substituted network realizes the full product tensor, so the replacement is exact under every contraction of the surrounding wires. Each full batch contributes $3tNh$ incident bits to $S$, so the resulting trivalent size is $O\bigl(h^\sigma S+tNh^{1+\sigma}\bigr)$.

Iteration. Set $\ell=\lceil h^b\rceil$ and begin with $j=N^2$ copies of the polynomial-size network for $U^*_\ell$. Let $x=s/(j\ell)$ be the size per exterior bit. After one descent with $d$ new copies, $$x'\le C'\Bigl(h^{-a+\sigma}x+h^{\sigma}(\log h)^C+\frac{tNh^{1+\sigma}}{dj\ell}\Bigr)\le h^{-a/2}x+h^{2\sigma}.$$ For the last term: $j\ge N^2$, $d\ge1$, $t\le2N$ and $\ell\ge h^b$ give a fraction at most $2h^{1+\sigma-b}$, which is $o(1)$ since $b\gt2$. This is why the iteration starts with $N^2$ copies. For the first term, $\sigma\lt a/4$ gives $C'h^{-a+\sigma}\le h^{-a/2}$ for large $h$.

Conclusion. Initially $x\le h^A$ for a fixed $A$. Take a fixed integer $L\gt2A/a$ and iterate $L$ times; the product of the replication factors is $N^{o(1)}$, so $j=N^{2+o(1)}$ and the size stays in the range of the descent. The recurrence gives $x=O(h^{2\sigma})$. Now $j\le2^{\ell^\xi}$, because $\log_2j=(2+o(1))h$ while $\ell^\xi\ge h^{b\xi}$ with $b\xi\gt2$. And $h^{2\sigma}=o(\ell^\delta)$, because $2\sigma\lt b\delta/2$. The final trivalent volume is $o(j\,\ell^{1+\delta})$, contradicting the batch lower bound.∎

Not verified — Theorem 2.2 rests on the companion papers. not verified Both cases of Theorem 2.2 end by citing [42] or [43]: the product theorem and graph entropy bound in the case without ports; the exact batch lower bound for $U^*_\ell$ and the tagged-permutation descent in the case with ports. The batch lower bound is an unconditional super-linear lower bound, with exponent $1+\delta$, on the trivalent tensor-network volume of an explicit family. By the remark in §2.1 that tensor networks generalize circuits, this is a strong statement of circuit-lower-bound type, and it is a separate claim from the matrix-multiplication theorem. The steps written above were checked for internal consistency of the exponents. The role of the bound $\sigma\lt1/4$ is not explained in the preprint and was not reconstructed.
Part I — Sphere tensors and their prices Paper §5–15, pp. 15–58. Sections 5–11 are unconditional. The hypothesis $\omega\lt9/4$ enters in §12.

5. Price extensions and simultaneous minimization pp. 15–20

Role in the chain. The compression argument introduces new sites at which to place tensor legs. A price on the old sites must be extended to the new ones. This section answers two questions. How cheap can a given tensor be made by a good choice of extension (Theorem 5.1, first part)? When can one extension make a whole family of tensors cheap at once (Theorem 5.1, second part)? Lemma 5.3 then makes the optimal new sites unique, so that they can be compared as a parameter varies. §7 uses Lemma 5.2; §12 uses Theorem 5.1 and Lemma 5.3; §14 uses Theorem 5.1 through Corollary 12.2.

5.1 Integral restrictions pp. 15–16

In §12 the tensors will depend on a parameter, and the parameter will be treated as an element of a field with a valuation. So the extension theory is developed for prices over valued fields from the start.

Background — nonarchimedean valuations. filled here A real additive valuation on a field $K$ is a map $\nu:K\to\mathbb R\cup\{+\infty\}$ with $\nu(0)=+\infty$, $\nu(ab)=\nu(a)+\nu(b)$ and $\nu(a+b)\ge\min\{\nu(a),\nu(b)\}$, with equality when $\nu(a)\ne\nu(b)$. Think of $\nu(a)$ as the order of vanishing of $a$: large valuation means small. Examples: the $p$-adic valuation on $\mathbb Q$; the order of vanishing at $t=0$ on $F(t)$; and the example used throughout the preprint, the field $K=\operatorname{Frac}(E[t^{\mathbb Q}])$ of fractions of finite sums of rational powers of $t$ with coefficients in a field $E$, with $\nu$ = least exponent, so that $\nu(t^r)=r$ and nonzero constants have valuation $0$. The valuation ring is $\mathcal O=\{c:\nu(c)\ge0\}$; its units are the elements of valuation $0$; two elements of $\mathcal O$ have the same residue when their difference has positive valuation. The trivial valuation has $\nu(c)=0$ for all $c\ne0$.
Definition (valued price). p. 15 paper Let $K$ have valuation $\nu$ and valuation ring $\mathcal O$, and use the given coordinate bases at all sites. A restriction is integral if each local matrix has entries in $\mathcal O$. A valued price $p$ of scale $D\gt0$ on $S$ is real on nonzero tensors, has $p(0)=-\infty$, $p(\mathbf 1)=0$, is nonincreasing under integral restriction, and satisfies $$p(cT)=p(T)-\nu(c)\ (c\ne0),\qquad p(T\otimes U)=p(T)+p(U),\qquad p\Bigl(\bigoplus_jT_j\Bigr)=D\log\sum_je^{p(T_j)/D}.\tag{5.1}$$ Multiplying by a scalar of positive valuation lowers the price. The trivial valuation recovers the ordinary prices of §3.
Gap fill — the direct-sum rule from the sum bound. filled here

The preprint constructs prices by checking a weaker-looking property, the ordinary sum bound: for tensors in common spaces, $p(\sum_jT_j)\le D\log\sum_je^{p(T_j)/D}$. It asserts (p. 16) that this, with $p(\Delta_S(q))=D\log q$, implies the direct-sum identity. Here is the argument.

Upper bound. The block inclusions express $\bigoplus_jT_j$ as an ordinary sum of the $T_j$ placed in separate blocks, so the sum bound applies.

Lower bound. Fix a probability vector $r$ with rational entries and $m$ with all $mr_j$ integral. Expand $(\bigoplus_jT_j)^{\otimes m}$ and project, at every site, onto the block-words in which summand $j$ occurs $mr_j$ times. After permuting tensor positions at every site simultaneously, the result is $\bigotimes_jT_j^{\otimes mr_j}$ tensored with a diagonal $\Delta_S(M)$, where $M=m!/\prod_j(mr_j)!$ counts the words. Monotonicity and additivity give $m\,p(\bigoplus_jT_j)\ge\sum_jmr_j\,p(T_j)+D\log M$. By Stirling, $\frac1m\log M\to-\sum_jr_j\log r_j$. Hence $$p\Bigl(\bigoplus_jT_j\Bigr)\ \ge\ \sup_r\Bigl(\sum_jr_j\,p(T_j)-D\sum_jr_j\log r_j\Bigr)=D\log\sum_je^{p(T_j)/D}.$$ The last equality is the Gibbs variational principle: the supremum is attained at $r_j\propto e^{p(T_j)/D}$.

For $T\ne0$ with local dimensions $q_i$ and minimum entry valuation $\nu_{\min}(T)$, $$-\nu_{\min}(T)\le p(T)\le-\nu_{\min}(T)+D\sum_i\log q_i.\tag{5.2}$$ The lower bound projects to an entry of minimum valuation. The upper bound expands $T$ in at most $\prod_iq_i$ coordinate tensors and uses the sum bound. Consequently the valued prices of a fixed scale form a compact space in the topology of pointwise convergence: the axioms are closed conditions in a product of the compact intervals (5.2). Compactness is used repeatedly below.

5.2 A test for extending a price pp. 16–18

Fix $p$ on $S$ and adjoin a finite set $L$ of new sites. An extension $\widehat p$ is a valued price of the same scale on $S\sqcup L$ that agrees with $p$ on tensors with trivial legs at $L$. Extensions exist: place each new site at an old one.

Definition (closure, assisted cost). p. 16 paper A closure applies one integral linear functional at each site of $L$ and leaves all base legs free. The assisted cost of a tensor $T$ on $S\sqcup L$ is $$\beta_p(T)=\sup_{m\ge1}\frac1m\sup_\ell\ p\bigl(\ell(T^{\otimes m})\bigr),\tag{5.3}$$ where $\ell$ runs over closures of $T^{\otimes m}$. At a site of dimension $q$ the functional acts on $K^{q^m}$, so it may couple the $m$ copies arbitrarily.

A closure removes the new legs and leaves a tensor on the old sites, where the price is known. Every extension satisfies $\widehat p(T)\ge\beta_p(T)$: a closure is an integral restriction, so $\widehat p(T^{\otimes m})\ge p(\ell(T^{\otimes m}))$, and $\widehat p(T^{\otimes m})=m\,\widehat p(T)$. The assisted cost is thus the obvious lower bound on the price of $T$ in any extension.

Example (p. 16). Take two wires of size $q$, from $a$ to $y$ and from $y$ to $b$, with $a,b$ old and $y$ new. Closing the two legs at $y$ by $e_i\otimes e_j\mapsto\delta_{ij}$ recovers the wire from $a$ to $b$. So $\beta\ge d(a,b)\log q$. Placing $y$ at $a$, or anywhere on the geodesic from $a$ to $b$, attains this value.

Theorem 5.1 (Price extension). p. 16 paper For every tensor $T$ on $S\sqcup L$, $\ \beta_p(T)=\min_{\widehat p}\widehat p(T)$. For an arbitrary family of nonzero tensors $T_i$ and real numbers $h_i$, a single extension satisfies $\widehat p(T_i)\le h_i$ for all $i$ if and only if $$\beta_p(T_{i_1}\otimes\cdots\otimes T_{i_j})\le h_{i_1}+\cdots+h_{i_j}\tag{5.4}$$ for every finite list of indices, with repetitions allowed.
AHA 6. Price extension is a duality theorem. The obvious lower bound (close the new legs, evaluate what remains, allow tensor powers) is sharp. And a family of bounds is simultaneously satisfiable exactly when every finite product passes the obvious test. The second statement is the form used later: to place a new site well for infinitely many tensors at once, it suffices to bound closures of their products.

Part 1, minimizing one tensor. Setup. Let $A_1,\dots,A_s$ be distinct nonzero tensors, $T$ among them. For $x\in\mathbb Q^s_{\ge0}$ put $$b(x)=\frac1n\,\beta_p\Bigl(\bigotimes_iA_i^{\otimes nx_i}\Bigr),\qquad nx\in\mathbb Z^s.$$ The value does not depend on $n$ because assisted cost is homogeneous under powers (in (5.3) the supremum can be restricted to multiples of any integer by repeating a closure independently). Taking independent closures on two factors shows $b(x+y)\ge b(x)+b(y)$. A superadditive, positively homogeneous function is concave. The increment of $b$ per unit of coordinate $i$ lies in the interval (5.2) for $A_i$, so $b$ is Lipschitz and extends continuously to the real orthant.

The price is a supporting hyperplane. At a strictly positive rational $x$ choose a linear form $u$ with $b(y)\le u\cdot y$ for all $y$ and $b(x)=u\cdot x$. The candidate price of $A_i$ is $u_i$. The supporting inequality, applied in suitable directions, shows that $u$ obeys every price axiom that relates tensors of the list:

  • Products. If $A_i=A_j\otimes A_k$ then $b(x+t(e_j+e_k-e_i))=b(x)$ for small $t$ of both signs, because the two exponent vectors describe the same tensor. The inequality $b(y)\le u\cdot y$ in both directions forces $u_i=u_j+u_k$.
  • Scalars. The same argument with the shift $-\nu(c)$ per copy.
  • Monotonicity. If $A_i$ restricts integrally to $A_j$, replacing copies of $A_j$ by copies of $A_i$ can only increase $b$, so the direction $e_i-e_j$ gives $u_i\ge u_j$.
  • Base tensors. A tensor on $S$ passes unchanged through every closure, so its increment is exactly its price $p$.
  • The full diagonal. Closing the added legs of $\Delta_{S\sqcup L}(q)$ by all-ones functionals yields $\Delta_S(q)$, and expanding its $q$ diagonal terms gives the reverse bound. Its increment is $D\log q$.
  • Sum bound. If $A_i=A_j+A_k$, expand $n\sigma$ copies of $A_i$ binomially. A term with $a$ copies of $A_j$ has closed price at most $nu\cdot x-n\sigma u_i+au_j+(n\sigma-a)u_k$. The sum bound on the base and the binomial formula give $u_i\le D\log(e^{u_j/D}+e^{u_k/D})$.

Limit and compactness. Let $x$ tend to the unit vector of $T$. The vectors $u$ lie in a compact set and a limit point has $u_T=\beta_p(T)$ by continuity of $b$. So every finite set of price relations, together with the equality $\widehat p(T)=\beta_p(T)$, is satisfiable. Compactness of the product of the intervals (5.2) gives one assignment satisfying all relations. By the diagonal test of §5.1 it is a valued price.

Part 2, several bounds. Necessity of (5.4) is additivity. For sufficiency take finitely many bounds $i=1,\dots,k$ and form $U=\bigoplus_iN_i\cdot T_i^{\otimes n}$ ($N_i$ copies). In a power of $U$ each word of summands is a mixed product, and a closure restricted to the blocks of that word is an integral closure, whose price is bounded by (5.4). Summing over words gives $\beta_p(U)\le D\log\sum_iN_ie^{nh_i/D}$. Choose $C\gt\max_ih_i$ and $N_i=\lceil e^{n(C-h_i)/D}\rceil$, so that every $N_ie^{nh_i/D}$ lies in $[e^{nC/D},2e^{nC/D}]$. At an extension minimizing $U$ (Part 1) the direct-sum identity gives $$D\log N_i+n\,\widehat p(T_i)\le\beta_p(U)\le nC+D\log(2k),\qquad\text{so}\qquad\widehat p(T_i)\le h_i+\frac{D\log(2k)}n.$$ Let $n\to\infty$ and use compactness, first for the finite list and then, by the finite intersection property, for the whole family.∎

Background — the shape of this proof. filled here The argument is the pattern of Strassen’s spectral theorem and of linear-programming duality. A quantity defined as a supremum over “tests” ($b$) is concave and homogeneous in the exponents of formal products. A supporting hyperplane of a concave function at a point is a linear functional that dominates the function and touches it there (the finite-dimensional Hahn–Banach theorem). A linear functional on exponents that respects products, sums and restriction is, by definition, a price. So the dual object to “all closures of all powers” is “a price extension”, and touching at $T$ means the extension attains $\beta_p(T)$. The trick in Part 2, a direct sum with multiplicities $N_i$ tuned so that all summands weigh the same, converts a simultaneous problem into a single minimization.

Two consequences (p. 18). (i) Given several extensions of $p$, there is a single extension whose evaluations are bounded above by any fixed convex combination of theirs: each extension bounds the assisted cost of every mixed product, so the averaged bounds pass (5.4). (ii) The base may be enlarged before applying the test: a tensor supported on the old base and the unknown sites has the same closures when extra base sites carry trivial legs, so its assisted cost is unchanged.

5.3 Geodesic placement p. 18

From here to the end of §5, $K$ contains a trivially valued subfield $F$. Consequence (i) produces geodesics. Given pairs of sites $a_j,b_j$, place new sites $y_j$ first at the $a_j$ and then at the $b_j$, and take an extension bounded by the average of the two with weights $1-\theta,\theta$. Applied to wires this gives $d(a_j,y_j)\le\theta\,d(a_j,b_j)$ and $d(y_j,b_j)\le(1-\theta)\,d(a_j,b_j)$. The triangle inequality forces equality in both. So $y_j$ is the point a fraction $\theta$ of the way along the geodesic from $a_j$ to $b_j$ in a complete CAT(0) spectral realization over $F$, and the same extension gives the interpolation bound for all tensors over $K$. This is the mechanism behind the joint convexity of prices along geodesics.

Lemma 5.2 (Geodesic determination and submean inequality). p. 18 paper Ordinary price data on any subset of a complete CAT(0) spectral space determine all prices on its closed geodesic convex hull, up to a price-preserving isometry fixing the given data. Moreover, suppose the prices at a central placement are at most the weighted average of prices at finitely many end placements, for every tensor on the original slots. In each placement adjoin points on corresponding geodesic segments. If the two distances of each new point to its endpoints at the center are the weighted averages of their values at the ends, the price inequality holds on the enlarged placements.

First assertion. For each new site prescribe its two distances to the endpoints of its segment (as prices of size-two wires). The claim is that the price of any $T\ne0$ on the enlarged list is the smallest $h$ for which the bound $h$ and the wire bounds pass the product test (5.4). The actual price passes, by closure. Conversely, apply Theorem 5.1 to duplicate sites carrying those bounds; in a common metric realization the triangle inequality forces the duplicate to have the prescribed distances, uniqueness of geodesics identifies it with the actual point, and transport (3.1) shows the prices agree. Iterating determines prices on all finite geodesic constructions; continuity and (3.1) extend to the closed hull.

Second assertion. Fix a product in the test, a power and a closure at the new sites. The assumed submean inequality bounds the central price of the closed tensor by the average of its closed prices at the ends, each of which is at most the price of the unclosed power there. So the averaged upper bounds pass the product test at the center, and the averaged wire bounds are the prescribed ones. The characterization of the first part concludes.∎

What the lemma is for. In §7 a group acts on a family of sites, and an inequality between prices (the star inequality) is known for tensors placed at the sites themselves. Lemma 5.2 transfers both the group action and the inequality to every point of the convex hull, for example to midpoints and to nearest-point projections.

5.4 Unique evaluations at minimizing sites pp. 19–20

A price minimum can be attained by several placements, as the two-wire example shows (any $y$ on the geodesic). To compare minimizers as a parameter varies, the preprint adds a strictly convex penalty at each unknown site. Fix a base site $o$.
