For a new site $y$, form the direct sum of $\mathbf 1$ and the size-two wire from $o$ to $y$. Its price is, by the direct-sum rule with $p(\mathbf 1)=0$ and $p(\text{wire})=r\log2$, $$f(r)=D\log\bigl(1+\exp(r\log(2)/D)\bigr),\qquad r=d(o,y).$$ For a nonzero tensor $U$ on the base and a list of new sites, and an integer $M\ge1$, the pin objective $V$ is the product of $U^{\otimes M}$ with one such penalty tensor for each new site.

Lemma 5.3 (Pinned evaluations). p. 19 paper For a finite family of objectives on pairwise disjoint lists of new sites, there is a same-scale extension minimizing each objective. All such extensions have the same evaluations on every tensor on the joint list. These evaluations agree on overlaps between subfamilies. For any such family, including an infinite one, the resulting evaluations on tensors over $F$ are realized in a complete CAT(0) spectral space.

Let $W$ be the product of a finite family, $G=\sum_V\beta_p(V)$, and $h_T$ the pinned evaluation of a nonzero test tensor $T$. For some $c_D\gt0$ depending only on $D$, and every $n\ge1$, $$0\le h_T-\bigl(\beta_p(W^{\otimes n}\otimes T)-nG\bigr)\le\frac{\sum_j(\log q_j)^2}{8c_Dn},\tag{5.5}$$ where $q_j$ are the dimensions of the test legs grouped at the added sites.

Existence. Enlarge the base successively and minimize the next objective with Theorem 5.1; consequence (ii) shows each minimization still attains its original minimum.

Uniqueness comes from strict convexity. For two sites $a,b$ with midpoint $m$, $$f(d(o,m))\le\tfrac12\bigl(f(d(o,a))+f(d(o,b))\bigr)-c_D\,d(a,b)^2.\tag{5.6}$$ To see this, note that wire distances are at most $D$ and that $f',f''$ have positive lower bounds on $[0,D]$. Choose $c_D$ so small that $g(r)=f(r)-4c_Dr^2$ is convex and nondecreasing on $[0,D]$. Then $f(d(o,m))=g(d(o,m))+4c_Dd(o,m)^2$. Apply convexity of the distance and of $g$ to the first term, and the CAT(0) midpoint inequality to the second: $4c_Dd(o,m)^2\le4c_D\bigl(\frac12d(o,a)^2+\frac12d(o,b)^2\bigr)-c_Dd(a,b)^2$. If two assignments minimize an objective, interpolate all their sites at midpoints (consequence (i)): the price of $U^{\otimes M}$ is at most the mean, and each penalty drops by $c_D\,d(a,b)^2$. The midpoint would beat the minimum unless all the distances vanish.

The limit formula (5.5). Adjoin an optimizing copy to an arbitrary competing assignment and let $d_j$ be the distances between corresponding sites. By (5.6), $p_{\rm comp}(W)-G\ge2c_D\sum_jd_j^2$. By transport, $p_{\rm comp}(T)\ge h_T-\sum_jd_j\log q_j$. Multiply the first by $n$ and add: $$p_{\rm comp}(W^{\otimes n}\otimes T)-nG\ \ge\ h_T+\sum_j\bigl(2c_Dn\,d_j^2-d_j\log q_j\bigr)\ \ge\ h_T-\sum_j\frac{(\log q_j)^2}{8c_Dn},$$ since $\min_d(\alpha d^2-\beta d)=-\beta^2/(4\alpha)$ with $\alpha=2c_Dn$, $\beta=\log q_j$. Minimizing over competitors (Theorem 5.1) gives the upper bound in (5.5). The optimizing assignment itself gives the lower bound.∎

Formula (5.5) is the bridge used in §12: the price of any test tensor at the pinned sites is the limit of an expression built only from assisted costs $\beta_p$, and inequalities known for $\beta_p$ transfer to it.

6. A valued lift and a submean inequality pp. 20–25

Role in the chain. §5 assumed that a valued price exists. This section constructs one from any ordinary price (Theorem 6.1) and proves the inequality that drives §7 and §12: for a tensor depending polynomially on a parameter $c$, the price at the generic point of a disk of parameters is at most a weighted average of the prices on neighbouring disks (Lemma 6.2, the star inequality).

6.1 The Gauss lift pp. 20–23

Background — the Gauss norm. filled here For a polynomial $f=\sum_ia_ix^i$ over a valued field, the Gauss valuation is $\nu(f)=\min_i\nu(a_i)$. Gauss’s lemma says it is multiplicative, $\nu(fg)=\nu(f)+\nu(g)$, so it extends to a valuation on rational functions. Geometrically it is the valuation of $f$ at the generic point of the unit disk: for $c$ in the disk, $\nu(f(c))\ge\nu(f)$, with equality for all $c$ outside finitely many residue classes. The preprint needs the same construction with tensors in place of scalar coefficients and an ordinary price $H$ in place of “$-\nu$ of a coefficient”. Multiplicativity is the hard part, as in Gauss’s lemma: the largest coefficients of two factors could cancel in the product.

Let $H$ be an ordinary price of scale $D$ over a field $E$ and put $\phi(A)=\exp(H(A)/D)$. Let $K=\operatorname{Frac}(E[t^{\mathbb Q}])$ with $\nu$ = least exponent, and write $[t^j]T$ for the coefficient tensor of $t^j$. A monomial tensor $t^jA$ should have price $H(A)-j$.

Theorem 6.1 (Gauss lift). p. 20 paper There is a valued price $p$ of scale $D$ on $K$ extending $H$. On tensors with entries in $E[t^{\mathbb Q}]$ it is given by $$p(T)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\max_{j\in\mathbb Q}\bigl(H([t^j]T^{\otimes n})-j\bigr).\tag{6.1}$$

Step 1: definition through a weighted sum. Write $x=t^{1/m}$ for a common denominator $m$, give $x$ a weight $b\gt0$, and for a Laurent tensor $A(x)$ put $N_b(A)=\sum_ie^{-bi/D}\phi([x^i]A)$ and $p_b(A)=\lim_n\frac Dn\log N_b(A^{\otimes n})$. The sum bound for $\phi$ makes $N_b$ subadditive, and applied to coefficient convolution it makes $N_b$ submultiplicative; so the limit exists (Fekete). Since $A^{\otimes n}$ has $O_A(n+1)$ exponents, replacing the sum by its largest term changes the normalized logarithm by $O(\log(n+1)/n)$. Hence $p_b$ is a limit of maxima of affine functions of $b$ with bounded slopes: it is convex and Lipschitz in $b$. Setting $b=1/m$ gives (6.1).

Step 2: multiplicativity, $p_b(T\otimes U)\ge p_b(T)+p_b(U)$. Submultiplicativity gives “$\le$”. For “$\ge$”, a maximal coefficient $[x^i]T^{\otimes n}\otimes[x^j]U^{\otimes n}$ contributes to the coefficient of $x^{i+j}$ in the product, where other terms could cancel it. The preprint isolates it by varying $x$ in the first factor alone: with a new variable $w$, $$C_s=[x^kw^s]\bigl(T((1+w)x)^{\otimes n}\otimes U(x)^{\otimes n}\bigr)=\sum_h\binom hs[x^h]T^{\otimes n}\otimes[x^{k-h}]U^{\otimes n},\qquad k=i+j.$$ Different $s$ weight the same products by different integers $\binom hs$. On a window $I_n$ of $m_n$ consecutive indices $h$ around $i$, the matrix $(\binom hs)_{0\le s\lt m_n,\,h\in I_n}$ has determinant one over $\mathbb Z$, so over $E$ (any characteristic) a combination $C$ of $C_0,\dots,C_{m_n-1}$ has multiplier $1$ at $h=i$ and $0$ elsewhere in the window. At a value of $b$ where $p_b(T)$ is differentiable, indices $h$ outside the window have exponentially smaller weighted size (inequality (6.2) of the preprint), so $C$ is the isolated product up to a negligible error, which gives the lower bound (6.3). On the other hand $C$ is a combination of coefficients of $w^s$, $s\le\eta n$, of a tensor that differs from $(T\otimes U)^{\otimes n}$ in at most $\eta n$ factors; this bounds its size from above by $\exp\bigl(\frac nD(p_b(T\otimes U)+O(\eta\log(1/\eta)))\bigr)$. Let $\eta\downarrow0$, and use density of differentiability points and continuity in $b$.

Step 3: scalars, integral maps, sums. For a scalar Laurent polynomial the lowest coefficient of each power has the largest weighted size, so $p(f)=-\nu(f)$, and $p(A/f)=p(A)+\nu(f)$ extends $p$ to fractions. A local map with entries of nonnegative degree, raised to the $n$-th power, expresses each output coefficient of degree $j$ as a sum of polynomially many ordinary restrictions of input coefficients of degree at most $j$; the loss is $O(\log(n+1))$ and vanishes after normalization. The sum bound follows from the binomial expansion of $(T+U)^{\otimes n}$. The lift agrees with $H$ on constants, including diagonals, so the diagonal test of §5.1 gives the direct-sum identity.∎

Gap fill — why the binomial matrix has determinant one. filled here Let $I_n=\{h_0,h_0+1,\dots,h_0+m-1\}$. Column $h$ of the matrix $(\binom hs)_{0\le s\lt m}$ lists the coefficients of $(1+w)^h$ modulo $w^m$. Multiplication by $(1+w)^{h_0}$ on $\mathbb Z[w]/(w^m)$ is unitriangular in the basis $1,w,\dots,w^{m-1}$ (its matrix is $\binom{h_0}{s-s'}$, with ones on the diagonal), and the columns for $(1+w)^0,\dots,(1+w)^{m-1}$ form the unitriangular Pascal matrix. The matrix in question is the product of these two, so its determinant is $1$. An integer matrix of determinant one is invertible over every field. This is why the isolation works in every characteristic, where a Vandermonde argument with $m$ distinct field elements would need a large field.

6.2 Prices on disks pp. 23–25

Generic prices. Take an algebraically closed field $F$ with an ordinary price on a fixed finite set of sites. Enlarge the coefficient field to $E=F(\mathcal U)$ for an uncountable set $\mathcal U$ of independent indeterminates. For a rational tensor depending on finitely many of these variables, define its price over $E$ as the supremum of the prices of its $F$-valued specializations. Because price sublevel sets are Zariski closed (§3.2, item 5), the specializations within any tolerance of the supremum contain a nonempty open set, finitely many such sets meet, and the price axioms pass to $E$. Then apply Theorem 6.1 over $E$ to get a valued price $p$ on $K=\operatorname{Frac}(E[t^{\mathbb Q}])$, with valuation ring $\mathcal O$.

Definition (fresh variable, disk, generic price). p. 23 paper An indeterminate $u\in\mathcal U$ is fresh if it occurs in none of the data being compared. For $r\in\mathbb Q$ the disk with center $a\in K$ and valuation bound $r$ is $a+t^r\mathcal O=\{c:\nu(c-a)\ge r\}$. Its generic price is the price at $a+t^ru$. Increasing $r$ shrinks the disk.
Background — disks form a tree. filled here In a nonarchimedean field, two disks are either nested or disjoint: if they share a point, the ultrametric inequality makes that point a center of both, and the one with the larger valuation bound is inside the other. Hence the disks containing a given point are totally ordered, and any two disks $a+t^r\mathcal O$, $a'+t^{r'}\mathcal O$ are contained in a smallest common disk, of valuation bound $\min\{r,r',\nu(a-a')\}$. Drawing an edge from each disk to the disks just above it gives a tree: the path between two disks climbs to the smallest common disk and descends. This tree of disks is the skeleton of the Berkovich projective line [7]. For a scalar rational function $g$, the function “disk $\mapsto-\nu(g)$ at the generic point of the disk” is piecewise linear along the tree; at each disk the outgoing slopes count zeros minus poles of $g$ in each direction, and they sum to zero. Such functions are called harmonic. Lemma 6.2 says that tensor prices are subharmonic on this tree: the value at a vertex is at most the weighted average of the values at the ends of a star around it.
Lemma 6.2 (Specialization and the star inequality). p. 23 paper For a polynomial tensor $T(c)$ over $K$, let $P(c)=p(T(c))$ on the base sites, or $P(c)=\beta_p(T(c))$ when $T$ has additional sites. With a fresh $u$ and $P_*=P(u)$, $$P(c)\le P_*\quad(c\in\mathcal O),\qquad P_*=\sup_{a\in F}P(a).$$ The generic price is unchanged by an affine change of variable $c\mapsto a+bc$ with $a\in\mathcal O$, $b\in\mathcal O^\times$ ($u$ fresh also for $a,b$).

Suppose $T$ is nonzero of degree $d$. Choose finitely many centers $c_j\in\mathcal O$ with distinct residues and positive rational numbers $r_j,r_\infty$. Let $P_j=P(c_j+t^{r_j}u)$ and $P_\infty=P(t^{-r_\infty}u)$. Then $$\sum_j\frac{P_*-P_j}{r_j}\ \le\ \frac{P_\infty-P_*}{r_\infty}\ \le\ d.\tag{6.4}$$ Equivalently, the first inequality bounds the center price $P_*$ by the weighted average of the prices at the ends of the star, with weights proportional to $1/r_j$ and $1/r_\infty$. The values on the star are called harmonic if the center equals this average.

Gap fill — the scalar model, computed. filled here Let $T(c)=\prod_j(c-c_j)^{m_j}$ with distinct $c_j\in F$; a scalar has price $p(g)=-\nu(g)$. Center. At $c=u$ every factor $u-c_j$ has valuation $0$, so $P_*=0$. Inner end at $c_j$. At $c=c_j+t^{r_j}u$ the factor $c-c_j=t^{r_j}u$ has valuation $r_j$ and the others have valuation $0$ (distinct residues), so $P_j=-m_jr_j$: the price drops by $m_j$ per unit of valuation length. Outer end. At $c=t^{-r_\infty}u$ every factor has valuation $-r_\infty$, so $P_\infty=r_\infty\sum_jm_j$. Then $\sum_j(P_*-P_j)/r_j=\sum_jm_j=(P_\infty-P_*)/r_\infty=d$. Both inequalities in (6.4) are equalities: the number of zeros inside the disk equals the growth rate outside it. For a polynomial with further zeros outside the residues $c_j$, the first inequality is strict.

Specialization. Let $Q(c)=T(c)^{\otimes n}$, of degree at most $nd$. Lagrange interpolation at $nd+1$ fresh indeterminates expresses $Q(c)$, for $c\in\mathcal O$, as a combination of the samples with integral weights, because differences of distinct fresh indeterminates are units. All samples have price $nP_*$ by permutation symmetry of $\mathcal U$. The sum bound gives $p(Q(c))\le nP_*+D\log(nd+1)$; divide by $n$. Interpolating instead at $nd+1$ points of $F$ and evaluating at $u$ gives $P_*\le\sup_{a\in F}P(a)$.

Outer growth. The inverse Vandermonde matrix at these nodes is integral, so interpolation controls coefficients: $p([c^\ell]Q)\le nP_*+D\log(nd+1)$. Evaluating at $t^{-r_\infty}u$ raises a coefficient price by at most $nd\,r_\infty$. Hence $P_\infty-P_*\le d\,r_\infty$.

The star inequality, by contradiction. Interpolating on each inner disk and on the outer disk gives jet bounds $$p([v^\ell]Q(c_j+v))\le nP_j+r_j\ell+D\log(nd+1),\qquad p([c^\ell]Q(c))\le nP_\infty-r_\infty\ell+D\log(nd+1).\tag{6.5}$$ Low-order Taylor coefficients at $c_j$ are cheap (first bound), and high-degree coefficients are cheap (second bound). Suppose the sum of inner drops per unit length exceeded the outer growth per unit length. Then there are numbers $a_j$ and $b$ with $\sum_ja_j\gt b\gt(P_\infty-P_*)/r_\infty$ and $0\le a_j\lt(P_*-P_j)/r_j$, such that the coefficients $[c^\ell]Q$ for $\ell\ge\lceil nb\rceil$ and the Taylor coefficients $[v^\ell]Q(c_j+v)$ for $\ell\lt\lfloor na_j\rfloor$ all have price at most $n(P_*-\epsilon)$. These data determine $Q$: subtract the high-degree part; the remainder has degree less than $m=\lceil nb\rceil$ and is known modulo $(c-c_j)^{m_j}$, $m_j=\lfloor na_j\rfloor$, at each center; the total multiplicity $\sum_jm_j$ is at least $m$ for large $n$; and the moduli are pairwise comaximal over $\mathcal O$ because the center differences are units. The Chinese remainder theorem recovers the remainder by an $\mathcal O$-linear map. So $Q(u)$ is an integral combination of $O(n)$ cheap coefficients, and the sum bound gives $nP_*=p(Q(u))\le n(P_*-\epsilon)+O(\log(n+1))$, a contradiction.

Assisted cost. For $P=\beta_p(T(c))$, fix a power $m$ and a closure with entries $\mathbf z$; the closed tensor $A(c,\mathbf z)$ is polynomial in $\mathbf z$, and by interpolation on a grid in $F^s$ the supremum over integral $\mathbf z$ equals the supremum over $\mathbf z\in F^s$. So $\beta_p(T(c))$ is a supremum of functions $p(A(c,\mathbf z))/m$ with constant closing data. Each satisfies specialization and the weighted-average inequality, and both pass to suprema because the weights are positive.∎

AHA 7. The parameter of a tensor family is treated as a point of a nonarchimedean disk. The Gauss lift makes “the price at the generic point of a disk” meaningful, and the star inequality says that this function on the tree of disks is subharmonic, with the same proof as for a polynomial: interpolation bounds jets, and the Chinese remainder theorem reassembles the polynomial from cheap jets. Tensor algebra has been converted into potential theory on a tree.

7. A moving family forces a lower bound of 9/4 pp. 25–30

Role in the chain. This section and the next contain the derivation of the number $9/4$. The setting: a family of sites $x_z$ indexed by a nonarchimedean parameter $z$, satisfying the disk comparisons of §6. Theorem 7.1 says that if such a family is not constant, then $\operatorname{rank}(T_n)\ge n^{9/4}$ for every $n$. §7 proves the geometric half: near the place where motion starts, a rescaled neighbourhood contains a tree, and a label shared by four tips of that tree has rate at most $3/2$ times the rate of a wire across the diameter. §8 converts this into the rate $8/3$ on a tripod. §12 uses the theorem in contrapositive form.

Setting (p. 25). Let $F$ be algebraically closed, $\mathcal U$ an uncountable set of indeterminates, and $$K_0=\operatorname{Frac}\bigl(F(\mathcal U)[t^{\mathbb Q}]\bigr),\qquad\mathcal O=\{z:\nu(z)\ge0\},\qquad J=\{z:\nu(z)\gt0\},$$ with the lowest-exponent valuation. Consider a family of sites $x_z$, $z\in J$, in a spectral space over $F$, subject to three conditions.

  1. Symmetry. Adding the same $w\in J$ to all indices, and permuting $\mathcal U$, preserve the prices of all tensors over $F$.
  2. Specialization and the star inequality. For a nonzero tensor on any finite list of slots, let $P(c)$ be its price at the sites $x_{a_i+b_ic}$, where $a_i,b_i\in J$. With $u$ fresh, $P(c)\le P(u)$ for $c\in\mathcal O$. For $c_j\in\mathcal O$ of distinct residues and positive rational lengths $s_j,s_\infty$, $$\sum_j\frac{P(c_j+t^{s_j}u)-P(u)}{s_j}+\frac{P(t^{-s_\infty}u)-P(u)}{s_\infty}\ \ge\ 0\tag{7.1}$$ whenever the indices at the outer end lie in $J$. In words: the price at the generic point of the disk is at most the weighted mean of its end prices, with weights proportional to the reciprocals of the lengths.
  3. Nonconstancy and continuity. Some two sites have positive distance, and $d(x_0,x_z)\to0$ as $\nu(z)\to+\infty$.

The inequalities apply in particular to distances: test a two-site wire and divide its price by the logarithm of its size. Conditions 1 and 2 are the ordinary-price versions of the statements of Lemma 6.2; §12 produces a family that satisfies them.

Theorem 7.1 (Rigidity of analytic motion). p. 25 paper If such a family exists, square matrix multiplication over $F$ has exact rank at least $n^{9/4}$ for each side $n$.
AHA 8. Either nothing moves, or $\omega\ge9/4$. A family of sites that depends on a parameter in a nonconstant way, and obeys the subharmonic comparison (7.1), is so constrained that it contains a tripod with shared-label rate $8/3$. The chain is: motion $\Rightarrow$ a threshold depth with a kink $\Rightarrow$ a tree in the rescaled limit $\Rightarrow$ four equal tips with rate at most $3/2$ per unit of diameter $\Rightarrow$ a tripod with rate $8/3$ $\Rightarrow$ $r\ge n^{6/(8/3)}$.

7.1 The threshold for displacement pp. 26–27

Reduction to a bounded convex set. By translation invariance $x_w\ne x_0$ for some $w\in J$. Replace $x_z$ by $x_{t^bz}$ for a rational $0\lt b\lt\nu(w)$; the new family is nonconstant, satisfies the three conditions, and is now indexed by all $z\in\mathcal O$ with radius bounded by the generic value of $d(x_0,x_{t^bc})$. Let $X$ be the closed convex hull of the sites in a complete CAT(0) spectral extension. By Lemma 5.2 the symmetries act on $X$ by price-preserving isometries; write $g_w$ for translation by $w$. Lemma 5.2 also extends Condition 2 from the sites $x_{a+bc}$ to sites $g_{a+bc}A$ for any $A\in X$: geodesic interpolations move rigidly under the translation, so the hypotheses of the submean part of the lemma hold.

The displacement function. For $r\gt0$ rational and $A\in X$ put $$R(A,r):=\sup_{\nu(w)\ge r}d(A,g_wA)=d(A,g_{t^ru}A),\tag{7.2}$$ with $u$ fresh for $A$. The equality is specialization: every $w$ of valuation at least $r$ is $t^rc$ with $c\in\mathcal O$, and the generic point dominates.

Gap fill — why $R(A,\cdot)$ is convex. filled here Apply (7.1) to the distance $P(c)=d(A,g_{t^rc}A)$ with one inner end at $c_1=0$ of length $b$ and the outer end of length $s$. At the center, $P(u)=R(A,r)$. At the inner end, $c=t^bu$ gives index $t^{r+b}u$ and value $R(A,r+b)$. At the outer end, $c=t^{-s}u$ gives index $t^{r-s}u$ and value $R(A,r-s)$. So $$\frac{R(A,r+b)-R(A,r)}b+\frac{R(A,r-s)-R(A,r)}s\ \ge\ 0,\qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad\frac{R(A,r)-R(A,r-s)}s\le\frac{R(A,r+b)-R(A,r)}b.$$ Slopes increase from left to right. This is convexity on rational depths; boundedness by $\operatorname{diam}X$ gives a continuous convex extension to real $r\gt0$, Lipschitz on compact subintervals uniformly in $A$. One-inner-end stars always give convexity in the depth; this device is used several more times.

A point with a kink. The subgroup $\{w:\nu(w)\ge r\}$ has a common fixed set $X_r=\{A:R(A,r)=0\}$, closed and convex. It is nonempty: the circumcenter of any orbit of the subgroup is fixed (§3.2, fact (e)), and since the orbit of $A$ lies in the ball of radius $R(A,r)$ about $A$, this gives $d(A,X_r)\le R(A,r)$. Let $f(r)=d(x_0,X_r)$. It is positive when the subgroup moves $x_0$, nonincreasing in $r$, and tends to zero by Condition 3. For $0\lt h\lt r$, the projection $A_r$ of $x_0$ to $X_r$ satisfies $$0\le f(r-h)-f(r)\le d(A_r,X_{r-h})\le R(A_r,r-h)-R(A_r,r),$$ using $R(A_r,r)=0$. So $f$ is locally Lipschitz, and since it decreases somewhere there is a depth $r_*$ with $f'(r_*)\lt0$. Fix $A=A_{r_*}$. It is fixed by every permutation of the indeterminates (they fix $x_0$ and preserve $X_{r_*}$). The convex function $R(A,\cdot)$ vanishes to the right of $r_*$ and has left derivative $-k\lt0$ at $r_*$: $$R(A,r_*+h)=k(-h)_++o(|h|).\tag{7.3}$$ In words: translations of valuation above $r_*$ fix $A$; translations of valuation slightly below $r_*$ move it by an amount proportional to the deficit.

7.2 Rescaling at the threshold p. 27

Take rational $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ and $r_0=r_*-\varepsilon+o(\varepsilon)$. Form the pointed spectral ultralimit at $A$ with distances and prices multiplied by $2/(k\varepsilon)$ (§3.2, items 3 and 4). For each $z\in K_0$ let $L_z$ be the point represented by the sequence $$g_{t^{r_0}z(t^\varepsilon)}A,$$ where $z(t^\varepsilon)$ substitutes $t^\varepsilon$ for $t$. The substitution multiplies valuations by $\varepsilon$, so a difference of valuation $\rho$ in $z$ becomes a translation of valuation $r_0+\varepsilon\rho=r_*-\varepsilon(1-\rho)+o(\varepsilon)$. By (7.3) it moves $A$ by $k\varepsilon(1-\rho)_++o(\varepsilon)$, which after rescaling is $2(1-\rho)_+$. The symmetries and the comparisons of Condition 2 pass to the limit: in (7.1) every star length is multiplied by $\varepsilon$ and every price by $2/(k\varepsilon)$, and these common factors cancel.

The limiting points are the leaves. The computation above gives $d(L_0,L_{t^ru})=2(1-r)_+$ at a generic parameter. The preprint upgrades this to every pair: $$d(L_z,L_{z'})=2\bigl(1-\nu(z-z')\bigr)_+.\tag{7.4}$$

(of (7.4))

Write $z'-z=t^rv$ with $\nu(v)=0$. Translation invariance and specialization give “$\le$”, which is already equality for $r\ge1$. For $r\lt1$ test $P(c)=d(L_0,L_{t^rc})$ on a star with two inner ends, centered at $0$ and at $v$, and the outer end. At the center $P=2(1-r)$. On the arm toward $0$, of any length $0\lt a\lt1-r$, the end value is $2(1-r-a)$: slope $-2$. On the outer arm of length $s$ the end value is $2(1-r+s)$: slope $+2$. These two contributions to (7.1) cancel, so the slope on the arm toward $v$ is $\ge0$. Specialization makes it $\le0$. So the value at $c=v+t^bu$ equals the generic value $2(1-r)$ for every $b\gt0$. For $b\ge1-r$ the upper bound already proved gives $d(L_{t^r(v+t^bu)},L_{t^rv})=0$, so the two leaves coincide and $d(L_0,L_{t^rv})=2(1-r)$.∎

Formula (7.4) says that the leaves $L_z$ are indexed by the disks of valuation bound $1$ (two parameters with $\nu(z-z')\ge1$ give the same leaf), and that their distance is an ultrametric function of the valuation of the difference. That is the metric of the set of leaves of a tree. The next step shows that the tree itself is there.

7.3 Geodesics of the disk tree pp. 27–28

Midpoints are branch points. Let $B=L_b$, $C=L_{b+v}$ with $d(B,C)=2h\gt0$, and let $m$ be their midpoint. Take a star with inner ends at $c=0$ and $c=1$, each of length $h$, and outer length $s$, for the family of leaves $L_{b+cv}$. Prescribe the point $m$ at the center and at the outer end, and the points $B$, $C$ at the two inner ends. The distances of the prescribed point to $B$ and $C$ are $(h,h)$ at the center and the outer end, and $(0,2h)$, $(2h,0)$ at the inner ends; with weights proportional to $1/h,1/h,1/s$ the center values are the weighted averages of the end values. Lemma 5.2 therefore allows (7.1) for the distance from the prescribed point to $L_{b+cv}$. At the inner ends that distance is $0$ by (7.4). At the outer end it is at most $3h+2s$ (go through $B$). So the generic center value $J_*=d(m,L_{b+uv})$ satisfies $$0\le-\frac{2J_*}h+\frac{3h+2s-J_*}s.$$ Let $s\to\infty$: $J_*\le h$. A convexity argument in $s$ gives more generally $$d(m,L_{b+cv})\le h+2s\qquad(\nu(c)\ge-s,\ s\ge0\text{ rational}).\tag{7.5}$$ If a leaf $L_z$ has $d(B,L_z)=2h+2s$, apply (7.5) with $c=(z-b)/v$: then $d(B,m)+d(m,L_z)\le h+h+2s=d(B,L_z)$, so equality holds and $m$ lies on the geodesic $[B,L_z]$. The midpoint of two leaves lies on the geodesic from either of them to any third leaf at least as far away.

The tree. For rational $\ell\le1$ let $P(z,\ell)$ be the point of $[L_z,L_{z'}]$ at distance $1-\ell$ from $L_z$, for any sufficiently distant $L_{z'}$; by the previous paragraph it does not depend on $z'$. The number $\ell$ is the depth; $P(z,1)=L_z$. Two distinct leaves at distance $2h$ meet at depth $1-h=\nu(z-z')$: above that depth their paths to a far third leaf lie in opposite halves of $[L_z,L_{z'}]$, below it they coincide. Hence $$d\bigl(P(z,\ell),P(z',\ell')\bigr)=\ell+\ell'-2\min\{\ell,\ell',\nu(z-z')\}.\tag{7.6}$$ The point $P(z,\ell)$ depends only on the disk $\{z':\nu(z'-z)\ge\ell\}$, and its ancestors correspond to the larger disks containing it. The convex hull of the leaves is the tree of disks of §6.2, with depth equal to the valuation bound.

Widget 4 — distances in the disk tree, formula (7.6)

Stars with prescribed depths. In a finite star comparison one can prescribe points $P(a+bc,\ell)$ whose depths are harmonic across the star, that is, the depth at the center is the weighted average of the depths at the ends: choose a far leaf $L_{t^{-N}}$; its distance to every $L_{a+bc}$ in the test is the same, the segment contains the prescribed point, and Lemma 5.2 applies. In particular the price of a fixed tensor along a ray $c=\xi+t^\rho u$ is convex in $\rho$ when each depth is affine in $\rho$.

7.4 The price of a common label at four sites pp. 28–30

Place the common label $\Delta_{[4]}(q)=\sum_{j=1}^qe_j^{\otimes4}$ at the tips of four equal branches. The first three centers are $z_1=0$, $z_2=1$, $z_3\in F\setminus\{0,1\}$; the fourth, $z_4=c$, varies. At a fresh $c=u$, the four tips $P(z_i,\ell)$ at rational depth $0\lt\ell\lt1$ have common origin $P(0,0)$ and, by (7.6), pairwise distance $2\ell$.

Definition ($D_\ell$ and $\underline D$). p. 28 paper $D_\ell$ is the price of the four-site label at these tips divided by $\log q$. It vanishes at $\ell=0$. Joint geodesic convexity and the upper bound $4\ell$ (four size-$q$ wires to the common origin) give a finite one-sided slope $$\underline D=\lim_{\ell\downarrow0}\frac{D_\ell}{2\ell}.$$ The denominator $2\ell$ is the rate of a single wire between two of the tips.

So $\underline D$ is the four-site common-label rate in units where the mutual distance is $1$, measured in the tangent cone at the origin. The preprint bounds it by moving the fourth center toward each of the three fixed centers.

The collision arms. On the arm toward $z_i$ let $c=z_i+t^ru$, $0\le r\le2\ell$. Increase the depths of sites $i$ and $4$ by $r/2$ and decrease those of the other pair by $r/2$ (take $2\ell\le1$ so that all depths stay in the tree). By (7.6):

  • within the pair $\{i,4\}$: $\nu(z_4-z_i)=r$, depths $\ell+r/2$, distance $2(\ell+r/2)-2r=2\ell-r$;
  • within the other pair: valuation $0$, depths $\ell-r/2$, distance $2\ell-r$;
  • across the pairs: $(\ell+r/2)+(\ell-r/2)-0=2\ell$.

Write $h=2\ell-r$ for the within-pair distance. As $r$ grows from $0$ to $2\ell$ the two pairs close up while all cross distances stay $2\ell$ (Fig. 6). At $r=2\ell$ both pairs have merged. The grouped vectors $e_j\otimes e_j$ are linearly independent at each merged site, so the four-site label is equivalent under local maps to one size-$q$ wire, of length $2\ell$. Its rate at the collision is exactly $2\ell$.

Making the star harmonic. To apply (7.1) with prescribed depths, the depth changes must balance at each of the four sites: with arm $j$ of length $s_j$ and depth slope $\sigma_j$ at a given site, the end depth is $\ell+\sigma_js_j$, and the weighted average with weights $1/s_j$ equals $\ell$ exactly when $\sum_j\sigma_j=0$. The three collision arms alone give slope sums $-\frac12$ at each of sites $1,2,3$ and $+\frac32$ at site $4$. Two more arms of length $\ell$ repair this.

armlengthparameter$2\times$ depth slope at sites 1, 2, 3, 4upper bound for the label rate at the end
toward $z_1$$2\ell$$c=z_1+t^ru$$+1,\ -1,\ -1,\ +1$$2\ell$ (one wire)
toward $z_2$$2\ell$$c=z_2+t^ru$$-1,\ +1,\ -1,\ +1$$2\ell$
toward $z_3$$2\ell$$c=z_3+t^ru$$-1,\ -1,\ +1,\ +1$$2\ell$
unused finite arm $a\in F\setminus\{z_1,z_2,z_3\}$$\ell$$c=a+t^ru$$+1,\ +1,\ +1,\ +1$$D_{3\ell/2}$
outer arm $\infty$$\ell$$c=t^{-r}u$$\phantom{+}0,\ \phantom{+}0,\ \phantom{+}0,\ -4$$D^-_\ell$
column sums$0,\ 0,\ 0,\ 0$

This table is the preprint’s Figure 4 (p. 29).

On the unused arm all four depths increase at slope $\frac12$, reaching $3\ell/2$ at the end; the parameter $a+t^\ell u$ is a specialization of the generic one, so the end rate is at most $D_{3\ell/2}$. On the outer arm only the fourth depth changes, at slope $-2$; at $r=\ell$ this site is $P(0,-\ell)$, above the common origin. $D^-_\ell$ denotes the label rate there.

Gap fill — from the five-arm star to $\underline D\le3/2$. filled here

Inequality (7.1) for the label price, divided by $\log q$, with center value $D_\ell$ and the five end bounds of the table: $$0\le3\,\frac{2\ell-D_\ell}{2\ell}+\frac{D^-_\ell-D_\ell}\ell+\frac{D_{3\ell/2}-D_\ell}\ell.\tag{7.7}$$ Now let $\ell\downarrow0$, using $D_\ell=2\ell\,\underline D+o(\ell)$.

  • Collision arms: $3\bigl(1-D_\ell/(2\ell)\bigr)\to3(1-\underline D)$.
  • Unused arm: $D_{3\ell/2}=3\ell\,\underline D+o(\ell)$, so $(D_{3\ell/2}-D_\ell)/\ell\to3\underline D-2\underline D=\underline D$.
  • Outer arm: the preprint shows $\limsup D^-_\ell/(2\ell)\le\underline D$, so the upper limit of $(D^-_\ell-D_\ell)/\ell$ is at most $0$. (Argument: start the outer ray at a small depth $\lambda$ and stop at $R\lt\lambda$; all four sites then have radius $\lambda$ about $P(0,0)$; contracting all radii from $\lambda$ to $\ell\le R$ gives the placement of $D^-_\ell$, and convexity gives $D^-_\ell/(2\ell)\le D(\lambda,R)/(2\lambda)$; then $R\downarrow0$, $\lambda\downarrow0$.)

Therefore $0\le3(1-\underline D)+0+\underline D=3-2\underline D$, that is, $$\underline D\le\frac32.\tag{7.8}$$

Reading (7.8). Four points at mutual distance $1$ at the tips of a four-armed star with arms $\frac12$ can share a label at rate at most $\frac32$, to first order. The trivial bounds are $1$ from below (restrict to two tips) and $2$ from above (four wires of length $\frac12$ to the centre). The mechanism is an averaging: at the generic position the rate is at most the mean of the rates at the three collisions, where it is known, corrected by the two balancing arms, which reproduce the generic rate itself at a different scale. The scaling relation $D_{3\ell/2}\approx\frac32D_\ell$ is what turns the inequality into a number.

8. A lower bound from the price of a shared label pp. 30–33

Role in the chain. This section finishes the proof of Theorem 7.1 and is the place where $9/4$ is computed. Input: the four-tip bound $\underline D\le3/2$ of (7.8). Output: on the tangent tripod at the origin of the disk tree, the three-site common-label rate with unit arms is exactly $8/3$, hence $r\ge n^{9/4}$. Two tools are needed. The tripod principle (Proposition 8.1, proved in §9–§11) gives the lower bound $D\ge D_*$ and says that equality at one shape spreads to all shapes. A decoder lemma for rectangular matrix multiplication (Lemma 8.2) supplies equality in the limit of one long arm.

Proposition 8.1 (Tripod principle). p. 30 paper Over an algebraically closed field, place three sites at pairwise distances $a_i+a_j$ for $i\ne j$, where $a_1,a_2,a_3\ge0$. Their common-label rate $D(a)$ satisfies $$D(a)\ \ge\ D_*(a):=a_1+a_2+a_3-\bigl(a_1^{-1}+a_2^{-1}+a_3^{-1}\bigr)^{-1}.$$ The reciprocal term is zero when any $a_i=0$.

Now fix three infinite rays in a spectral space that form a metric tripod with origin $O$, and suppose that their prices are homogeneous under radial dilation. In $D(a)$, place site $i$ on ray $i$ at distance $a_i$ from $O$. If $D(a)-D_*(a)$ vanishes at one positive triple, it vanishes at every positive triple. The same conclusion holds if the defect tends to zero as one arm tends to infinity with the other two held at fixed positive lengths.

The first assertion holds for any three sites with the stated distances, in any spectral space. The second compares placements on one tripod. Three numbers $a_i\ge0$ with $d(i,j)=a_i+a_j$ exist for any three points of a metric space ($a_1=\frac12(d_{12}+d_{13}-d_{23})$, the Gromov product), so the first assertion is a universal inequality between the three pairwise wire rates and the three-site label rate.

Gap fill — sanity checks on $D_*$. filled here
  • Degenerate triangle. If $a_3=0$ then $D_*=a_1+a_2=d(1,2)$. The inequality says that a label shared by all three sites costs at least a label shared by two of them, which is restriction.
  • Upper bound on a tripod. On a tripod, $D\le a_1+a_2+a_3$: take three wires of size $q$ from $O$ to the tips and contract at $O$ with the functional $e_i\otimes e_j\otimes e_k\mapsto\mathbf 1\{i=j=k\}$; the result is $\Delta_{123}(q)$. So $D_*\le D\le\sum_ia_i$, and the reciprocal term $h(a)=(\sum_i1/a_i)^{-1}$ is the largest possible saving over the spanning tree.
  • Homogeneity. $D_*(\lambda a)=\lambda D_*(a)$.
  • Equal arms. $D_*(1,1,1)=3-\frac13=\frac83$.
  • One long arm. $D_*(1,1,b)=2+b-\dfrac{1}{2+1/b}=b+2-\dfrac b{2b+1}=b+\dfrac32+\dfrac1{4b+2}$, using $\dfrac b{2b+1}=\dfrac12-\dfrac1{4b+2}$.

8.1 A decoder for rectangular multiplication pp. 31–32

Multiply an $s\times q$ matrix by a $q\times s$ matrix over $F$, with $s=N^{1/2}$ and $q=N^{1/4}$ for a fourth power $N$. The product has $N$ entries. Place its output at a site $A$ and its inputs at $C,D'$, on a tripod with respective arm lengths $c,a,b$. The outer indices give wires of size $s$ on $AC$ and $AD'$; the inner index gives a wire of size $q$ on $CD'$. Its price divided by $\log N$ is therefore $$\frac{c+a}2+\frac{c+b}2+\frac{a+b}4=c+\tfrac34(a+b)\ \le\ c+a+b-\frac{ab}{a+b}\ \le\ D_*(c,a,b)\ \le\ D(c,a,b).$$ The first inequality is $4ab\le(a+b)^2$. The second drops the term $1/c$ from the harmonic sum. The third is Proposition 8.1.

Consequence: exact rank $N^{1+\eta}$. The displayed inequality holds at every spectral point, in the form $\varphi(\text{product})\le\varphi(\Delta(N))$. Compare the product with a full diagonal of size $\lceil N_0^{1+\eta/2}\rceil$ at a large fourth power $N_0$: the inequality is then strict at every spectral point, and strict spectral comparison (§3.2, item 7) makes each tensor power of the product a restriction of a bounded number of copies of the corresponding power of the diagonal. So for every $\eta\gt0$ there are arbitrarily large fourth powers $N$ for which this rectangular product has exact rank at most $N^{1+\eta}$. This is the rectangular bound “$\alpha\ge1/2$” of [55, 5], obtained here from the tripod inequality.

Lemma 8.2 (A decoder with short rows). p. 31 paper For every $\epsilon\gt0$ there is a $\delta\gt0$ such that for unbounded fourth powers $N$ the $N$ output bilinear forms $B_e$ of this product admit decompositions $$B_e=S_e+R_e,\qquad\operatorname{rank}S_e\le N^\epsilon,\qquad\dim\langle R_e:1\le e\le N\rangle\le N^{1-\delta}.$$

The two bounds have different uses. Each $S_e$ is a short sum of simple products, cheap to compute once the output index $e$ is known. The corrections $R_e$ may be expensive individually, but together they span a space of dimension $N^{1-\delta}$, which can be sent across a single wire.

Setup. Take a base format with $d$ output entries and rank $r$, $d\le r\le d^{1+\eta}$, and let $M$ be the $d\times r$ decoder matrix expressing the outputs in terms of the $r$ simple products. Fix $K\gt10/\epsilon$. The $Km$-th tensor power has $N=d^{Km}$ entries and decoder $(M^{\otimes m})^{\otimes K}$. Pad each block $M^{\otimes m}$ to side $2^t$, $t=\lceil m\log_2r\rceil$. An index is $K$ registers of $t$ bits; the padded decoder has side $T=2^{Kt}\le2^KN^{1+\eta}$.

One work register. Apply the first block to the first register. For each later block, swap its register with the first, apply the block there, and swap back. A register swap is $t$ simultaneous bit swaps, and a bit swap factors into a fixed number of elementary shears and diagonal signs over any field.

Truncation. Before and after each shear stage, project onto the coordinates whose register pair contains between $t/4-\rho t$ and $t/4+\rho t$ “high” letters. By the binomial tail the discarded fraction is at most $e^{-c_\rho t}$; over the $O(K)$ projections the discarded part of the decoder has rank at most $O(K)Te^{-c_\rho t}\le T^{1-\gamma}$. On a retained path each shear stage changes at most $2\rho t$ positions.

Row sparsity. Only the first register is acted on by a block matrix. Every other register takes part in two swaps, so on a retained path its input is within Hamming distance $C\rho t$ of its output. Each row of the retained decoder has at most $2^t\bigl(\sum_{j\le C\rho t}\binom tj\bigr)^{K-1}\le T^{1/K+o_{\rho\to0}(1)}$ nonzero entries. Choose $\rho$ with exponent below $\epsilon/2$, then $\eta$ small. The retained rows give the $S_e$ (at most $N^\epsilon$ simple products each); the discarded part, of rank at most $N^{1-\delta}$ with $\delta=\gamma/2$, spans the $R_e$.∎

Not verified — details of the truncation. not verified The sketch follows pp. 31–32. This tutorial did not reconstruct why the number of high letters in a register pair concentrates near $t/4$ for the coordinates that matter, nor the constant $C$. The structure (few changes per retained path, hence sparse rows; small discarded rank) is as stated in the preprint.

8.2 Two copies of the output near a collision pp. 32–33

Return to the disk tree of §7 and take the collision in which site $4$ approaches site $3$. Rename the sites $1,2,3,4$ as $A,B,C,D'$. Write $L=2\ell$ and $h=L-r$, $0\lt h\lt L$. From §7.4: the distances within $AB$ and within $CD'$ are both $h$, and all four cross distances are $L$. Let $D_\ell(h)$ be the four-site common-label rate. Then $D_\ell(0)=L$ (collision) and $D_\ell(L)=D_\ell$ (generic placement). Convexity along the arm gives $$D_\ell(h)\le L+\frac hL(D_\ell-L).$$

The test tensor. Duplicate the output index $e=(i,i')$ of the rectangular product at $A$ and at $B$, and leave its two inputs at $C$ and $D'$: $$U_N=\sum_ee_e\otimes e_e\otimes B_e=\sum_{\substack{1\le i,i'\le s\\1\le j\le q}}e_{ii'}\otimes e_{ii'}\otimes e_{ij}\otimes e_{ji'}.$$ The index $i$ is a label shared by $A,B,C$; the index $i'$ is a label shared by $A,B,D'$; and $j$ travels on the short wire $CD'$. With $D_{ABC},D_{ABD'}$ the two three-site rates, and $s=N^{1/2}$, $q=N^{1/4}$, $$\frac{H(U_N)}{\log N}=\frac{D_{ABC}+D_{ABD'}}2+\frac h4.$$

Gap fill — the computation that produces $8/3$, line by line. filled here

(a) Lower bounds from the tripod principle. The triple $A,B,C$ has distances $h,L,L$, hence arms $(h/2,\,h/2,\,L-h/2)$. Their sum is $L+h/2$, and the reciprocal term is at most $(2/h+2/h)^{-1}=h/4$. So $D_{ABC}\ge L+h/4$, and likewise $D_{ABD'}\ge L+h/4$.

(b) An upper bound for $H(U_N)$ from the decoder. Split $B_e=S_e+R_e$ by Lemma 8.2. In units of $\log N$:

  • The tensor built from the $S_e$ costs at most $D_\ell(h)+\epsilon h$: share the label $e$ at all four sites (rate $D_\ell(h)$, size $N$), then use a wire of size at most $N^\epsilon$ on $CD'$ (length $h$) to supply the simple products for that $e$.
  • The tensor built from the $R_e$ has matrix rank at most $N^{1-\delta}$ across the cut $AB\mid CD'$. A wire between $A$ and $C$ supplies this rank, at cost $(1-\delta)L$, and wires within the two pairs deliver the legs at $B$ and $D'$, each of dimension at most $N$, at cost $2h$. Total at most $(1-\delta)L+2h$.

For $h/L\lt\delta/2$ the second cost is less than $L\le D_\ell(h)$. The price of a sum of two tensors exceeds the larger price by at most the additive constant $D\log2$, which disappears after dividing by $\log N$ and letting $N\to\infty$. So $$\frac{D_{ABC}+D_{ABD'}}2+\frac h4\ \le\ D_\ell(h)+\epsilon h.$$

(c) Insert the four-tip bound. By (7.8), $D_\ell=(\underline D+o(1))\,L\le(\tfrac32+o(1))L$ as $\ell\downarrow0$. The convexity bound gives $D_\ell(h)\le L+\frac hL\bigl(\frac12+o(1)\bigr)L=L+\bigl(\frac12+o(1)\bigr)h$. Hence $$\frac{D_{ABC}+D_{ABD'}}2+\frac h4\ \le\ L+\bigl(\tfrac12+\epsilon+o_{\ell\downarrow0}(1)\bigr)h.$$

(d) Isolate one triple. Multiply by $2$ and subtract $h/2$: $D_{ABC}+D_{ABD'}\le2L+\bigl(\frac12+2\epsilon+o(1)\bigr)h$. Subtract the lower bound $D_{ABD'}\ge L+h/4$ from (a): $$D_{ABC}\ \le\ L+\frac h4+\bigl(2\epsilon+o_{\ell\downarrow0}(1)\bigr)h.$$ Compare with (a): $D_{ABC}\ge L+h/4$. The upper and lower bounds agree up to $(2\epsilon+o(1))h$.

(e) Rescale to a tangent tripod. The sites $A,B,C$ lie on the three fixed rays toward $z_1,z_2,z_3$ out of the depth-zero point $P(0,0)$, at depths $(\ell-r/2,\ \ell-r/2,\ \ell+r/2)=(h/2,\,h/2,\,L-h/2)$. Take the tangent tripod there: place sites at depths $ta_i$, divide prices by $t$, and let $t\downarrow0$ in a spectral ultralimit. On fixed rays the rescaled price of a tensor has a limit, because its price is convex in $t$ and vanishes at $t=0$; the limiting prices are homogeneous under radial dilation. Set $t=h/2$ and hold $b=2L/h-1$ fixed, so that the depths are $t(1,1,b)$. Dividing (d) by $t=h/2$: $$D(1,1,b)\ \le\ \frac{2L}h+\frac12+4\epsilon=b+\frac32+4\epsilon,\qquad\text{while}\qquad D_*(1,1,b)=b+\frac32+\frac1{4b+2}.$$

(f) Contact at infinity. So $0\le D(1,1,b)-D_*(1,1,b)\le4\epsilon$ for every sufficiently large $b$ (the condition $h/L\lt\delta/2$ in (b) is “$b$ large”). Let $b\to\infty$, then $\epsilon\to0$: the defect $D-D_*$ tends to zero as one arm tends to infinity with the other two fixed at $1$.

(g) Propagate and conclude. The second assertion of Proposition 8.1 applies to this homogeneous tripod: the defect vanishes at every positive triple. In particular $$D(1,1,1)=D_*(1,1,1)=3-\frac13=\frac83.$$ Place $T_n$ at the three unit tips. Its price is $6\log n$ (three wires of length $2$, §1.3). A rank-$r$ decomposition makes it a restriction of $\Delta(r)$ at these tips, of price $\frac83\log r$. Therefore $$6\log n\le\frac83\log r,\qquad r\ge n^{9/4}.$$ This proves Theorem 7.1.

Widget 5 — the collision, the two triples and the tangent tripod

AHA 9. Rectangular matrix multiplication at inner dimension $N^{1/4}$ against outer dimension $N^{1/2}$ supplies equality in the tripod inequality in the limit of one long arm (“contact at infinity”). The reason is numerical: the four-tip bound $\frac32$ and the rectangular price $c+\frac34(a+b)$ meet the lower bound $L+\frac h4$ from both sides. A rigidity statement, the strong minimum principle of §11, then carries equality from the degenerate shape $(1,1,\infty)$ to the balanced shape $(1,1,1)$, where $D_*=3-\frac13=\frac83$ and $6/(8/3)=9/4$.
Remark — where each number comes from. filled here
numbersource
$\frac32$(7.8): three collision arms contribute $3(1-\underline D)$, the balancing arm contributes $\underline D$; $3-2\underline D\ge0$.
$\frac12,\frac14$exponents of $s=N^{1/2}$ and $q=N^{1/4}$ in the rectangular product; they make $H(U_N)/\log N=\frac12(D_{ABC}+D_{ABD'})+\frac h4$.
$L+\frac h4$$D_*$ of the thin triple $(h/2,h/2,L-h/2)$ to first order in $h$; matched from above by step (d).

What has and has not been proved at this point. Theorem 7.1 is a conditional statement: if a moving family exists, then $\operatorname{rank}(T_n)\ge n^{9/4}$. No moving family has been exhibited, and the preprint never exhibits one. The theorem is used in the other direction: under the hypothesis $\omega_F\lt9/4$ the conclusion is false, so no moving family exists (§12). The lower bound $\omega\ge9/4$ itself comes from the list-distinctness contradiction of §2, and the tripod with rate $8/3$ is the configuration that the hypothesis forbids.

9. Polynomial multiplication near contact pp. 33–37

Role in the chain. Sections 9, 10 and 11 prove the tripod principle, Proposition 8.1. They are unconditional. The measuring device is the tensor of polynomial multiplication, the same tensor that gives the upper bound $9/4$ in [41]. §9 proves the inequality $D\ge D_*$ and shows that when the defect $\Delta=D-D_*(a)$ is small, the price of polynomial multiplication is determined up to $O(\Delta)$. Equality $\Delta=0$ is called contact. §10 and §11 use this to move contact from one triple of arm lengths to another.

Throughout §9–11 the field is algebraically closed, prices use natural logarithms, and the three sites are fixed. On an embedded tripod, including the origin among the label sites gives the same price; a duplicate of a label at either of two terminals is free, and convexity bounds its cost at every point of the geodesic between them.

9.1 The continuum profile pp. 33–34

Place a finite nonempty set of inputs and one output in a spectral space, with common-label rate $D\gt0$. For integer lengths $L_i\ge1$ let $M(L)$ be the tensor that multiplies univariate polynomials of degrees less than $L_i$, one at each input, and retains all coefficients of the product at the output.

Lemma 9.1. p. 34 paper There is a continuous, coordinatewise nondecreasing function $P$ on the positive orthant such that, if $L_i/N\to t_i\gt0$, then $$H(M(L))=D\log N+P(t)+o(1),\qquad P(ct)=P(t)+D\log c.$$ For polynomials in $m$ variables with total input degrees at most $\lfloor Nt_i\rfloor$, the corresponding price is $mD\log N+mP(t)-D\log(m!)+o(1)$ for each fixed $m$.

Upper bound. The product has fewer than $\sum_iL_i$ coefficients. Evaluating the inputs at $\sum_iL_i$ points, multiplying pointwise and interpolating is a bilinear algorithm, so $M(L)\le\Delta(\sum_iL_i)$ and $H(M(L))\le D\log\sum_iL_i$.

Superadditivity. $M(kL)$ degenerates to the direct sum of $k$ copies of $M(L)$. Divide $[0,1]$ into $k$ equal intervals and choose a continuous convex piecewise affine function $\psi$ with a strict change of slope at each division point. Give an input monomial of degree $d_i$ the weight $kL_i\,\psi\bigl((d_i+\frac12)/(kL_i)\bigr)$ and the output monomial the opposite weight, evaluated at the weighted mean of the input positions with coefficient $k\sum_iL_i$; the output weight depends only on the sum of the degrees. By Jensen’s inequality the total weight of a term is nonnegative, and it is zero exactly when all inputs lie in one interval. Rescaling coordinates by powers of a parameter keeps exactly those terms in the limit. They form $k$ blocks on disjoint coordinates, each a translated copy of $M(L)$. Prices are monotone under this limit because sublevel sets are Zariski closed. Hence $H(M(kL))\ge H(M(L))+D\log k$.

Limit. The normalized price $H(M(\lfloor Nt\rfloor))-D\log N$ at scale $N$ therefore dominates its value at any fixed scale $M$ up to $D\log(M\lfloor N/M\rfloor/N)$; taking $\liminf$ in $N$ and $\limsup$ in $M$ proves convergence. Changing the unit of $N$ gives $P(ct)=P(t)+D\log c$. Monotonicity in each coordinate and squeezing give continuity.

Several variables. On an axis-parallel cube of exponents, multiplication factors over the $m$ coordinates, so the exponentiated price divided by $N^m\exp(mP(t)/D)$ tends to the volume of the cube. Cutting the simplex of total degree by hyperplanes with the same convex weighting, and packing it with small cubes from inside and from outside, shows that the normalized exponentiated price tends to the volume $1/m!$ of the simplex.∎

Background — degeneration by weights. filled here Give every basis vector at every site an integer weight, and let $g_\varepsilon$ rescale each basis vector by $\varepsilon^{\text{weight}}$. A coordinate of $g_\varepsilon T$ is the original coordinate times $\varepsilon^{\text{total weight}}$. If every nonzero coordinate has total weight $\ge0$, then $\lim_{\varepsilon\to0}g_\varepsilon T$ exists and consists of the coordinates of total weight exactly $0$. The limit is a degeneration of $T$. Each $g_\varepsilon T$ is equivalent to $T$ under invertible local maps and has the same price; the limit lies in the Zariski closure of that orbit; closedness of sublevel sets gives $H(\lim)\le H(T)$. A convex weight function is the standard way to select a block-diagonal part of a multiplication tensor: Jensen’s inequality is an equality only when all arguments lie in one affine piece.

The function $P$ is the continuum profile: the price of multiplying polynomials whose lengths are $t_i$ in units of $N$, after removing the universal term $D\log N$. The scaling law says that only the ratios of the lengths matter, up to the additive term $D\log c$.

9.2 A label visible at two sites pp. 34–35

Suppose $T=\sum_bT_b$ with $T_b\ne0$ and $T_b\in X\otimes Y_b\otimes Z_b$, where the $Y_b$ are independent subspaces (a direct sum) at site $Y$ and the $Z_b$ are independent subspaces at site $Z$. The block label $b$ is visible at $Y$ and $Z$ but not at $X$. For every probability law $q$ on the block labels, $$H(T)\ge\sum_bq_bH(T_b)+d(Y,Z)\operatorname{Ent}(q),\qquad\operatorname{Ent}(q)=-\sum_bq_b\log q_b.\tag{9.1}$$

Imported — shared-leg separation, [41, Proposition 3.1] in the interpolation form of [39, Proposition 2.1]. p. 34 imported For a rational $q$ and $n$ with all $nq_b$ integral, retain at $Y$ and $Z$ the $M=n!/\prod_b(nq_b)!$ words of type $q$. Then $2M-1$ direct copies of the retained tensor degenerate to the full direct sum of the $M$ word blocks, each tensored with an $M$-dimensional wire on $Y,Z$. The interpolation uses $2M-1$ distinct field elements.

Given this, comparing prices yields $nH(T)+D\log(2M-1)\ge n\sum_bq_bH(T_b)+(D+d(Y,Z))\log M$: the left side is the price of $2M-1$ copies of (a restriction of) $T^{\otimes n}$; the right side is the price of a direct sum of $M$ blocks ($D\log M$), each of price $n\sum_bq_bH(T_b)$ and each carrying a wire of size $M$ between $Y$ and $Z$. Since $\log((2M-1)/M)\le\log2$ and $\frac1n\log M\to\operatorname{Ent}(q)$, dividing by $n$ gives (9.1).

Reading (9.1). If the block label were visible at all three sites, the direct-sum rule would give the entropy term with coefficient $D$. If it were invisible, there would be no entropy term. Visibility at two sites gives the coefficient $d(Y,Z)$, the rate of a label shared by those two sites. This is the step of [41] that the preprint’s history paragraph calls “the central degeneration”.

9.3 Slopes and intercepts on a triangle pp. 35–37

Let $i,j,k$ denote the three sites in any order, with positive arms $a_i$. Put $d_i=d(j,k)=a_j+a_k$ and $D=\delta(\{i,j,k\})$. Write $P_k(x,y)$ for the profile with output at $k$, input width $x$ at $i$ and $y$ at $j$. For a fixed first input define $$F_{ik}(t)=\exp\bigl(P_k(t_i=1,\,t_j=t)/d_i\bigr).$$

Imported — [41, proofs of Lemmas 4.1 and 4.2]. p. 35 imported $$F_{ik}\text{ is concave},\qquad F_{ik}(3t+1)\ge2F_{ik}(t)+F_{ij}(t).$$ The first comes from a degeneration in which a two-dimensional wire on $j,k$ supplies two blocks, with the length at $j$ increased and decreased by one (a degree-one Clebsch–Gordan determinant sequence). The second comes from a three-block degeneration in which the longer length is $3L_j+L_i-1$ and one of the three blocks exchanges $j$ and $k$. In both, the blocks are direct on the pair $j,k$, and optimizing (9.1) over the block probabilities adds their exponentiated prices at scale $d_i$. Both work in every characteristic.

Consequences. A positive increasing concave function has a finite limiting slope at infinity. Dividing the three-block inequality by $t$ shows that $F_{ik}$ and $F_{ij}$ have the same slope. For $S=F_{ik}+F_{ij}$ one gets $S(3t+1)\ge3S(t)$; iterating along $t_n=3^n(t+\frac12)-\frac12$ gives $S(t)/(t+\frac12)\le$ twice the common slope, so the slope is positive. Write it $\exp(c_i/d_i)$. The excess of each function over the line through the origin with that slope is nonnegative and increasing, and the bound on $S$ gives numbers $\beta_{ik},\beta_{ij}\ge0$ with $\beta_{ik}+\beta_{ij}\le1$ and $$t\le e^{-c_i/d_i}F_{ik}(t)\le t+\beta_{ik}.$$ $\beta_{ik}$ is the limiting excess after division by the slope: the intercept.

Now set $$h=\Bigl(\sum_i\frac1{a_i}\Bigr)^{-1},\quad b_i=a_i-h,\quad u_i=\frac{b_i}{a_i},\quad\beta^*_{ik}=\frac{a_k}{d_i},\quad\Delta=D-D_*(a),\quad D_*(a)=\sum_ia_i-h.$$ Since $P_k(x,y)=D\log x+P_k(1,y/x)$ and $D-d_i=b_i+\Delta$, the intercept bound gives an upper envelope valid at all widths: $$P_k(x,y)\le U_{ik}(x,y):=c_i+(b_i+\Delta)\log x+d_i\log(y+\beta_{ik}x).\tag{9.2}$$

Proposition 9.2 (Profile near contact). p. 36 paper We have $\Delta\ge0$. If $\min_iu_i\ge\eta\gt0$, there is a number $C$ common to all three sites such that $$d_i(\beta_{ik}-\beta^*_{ik})=O_\eta(\Delta),\qquad c_i+b_i\log u_i=C+O_\eta(\Delta).\tag{9.3}$$ In the sector $y/x\ge u_j/u_i$, $$0\le U_{ik}(x,y)-P_k(x,y)\le O_\eta(\Delta).\tag{9.4}$$

The statement $\Delta\ge0$ is the first assertion of the tripod principle, $D\ge D_*$. The rest says that near contact the intercepts are forced to the values $\beta^*_{ik}=a_k/(a_j+a_k)$ and the envelope (9.2) is attained.

Gap fill — the inequality behind $\Delta\ge0$. filled here

The preprint’s proof displays one inequality and derives everything from it. Here it is derived in steps. Let $E_{ik}=U_{ik}-P_k\ge0$ and $z_{ik}=d_i(\beta_{ik}-\beta^*_{ik})$.

(i) Euler’s relation. $P_k(cx,cy)=P_k(x,y)+D\log c$ gives $x\,\partial_xP_k+y\,\partial_yP_k=D$ at differentiability points.

(ii) A lower bound for each partial derivative. $P_k(x,y)=D\log x+d_i\log F_{ik}(y/x)$, so $\partial_yP_k=d_iF'_{ik}/(xF_{ik})$. Concavity makes $F'_{ik}$ at least its limiting slope $e^{c_i/d_i}$. From the definitions, $e^{E_{ik}/d_i}=e^{c_i/d_i}(y+\beta_{ik}x)/(xF_{ik})$, because the exponent of $x$ is $(b_i+\Delta-D)/d_i=-1$. Hence $$\partial_yP_k(x,y)\ \ge\ \frac{d_i}{y+\beta_{ik}x}\exp\bigl(E_{ik}(x,y)/d_i\bigr),$$ and symmetrically for $\partial_xP_k$ with $i,j$ interchanged.

(iii) Evaluate at $(x,y)=(u_i,u_j)$. Two identities hold there: $$u_j+u_i\beta^*_{ik}=1,\qquad u_jd_i+u_id_j=D_*.$$ For the first: $u_i\beta^*_{ik}=(1-h/a_i)\,a_k/(a_j+a_k)$ equals $h/a_j=1-u_j$ exactly when $a_ja_k(1-h/a_i)=h(a_j+a_k)$, that is $a_ja_k=h\,a_ja_k(1/a_i+1/a_j+1/a_k)$, which is the definition of $h$. For the second: $(1-h/a_j)(a_j+a_k)+(1-h/a_i)(a_i+a_k)=\sum a+a_k-2h-ha_k(1/a_i+1/a_j)=\sum a+a_k-2h-a_k+h=D_*$, using $h(1/a_i+1/a_j)=1-h/a_k$.

(iv) Combine. By (i) and (ii), with $y+\beta_{ik}x=1+u_iz_{ik}/d_i$ at this point, $$D\ \ge\ \frac{u_jd_i}{1+u_iz_{ik}/d_i}\,e^{E_{ik}/d_i}+\frac{u_id_j}{1+u_jz_{jk}/d_j}\,e^{E_{jk}/d_j}.$$ Expand with $(1+r)^{-1}=1-r+r^2/(1+r)$ and $e^s\ge1+s$. The constant terms sum to $D_*$ by (iii). The linear terms are $-u_iu_j(z_{ik}+z_{jk})$. The remaining terms are nonnegative multiples of $E_{ik}$, $E_{jk}$, $z_{ik}^2/d_i$, $z_{jk}^2/d_j$. Dividing by $u_iu_j$, $$\frac\Delta{u_iu_j}\ \ge\ -(z_{ik}+z_{jk})+c_\eta\Bigl(E_{ik}+E_{jk}+\frac{z_{ik}^2}{d_i}+\frac{z_{jk}^2}{d_j}\Bigr).$$

(v) Sum over the output site $k$. The row sums $z_{ik}+z_{ij}=d_i(\beta_{ik}+\beta_{ij}-1)$ are $\le0$, because $\beta_{ik}+\beta_{ij}\le1$ and $\beta^*_{ik}+\beta^*_{ij}=1$. Summing (iv) over the three choices of $k$, the linear terms are $-\sum(\text{column sums})=-\sum(\text{row sums})\ge0$. So $\Delta\cdot\sum1/(u_iu_j)\ge0$ plus nonnegative remainders: $\Delta\ge0$, and every remainder is $O_\eta(\Delta)$.

The rest of the proof of Proposition 9.2 (pp. 36–37) is bookkeeping with the six numbers $z_{ik}$: row sums lie in $[-O(\Delta),0]$, column sums are then within $O(\Delta)$ of zero, comparing the two envelopes of $P_k(u_i,u_j)$ shows the entries alternate in sign up to $O(\Delta)$, and summing three comparisons gives $2(u_1+u_2+u_3)z_{12}=O_\eta(\Delta)$. For (9.4), $E_{ik}$ depends only on $y/x$ and decreases in it.

Example (p. 37). At contact on the unit tripod, $h=\frac13$, $u_i=\frac23$, $\beta^*_{ik}=\frac12$, and $e^{-c_i/d_i}F_{ik}(t)=t+\frac12$ for $t\ge1$. The estimates (9.3) remain uniform as a single arm tends to infinity while the other two stay in a compact subset of $(0,\infty)$; this uniformity is what allows “contact at infinity” in §8.2.

10. Energy and radial contraction pp. 37–39

Role in the chain. §9 describes polynomial multiplication at one placement. To compare two placements on the same tripod, the preprint introduces a Legendre transform of the profile, the energy, and proves a monotonicity statement: moving inputs toward the output, with costs reduced in proportion, can only decrease a certain energy drop (Lemma 10.3). §11 differentiates this comparison.

Definition (energy). p. 37 paper Fix an output and a nonempty finite set $A$ of inputs with common-label rate $D\gt0$ on all the terminals. For costs $\ell_i\gt0$ per unit of input width, $$Q_A(\ell)=D+\sup_{t_i\gt0}\bigl(P(t)-\ell\cdot t\bigr).\tag{10.1}$$
Background — what the energy measures. filled here $Q_A-D$ is the convex conjugate (Legendre transform) of $-P$: it is a supremum of affine functions of $\ell$, hence convex. It is finite because $P(t)\le D\log\sum_it_i$ (the evaluation bound) grows only logarithmically while the cost is linear. Substituting $t=t'/c$ and using $P(t'/c)=P(t')-D\log c$ gives the scaling law $Q_A(c\ell)=Q_A(\ell)-D\log c$. By the envelope theorem, the derivative of $Q_A$ in a direction $\mathbf e$ is $-\mathbf e\cdot t^{\rm opt}$. In particular a common surcharge $s$ on all costs lowers the energy at the rate of the total optimal width $\sum_it_i^{\rm opt}$. The finite difference $Q_A(\ell)-Q_A(\ell+s\mathbf 1)$ used below is the integrated form of this.

The comparison is proved first for homogeneous polynomials, where an input can be moved from one multiplication to another. Let $M^{\rm hom}_m(r)$ multiply homogeneous polynomials in $m$ variables with input degrees $r=(r_i)_{i\in A}$, charge $f_i\gt0$ per degree, and define $$B_A(f)=\sup_{m\ge1,\ r_i\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}}\frac{H(M^{\rm hom}_m(r))-\sum_if_ir_i}m.$$

Lemma 10.1 (Adding an input). p. 37 paper If $\varnothing\ne A'\subseteq A$ and $s\gt0$, then $$B_A(f)-B_A(f+s\mathbf 1)\ \ge\ B_{A'}(f)-B_{A'}(f+s\mathbf 1),\tag{10.2}$$ with costs restricted to the indicated input set. If all sites in a restricted placement coincide, its energy is zero.

It suffices to add one input $e$. Rewrite the claim as $B_{A'}(f)+B_A(f+s\mathbf 1)\le B_A(f)+B_{A'}(f+s\mathbf 1)$ and take two multiplications approaching the suprema on the left, in variables $u=(u_1,\dots,u_m)$ and $v=(v_1,\dots,v_m)$. Their tensor product accepts, at each common input, a bihomogeneous polynomial in $(u,v)$, and at $e$ a polynomial $h(v)$ of degree $q$. Realize it by multiplications in which $e$ belongs to the $u$ group: rename $h(v)$ as $h(u)$, substitute $u-v$ for $u$ at the common inputs, multiply, substitute $u+v$ for $u$ at the output, and project to the right bidegree. The substitutions at the common inputs cancel and only the term $h(v)$ of $h(u+v)$ survives. Expanding into bidegree sectors, each sector factors through a multiplication on $u$ with all inputs and one on $v$ with inputs $A'$; total degrees at each input are preserved, and the surcharge on $v$ is at most what it was. Polynomially many sectors cost $O(\log n)$ in the sum bound, which vanishes after normalization.∎

In words: the benefit of a common price reduction is larger when more inputs take part (“increasing returns”). The identity $h(u)\mapsto h(u+v)$ is a Taylor shift and uses no division, so it holds in every characteristic.

Lemma 10.2 (Continuum limit of the degree energy). p. 38 paper At a placement with common-label rate $D\gt0$, $$B_A(\ell/N)=D\log N+Q_A(\ell)+o(1)\qquad(N\to\infty).\tag{10.3}$$
Charging $\ell_i/N$ per degree makes degrees of order $N$ correspond to widths of order one. The additive $D$ in (10.1) is the limit of $D(\log m-\frac1m\log m!)$, which comes from the factor $1/m!$ for total-degree polynomials in Lemma 9.1. For the lower bound, compare homogeneous multiplication with total-degree multiplication $S_m(r)$: $n$ copies of $S_m(r)$ on disjoint variable groups restrict from homogeneous multiplication in $nm+1$ variables (homogenize with the last variable). For the upper bound, split $Km$ variables into $K$ groups of $m$, record the total degree of each group in bins of length $N$, bound each bin pattern by a product of tensors $S_m(Nb)$, and sum over patterns with the sum bound; Lemma 9.1 and dominated convergence give the limit as $m\to\infty$.∎
Lemma 10.3 (Radial contraction). p. 39 paper Move each input $i$ towards the output along its geodesic, to a fraction $0\lt\lambda_i\le1$ of its original distance. Write $A'$ for the resulting placement and $\ell'_i=\lambda_i\ell_i$. For $s\gt0$, $$Q_A(\ell)-Q_A(\ell+s\mathbf 1)\ \ge\ Q_{A'}(\ell')-Q_{A'}(\ell'+s\mathbf 1)\tag{10.4}$$ when both placements have positive common-label rate.

Duplicating an input at its own site and cost leaves $B$ unchanged (multiply locally), and so does an extra input at the output at zero cost. For each fixed multidegree the price is convex as an added input moves along a geodesic, and the subtracted cost is affine; so an added input at radial fraction $\lambda_i$ with cost at least $\lambda_if_i$ leaves $B$ unchanged. This holds at $f=\ell/N$ and after raising every cost by $s/N$, since $\lambda_i\ell_i+s\ge\lambda_i(\ell_i+s)$. Insert the moved inputs, then delete the original ones with Lemma 10.1: the energy drop of the moved placement is at most that of the original. Let $N\to\infty$ in (10.3); the term $D\log N$ cancels at each placement separately.∎

11. Propagation of equality on a tripod pp. 39–43

Role in the chain. This section proves the second assertion of Proposition 8.1: on a homogeneous metric tripod, contact at one triple of arm lengths, or contact approached as one arm tends to infinity, forces contact at every triple. §8.2 used exactly this to pass from $(1,1,\infty)$ to $(1,1,1)$. The last subsection re-derives the upper bound $\omega\le9/4$ from the first assertion.

Fix three infinite rays forming a metric tripod with origin $O$, with prices homogeneous under radial dilation. Use reciprocal lengths $$v_i=\frac1{a_i},\qquad h(a)=\frac1{v_1+v_2+v_3},\qquad\Delta(v)=D(a)-D_*(a).$$ An infinitely long arm corresponds to a coordinate face $v_i=0$.

11.1 Comparing the three directional integrals pp. 39–42

Gap fill — the basic bounds (11.1). filled here From §8, $D_*(a)\le D(a)\le\sum_ia_i$ on a tripod, and $\sum_ia_i-D_*(a)=h(a)$. Replacing $v$ by $rv$ divides all arms by $r$, and $D$, $D_*$ are homogeneous of degree one in the arms. Hence $$0\le\Delta(v)\le\frac1{v_1+v_2+v_3},\qquad\Delta(rv)=r^{-1}\Delta(v)\quad(r\gt0).\tag{11.1}$$

Regularity. On the positive octant $\Delta$ is locally Lipschitz and locally semiconvex (adding $C|v|^2$ makes it convex on a small ball): $D$ is convex in the arm lengths by joint geodesic convexity, transport of one tip through distance $t$ changes $D$ by at most $t$, and the change of variables $a_i=1/v_i$ is smooth with bounded derivatives on compact subsets of the interior. $\Delta$ extends continuously to each open face, with the same regularity in the tangential variables.

The integrals. For interior $v$ and a coordinate vector $e_i$ set $$J_i(v)=\int_0^\infty\Bigl[\Delta(v+te_i)-\mathbf 1_{\{t\le1\}}\Delta(v)\Bigr]\frac{dt}t.$$ Increasing $v_i$ shortens arm $i$. $J_i$ is a logarithmically weighted average of the defect along the family of tripods obtained by shortening arm $i$ only. The subtraction makes the integral converge at $0$; (11.1) gives convergence at infinity. The key estimate is $$|J_i(v)-J_j(v)|\le C\,\Delta(v),\tag{11.2}$$ with $C$ locally uniform up to each open face. At a point of contact the three integrals coincide.

(of (11.2))

An energy derivative. Keep $v$ fixed, put the three inputs at the tips $a_i=1/v_i$ and the output at $O$, and let $Q$ be their energy (10.1). For $s\in(0,\infty)^3$ define $$G_v(s)=Q\bigl((a_is_i)_i\bigr)+\sum_ia_i\log s_i-h(a)\log\Bigl(\sum_is_i\Bigr).$$ Moving $s$ in the direction $v$ raises every cost $a_is_i$ at the same unit rate, because $a_iv_i=1$. Compare with the placement that has tip $i$ at distance $r/s_i$ and cost $r$ on every input: its cost-to-distance ratios are also $s_i$. For small $r$ it lies inside the fixed placement and for large $r$ outside, so Lemma 10.3 bounds the energy drop of the fixed placement between the drops of these comparison placements. At a comparison placement the common-label rate is $rD((1/s_i)_i)$ by homogeneity, and raising all costs from $r$ to $r+t$ lowers the energy by $rD((1/s_i)_i)\log(1+t/r)$ by the scaling law. Dividing by $t$ and letting $t\downarrow0$, both bounds tend to $D((1/s_i)_i)$. The two logarithmic terms of $G_v$ contribute $$\sum_i\frac{a_iv_i}{s_i}-h(a)\frac{\sum_iv_i}{\sum_is_i}=\sum_i\frac1{s_i}-\frac1{\sum_is_i}=D_*\bigl((1/s_i)_i\bigr).$$ Hence $$\frac d{dt}\Big|_{t=0+}G_v(s+tv)=-\Delta(s).\tag{11.3}$$ The derivative of $G_v$ along the direction $v$, at the point $s$, is minus the defect of the tripod with reciprocal lengths $s$. This identity is the link between polynomial multiplication (through $Q$) and the function $\Delta$ on the space of tripod shapes.

Starting values near the axes. For $s_i=1$, $s_j=s_k=\varepsilon$, with $B_i=b_i+\Delta(v)$, $$\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}G_v(s)=c_i+B_i\log\frac{B_i}{a_i}.\tag{11.4}$$ The upper bound uses the envelope (9.2) and convexity in the output position; the lower bound extracts one coefficient of the product at $O$ and uses the asymptotic slope of §9.3.

Integration. Start two rays near $e_i$ and $e_j$, integrate (11.3) in the direction $v$ on both, and subtract. The terminal values agree in the limit because $G_v(rs)=G_v(s)-\Delta(v)\log r$. So the difference of the two axis limits (11.4) equals $$\int_0^\infty\bigl[\Delta(e_i+tv)-\Delta(e_j+tv)\bigr]dt=J_i(v)-J_j(v),$$ where the last step uses $\Delta(e_i+tv)=t^{-1}\Delta(v+t^{-1}e_i)$ and the substitution $u=1/t$, under which $dt/t$ becomes $du/u$ and the regularizing terms cancel in the difference. By (9.3) the quantities $c_i+b_i\log(b_i/a_i)$ agree for the three sites up to $O(\Delta(v))$, and replacing $b_i$ by $B_i$ changes each by $O(\Delta(v))$.∎

11.2 The strong minimum principle pp. 42–43

Suppose $\Delta(v)=0$ at an interior point (or on an open face, using its positive coordinates). Since $\Delta\ge0$, $v$ is a minimum of $\Delta$. By (11.2) all $J_i(v)$ equal a common value $J$, and $J\ge0$ because at a zero of $\Delta$ the integrand of $J_i$ is $\Delta(v+te_i)/t\ge0$.

Dilation. By homogeneity, $J_i((1+\varepsilon)v)=J/(1+\varepsilon)$ exactly. Perform the dilation one coordinate at a time, through intermediate points $v^i=v+\varepsilon\sum_{j\le i}v_je_j$, switching from $J_i$ to $J_{i+1}$ at $v^i$ at a cost of $C\Delta(v^i)$ by (11.2). With $g_i(t)=\Delta(v+te_i)$ and $K=\limsup_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\max_i\Delta(v^i)/\varepsilon$, the preprint obtains $$-J+CK\ \ge\ \liminf_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\frac{S_\varepsilon}\varepsilon\ \ge\ \sum_{i=1}^kv_i\int_0^\infty g_i'(t)\frac{dt}t,\tag{11.5}$$ where $S_\varepsilon$ is the sum of the increments of the $J_i$ along the path. The second inequality is Fatou’s lemma; semiconvexity supplies the pointwise convergence of difference quotients and the integrable lower bound.

First derivatives vanish. The right derivatives $g_i'(0+)$ exist and are $\ge0$ (minimum). If one were positive, semiconvexity would keep $g_i'$ bounded below by a positive number near $0$, and $\int_0g_i'(t)\,dt/t$ would diverge, contradicting (11.5). So all $g_i'(0+)=0$. The directional derivative of a semiconvex function at a minimum is sublinear and nonnegative; vanishing on the coordinate vectors, it vanishes on their positive span, so $K=0$.

Integration by parts. With $K=0$, and boundary terms vanishing by $g_i(t)=o(t)$ at $0$ and (11.1) at infinity, $$-J\ \ge\ \sum_{i=1}^kv_i\int_0^\infty\frac{g_i(t)}{t^2}\,dt\ \ge\ 0.$$ As $J\ge0$, both sides vanish and $g_i(t)=0$ for all $i$ and $t\ge0$: the defect vanishes along the three coordinate rays out of $v$.

Spreading. Repeating the argument along coordinate rays reaches every point above $v$ coordinatewise. Any positive triple can be dilated into that region, and $\Delta$ is homogeneous, so $\Delta\equiv0$ on the interior (or on the face).

From a face to the interior. If $v_1=0$ and $\Delta(v)=0$ (contact with one infinite arm), then $J_2(v+re_1)\to J_2(v)=0$ as $r\downarrow0$, hence $J_1(v+re_1)\to0$ by (11.2), and Fatou’s lemma in the normal variable gives $\int_0^\infty\Delta(v+te_1)\,dt/t\le0$. The nonnegative continuous integrand vanishes, which gives an interior zero.∎

Background — the analytic tools. filled here A function is semiconvex near a point if adding a multiple of $|v|^2$ makes it convex. It then has one-sided directional derivatives everywhere, they are sublinear in the direction, and it is differentiable almost everywhere along each line. Fatou’s lemma: if $f_n\ge\phi$ with $\phi$ integrable, then $\int\liminf f_n\le\liminf\int f_n$. The name strong minimum principle comes from elliptic equations: a nonnegative supersolution that vanishes at an interior point vanishes identically. Here the role of the equation is played by (11.2), which ties together the averages of $\Delta$ in the three coordinate directions, and the role of ellipticity by homogeneity, which makes a dilation a combination of the three directions.
Not verified — analytic details of §11. not verified The following steps are reproduced from the preprint and were not re-derived line by line: the proof of the axis limit (11.4), both bounds; the domination estimates that justify Fatou’s lemma in (11.5), including the bound $-C\varepsilon t$ for paired increments near $t=0$ and the $O(\varepsilon t^{-2})$ bound for large $t$; the uniformity of the constant in (11.2) up to the faces. The algebraic identities, namely (11.1), the derivative of the logarithmic terms in (11.3), and the substitution turning the $dt$-integral into $J_i-J_j$, were checked.

11.3 The three wire lengths at the maximal price p. 43

The unconditional inequality $D\ge D_*$ also gives a geometric proof of the upper bound. Work at scale one on three sites, so that a label shared by all three has rate one. Write the three wire lengths as $a_i+a_j$; the triangle inequalities give $a_i\ge0$. Then $$1\ \ge\ \sum_ia_i-\Bigl(\sum_ia_i^{-1}\Bigr)^{-1}\ \ge\ \frac89\sum_ia_i.\tag{11.6}$$

Gap fill — the second inequality in (11.6). filled here The harmonic mean is at most the arithmetic mean: $\dfrac3{\sum_i1/a_i}\le\dfrac{\sum_ia_i}3$, so $\Bigl(\sum_i1/a_i\Bigr)^{-1}\le\dfrac19\sum_ia_i$ and $\sum_ia_i-\bigl(\sum_i1/a_i\bigr)^{-1}\ge\dfrac89\sum_ia_i$. Equality holds exactly when $a_1=a_2=a_3$. Consequently $\sum_ia_i\le\frac98$ and the perimeter $2\sum_ia_i$ is at most $\frac94$. In Widget 2, the button “scale one (3/8, 3/8, 3/8)” shows the extremal case: $D_*=1$, perimeter $\frac94$.

Since $T_n$ consists of one $n$-dimensional wire on each side, every spectral point assigns it the value $n^{2\sum_ia_i}\le n^{9/4}$. (The joint values of the three wires are preserved on passing to an algebraic closure, §3.2 item 6, so the bound holds over every field.) Strassen’s maximum formula (A.6) gives $\widetilde{\operatorname{rank}}(T_n)\le n^{9/4}$, and the recursion of Lemma 2.5 gives $\omega_F\le9/4$.

Corollary 11.1. p. 43 paper Fix $n\ge2$ and a field $F$. At every scale-one spectral point maximizing the value of $T_n$, all three wire lengths are $3/4$. There exists such a point, and it assigns $T_{a,b,c}$ the value $(abc)^{3/4}$ for every choice of positive integers $a,b,c$.
The spectral maximum is attained (compactness). Corollary 1.2 identifies its value as $n^{9/4}$, so $\sum_ia_i=9/8$ and both inequalities in (11.6) are equalities. All arms are positive, since a zero arm would give $1\ge9/8$. Equality of the arithmetic and harmonic means gives $a_i=3/8$, so each wire has rate $3/4$. The rectangular tensor has one wire of each dimension $a,b,c$, and their values multiply.∎
AHA 10. The same tripod inequality gives both directions. With the arithmetic–harmonic mean inequality it is the upper bound: no spectral point values $T_n$ above $n^{9/4}$. With equality (contact) it is the lower bound. Once the theorem is granted, the extremal spectral points are rigid: every wire has length exactly $3/4$ at scale one. Corollary 11.1 depends on Corollary 1.2, that is, on the whole preprint; inequality (11.6) and the upper bound do not.

12. Synchronization of tensor parameters pp. 43–47

Role in the chain. This is the section where the hypothesis $\omega_F\lt9/4$ enters the proof. Theorem 5.1 tests whether one price extension works for a whole family; its tests multiply arbitrary members of the family and close their extra legs jointly. For a family depending on a parameter $s$, two factors in such a test can have different parameters. The sphere calculation of §14 only gives estimates when all factors have the same parameter. Theorem 12.1 says that, under the hypothesis, the same-parameter test already controls every choice of parameters. Its proof calls Theorem 7.1 in contrapositive form.

Setting (p. 43). Fix an algebraically closed field $F$, an ordinary price $H$ of scale $D\gt0$ on base sites $S$, and a finite set $L$ of additional sites, called openings. Recall the assisted cost $\beta_H$ of (5.3). For a polynomial tensor family $T(\mathbf s)$, $\mathbf s=(s_1,\dots,s_d)$, put $$\beta_H^{\rm gen}(T(\mathbf s))=\sup_{\mathbf c\in F^d}\beta_H(T(\mathbf c)).$$ This is the assisted cost at independent indeterminates for the generic extension of $H$ from §6.2.

Theorem 12.1 (Synchronization). p. 44 paper Suppose $\omega_F\lt9/4$. For any polynomial tensor families $U_1,\dots,U_a$ on $F^d$ with common base and openings, $$\beta_H^{\rm gen}\Bigl(\bigotimes_{i=1}^aU_i(\mathbf s_i)\Bigr)=\beta_H^{\rm gen}\Bigl(\bigotimes_{i=1}^aU_i(\mathbf s)\Bigr).$$ On the left the $a$ parameter vectors vary independently. On the right they are equal. Both products group their legs at each opening, so a closure on either side may couple all the factors.
AHA 11. Under $\omega\lt9/4$ the optimal site for a tensor family cannot depend on the parameter. If the price-minimizing sites $x_z$ of the family $U(s+z)$ moved with $z$, they would be a moving family in the sense of §7, Theorem 7.1 would give $\operatorname{rank}(T_n)\ge n^{9/4}$, and Lemma 2.4 would give $\omega\ge9/4$. So they do not move, and a product of factors with different parameters can be priced as if the parameters were equal. The strict-saving hypothesis has been converted into a rigidity statement.

“$\ge$” is clear (choose equal parameters). For “$\le$”, first take $a$ copies of one univariate nonzero family $U(s)$ and write $B=\beta_H^{\rm gen}(U(s))$; the right side is then $aB$.

Step 1: a valued price above one residue. Let $\mathcal U$ be an uncountable set of indeterminates and $s$ one more. Put $E=F(\mathcal U,s)$, $K_0=\operatorname{Frac}(F(\mathcal U)[t^{\mathbb Q}])$, $J=\{z\in K_0:\nu(z)\gt0\}$. The Gauss lift (Theorem 6.1) of the generic extension of $H$ is a valued price $p$ on $K_0(s)$. For $z\in J$, translation by $z$ preserves the unit disk, so Lemma 6.2, applied to the translation and its inverse with $s$ as the fresh residue variable, gives $$\beta_p(U(s+z))=\beta_p(U(s))=B.\tag{12.1}$$

Step 2: pinned sites. Fix $M\ge1$ and an anchor site in $S$. For each $z\in J$ let $V_z$ be the pin objective of §5.4: the product of $U(s+z)^{\otimes M}$ with one penalty tensor per opening. By translation invariance $b=\beta_p(V_z)$ is the same for every $z$. Give each objective its own copy of $L$. Lemma 5.3 realizes the minimizing sites $x_{z,\ell}$, $\ell\in L$, in one complete CAT(0) spectral space over $F$, and they are unique.

Step 3: the sites satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem 7.1. By (5.5), for every nonzero test tensor $T$ over $F$ placed at sites from a finite list $z_1,\dots,z_m$, its price is $$\lim_{n\to\infty}\Bigl[\beta_p\bigl((V_{z_1}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{z_m})^{\otimes n}\otimes T\bigr)-nmb\Bigr].\tag{12.2}$$ Condition 1 (symmetry): permuting $\mathcal U$ preserves (12.2) after relabeling the $z_j$; translating every $z_j$ by $w\in J$ replaces $s$ by $s+w$, which preserves each finite-$n$ expression by the disk comparison. Condition 2 (specialization and star inequality): for an affine test $z_j=a_j+b_jc$ the tensor inside $\beta_p$ is polynomial in $c$, so Lemma 6.2 gives both inequalities for each $n$; subtracting the constant $nmb$ at every end preserves them, and they pass to the limit. Condition 3 (continuity at $0$): the entries of $U(s+z)-U(s)$ have valuation at least $\nu(z)$, so by (5.2) and the sum bound the prices of $U(s+z)$ and $U(s)$ at any placement differ by at most $\eta(z)=D\log\bigl(1+\exp((C-\nu(z))/D)\bigr)$. Evaluating $V_0$ at the sites minimizing $V_z$ gives a price at most $b+M\eta(z)$, and the midpoint estimate (5.6) gives $$2c_D\sum_{\ell\in L}d(x_{z,\ell},x_{0,\ell})^2\le M\eta(z)\ \longrightarrow\ 0\qquad(\nu(z)\to\infty).$$

Step 4: so the sites cannot move. If $z\mapsto x_{z,\ell}$ were nonconstant for some $\ell$, Theorem 7.1 would give exact rank of $T_n$ at least $n^{9/4}$ for every $n$, and Lemma 2.4 would then give $\omega_F\ge9/4$, contrary to the hypothesis. Hence for every $\ell$ all the $x_{z,\ell}$ coincide.

Step 5: remove the penalty. The sum of the penalties lies between $0$ and a constant $C_0=C_0(D,|L|)$. Using a placement that minimizes $U(s+z)$ as a competitor gives $b\le MB+C_0$, so at its pinned sites $U(s+z)$ has price at most $B+C_0/M$. For $z_1,\dots,z_a\in J$ take a common valued extension minimizing all their pin objectives. Corresponding openings are at distance zero, so transport identifies them, and additivity at the common openings gives $$\beta_p\Bigl(\bigotimes_{i=1}^aU(s+z_i)\Bigr)\le a\bigl(B+C_0/M\bigr).$$ Let $M\to\infty$: the bound $aB$ holds for all parameters in the disk above $s$.

Step 6: from the disk to independent parameters. Take independent $u_1,\dots,u_a\in\mathcal U$, a power $h$ and a closure over $F$. Applying the closure to $\bigl(\bigotimes_iU(s+vu_i)\bigr)^{\otimes h}$ gives a polynomial tensor $P(v)$ on the base. For rational $r\gt0$ the parameters $s+t^ru_i$ lie above $s$, so $p(P(t^r))\le haB$. The Gauss formula gives $H_E(P(1))\le p(P(t^r))+r\deg P$: the coefficients of $P(v)^{\otimes n}$ sum to $P(1)^{\otimes n}$, there are at most $n\deg P+1$ of them, and by (6.1) each has ordinary price at most $n\,p(P(t^r))+rn\deg P+o(n)$. Let $r\downarrow0$: $H_E(P(1))\le haB$. The substitution $u_i\mapsto s+u_i$ is an invertible change of variables over $F(s)$, so $P(1)$ tests independent generic parameters. Taking all powers and closures proves the case of one family.

Step 7: several families, several coordinates.
