For univariate families $U_i$, apply the result to $U(s)=\bigotimes_iU_i(s)$ and regroup $\bigotimes_jU(s_j)$ cyclically as a product of $a$ tensors of the form $\bigotimes_iU_i(s_{i+j})$, each with the same generic assisted cost; superadditivity of assisted cost gives the claim. For $\mathbf c_1,\dots,\mathbf c_a\in F^d$, interpolate a polynomial curve through these points and apply the univariate result along it.∎

Corollary 12.2 (Simultaneous pricing). p. 46 paper Under the hypotheses of Theorem 12.1, let $\{U_i(\mathbf s)\}_{i\in I}$ be a collection of polynomial families with common openings. For every finite list $i_1,\dots,i_m$, allowing repetitions, suppose every closure of $\bigotimes_jU_{i_j}(\mathbf s)$ at a shared generic parameter has price at most $\sum_jh_{i_j}$. Then one ordinary extension $\widehat H$ of $H$, of the same scale, satisfies $\widehat H(U_i(\mathbf c))\le h_i$ for every $i$ and every $\mathbf c\in F^d$. The conclusion also holds for rational families on a common nonempty open domain of regularity. In particular, for one such family $U$ on its domain $\Omega$, $$\min_{\widehat H\supset H}\ \sup_{\mathbf c\in\Omega}\widehat H(U(\mathbf c))=\sup_{\mathbf c\in\Omega}\beta_H(U(\mathbf c)).$$
Repeating each finite list bounds closed powers, hence the synchronized assisted cost. Theorem 12.1 transfers the bound to independently chosen parameters, and then Theorem 5.1 yields one extension. For rational families, clear denominators on a dense open set and use Zariski-closedness of sublevel sets to extend the tests to the whole domain.∎

How this is used: for the sphere tensor of §13, the parameter $s$ is the position of an additional marked point and the family index records its energies. Corollary 12.2 places the corresponding leg once, for all regular positions and all energies. Each collision of $s$ with an original marked point can then measure a distance from that single site.

13. Fermionic sphere tensors pp. 47–51

Role in the chain. This section constructs the family of tensors that the rest of the proof prices and manipulates. It is unconditional and purely algebraic. The tensors have legs attached to marked points $z_i$ on the projective line; each leg has an energy that fixes its dimension. Two properties are established: when marked points collide, each Laurent coefficient passes through a single leg whose energy is read from the order (Proposition 13.1, fusion); and at a two-point collision, varying one input over a bounded range of energies realizes every linear map from the other input to the fused leg (Lemma 13.3, fusion width). §14 prices these tensors; Parts II and III build networks from them.

13.1 The kernel and its coefficients pp. 47–48

Definition (the spaces $V_h$ and the forms $C(\mathbf h;\mathbf z)$). p. 47 paper Let $F$ be a field. Take the exterior algebra on generators $\psi_m$, $m=0,1,\dots$, and give $\psi_m$ energy $m+\frac12$. $V_h$ is the subspace of energy $h\in\frac12\mathbb Z_{\ge0}$, with basis the products $\psi_A=\bigwedge_{m\in A}\psi_m$ (in increasing order) with $\sum_{m\in A}(m+\frac12)=h$. In particular $V_0=F$.

For distinct $z_1,\dots,z_k\in F$ the multilinear form $C(\mathbf h;\mathbf z)$ on $\prod_iV_{h_i}$ is defined on basis inputs as follows. List all generators in site order. The value is a signed sum over perfect matchings of this list (Wick’s rule [58]). Two generators at one site have contraction zero. At different sites, the contraction of $\psi_m$ at $z_i$ with $\psi_n$ at $z_j$, in that order, is $$[x^my^n]\,\frac1{z_j+y-z_i-x}=\frac{(-1)^n\binom{m+n}m}{(z_j-z_i)^{m+n+1}}.\tag{13.1}$$ Pair the entries of each oriented matching consecutively, multiply their contractions, and insert the sign of the permutation from the original list to the paired list. This sum is the Pfaffian of the alternating matrix of contractions. The empty list has value one and an odd list has value zero.

Background — Pfaffians, Wick’s rule, and what the modes mean. filled here

For an antisymmetric $2N\times2N$ matrix $K$, the Pfaffian is $\operatorname{Pf}(K)=\sum_{\text{matchings}}\operatorname{sgn}\cdot\prod_{\{a\lt b\}}K_{ab}$, summed over the $(2N-1)!!$ perfect matchings of $\{1,\dots,2N\}$; $\operatorname{Pf}(K)^2=\det K$. Wick’s rule says that every correlation function of a free (Gaussian) fermionic field is the Pfaffian of its two-point functions. For the free fermion on the sphere the two-point function is $1/(z_j-z_i)$.

Formula (13.1) is the two-point function $1/\bigl((z_j+y)-(z_i+x)\bigr)$ expanded in the displacements $x,y$ of the two points. So the generator $\psi_m$ at $z_i$ stands for the $m$-th Taylor coefficient of the field at $z_i$, and its energy $m+\frac12$ is the scaling weight: $\frac12$ for the field, $1$ for each derivative. This is this tutorial’s reading of the definition; the preprint cites [31, Section 4] for the grading. Verification of (13.1): with $Z=z_j-z_i$, $\frac1{Z+y-x}=\sum_k\frac{(x-y)^k}{Z^{k+1}}$, and the coefficient of $x^my^n$ in $(x-y)^{m+n}$ is $(-1)^n\binom{m+n}m$.

Example (p. 47). Put one generator $\psi_0$ at each of four sites and write $K_{ij}=(z_j-z_i)^{-1}$. The value is $K_{12}K_{34}-K_{13}K_{24}+K_{14}K_{23}$. At a collision of $z_1$ with $z_2$ the first matching has an internal pair and a pole; the other two connect the cluster to the outside. This distinction governs the fusion expansion.

Properties (p. 48). The formulas have integer coefficients and commute with change of field. Repeated generators at a site give canceling pairs of matchings, so the forms descend to exterior powers also in characteristic two. Each coefficient is a rational function of the positions, homogeneous of degree $-\sum_ih_i$ (a contraction has degree minus the sum of its two energies). Its only possible poles are collisions of sites. As a function of one $z_i$ it is bounded at infinity, and vanishes there when $h_i\gt0$.

13.2 Fusing a cluster at a marked point pp. 48–49

Partition the sites into clusters $J_1,\dots,J_s$. In cluster $J_j$ write $z_i=p_j+ua_i$, with distinct centers $p_j$ and distinct offsets $a_i$ within each cluster. As $u\to0$ each cluster collapses to its center.

Proposition 13.1 (Fusion). p. 48 paper For each cluster $J$ there are maps $$I^J_e:\bigotimes_{i\in J}V_{h_i}\longrightarrow V_e\qquad(e\in\tfrac12\mathbb Z_{\ge0})$$ depending only on its offsets and input energies. Put $H_J=\sum_{i\in J}h_i$. The maps vanish unless $e-H_J\in\mathbb Z$. The Laurent expansion at $u=0$ is $$C(\mathbf h;\mathbf z)=\sum_{\mathbf e}u^{\sum_je_j-\sum_ih_i}\,C(\mathbf e;\mathbf p)\circ\bigl(I^{J_1}_{e_1},\dots,I^{J_s}_{e_s}\bigr).\tag{13.2}$$ Only finitely many terms contribute to each coefficient.

Group the perfect matchings by the pairs they use inside each cluster. An internal contraction at positions $p+ua_i,p+ua_{i'}$ is the contraction at the offsets times $u^{-(\text{energy of the pair})}$, by homogeneity. A contraction between different clusters is regular at $u=0$; Taylor expansion in the offsets, $[x^m](ua_i+x)^\ell=\binom\ell m(ua_i)^{\ell-m}$, replaces an unmatched generator at offset $a_i$ by $$\psi_m\longmapsto\sum_{\ell\ge m}\binom\ell ma_i^{\ell-m}\psi_\ell,\tag{13.3}$$ with one factor of $u$ per unit of index increase. The map $I^J_e$ contracts the internal pairs, translates the unmatched generators by (13.3), wedges them in order and keeps energy $e$. Removing a pair lowers the energy by the pair’s energy and gives the same negative power of $u$; raising a mode index by one raises the energy by one and gives one factor of $u$. So the total power of $u$ from a cluster with output energy $e$ is $e-H_J$.∎

Example (p. 49). Fuse two generators $\psi_0,\psi_0$ at offsets $0,1$; total energy $1$. The internal contraction gives the vacuum (energy $0$) with coefficient $u^{-1}$. If the two generators stay unmatched, their first nonzero translated wedge is $\psi_0\wedge\psi_1$, of energy $2$ and order $u^1$.

Reading (13.2). A collision replaces a cluster of legs by a single leg at the center. The order of the Laurent coefficient tells which energy that leg has: order $=$ (fused energy) $-$ (sum of the original energies). Coefficients of negative order (the principal part) pass through legs of energy smaller than the total. Extracting a Laurent coefficient is a linear operation on the tensor, so the tensor network identity “cluster $=$ fusion maps followed by a smaller sphere tensor” can be realized by interpolation in $u$.

13.3 Energy and dimension p. 49

Lemma 13.2 (Dimension of a level). p. 49 paper For every $h\in\frac12\mathbb Z_{\ge0}$ one has $d_h=\dim V_h\le e^{c\sqrt h}$, with $c=\pi/\sqrt3$. As $h\to\infty$ through all half-integral levels, $$d_h\sim\frac{\exp(c\sqrt h)}{2^{5/2}3^{1/4}h^{3/4}}.$$

For $t\gt0$ the generating function is $Z(t)=\sum_hd_he^{-th}=\prod_{m\ge0}\bigl(1+e^{-t(m+1/2)}\bigr)$: each mode is either absent or present. The function $f(x)=\log(1+e^{-x})$ is convex, so its value at the midpoint of $[mt,(m+1)t]$ is at most its average over that interval, and $$\log Z(t)=\sum_{m\ge0}f\bigl(t(m+\tfrac12)\bigr)\le\frac1t\int_0^\infty f(x)\,dx=\frac bt,\qquad b=\sum_{j\ge1}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j^2}=\frac{\pi^2}{12}.$$ Since $d_he^{-th}\le Z(t)$, $d_h\le\exp(th+b/t)$. The minimum over $t$ is at $t=\sqrt{b/h}$ and equals $\exp(2\sqrt{bh})=\exp\bigl(\tfrac\pi{\sqrt3}\sqrt h\bigr)$. For the asymptotic formula, doubling the energies identifies basis wedges of energy $h$ with partitions of $2h$ into distinct odd parts, which are in bijection with self-conjugate Young diagrams of size $2h$ (the diagonal hooks have distinct odd lengths). The number of self-conjugate partitions of $n$ is asymptotically $\exp(\pi\sqrt{n/6})/(2^{7/4}3^{1/4}n^{3/4})$; set $n=2h$.∎

The width of a leg is the logarithm of its dimension. Lemma 13.2 says: width $\approx c\sqrt{\text{energy}}$, equivalently energy $\approx(\text{width}/c)^2$. This square is the reason for the exponent $p\lt2$ in Part II and the “sum of squared widths” in Parts III–IV.

13.4 The width needed to transmit a leg pp. 49–51

Consider a two-site cluster at offsets $0,1$ with incoming energies $h,g$ and fusion map $I_e$ to energy $e$. Fixing the first input turns $I_e$ into a linear map $V_g\to V_e$.

Lemma 13.3 (Fusion width). p. 50 paper For $e,g\in\frac12\mathbb Z_{\ge0}$ put $$H(e,g)=(\sqrt e+\sqrt g)^2+(\sqrt e+\sqrt g)/\sqrt2.$$ The map $$\bigoplus_{h\le H(e,g)}V_h\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(V_g,V_e),\qquad(v_h)_h\longmapsto\sum_hI_e(v_h,-)$$ is surjective. In the wedge bases, each matrix unit has an integral preimage.

Fix mode sets $A,B$ of energies $e,g$. The goal is an input at the first point that sends $\psi_B$ to $\psi_A$ and kills every other basis vector of $V_g$. The input is a wedge of test vectors $\sum_mt_m\psi_m$, each encoded by the Laurent polynomial $f(X)=\sum_mt_mX^{-m-1}$. In a matrix coefficient of $I_e$ a test either contracts with an input mode $b$ at offset one, with coefficient $[y^b]f(1+y)$ by (13.1), or occupies an output mode $\ell$, with coefficient $t_\ell$. Assign weights $(\ell+\frac12)/\sqrt e$ to output slots and $(b+\frac12)/\sqrt g$ to input slots. For each target slot the preprint chooses a test of the form $X^{-\ell-1}(1-X^{-1})^r$ or $X^{-r-1}(1-X^{-1})^b$, with $r$ chosen so that the test can only use its designated slot or slots of strictly larger weight. The total weight of all slots in any matrix coefficient of the given energies is $\sqrt e+\sqrt g$, equal to the total target weight; so every nonzero term uses every test at its designated slot, and the coefficient is $\pm1$. Bounding the energies of the tests (at most $\sqrt2(\sqrt e+\sqrt g)$ of them) gives the cutoff $H(e,g)$.∎

The cutoff is sharp to leading order (p. 51). If the map is surjective with cutoff $H$, then $d_ed_g\le\sum_{h\le H}d_h\le(2H+1)e^{c\sqrt H}$, and Lemma 13.2 gives $\sqrt H\ge\sqrt e+\sqrt g-O(\log(e+g+2))$. In width language: to transmit a leg of width $w_g$ into a leg of width $w_e$ through a collision, the controlling leg needs width $w_e+w_g$ to leading order, which is the information-theoretic minimum, and Lemma 13.3 attains it in every characteristic.

14. The price of a sphere tensor pp. 51–55

Role in the chain. This section computes, under the hypothesis $\omega_F\lt9/4$, how expensive a sphere tensor can be when its legs are placed at given sites of a spectral space. The answer is the trivial upper bound: prepare the tensor at one site and transport each leg. With a quadratic penalty on leg widths, the optimal widths are distances from a weighted barycenter (Theorem 14.1). The inputs are Corollary 12.2 (one extra site serves all collisions), fusion (Proposition 13.1) and fusion width (Lemma 13.3). The outputs, Corollaries 14.3 and 14.4, are used in §15 and in Part III.

The transport bound. Prepare a tensor at a site $o$ and send each leg to its destination $x_i$ along a wire. By (3.1), for every nonzero sphere tensor, $$H\bigl(C(\mathbf h;\mathbf z);x_1,\dots,x_k\bigr)\le\sum_{i=1}^kd(o,x_i)\log\dim V_{h_i}.\tag{14.1}$$ Two sets of points must be kept apart. The marked points $z_i\in F$ determine the tensor’s entries. The sites $x_i$ in a spectral space determine its price. They are unrelated.

Setting (p. 51). Fix an algebraically closed $F$, distinct $z_1,\dots,z_k\in F$ and sites $\mathbf x=(x_1,\dots,x_k)$ in a complete CAT(0) spectral space $X$ over $F$, $k\ge2$; $H_{\mathbf x}$ is the price at this placement and $D$ its scale. For an integer $N\ge1$ define the normalized width of energy $h$ by $w_N(h)=c\sqrt h/N$. For positive penalties $\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_k$ set $$B_N=\max_{\mathbf h}\Bigl\{N^{-1}H_{\mathbf x}(C(\mathbf h;\mathbf z))-\sum_i\lambda_iw_N(h_i)^2\Bigr\},\tag{14.2}$$ $$E=\min_{o\in X}\sum_i\frac{d(o,x_i)^2}{4\lambda_i}.\tag{14.3}$$ The unique minimizer $o$ in (14.3) is the barycenter with weights proportional to $1/\lambda_i$ (§3.2, fact (d)). Write $r_i=d(o,x_i)$ and $\rho_N=\sqrt{\log(2N)/N}$.

Theorem 14.1 (Sphere prices). p. 51 paper Suppose $\omega_F\lt9/4$. Then $$0\le E-B_N\le O(\rho_N).\tag{14.4}$$ For every choice of maximizing energies $\mathbf e$ at each $N$, $$w_N(e_i)=\frac{r_i}{2\lambda_i}+O(\sqrt{\rho_N}),\qquad\frac{H_{\mathbf x}(C(\mathbf e;\mathbf z))}N=2E+O(\sqrt{\rho_N}).\tag{14.5}$$ The constants are uniform for fixed $k$, bounded scales $D$, and all $\lambda_i$ in a fixed compact subinterval of $(0,\infty)$.
Gap fill — the upper bound $B_N\le E$ by completing squares. filled here By Lemma 13.2, $\log\dim V_h\le c\sqrt h=N\,w_N(h)$. So (14.1), with $o$ the barycenter, gives $H_{\mathbf x}(C)/N\le\sum_ir_iw_N(h_i)$. For each $i$, $r_iw-\lambda_iw^2=\dfrac{r_i^2}{4\lambda_i}-\lambda_i\Bigl(w-\dfrac{r_i}{2\lambda_i}\Bigr)^2$. Summing, $$\frac{H_{\mathbf x}(C)}N-\sum_i\lambda_iw_N(h_i)^2\le E-\sum_i\lambda_i\Bigl(w_N(h_i)-\frac{r_i}{2\lambda_i}\Bigr)^2.\tag{14.6}$$ Hence $B_N\le E$; the vacuum (all energies zero) gives $B_N\ge0$. Moreover, once $E-B_N$ is known to be small, (14.6) forces every maximizing width to be close to $r_i/(2\lambda_i)$. This is how (14.5) follows from (14.4).

Example (p. 52). For two sites at distance $L$ the barycenter divides the segment in the ratio $\lambda_1:\lambda_2$ (so $r_1=L\lambda_1/(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)$), and both limiting widths are $L/[2(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)]$. To weight site $i$ by a mass $a_i\gt0$ choose $\lambda_i=1/(2a_i)$; its width then approaches $a_ir_i$ and the normalized price approaches $\sum_ia_ir_i^2$.

14.1 Pricing one additional leg pp. 52–53

The lower bound $B_N\ge E-O(\rho_N)$ needs a point $o$ with small $\sum_id(o,x_i)^2/(4\lambda_i)$. The preprint manufactures it as a new site $y_N$ for one additional leg, attached to an additional marked point $s$.

Lemma 14.2. p. 52 paper Suppose $\omega_F\lt9/4$, fix $R\gt0$ and put $\mu=\max_i\lambda_i$. For every $N\ge2$ the base price has an extension of scale $D$ to one more site $y_N$ such that $$H_{\mathbf x,y_N}\bigl(C((\mathbf h,g);(\mathbf z,s))\bigr)\le N\Bigl(B_N+\sum_j\lambda_jw_N(h_j)^2+\mu\,w_N(g)^2\Bigr)+O_{k,R}(D\log(2N))\tag{14.7}$$ simultaneously for all $h_j,g\le RN^2$ and $s\in F\setminus\{z_j\}$.

Reduce to a shared parameter. The family is indexed by the energies and depends rationally on $s$. By Corollary 12.2 it suffices to bound every closure of a product of $m$ such tensors at a shared generic $s$. This is the only use of the hypothesis in §14. A closure inserts one joint state in the tensor product of the $m$ additional argument spaces.

Partial fractions. If all $g_u=0$ the closed product is a multiple of a base product and (14.2) applies. Otherwise every matching contracts a generator at $s$ with one at a fixed point, and those contractions vanish as $s\to\infty$. So the closed product is a rational function of $s$ vanishing at infinity with poles only at the $z_i$: it equals the sum of its principal parts.

Each principal part lives at one old site. At the pole $z_i$, fuse the additional point with the $i$-th point in each factor (offsets $1$ and $0$). By (13.2) a term with intermediate energies $e_u$ has Laurent order $\sum_u(e_u-h_{ui}-g_u)$. After inserting the joint state, the $m$ fusion maps and the state all act at the single site $x_i$, so the term is a restriction of a product of base sphere tensors with the $i$-th energies replaced by $e_u$, whose price is bounded by (14.2). A principal-part term has negative order, $\sum_ue_u\lt\sum_u(h_{ui}+g_u)$. Because $w_N(h)^2=c^2h/N^2$ is linear in the energy, $$\sum_u\lambda_i\bigl(w_N(e_u)^2-w_N(h_{ui})^2\bigr)\lt\lambda_i\sum_uw_N(g_u)^2\le\mu\sum_uw_N(g_u)^2.$$ The number of terms is polynomial in $N$ per factor, so the sum bound costs $O_{k,R}(mD\log(2N))$.∎

AHA 12. Collisions of marked points are fusion, and the order of a pole measures energy. Because width is $c\sqrt{\text{energy}}$, a penalty on the square of the width is linear in the energy, so a single inequality between total energies (negative Laurent order) becomes an inequality between penalties. Partial fractions in the position $s$ then reduce the price of a tensor with an extra leg to prices of tensors without it. The square is forced by the partition count of Lemma 13.2.

14.2 Recovering a wire by a collision pp. 53–55

(of Theorem 14.1)

Probe leg. Fix maximizing energies $\mathbf e$, write $u_i=w_N(e_i)$ and $Q_N=\sum_i\lambda_iu_i^2$. Choose a half-integer $g$ nearest to $N\log(2N)$. By Lemma 13.2, $$v=w_N(g)\asymp\sqrt{\frac{\log(2N)}N},\qquad\log\dim V_g=Nv-O(\log(2N)).\tag{14.8}$$ This small but growing space probes the distance from $y_N$ to each $x_i$.

A collision produces a wire. Hold $h_j=e_j$ for $j\ne i$. By Lemma 13.3 the fusion maps at $z_i$ give a surjection $$\bigoplus_hV_h\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(V_g,V_{e_i}),\qquad(a_h)_h\longmapsto\sum_hI_{e_i}(a_h,-),\tag{14.9}$$ using only $h$ with $\sqrt h\le\sqrt{e_i}+\sqrt g+O(1)$. In the sphere tensor with $h_i=h$ and the extra leg $(g,s)$, extract the coefficient of $(s-z_i)^{e_i-h-g}$: by fusion it is $C(\mathbf e;\mathbf z)$ precomposed at $i$ with $I_{e_i}:V_h\otimes V_g\to V_{e_i}$. (The coefficient is extracted by interpolation at polynomially many regular values of $s$.) Combining over $h$ with a right inverse of (14.9) gives the form $$(a\otimes f,\,b)\longmapsto f(b)\,C(\mathbf e;\mathbf z)(\dots,a,\dots),\qquad a\otimes f\in V_{e_i}\otimes V_g^*\text{ at }x_i,\quad b\in V_g\text{ at }y_N.$$ This tensor is the optimizer $C(\mathbf e;\mathbf z)$ times a wire of dimension $\dim V_g$ between $x_i$ and $y_N$ (the preprint’s Figure 5, p. 54). Its price is the sum of the two prices.

Distance bound. By (14.7), producing the wire raises the $i$-th width from $u_i$ to at most $u_i+v+O(N^{-1})$ and adds the new width $v$. Subtract the optimizer’s price $N(B_N+Q_N)$: $$d(x_i,y_N)\frac{\log\dim V_g}N\le\lambda_i\bigl((u_i+v+O(N^{-1}))^2-u_i^2\bigr)+\mu v^2+O\Bigl(\frac{\log(2N)}N\Bigr)\le2\lambda_iu_iv+O\Bigl(v^2+\frac{\log(2N)}N\Bigr).$$ Divide by $v$, using (14.8): $d(x_i,y_N)\le2\lambda_iu_i+O(v)$. The choice $v\asymp\rho_N$ balances the quadratic cost $v^2$ against the interpolation cost $\log(2N)/N$.

The optimizer pays at least $E$. Project $y_N$ to the convex hull of the old sites; distances to them do not increase. Then $$E\le\sum_i\frac{d(x_i,y_N)^2}{4\lambda_i}\le Q_N+O\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{\log(2N)}N}\Bigr).\tag{14.10}$$

From penalty to price. Reuse the energies $\mathbf e$ in the objective for $B_{N+1}$. The price $H$ is unchanged; the penalty is $\sum_i\lambda_ic^2e_i/(N+1)=N^2Q_N/(N+1)$ in $(N+1)B_{N+1}$ against $NQ_N$ in $NB_N$. So $(N+1)B_{N+1}-NB_N\ge\frac N{N+1}Q_N$. Summing over scales up to $N$ with (14.10), the errors add up to $O(\sqrt{N\log(2N)})$, and $NB_N\ge NE-O(\sqrt{N\log(2N)})$. With $B_N\le E$ this is (14.4). Then (14.6) gives the widths in (14.5), and $H/N=B_N+Q_N=2E+O(\sqrt{\rho_N})$.∎

14.3 Prescribed widths and uniform comparison p. 55

Corollary 14.3 (Prescribed widths). p. 55 paper Under the hypotheses of Theorem 14.1, fix positive width caps $a_i$. Then $$\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac1N\max_{\mathbf h:\,w_N(h_i)\le a_i}H_{\mathbf x}(C(\mathbf h;\mathbf z))=\inf_{o\in X}\sum_ia_id(o,x_i).\tag{14.11}$$ Convergence is uniform over all placements of bounded scale.
Transport gives “$\le$”. For “$\ge$”, fix $\tau\gt0$ and let $o$ minimize the smoothed objective $\sum_ia_i\sqrt{d(o,x_i)^2+\tau^2}$ (a minimizer exists by the CAT(0) midpoint inequality and completeness). With $r_i=d(o,x_i)$ set $2\lambda_i=\sqrt{r_i^2+\tau^2}/a_i$. The tangent-line bound for the concave function $s\mapsto\sqrt{s+\tau^2}$ shows that the same $o$ minimizes $\sum_id(o,x_i)^2/(4\lambda_i)$. Theorem 14.1 gives limiting widths $a_ir_i/\sqrt{r_i^2+\tau^2}\lt a_i$, so maximizers meet the caps for large $N$, and their normalized price is $2E+o(1)=\sum_ia_ir_i^2/\sqrt{r_i^2+\tau^2}+o(1)\ge\inf_z\sum_ia_id(z,x_i)-\tau\sum_ia_i+o(1)$. Let $N\to\infty$, then $\tau\downarrow0$.∎
Corollary 14.4 (A common comparison tensor). p. 55 paper Suppose $\omega_F\lt9/4$. Fix the $k$ marked positions and positive caps $a_1,\dots,a_k$. Let $A_N$ be the direct sum of all nonzero $C(\mathbf h;\mathbf z)$ with $w_N(h_i)\le a_i$ at every leg. Then $$\frac{H_{\mathbf x}(A_N)}N=\inf_{o\in X}\sum_ia_id(o,x_i)+o(1)$$ uniformly over placements of bounded scale in complete CAT(0) spectral spaces over $F$. The $i$-th leg of $A_N$ has log dimension at most $Na_i+O_{k,\mathbf a}(\log(2N))$.
Each leg admits $O_{\mathbf a}(N^2)$ half-integer energies, so there are $O_{k,\mathbf a}(N^{2k})$ summands. Each summand is a restriction of $A_N$, and the direct-sum rule bounds $H(A_N)$ by the largest summand price plus $O_{k,\mathbf a}(D\log(2N))$. Corollary 14.3 gives the limit. Each summand leg has dimension at most $e^{Na_i}$ by Lemma 13.2.∎

Reading the two corollaries. The right side $\inf_o\sum_ia_id(o,x_i)$ is the cost of a star network: a hub at the best point $o$ and a wire of width $Na_i$ to each $x_i$. Corollary 14.4 says that one explicit tensor $A_N$, with legs of width about $Na_i$, is as expensive as that star at every spectral point simultaneously. By strict spectral comparison (§3.2, item 7), price inequalities that hold at every spectral point become exact tensor restrictions. So sphere tensors can stand in for bundles of wires through a hub, and wires can stand in for sphere tensors. This exchange is the raw material of Parts II and III. It holds only under the hypothesis $\omega_F\lt9/4$.

15. Incidence expansion pp. 56–58

Role in the chain. This is the last section of Part I of the paper (the heading "Part II" is printed on p. 59). It receives the price formula for sphere tensors (Theorem 14.1 in §14) and the fusion expansion (Proposition 13.1 in §13). It delivers one inequality, (15.1), which bounds how strongly the points on the secant lines of a planar point configuration can pull a common center when the points are placed in a spectral metric space. Its two consumers are Corollary 15.2 in this section and Lemma 17.1 in §17; Lemma 17.1 is the only place where the sphere-tensor results of §13–§14 enter the sorting argument of §17–§18. (That argument also uses the general price theory of §3 and §5.)

Throughout this section $F$ is a field, and all statements that carry the tag paper are conditional on the hypothesis $\omega_F \lt 9/4$, where $\omega_F$ is the exponent of matrix multiplication over $F$. The paper's final result is that this hypothesis is false, so every conditional statement here describes a situation that the paper later shows cannot occur. The statements are used inside a proof by contradiction.

The identity that the section generalizes (unnumbered introduction, p. 56)

Definition (secants). p. 56 paper Let $S$ be a finite set of distinct points of an affine plane. A secant is a line that contains at least two points of $S$. The set of secants is $\mathcal L$, and $t_i$ is the number of secants through the point $i\in S$.

The paper starts from an identity for real labels. If $v_i\in\mathbb R$ for $i\in S$ and $\sum_{i\in S} v_i=0$, then

$$\sum_{L\in\mathcal L}\Bigl|\sum_{i\in S\cap L}v_i\Bigr|^2=\sum_{i\in S}(t_i-1)\,|v_i|^2 .$$
Gap fill — the two-line computation, and the Hilbert-space version. filled here Expand each square. The term $v_i^2$ appears once for each secant through $i$, so $t_i$ times. For $i\ne j$ the product $v_iv_j$ appears once as an ordered pair, because exactly one line passes through two distinct points and that line is a secant. Hence the left side is $\sum_i t_i v_i^2+\sum_{i\ne j}v_iv_j$. The second sum is $(\sum_i v_i)^2-\sum_i v_i^2=-\sum_i v_i^2$ by the centering hypothesis. This gives the identity.

The same computation holds for vectors $v_i$ in a real Hilbert space with $\sum_i v_i=0$, with $|\cdot|$ the norm and products replaced by inner products. So in a Hilbert space the relation between "secant sums" and "point norms" is an equality. Theorem 15.1 below asserts the inequality "$\le$" for a replacement of the secant sum that makes sense in a metric space without linear structure.

15.1 An inequality for secant lines

The objects of this subsection are the following. Readers who arrive here directly need four notions from earlier sections; each is restated in one sentence.

  • A spectral space over $F$ (§3) is a metric space $(X,d)$ in which every nonzero tensor over $F$, with its legs placed at finitely many points ("sites") of $X$, has a real number called its price $H$. Prices do not increase under local linear maps, add under tensor product, and the price of a wire of dimension $q$ between sites $a,b$ is $d(a,b)\log q$.
  • A complete metric space is CAT(0) if every pair $a,b$ has a midpoint $m$ with $d(o,m)^2\le\tfrac12 d(o,a)^2+\tfrac12 d(o,b)^2-\tfrac14 d(a,b)^2$ for every point $o$. Hilbert spaces and metric trees are examples. In such a space geodesics are unique, and every finitely supported probability measure has a unique barycenter, the minimizer of the mean squared distance.
  • The Alexandrov angle $\angle_o(x,y)\in[0,\pi]$ between the geodesics from $o$ to $x$ and from $o$ to $y$ is the limit, as two points move toward $o$ along the two geodesics, of the Euclidean comparison angle at $o$. In a Hilbert space it is the usual angle.
  • The transport bound (3.1) of §3: moving a leg of dimension $q$ from site $x$ to site $y$ changes the price by at most $d(x,y)\log q$.
Definition (weighted configuration and the pull $K_C$). p. 56 paper Let $S\subset F^2$ be finite with $|S|\ge 2$. For each $i\in S$ choose a site $x_i$ in a complete CAT(0) spectral space $X$ over $F$ and a mass $a_i\gt 0$. Two points of $S$ may have the same site. Let $o\in X$ be the minimizer of $\sum_i a_i\,d(o,x_i)^2$ and put $r_i=d(o,x_i)$. For a subset $C\subset S$ define $$K_C=\sup_{y\ne o}\Bigl(\sum_{i\in C}a_i r_i\cos\angle_o(x_i,y)\Bigr)_+ ,$$ where $y$ ranges over $X$, $(\cdot)_+$ is the positive part, and a summand with $r_i=0$ is zero. If $X$ is one point, $K_C=0$. For a secant $L$ write $K_L=K_{S\cap L}$.

The paper gives the following interpretation. Suppose the legs belonging to $C$ have "widths" $a_ir_i$, that is, the logarithm of the dimension of leg $i$ is proportional to $a_ir_i$. Sending these legs from a common site $m$ to their destinations costs $\sum_{i\in C}a_ir_i\,d(m,x_i)$. If $m$ moves away from $o$ toward $y$ at unit speed, this cost initially changes at the rate $-\sum_{i\in C}a_ir_i\cos\angle_o(x_i,y)$ (first variation formula, stated below). So $K_C$ is the largest initial saving per unit distance that the group $C$ gains by moving its common site off $o$; the positive part accounts for the option of staying at $o$.

Gap fill — $K_C$ on a line and in a Hilbert space. filled here Let $X=\mathbb R$. Then $o=\sum_i a_ix_i/\sum_i a_i$, and $\cos\angle_o(x_i,y)=\operatorname{sign}(x_i-o)\operatorname{sign}(y-o)$. With $v_i=a_i(x_i-o)$ one gets $\sum_i v_i=0$, $|v_i|=a_ir_i$, and $K_C=\bigl|\sum_{i\in C}v_i\bigr|$ (take $y$ on the side of $o$ where the sum is positive). More generally, in a Hilbert space $K_C=\bigl\|\sum_{i\in C}v_i\bigr\|$, because $\sum_{i\in C}a_ir_i\cos\angle_o(x_i,y)$ is the inner product of $\sum_{i\in C}v_i$ with the unit vector toward $y$. With these values, (15.1) below is the opening identity and holds with equality. In a metric tree $K_C$ can be smaller: for three unit masses at the tips of a tripod with legs of length $r$, $o$ is the branch point, $K_{\{i\}}=r$, and $K_{\{i,j\}}=0$, while the Hilbert-space value would be $r$.
Theorem 15.1 (Incidence inequality). p. 56 paper If $F$ is algebraically closed and $\omega_F\lt 9/4$, then for every finite configuration of affine points $S\subset F^2$, weighted sites $(x_i,a_i)_{i\in S}$ in a complete CAT(0) spectral space over $F$, and secants $\mathcal L$ as above, $$\sum_{L\in\mathcal L}K_L^2\ \le\ \sum_{i\in S}(t_i-1)\,a_i^2r_i^2 .\tag{15.1}$$

The proof has five steps. Its hinge is stated first, because the steps are otherwise hard to motivate.

AHA. The points of $S$ are used twice, in two unrelated roles. As points of $F^2$ they are projected to the line $\mathbb P^1$ along a direction that depends on a parameter $s$; two projected points collide exactly when $s$ is the direction of the secant through them. As labels they index the legs of one sphere tensor whose marked points are the projections. A single nonzero rational function of $s$ on $\mathbb P^1$ has orders that sum to zero, and at each collision parameter the order of vanishing equals the excess energy of the fused legs. This degree count is linear in energy. Because the width of a fermionic leg of energy $h$ is proportional to $\sqrt h$, it becomes a bound on a sum of squared widths, which is the form of (15.1).
Imported — Theorem 14.1 (Sphere prices), as used here. imported Assume $\omega_F\lt 9/4$, $F$ algebraically closed. Let $z_1,\dots,z_k\in F$ be distinct, $k\ge2$, and let $x_1,\dots,x_k$ be sites in a complete CAT(0) spectral space over $F$. Let $C(\mathbf h;\mathbf z)$ be the sphere tensor of §13 with half-integer energies $h_i$; its $i$th leg is the space $V_{h_i}$, with $\log\dim V_h\le c\sqrt h$, $c=\pi/\sqrt3$ (Lemma 13.2). Put $w_N(h)=c\sqrt h/N$ (the "normalized width") and let $H_{\mathbf x}$ be the price at the placement $\mathbf x$. For positive penalties $\lambda_i$, let $o$ be the minimizer of $E=\min_o\sum_i d(o,x_i)^2/(4\lambda_i)$ and $r_i=d(o,x_i)$. Then every tuple of energies $\mathbf e=\mathbf e(N)$ that maximizes $N^{-1}H_{\mathbf x}(C(\mathbf h;\mathbf z))-\sum_i\lambda_iw_N(h_i)^2$ satisfies $$w_N(e_i)=\frac{r_i}{2\lambda_i}+o(1),\qquad \frac{H_{\mathbf x}(C(\mathbf e;\mathbf z))}{N}=2E+o(1)\qquad(N\to\infty).$$ See §14 for the statement with error terms and the proof.
Imported — Proposition 13.1 (Fusion), as used here. imported Partition the marked points into clusters $J$. Write $z_i=p_J+u\,\delta_i$ for $i\in J$, with distinct centers $p_J$ and, inside each cluster, distinct offsets $\delta_i$. There are linear maps $I^J_e:\bigotimes_{i\in J}V_{h_i}\to V_e$, depending only on the offsets and the input energies, such that the Laurent expansion at $u=0$ is $$C(\mathbf h;\mathbf z)=\sum_{\mathbf e}u^{\sum_J e_J-\sum_i h_i}\;C(\mathbf e;\mathbf p)\circ\bigl(I^{J}_{e_J}\bigr)_J ,$$ with finitely many terms for each power of $u$. Each summand $C(\mathbf e;\mathbf p)\circ(I^J_{e_J})_J$ is called a fusion term; $e_J\ge0$ is the intermediate energy of cluster $J$. The paper also uses homogeneity: each entry of $C(\mathbf h;\mathbf z)$ is a rational function of the positions, homogeneous of degree $-\sum_ih_i$, with poles only where two positions coincide (§13, 13.1).

Step 1: from secants to fibers of projections; (15.1) is equivalent to (15.2). Write the point $i\in S$ as $(u_i,v_i)\in F^2$ and set $z_i(s)=u_is+v_i$ for $s\in F$. For $i\ne j$ the equation $z_i(s)=z_j(s)$ has the unique solution $s=-(v_i-v_j)/(u_i-u_j)$ if $u_i\ne u_j$ and no solution if $u_i=u_j$. In the second case the paper says that the pair collides at $s=\infty$, where the projection is $i\mapsto u_i$. So each pair collides at exactly one parameter of $\mathbb P^1(F)=F\cup\{\infty\}$. Let $D\subset\mathbb P^1(F)$ be the set of finite collision parameters together with $\infty$ (the point $\infty$ is included in $D$ whether or not a collision occurs there). For $\alpha\in D$ let $\mathcal P_\alpha$ be the partition of $S$ into fibers of the projection $\pi_\alpha$, where $\pi_\alpha(u,v)=u\alpha+v$ for finite $\alpha$ and $\pi_\infty(u,v)=u$. The paper states that (15.1) is equivalent to

$$\sum_{\alpha\in D}\sum_{C\in\mathcal P_\alpha}K_C^2\ \le\ (|D|-1)\sum_{i}a_i^2r_i^2 .\tag{15.2}$$
Gap fill — why the two inequalities are equivalent. filled here The fibers of $\pi_\alpha$ are the lines of one parallel class of $F^2$ (direction $(1,-\alpha)$ for finite $\alpha$, direction $(0,1)$ for $\alpha=\infty$), and the map from $\alpha\in\mathbb P^1(F)$ to parallel classes is a bijection. A fiber with at least two points of $S$ is $S\cap L$ for a secant $L$ of that direction, and every secant arises once, at the parameter of its direction. The $t_i$ secants through $i$ have $t_i$ different directions, all in $D$. Hence the point $i$ is a singleton fiber at exactly $|D|-t_i$ parameters of $D$. For a singleton, $K_{\{i\}}=a_ir_i$: if $r_i\gt0$ take $y=x_i$, where the cosine is $1$; if $r_i=0$ both sides are zero. Therefore $$\sum_{\alpha\in D}\sum_{C\in\mathcal P_\alpha}K_C^2=\sum_{L\in\mathcal L}K_L^2+\sum_{i\in S}(|D|-t_i)\,a_i^2r_i^2 .$$ Subtracting $\sum_i(|D|-t_i)a_i^2r_i^2$ from both sides of (15.2) gives (15.1) and conversely.

So it suffices to prove (15.2).

Step 2: choosing the external widths. Fix $s_0\in F\setminus D$; this is possible because $F$ is algebraically closed, hence infinite, and $D$ is finite. The positions $z_i(s_0)$ are distinct. Apply Theorem 14.1 with marked points $z_i(s_0)$, sites $x_i$ and penalties $\lambda_i=1/(2a_i)$. Then $\sum_id(o,x_i)^2/(4\lambda_i)=\tfrac12\sum_ia_id(o,x_i)^2$, so the minimizer is the point $o$ of the definition above, and $2E=\sum_ia_ir_i^2$. The theorem gives energies $h_i=h_i(N)$ with

$$w_N(h_i)\longrightarrow a_ir_i,\qquad \frac1N H_{\mathbf x}\bigl(C(\mathbf h;\mathbf z(s_0))\bigr)\longrightarrow P:=\sum_ia_ir_i^2 .\tag{15.3}$$

The number $P$ has a second description. Preparing the tensor at $o$ and sending leg $i$ to $x_i$ costs at most $\sum_ir_i\log\dim V_{h_i}\le N\sum_ir_iw_N(h_i)$ by the transport bound, and this is $N(P+o(1))$. So (15.3) says that the chosen sphere tensor attains, to leading order in $N$, the cost of supplying it from the barycenter $o$. This is the only place where the hypothesis $\omega_F\lt9/4$ enters the proof.

If $P=0$ then every $r_i=0$, every $K_C=0$, and (15.2) holds. Assume $P\gt0$ and take $N$ large. The total energy $H_0=\sum_ih_i$ is $O(N^2)$, because the widths $w_N(h_i)=c\sqrt{h_i}/N$ are bounded. It is an integer: a nonzero entry of the sphere tensor requires a perfect matching of the fermionic generators, and a matched pair $\psi_m,\psi_n$ has total energy $(m+\tfrac12)+(n+\tfrac12)\in\mathbb Z$.

Step 3: one functional, and a joint bound on the collision energies. Write $T(s)=C(\mathbf h;\mathbf z(s))$. Its entries are rational functions of $s$ with poles only at finite collision parameters. Near a parameter $\alpha\in D$ the paper uses a local coordinate $u$ and a tensor-valued function of $u$ ("the tensor in the chart at $\alpha$"):

  • finite $\alpha$: $u=s-\alpha$, and the chart tensor is $T(\alpha+u)$, with positions $z_i=\pi_\alpha(i)+u\,u_i$;
  • $\alpha=\infty$: $u=1/s$, and the chart tensor is $C(\mathbf h;(u_i+u\,v_i)_i)$, with positions $u_i+u\,v_i$.

In both charts the points of one fiber $C\in\mathcal P_\alpha$ share a center and have distinct offsets, so Proposition 13.1 applies with the fibers as clusters: the coefficient of $u^l$ in the chart tensor is a sum of fusion terms with intermediate energies $e_C\ge0$, $\sum_{C\in\mathcal P_\alpha}e_C=H_0+l$.

Gap fill — the chart at infinity, singleton fibers, and the derivation of (15.4). filled here Chart at infinity. Since $u_i+u\,v_i=u\,(u_is+v_i)$ with $s=1/u$, homogeneity of degree $-H_0$ gives $C(\mathbf h;(u_i+uv_i)_i)=u^{-H_0}\,T(1/u)$.

Singleton fibers. A singleton fiber $\{i\}$ has an offset ($u_i$ or $v_i$) that is in general not zero. Proposition 13.1 states that a singleton with offset zero has the identity as fusion map; with a nonzero offset the fusion map is the translation (13.3) of §13, which has components at all energies $e_{\{i\}}\in h_i+\mathbb Z_{\ge0}$. The proof below treats singleton fibers exactly like other fibers, so this causes no change.

The order count. Let $\ell$ be a linear functional on the space of tensors of the format of $T(s)$ such that $f(s)=\ell(T(s))$ is not the zero function. For $\alpha\in D$ let $l_\alpha$ be the order in $u$ of the first nonzero coefficient of $\ell$ applied to the chart tensor at $\alpha$. For finite $\alpha$ this is $\operatorname{ord}_\alpha f$. At infinity, $\ell(u^{-H_0}T(1/u))=u^{-H_0}f(1/u)$, so $l_\infty=\operatorname{ord}_\infty f-H_0$. A nonzero rational function on $\mathbb P^1$ satisfies $\sum_{\beta\in\mathbb P^1}\operatorname{ord}_\beta f=0$. The function $f$ has no pole outside $D$, so the orders outside $D$ are $\ge0$ and $\sum_{\alpha\in D}\operatorname{ord}_\alpha f\le0$. Hence $\sum_{\alpha\in D}l_\alpha\le-H_0$, and $$\sum_{\alpha\in D}(H_0+l_\alpha)\le|D|H_0-H_0=(|D|-1)H_0 .$$ Each summand $H_0+l_\alpha$ is the common value of $\sum_Ce_C$ for the fusion terms of the coefficient of order $l_\alpha$, and that coefficient is nonzero, so $H_0+l_\alpha\ge0$.
$$\sum_{\alpha\in D}(H_0+l_\alpha)\le(|D|-1)\,H_0 .\tag{15.4}$$

The paper stresses that the same functional $\ell$ is used at all $\alpha\in D$. With a different functional at each parameter, each $H_0+l_\alpha$ would only be bounded separately and no joint bound on the total intermediate energy would follow.

Step 4: choosing $\ell$ so that the selected fusion terms keep the price in (15.3). A fusion term is a tensor on the original legs $V_{h_i}$, so it has a price $H_{\mathbf x}$ at the placement $\mathbf x$. In every chart collect the fusion terms with $\sum_Ce_C\le(|D|-1)H_0$. Let $W$ be the linear span of the collected terms whose price is less than $H_{\mathbf x}(T(s_0))-\sqrt N$.

Gap fill — the price bound on $W$ (the one-sentence step on p. 57). filled here Count. A collected term in the chart at $\alpha$ is determined by one half-integer $e_C\in[0,(|D|-1)H_0]$ per fiber. There are at most $|S|$ fibers and $|D|$ charts, and $H_0=O(N^2)$, so the number $M_N$ of collected terms is at most $|D|\,\bigl(2(|D|-1)H_0+1\bigr)^{|S|}$, a polynomial in $N$.

Ordinary-sum inequality. Let $\varphi$ be the spectral point behind the price, so $H=D_{\mathbf x}\log\varphi$ where $D_{\mathbf x}$ is the scale of the placement (a fixed number, independent of $N$). A sum $T_1+T_2$ of tensors in common spaces is a restriction of $T_1\oplus T_2$ (add the two blocks at every site), and $\varphi$ is additive on direct sums, so $\varphi(\sum_jT_j)\le\sum_j\varphi(T_j)$. A nonzero scalar multiple has the same price. Therefore every nonzero $\tau\in W$, written as a combination of at most $M_N$ collected terms of price below the threshold, satisfies $$H_{\mathbf x}(\tau)\ \lt\ H_{\mathbf x}(T(s_0))-\sqrt N+D_{\mathbf x}\log M_N=H_{\mathbf x}(T(s_0))-\sqrt N+O(\log N).$$ For large $N$ the right side is less than $H_{\mathbf x}(T(s_0))$. Since $T(s_0)\ne0$, this proves $T(s_0)\notin W$.

Choose a linear functional $\ell$ that vanishes on $W$ and is nonzero on $T(s_0)$. Then $f=\ell\circ T$ is not identically zero, and Step 3 applies. At each $\alpha\in D$, $\ell$ of the coefficient of order $l_\alpha$ is nonzero, so at least one fusion term $\tau_\alpha$ of that coefficient has $\ell(\tau_\alpha)\ne0$. By (15.4) and the nonnegativity of the summands, $\sum_Ce_C=H_0+l_\alpha\le(|D|-1)H_0$, so $\tau_\alpha$ was collected. Since $\ell(\tau_\alpha)\ne0$, $\tau_\alpha\notin W$, and therefore

$$H_{\mathbf x}(\tau_\alpha)\ \ge\ H_{\mathbf x}(T(s_0))-\sqrt N .$$

Select one such term per $\alpha\in D$, with intermediate energies $e_{\alpha,C}$. Summing $\sum_Ce_{\alpha,C}=H_0+l_\alpha$ over $\alpha$ and using (15.4), then multiplying by $c^2/N^2$ and using $w_N(e)^2=c^2e/N^2$, gives $\sum_{\alpha,C}w_N(e_{\alpha,C})^2\le(|D|-1)\sum_iw_N(h_i)^2$. The right side converges by (15.3). There are finitely many pairs $(\alpha,C)$, so along a subsequence of $N$ the widths $w_N(e_{\alpha,C})$ converge to limits $b_{\alpha,C}\ge0$ with

$$\sum_{\alpha\in D}\sum_{C\in\mathcal P_\alpha}b_{\alpha,C}^2\ \le\ (|D|-1)\sum_ia_i^2r_i^2 .$$

Step 5: the width needed by one fiber, $b_{\alpha,C}\ge K_C$. Fix $\alpha\in D$, a fiber $C\in\mathcal P_\alpha$, and a point $y\ne o$. The selected term is $\tau_\alpha=C(\mathbf e;\mathbf p)\circ(I^{C'}_{e_{\alpha,C'}})_{C'}$. It can be supplied as follows. Prepare the fused tensor $C(\mathbf e;\mathbf p)$ at the single site $o$, at price zero. For each fiber $C'\ne C$ apply its fusion map at $o$ and send each external leg $i\in C'$ from $o$ to $x_i$. For the fiber $C$, carry the intermediate leg $V_{e_{\alpha,C}}$ a distance $t$ along the geodesic $[o,y]$ to a site $m=m_t$, apply the fusion map there, and send the legs $i\in C$ from $m$ to $x_i$. Applying a fusion map at a site is a local linear map, which does not increase the price. By the transport bound and $\log\dim V_h\le c\sqrt h=N\,w_N(h)$,

$$H_{\mathbf x}(\tau_\alpha)\le N\Bigl(\sum_{i\notin C}r_i\,w_N(h_i)+t\,w_N(e_{\alpha,C})+\sum_{i\in C}d(m,x_i)\,w_N(h_i)\Bigr).$$

The left side is at least $H_{\mathbf x}(T(s_0))-\sqrt N$. Divide by $N$ and let $N\to\infty$ along the subsequence, using (15.3):

$$P\ \le\ \sum_{i\notin C}a_ir_i^2+b_{\alpha,C}\,t+\sum_{i\in C}a_ir_i\,d(m_t,x_i).$$

At $t=0$ the right side equals $P$. So the right side, as a function of $t\ge0$, has a nonnegative right derivative at $t=0$.

Imported — first variation formula [12, Chapter II.3]. imported In a CAT(0) space, if $m_t$ is the point at distance $t$ from $o$ on the geodesic $[o,y]$ and $x\ne o$, then $\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0^+}d(m_t,x)=-\cos\angle_o(x,y)$. The paper cites Bridson–Haefliger, Metric spaces of non-positive curvature, Chapter II.3.

The derivative is $b_{\alpha,C}-\sum_{i\in C}a_ir_i\cos\angle_o(x_i,y)$; the terms with $r_i=0$ carry the factor $r_i$ and vanish. Hence $b_{\alpha,C}\ge\sum_{i\in C}a_ir_i\cos\angle_o(x_i,y)$ for every $y\ne o$. Also $b_{\alpha,C}\ge0$. Taking the supremum over $y$ of the positive part gives $b_{\alpha,C}\ge K_C$. Together with the bound on $\sum b_{\alpha,C}^2$ at the end of Step 4 this proves (15.2), and by Step 1 it proves (15.1). ∎

15.2 The sharp local link inequality

The second statement specializes Theorem 15.1 to the affine plane over a finite field and rewrites it as a lower bound for the energy of an incidence graph. Some background on finite planes is needed first.

Background — finite affine planes, parallel classes, and the linear spectral gap. filled here Let $q$ be a prime power. The affine plane $\mathbb F_q^2$ has $q^2$ points and $q^2+q$ lines. The lines fall into $q+1$ parallel classes (one for each direction, i.e. each point of $\mathbb P^1(\mathbb F_q)$); a class consists of $q$ disjoint lines of $q$ points each. Every point lies on exactly one line of each class, and two lines of different classes meet in exactly one point.

Retain the lines of $s$ classes. The bipartite incidence graph has $q^2$ point vertices of degree $s$, $sq$ line vertices of degree $q$, and $sq^2$ edges. Its normalized incidence operator $M$ sends a function $f$ on points to the function $(Mf)(L)=(sq)^{-1/2}\sum_{i\in L}f(i)$ on retained lines. The space of functions on points decomposes orthogonally as the constants, plus, for each of the $q+1$ classes $\mathcal C$, the $(q-1)$-dimensional space $W_{\mathcal C}$ of mean-zero functions that are constant on the lines of $\mathcal C$ (dimension check: $1+(q+1)(q-1)=q^2$). For $f\in W_{\mathcal C}$ and a line $L'$ of another class, $\sum_{i\in L'}f(i)=0$ because $L'$ meets each line of $\mathcal C$ once. Hence $M^*Mf=\tfrac1s f$ if $\mathcal C$ is retained and $Mf=0$ otherwise. The singular values of $M$ are therefore $1$ (constants), $s^{-1/2}$ (multiplicity $s(q-1)$) and $0$.

Consequence for Hilbert-space placements. Put the point vertices at vectors $x_i$ with mean $o$ and the line vertices at vectors $y_L$. With $A=q^{-2}\sum_i\|x_i-o\|^2$ and $B=(sq)^{-1}\sum_L\|y_L-o\|^2$, the mean over edges of $\langle x_i-o,y_L-o\rangle$ is at most $\sqrt{AB/s}$ in absolute value (apply the singular value bound $s^{-1/2}$ coordinatewise to the centered function $x-o$). The mean squared edge length is therefore at least $A+B-2\sqrt{AB/s}$. Corollary 15.2 states that, under the hypothesis, the same bound with the same constant holds for placements in CAT(0) spectral spaces, where no linear operator is available.
Definition (the quantities $e$ and $V$). p. 58 paper Suppose $F\supseteq\mathbb F_q$. Take the $q^2$ points of $\mathbb F_q^2$ and the lines of $s$ parallel classes, $1\le s\le q+1$. A placement assigns to each point $i$ a site $x_i\in X$ and to each retained line $L$ a site $y_L\in X$. The number $e$ is the mean of $d(x_i,y_L)^2$ over the $sq^2$ incidences $i\in L$. Give each side of the graph total mass $\tfrac12$, uniformly distributed on its vertices; $V$ is the minimum over $z\in X$ of the mean squared distance from $z$ to this distribution.
Corollary 15.2 (Affine incidence links). p. 58 paper Under the hypotheses of Theorem 15.1 (so $F$ is algebraically closed, $\omega_F\lt9/4$, and $X$ is a complete CAT(0) spectral space over $F$), $$e\ \ge\ 2\,(1-s^{-1/2})\,V .$$ The coefficient is optimal in every target containing a nontrivial geodesic segment. Omitting one parallel class gives $s=q$ and a $q$-regular graph on $2q^2$ vertices.

The inequality. Let $o$ be the barycenter of the point sites alone, with unit masses. Write $r_i=d(o,x_i)$ and $r_L=d(o,y_L)$, and

$$A=q^{-2}\sum_ir_i^2,\qquad B=(sq)^{-1}\sum_{L\ \mathrm{retained}}r_L^2 .$$

In a CAT(0) space the Alexandrov angle at $o$ is at most the Euclidean comparison angle, so the law of cosines holds as an inequality ("triangle comparison"):

$$d(x_i,y_L)^2\ \ge\ r_i^2+r_L^2-2r_ir_L\cos\angle_o(x_i,y_L),$$

with the cross term read as zero when $r_i=0$ or $r_L=0$. Sum over the $sq^2$ incidences. Each point lies on $s$ retained lines and each line carries $q$ points, so the first two terms contribute $sq^2A$ and $sq^2B$. For a fixed line $L$ with $r_L\gt0$, taking $y=y_L$ in the definition of $K_L$ (unit masses) gives $\sum_{i\in L}r_i\cos\angle_o(x_i,y_L)\le K_L$.

Apply Theorem 15.1 to $S=\mathbb F_q^2\subset F^2$ with all masses one. The point $o$ is the barycenter required there. The secants of $S$ are the $q^2+q$ lines of $\mathbb F_q^2$, and $t_i=q+1$. So (15.1) reads $\sum_LK_L^2\le q\sum_ir_i^2$, the sum being over all lines, hence also over the retained lines. By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality the mean cross term over the $sq^2$ retained incidences is at most

$$\begin{aligned}\frac{2}{sq^2}\sum_{L\ \mathrm{retained}}r_LK_L\ &\le\ \frac{2}{sq^2}\sqrt{\,q\sum_ir_i^2\cdot\sum_{L\ \mathrm{retained}}r_L^2}\\ &=\ \frac{2}{sq^2}\sqrt{q\cdot q^2A\cdot sqB}\ =\ 2\sqrt{AB/s}.\end{aligned}$$

Consequently, using $2\sqrt{AB}\le A+B$,

$$e\ \ge\ A+B-2\sqrt{AB/s}\ \ge\ (1-s^{-1/2})(A+B).$$

Evaluating the mean squared distance of the half-and-half distribution at the point $o$ gives $V\le(A+B)/2$. This proves $e\ge2(1-s^{-1/2})V$.

Optimality. Choose one retained class $\mathcal C_0$ and give its $q$ lines real labels $\lambda_L$ with mean zero and mean square $A\gt0$. Give each point the label of its line in $\mathcal C_0$; give the line site of $L\in\mathcal C_0$ the label $\sqrt s\,\lambda_L$ and every other line site the label $0$. Each line of $\mathcal C_0$ has $q$ points, so the point labels have mean zero and mean square $A$. The line labels have mean zero and mean square $(sq)^{-1}\sum_{L\in\mathcal C_0}s\lambda_L^2=A$, so $B=A$. A fraction $1/s$ of the edges lies in $\mathcal C_0$, and on such an edge the product of the endpoint labels is $\sqrt s\lambda_L^2$; the mean product over all edges is $A/\sqrt s$. On the real line this gives $e=A+B-2A/\sqrt s=2(1-s^{-1/2})A$ and, since both sides have mean zero, $V=(A+B)/2=A$. So $e=2(1-s^{-1/2})V$. To realize the example in $X$, map the labels by a similarity into a geodesic segment of $X$. Nearest-point projection onto the segment does not increase distances, so the minimum defining $V$ is attained on the segment and has the same value.

The last sentence. With $s=q$ there are $q^2$ points and $q\cdot q=q^2$ lines, and both degrees equal $q$. ∎

The paper adds a remark that matters for §17: the intermediate bound $e\ge(1-s^{-1/2})(A+B)$ refers to the barycenter $o$ of the point sites, not to the minimizer of $V$. Lemma 17.1 uses this form for affine planes, and applies the secant inequality (15.1) directly for projective spaces.

Gap fill — why the constant $1-s^{-1/2}$ is the relevant one. filled here In the local-to-global arguments used in §17 (Garland's method), an inequality $e\ge2\lambda V$ on a link gives a contraction factor $1/(2\lambda)$ for an energy, and the argument needs $1/(2\lambda)\lt1$, that is $\lambda\gt\tfrac12$. With $\lambda=1-s^{-1/2}$ this holds exactly when $s\gt4$. A bound with a constant smaller than $\tfrac12$ would be of no use there; the corollary gives the same constant as the linear spectral gap computed in the background box, and the optimality statement shows that no larger constant is possible.
Part II — Sorting and table lookup Paper §16–18, pp. 59–72. All results here assume the strict saving.

16. Stable sorting under a strict square saving pp. 59–60

Role in the chain. This section opens Part II ("Sorting and table lookup"). It states the conditional sorting theorem (Theorem 16.1), whose proof occupies §17 and §18 and ends on p. 73. It proves Lemma 16.2, which converts the sorting bound into a bound for evaluating many copies of a network of tables. It receives the incidence inequality of §15 (through §17) and the tagged-permutation tensor of §4. It delivers two things to Part IV: the exponent $p\in(1,2)$ of part 1 of the theorem, which enters §27 as $\rho=p-1\lt1$ through Lemma 16.2, and the wide-record bound of part 2, which §26 uses to sort transcripts.

The standing objects are these. The field $k$ is the fixed finite field of §2, and $n=2^h$. A trivalent network over $k$ (§2) is a tensor network whose vertices carry tables $\{0,1\}^u\to k$ with $u\le3$; its size is the number of vertices plus the number of exterior bits; it represents the tensor obtained by multiplying the vertex entries and summing over all values of the internal wires. A network is exact for a tensor if it represents that tensor, not an approximation or a power of it.

Definition (tagged permutation and stable-sort tensors). p. 59 paper The inputs of $\operatorname{Tag}(h,w)$ are $n=2^h$ source records $(j_i,x_i)$, where $j_i$ is an $h$-bit destination ("tag") and $x_i$ a $w$-bit payload, and $n$ proposed records $y_j$ of $w$ bits, one for each position $j$. The tensor $\operatorname{Tag}(h,w)$ is the indicator ($0$ or $1$ in $k$) of the event that the $j_i$ are distinct and $y_{j_i}=x_i$ for every $i$. The stable-sort tensor is the indicator that a proposed output list sorts the input records by a designated key and preserves the input order among records with equal keys. Both indicators compare the payload bits as well.
Background — stable sorting, and what a network for an indicator does. filled here A sorting procedure is stable if records with equal keys leave in the order in which they entered. For a given input list the stably sorted output is unique, even when records repeat. This uniqueness is what makes the stable-sort tensor usable inside a tensor network: once the inputs are fixed, exactly one setting of the output legs has value $1$. Stability also makes radix sorting work: sorting by the least significant digit first and then stably by more significant digits yields the full order, because a later pass does not disturb the order that earlier passes created among records that agree on the later digit.

A network for such an indicator is a verifier, not a procedure. The proposed output is part of the exterior bits, and the network has value $1$ on exactly the consistent (input, output) pairs. A Boolean circuit that computes the output from the input yields a verifier of the same size up to a constant factor: replace every gate by its $0/1$ table, copy bits with equality tables, and compare the computed output with the proposed one (§2, proof of Proposition 2.3). With a classical sorting network of $O(nh^2)$ comparators (Batcher; see §18) and $O(h)$ gates per comparison of $O(h)$-bit records this gives size $O(nh^3)$ for $\operatorname{Tag}(h,h)$. Theorem 16.1 claims the exponent of $h$ can be lowered below $2$ under the hypothesis.
Theorem 16.1 (Conditional stable sorting). p. 59 paper Suppose $\omega_k\lt9/4$, and let $n=2^h$.
  1. For some $1\lt p\lt2$, $\operatorname{Tag}(h,h)$ has an exact trivalent network over $k$ of size $O(nh^p)$. The same bound holds for stable sorting of records of at most $ch$ bits, for every fixed $c$.
  2. For every $\zeta\gt0$ there is $a\gt1$ such that, for every $w\ge h^a$, both $\operatorname{Tag}(h,w)$ and stable sorting of $w$-bit records have exact trivalent networks over $k$ of size $O_\zeta(nwh^\zeta)$.

The proof is in §18.2. Three comments on the statement follow.

  • Units. The input of $\operatorname{Tag}(h,w)$ has $n(h+2w)$ bits. Part 2 says that for wide records the size per payload bit is $O(h^\zeta)$ with $\zeta$ arbitrarily small. Part 1 says that for records of width $h$ the size per input bit is $O(h^{p-1})$ with $p-1\lt1$.
  • Value of $p$. The paper gives no numerical value. The proof yields $p=2-1/(2a)$, where $a\gt1$ is the width threshold that part 2 provides for $\zeta=\tfrac12$ (p. 73). The threshold $a$ comes out of an argument by contradiction with ultralimits (Lemma 17.3), so neither $a$ nor $p$ is effective.
  • Why $p\lt2$ is the needed property. In §27 the cost per wire end of evaluating a station of arity $u$ is $O((1+u)^{p-1})$ (Lemma 16.2 below). Part IV sets $\rho=p-1$ and needs $1-\delta\gt\rho(1+\delta)$ for some $\delta\gt0$, which is possible exactly when $\rho\lt1$.

16.1 Evaluating a table by sorting its queries

A station of arity $t$ is a vertex of a network that carries an arbitrary table $f:\{0,1\}^t\to K$. A trivalent network that evaluates one such table on one query can need a number of gates proportional to $2^t$, because the table has $2^t$ independent entries. The lemma shows that $2^t$ queries to the same table can be answered together for $O((1+t)^p)$ gates per query.

Lemma 16.2 (Batched table evaluation). p. 59 paper Assume the ordinary-width stable-sort bound of Theorem 16.1 over $k$, with exponent $p\gt1$. Let a network of binary wires and $K$-valued tables represent a tensor $T$, where $K/k$ is any field extension. If $t_v\le u$ is the arity of station $v$ and $s=\sum_v(1+t_v)$, then $T^{\otimes2^u}$ has an exact trivalent $K$-network of size $$O\bigl(2^u\,s\,(1+u)^{p-1}\bigr).$$ The constant is independent of $K$ and of the entries in the tables.
AHA. The product $\prod_if(x_i)$ over a batch of queries depends only on how many times each address $z$ occurs: it equals $\prod_zf(z)^{c_z}$. Sorting the queries makes the counts $c_z$ readable by a single pass, and the power $f(z)^{c_z}$ is then produced by $t+1$ fixed one-bit factors $f(z)^{2^j}$ attached to the binary digits of $c_z$. The table entries enter only as fixed coefficients at fixed positions, so no gate has to "look up" anything.

One table, one batch. Let $f:\{0,1\}^t\to K$ with $t\ge1$, and let the $2^t$ query addresses $x_1,\dots,x_{2^t}\in\{0,1\}^t$ be exterior legs. Let $c_z$ be the number of $i$ with $x_i=z$. The required value is $\prod_if(x_i)=\prod_zf(z)^{c_z}$, with $f(z)^0=1$ also when $f(z)=0$.

Create one dictionary row for each address $z$, with a count field of $t+1$ bits whose value is summed over (a "guess"). The count field has $t+1$ bits because $0\le c_z\le2^t$. On bit $j$ of the count field of row $z$ ($0\le j\le t$) place a one-leg table with entries $(1,\ f(z)^{2^j})$: value $1$ if the bit is $0$ and $f(z)^{2^j}$ if it is $1$. The row position determines $z$, so these two entries are constants of the network. If the count field holds the number $c=\sum_j\beta_j2^j$, the product of the $t+1$ factors is $f(z)^c$.

Create one query row for each query, holding its address and zeros in the count field. Sort the $2^{t+1}$ rows stably by address and, within an address, by row type, so that the dictionary row of $z$ follows all queries to $z$. Run a counter along the sorted list: it starts at zero, increases by one at each query row, and at a dictionary row it is required to equal the carried count and is then reset to zero.

Exactness. Fix the exposed addresses. The sorted list is unique because the sort is stable. The counter states are then forced, and at the dictionary row of $z$ the counter holds $c_z$, so the only count field that passes the check is $c_z$, including $c_z=0$ at an address that receives no query. So exactly one setting of the internal wires has a nonzero contribution, and its contribution is $\prod_zf(z)^{c_z}$. No multiplicity occurs, so the identity holds in every characteristic and also when some table entries are zero.

Size of one batch. The counter and the equality checks are ripple-carry operations on $(t+1)$-bit numbers at $2^{t+1}$ rows: $O(2^t(1+t))$ gates. The rows have $O(t+1)$ bits and there are $2^{t+1}$ of them, so part 1 of Theorem 16.1 (with $h$ replaced by $t+1$) gives a sorting network of size $O(2^t(1+t)^p)$. That network has tables with entries in $k$ and represents a $0/1$ tensor; read over $K\supseteq k$ it represents the same tensor.

All stations, all copies. Take $2^u$ copies of the original network. For each station $v$ of arity $t=t_v\ge1$, group its $2^u$ copies into $2^{u-t}$ batches of $2^t$ and replace each batch by the gadget above; a station of arity zero is a scalar. Connect the exposed address bits as the wires of the original network prescribe, separately in each copy. Summing over the wires of all copies gives $T^{\otimes2^u}$. The size is at most a constant times

$$\begin{aligned}\sum_v2^{u-t_v}\cdot2^{t_v}(1+t_v)^p&=2^u\sum_v(1+t_v)^p\\ &\le\ 2^u(1+u)^{p-1}\sum_v(1+t_v)=2^u\,s\,(1+u)^{p-1}.\end{aligned}$$ ∎

Per copy of $T$, the bound is $s\,(1+u)^{p-1}$: each unit of $s$ (a station or a wire end) is charged $(1+u)^{p-1}$. This is the form in which §27 uses the lemma, with $2^u$ as part of the replication factor $d$ of Theorem 2.1.

16.2 The two scales of the sorting proof

This subsection has no numbered statement. It fixes the plan of §17–§18 and three conventions used there. The hypothesis $\omega_k\lt9/4$ is assumed throughout Part II.

The plan. The proof of Theorem 16.1 treats wide records first.

  1. Wide records by recursion on the address length. A permutation of $n=2^h$ addresses is written as a sum of networks in which records only make "hops" inside sets of about $2^{th}$ addresses, with a fixed factor $t\lt1$. A hop is a tagged permutation on a shorter address, so it can be handled by a recursive call. The requirement is that $t$ can be made arbitrarily small while the total width moved in each summand stays below $C\cdot nw$ for a constant $C$ that does not depend on $t$ (Proposition 18.1). The recursion then gives part 2 of the theorem.
  2. Ordinary width by two levels of radix sorting. A short key selects a digit inside a block; whole packs of records with the same digit are then moved together as one wide record. Both levels call the wide-record bound (§18.2).

Step 1 has a geometric part and a combinatorial part. §17 compares the price of the target permutation with the prices of the hops: first for a group acting on a CAT(0) space (Lemma 17.2), then for finite price data that pass a local consistency test (Lemma 17.3). §18 handles price data that fail the test: failure yields, by spectral comparison, an exact tensor identity that lowers a cost account. The two cases together bound the routed width.

Convention 1: units. From here to the end of Part IV, ordinary prices use base-two logarithms. A wire of one bit between sites $a,b$ then has price $d(a,b)$: the distance is the price of carrying one bit.

Convention 2: compactness of price data. The paper states that on a fixed finite list of sites, the price data of bounded scale (including scale zero) form a compact set, by (5.2) of §5 and §3, and that a bound on the diameter suffices.

Gap fill — what is compact, and why a diameter bound bounds the scale. filled here "Price data" on a finite list $S$ of sites means the function $T\mapsto H(T)$ on all nonzero tensors over the field with legs at the sites of $S$. The scale is the rate $D$ with $H(\Delta_S(q))=D\log q$ for the diagonal tensor $\Delta_S(q)$ that shares a $q$-valued label among all sites. Inequality (5.2) with trivial valuation reads $0\le H(T)\le D\sum_i\log q_i$, where the $q_i$ are the local dimensions of $T$. So price data of scale at most $D_0$ lie in a product of compact intervals, one per tensor. The price axioms (monotonicity under restriction, additivity on products, the direct-sum rule) are closed conditions in the product topology, so the set is compact by Tychonoff's theorem. A limit of prices whose scales tend to $0$ is the collapsed price, which gives every nonzero tensor price zero; that is the meaning of "including scale zero".

The scale is bounded by the diameter. Choose a spanning tree on $S$ and one binary wire along each tree edge. At each site, project the tensor product of the incident wire ends onto the settings in which all of them are equal. This is a restriction of the product of wires to $\Delta_S(2)$. Hence $D\le\sum_{\text{tree edges }(a,b)}d(a,b)\le(|S|-1)\operatorname{diam}(S)$. The paper's sentence "Equality along a spanning tree of binary wires realizes the full diagonal and bounds its price" is this argument.

Convention 3: continuity of distances between interpolated points. The paper states that distances between sites obtained from a finite list by finitely many geodesic interpolations at fixed fractions depend continuously on the original price data.

Imported — Lemma 5.2 (Geodesic determination), first assertion. imported Ordinary price data on any subset of a complete CAT(0) spectral space determine all prices on its closed geodesic convex hull, up to a price-preserving isometry fixing the given data (§5).
Gap fill — the compactness argument behind Convention 3. filled here Let price data $H_\nu$ on $S$ converge to $H$, with bounded scales. Realize each $H_\nu$ in a complete CAT(0) spectral space and adjoin the interpolated points (for example the point at fraction $\theta$ on the geodesic from $a$ to $b$). The enlarged lists lie in the convex hull of $S$, so their diameters, and by the previous box their scales, stay bounded. By Convention 2 a subnet of the enlarged price data converges. Each interpolation is characterized by two equalities, $d(a,y)=\theta\,d(a,b)$ and $d(y,b)=(1-\theta)\,d(a,b)$, and these pass to the limit. In a CAT(0) realization of the limit, uniqueness of geodesics identifies the limiting $y$ with the point at fraction $\theta$ on $[a,b]$. By Lemma 5.2 the limiting prices on the enlarged list, in particular the distances between interpolated points, are determined by $H$. Every convergent subnet therefore has the same limit, and this is continuity.

Conventions 2 and 3 are used in the proof of Lemma 17.3 (comparison of two realizations on overlapping balls) and in §18.1 (finite menus of replacements). Both uses are arguments by compactness, so the constants they produce are not effective.

17. From incidence to permutation actions pp. 61–67

Role in the chain. This section receives the incidence inequality (Theorem 15.1 and the unit-mass estimate in the proof of Corollary 15.2, §15) and the conventions of §16.2. It delivers Lemma 17.3: if prices assigned to the "payload wires" $i\to\pi(i)$ of a permutation $\pi$ are locally consistent with spectral prices in which every short hop costs at most $1$, then their mean is at most $C_0+1$, where $C_0=100$ does not depend on the number of hops into which $\pi$ is factored. Proposition 18.1 in §18 uses the contrapositive: a mean above this value forces many local inconsistencies, each of which yields a cost-reducing tensor identity.

The problem addressed is the following. A permutation of $n=2^h$ addresses can be factored into a bounded number of hops, each of which only changes a short part of the address. A record that follows the factorization pays for every hop, so the cost per payload bit is proportional to the number of factors. That number grows as the hops are made shorter, and the recursion of §18.2 needs short hops with a cost that does not grow. The section obtains the bound in three stages: an inequality on incidence graphs (17.1), a fixed-point theorem for a group generated by hop subgroups (17.3, after the definitions of 17.2), and a transfer from actions on a space to finite price data (17.4).

17.1 Two incidence inequalities

Fix a power $q\ge100$ of the characteristic of $k$. A placement of a bipartite incidence graph in a metric space assigns a site to every vertex on either side. Its incidence energy $e$ is the mean squared distance between the two sites of a uniformly chosen incidence (edge).

Background — the projective space $\mathrm{PG}(\ell-1,q)$. filled here The points of $\mathrm{PG}(\ell-1,q)$ are the one-dimensional subspaces of $\mathbb F_q^\ell$ and its lines are the two-dimensional subspaces; a point is incident to a line if it is contained in it. Every line contains $q+1$ points. Every point lies on $j=(q^{\ell-1}-1)/(q-1)$ lines (the lines through a point correspond to the points of the quotient space $\mathbb F_q^\ell/\text{point}$). Two distinct points lie on exactly one line.
Lemma 17.1. p. 61 paper Suppose $\omega_k\lt9/4$. In any complete CAT(0) spectral space over $k$, consider a placement of either of the following graphs.
  1. The points and lines of the projective space $\mathrm{PG}(\ell-1,q)$, where $\ell\ge3$.
  2. The points of $\mathbb F_Q^2$ and the affine lines in the $q+1$ directions defined over $\mathbb F_q$, where $Q=q^R$.
Let $o$ be the barycenter of the point sites. Let $A$ and $B$ be the mean squared distances to $o$ on the point and line sides, respectively, with uniform weights on each side. Then $$e\ \ge\ (1-q^{-1/2})(A+B).$$

Step 0: reduction to the algebraic closure. Theorem 15.1 needs an algebraically closed field that contains the coordinates of the configuration, and the statement here is over the finite field $k$. The algebraic closure $\bar k$ contains every $\mathbb F_Q$ in the statement, and $\omega_{\bar k}\le\omega_k\lt9/4$ (a bilinear algorithm over $k$ is one over $\bar k$).

Imported — scalar extension and ultralimits of prices (from §3). imported (a) "Under a field extension, the possible joint values of spectral points on tensors defined over the original field are preserved [42, Section 2]." (b) "Every spectral space embeds, with all its prices, in a complete CAT(0) spectral space." (c) "In a pointed metric ultralimit of these spaces, the price of a fixed tensor on a bounded sequence of placements is the ultralimit of its prices [42, Sections 2 and 2.3, Theorem 4.3 and Proposition 3.3]." The paper deduces from (a) and (b) that each finite subset of a spectral space over $k$ has an isometric realization in a complete spectral space over $\bar k$.
Background — ultralimits of metric spaces, and why they make constants non-effective. filled here An ultrafilter $\mathcal U$ on an index set $\Lambda$ is a finitely additive $\{0,1\}$-valued measure on all subsets of $\Lambda$. Every bounded family of real numbers $(c_\lambda)$ has a limit $\lim_{\mathcal U}c_\lambda$, the unique number $c$ such that $\{\lambda:|c_\lambda-c|\lt\varepsilon\}$ has measure $1$ for every $\varepsilon\gt0$. Given pointed metric spaces $(X_\lambda,d_\lambda,*_\lambda)$, the pointed ultralimit consists of the families $(x_\lambda)$ with $d_\lambda(x_\lambda,*_\lambda)$ bounded, with distance $\lim_{\mathcal U}d_\lambda(x_\lambda,y_\lambda)$, modulo distance zero. It is always complete. If every $X_\lambda$ is CAT(0), so is the ultralimit: midpoints are limits of midpoints, and the midpoint inequality passes to the limit. The barycenter of finitely many weighted points of the ultralimit is the limit of the coordinate barycenters $b_\lambda$: the variance inequality $\sum_i\mu_id_\lambda(z_\lambda,x_{i,\lambda})^2\ge\sum_i\mu_id_\lambda(b_\lambda,x_{i,\lambda})^2+d_\lambda(z_\lambda,b_\lambda)^2$ passes to the limit and shows that $\lim_{\mathcal U}b_\lambda$ is the unique minimizer.

Non-principal ultrafilters exist by the axiom of choice and cannot be written down. A proof that derives a contradiction from an ultralimit of hypothetical counterexamples shows that a constant exists and gives no bound for it. This is the source of the non-effective constants in Lemma 17.3.

Let $X$ be the given space over $k$. For every finite subset $\Phi\subset X$ containing a fixed basepoint, take a realization of $\Phi$ in a complete CAT(0) spectral space $X_\Phi$ over $\bar k$. Take the pointed ultralimit $X'$ of the $X_\Phi$ over the set of all such $\Phi$, with an ultrafilter that contains every "tail" $\{\Phi:\Phi\supseteq\Phi_0\}$. Each $x\in X$ is present in almost all $\Phi$, with all its distances to other points of $\Phi$, so $X$ maps isometrically into $X'$. By the imported item (c), $X'$ is a complete CAT(0) spectral space over $\bar k$. The image of $X$ is complete, hence closed, and it is convex because the image of a geodesic of $X$ is a geodesic of $X'$ and geodesics in $X'$ are unique. Nearest-point projection onto a closed convex set does not increase distances and fixes the set; so for a distribution supported in the image, the barycenter computed in $X'$ lies in the image and equals the barycenter computed in $X$. The quantities $e$, $A$, $B$ are therefore the same in $X$ and in $X'$, and it suffices to prove the inequality for placements in complete CAT(0) spectral spaces over $\bar k$.

Step 1: the affine plane (graph 2). Apply Theorem 15.1 to $S=\mathbb F_Q^2\subset\bar k^2$ with unit masses. The secants are the $Q^2+Q$ lines of $\mathbb F_Q^2$ and $t_i=Q+1$, so $\sum_LK_L^2\le Q\sum_ir_i^2$, the sum being over all lines. Retain the $(q+1)Q$ lines in the $q+1$ directions defined over $\mathbb F_q$; there are $(q+1)Q^2$ retained incidences. With $A=Q^{-2}\sum_ir_i^2$ and $B=((q+1)Q)^{-1}\sum_{L\text{ retained}}r_L^2$, the computation in the proof of Corollary 15.2 gives for the mean cross term

$$\frac{2}{(q+1)Q^2}\sum_{L\text{ retained}}r_LK_L\le\frac{2}{(q+1)Q^2}\sqrt{Q\cdot Q^2A\cdot(q+1)Q\,B}=2\sqrt{\frac{AB}{q+1}} .$$

Hence $e\ge A+B-2\sqrt{AB/(q+1)}\ge\bigl(1-(q+1)^{-1/2}\bigr)(A+B)\ge(1-q^{-1/2})(A+B)$.

Step 2: the projective space (graph 1). Theorem 15.1 is about points of an affine plane, so the configuration $\mathrm{PG}(\ell-1,q)$ is first moved into a plane. Choose a linear map $\bar k^\ell\to\bar k^3$ that preserves the rank of every set of at most three vectors representing points of $\mathrm{PG}(\ell-1,q)$. For one such set the condition is nonempty and Zariski open in the space of linear maps; there are finitely many sets; $\bar k$ is infinite; so a common solution exists. The images of the points are then distinct points of the projective plane over $\bar k$, and three images are collinear exactly when the three original points are. Choose an affine chart of that plane that contains all the images (a line of the plane that avoids finitely many points exists). The result is a finite set $S\subset\bar k^2$ whose secants are in bijection with the lines of $\mathrm{PG}(\ell-1,q)$, with $t_i=j$ secants through each point and $s=q+1$ points on each secant.

Write $r_i=d(o,x_i)$ for point sites and $r_L=d(o,y_L)$ for line sites, and let $K_L$ be the pull of §15.1 with unit masses. If the space is a single point the claim is trivial. Otherwise Theorem 15.1 gives $\sum_LK_L^2\le(j-1)\sum_ir_i^2$. Let $n_{\mathrm P}$ and $n_{\mathrm L}$ be the numbers of points and lines and $M=j\,n_{\mathrm P}=s\,n_{\mathrm L}$ the number of incidences. Triangle comparison and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality bound the mean cross term by

$$\frac2M\sum_Lr_LK_L\le\frac2M\sqrt{n_{\mathrm L}B\cdot(j-1)\,n_{\mathrm P}A}=2\sqrt{\frac{j-1}{js}\,AB}\ \le\ q^{-1/2}(A+B),$$

where the equality uses $M^2=j\,n_{\mathrm P}\cdot s\,n_{\mathrm L}$ and the last step uses $(j-1)/j\lt1$, $s\gt q$ and $2\sqrt{AB}\le A+B$. The sums $\sum_{(i,L)}r_i^2=MA$ and $\sum_{(i,L)}r_L^2=MB$ give $e\ge A+B-q^{-1/2}(A+B)$. ∎

17.2 Permutations on short address sets

This subsection has no numbered statement. It defines the address set, the group $G$, the hop subgroups $K_V$, the number $t$ of (17.1), and it shows that the $K_V$ generate $G$.

Definition (padded address set). p. 62 paper Choose an integer $\ell\ge3$ and a number $m$ of auxiliary coordinates; $m$ will depend only on $q$. For $n=2^h$ put $$r=\left\lfloor\frac{\log_qn}{\ell+m}\right\rfloor,\qquad U=\mathbb F_q^r,\quad N=q^r,\quad c=\left\lceil n/N^{\ell+m}\right\rceil,\qquad\mathcal I=U^\ell\times[c]\times U^m ,$$ where $[c]=\{1,\dots,c\}$. The original $n$ addresses are identified with the first $n$ elements ("labels") of $\mathcal I$ in a fixed ordering, and an input permutation $\pi$ is extended by fixed points.
Gap fill — the two size bounds. filled here $|\mathcal I|=cN^{\ell+m}$. Since $N^{\ell+m}=q^{r(\ell+m)}\le n$, we have $n\le cN^{\ell+m}\lt n+N^{\ell+m}\le2n$. Since $q^{(r+1)(\ell+m)}\gt n$, we have $n/N^{\ell+m}\lt q^{\ell+m}$ and so $c\le q^{\ell+m}$, a constant for fixed $q,\ell,m$.
Definition (the group $G$ and the hop subgroups $K_V$). p. 62 paper A permutation function takes a height $h$ and a valid input permutation as parameters and outputs a permutation of $\mathcal I$. It is required to be given by a single deterministic program that uses $nh^{O(1)}$ operations on words of $O(h)$ bits. These functions form a countable group $G$ under pointwise composition; the input permutation $\pi$ itself is an element of $G$. Identify $U^\ell$ with $\mathbb F_q^\ell\otimes U$. For a subspace $V\le\mathbb F_q^\ell$ of dimension at most two, $K_V\le G$ is the subgroup of permutation functions that preserve every fiber of the projection $\mathcal I\to(\mathbb F_q^\ell/V)\otimes U$.
AHA. An element of $G$ is a program, not a permutation of one fixed set. The same element acts on the address sets of all heights $h$ and for all inputs. This is what allows one group to act on a limit space built from problems of growing size (Lemma 17.3), and the countability of the set of programs is what allows the hops to be exhausted by an increasing sequence of finite sets.

A fiber of $\mathcal I\to(\mathbb F_q^\ell/V)\otimes U$ consists of the labels with a prescribed image; all auxiliary labels $[c]\times U^m$ lie in it, so it has $cN^{m+\dim V}$ elements. Since $N\le n^{1/(\ell+m)}$ and $c\le q^{m+\ell}$, every element of $K_V$ (a hop) permutes sets of size at most $2^{th+O(1)}$, where

$$t=\frac{m+2}{m+\ell}.\tag{17.1}$$

For fixed $m$, $t\to0$ as $\ell\to\infty$: increasing $\ell$ shortens the address inside a fiber. For a flag $0\subset L\subset P$ (a line inside a plane of $\mathbb F_q^\ell$) the subgroups are nested, $K_0\subset K_L\subset K_P$, and $K_0$ permutes only the auxiliary labels.

Background — rearrangeable (Beneš) networks as a three-factor decomposition. filled here Beneš's theorem [9], in the form used here: every permutation $\sigma$ of a product $\mathsf A\times\mathsf B$ can be written $\sigma=\sigma_3\sigma_2\sigma_1$, where $\sigma_1$ and $\sigma_3$ change only the $\mathsf A$-coordinate and $\sigma_2$ changes only the $\mathsf B$-coordinate.

Proof. Let $a=|\mathsf A|$. Form a bipartite multigraph with a left copy and a right copy of $\mathsf B$ and one edge for every element $(\alpha,\beta)$: if $\sigma(\alpha,\beta)=(\alpha',\beta')$ the edge joins left $\beta$ to right $\beta'$. Every vertex has degree $a$. By König's edge-coloring theorem an $a$-regular bipartite multigraph has a proper coloring of its edges with $a$ colors, which we take to be the elements of $\mathsf A$; at each vertex every color occurs once. Let $\gamma(\alpha,\beta)$ be the color of the edge of $(\alpha,\beta)$. Define $\sigma_1(\alpha,\beta)=(\gamma,\beta)$; it is a bijection because the colors at the left vertex $\beta$ are distinct. Define $\sigma_2(\gamma,\beta)=(\gamma,\beta')$; it is a bijection because the edges of one color form a perfect matching. Define $\sigma_3(\gamma,\beta')=(\alpha',\beta')$; it is a bijection because the colors at the right vertex $\beta'$ are distinct. $\square$

The middle factor is a family of permutations of $\mathsf B$, one for each color, and can be decomposed again if $\mathsf B$ is itself a product. For a product of $\ell+1$ coordinates this gives $2\ell+1$ factors, each changing a single coordinate. With two-element coordinates this is the classical Beneš network of $2\log_2n-1$ switching stages.

The $K_V$ generate $G$. paper Treat $[c]\times U^m$ as one coordinate, next to the $\ell$ coordinates of $U^\ell$. Applying the decomposition repeatedly writes every element of $G$ as a product of a bounded number of stages (at most $2\ell+1$ by the count in the box), each changing one coordinate. A stage that changes the $a$th coordinate of $U^\ell$ lies in $K_{L_a}$ for the coordinate line $L_a\subset\mathbb F_q^\ell$; a stage that changes the auxiliary coordinate lies in $K_0$. The paper spells out the factorization because the routing protocol of §18 uses its factors as the designated path from $i$ to $\pi(i)$.

The stages are in $G$: computing the coloring. paper Membership in $G$ requires a program with $nh^{O(1)}$ word operations, so the edge coloring must be computed in that time. The paper gives the following procedure (see also [21]).

  • Even degree. An even-regular bipartite multigraph splits into two regular halves by alternating two colors around Euler tours. (Every closed walk in a bipartite graph has even length, so the alternation closes up, and at each vertex half of the edges receive each color.)
  • Odd degree $a$. First remove one perfect matching, found as follows. Let $v$ be the number of vertices on each side and $H$ the least integer with $2^H\gt av$.
