Multiply every edge multiplicity by $\lfloor2^H/a\rfloor$ and add copies of an arbitrary "dummy" perfect matching to reach degree exactly $2^H$. The number of dummy copies per vertex is $2^H-a\lfloor2^H/a\rfloor\lt a$, so the total dummy multiplicity is less than $av\lt2^H$. Split into two regular halves $H$ times, each time keeping the half with fewer dummy edges. The dummy count at least halves in each step, so after $H$ steps it is less than $1$, that is, zero; the degree is then $2^H/2^H=1$. The kept graph is a perfect matching made of original edges. In each split, an edge of multiplicity $\mu$ contributes $\lfloor\mu/2\rfloor$ copies to each half and only the odd leftover copies are distributed by Euler tours; so it suffices to store the original edges and the dummy matching with their multiplicities.
  • After the matching is removed the degree is even and is halved. The recursion has $O(\log a)$ levels.

The total time is linear in the number of edges up to logarithmic factors. Here the number of edges is $|\mathcal I|\lt2n$.

17.3 Uniform displacement

If $G$ acts by isometries on a metric space and every hop moves a point $x$ by at most $1$, the triangle inequality along the coordinate factorization gives $d(x,\pi x)\le2\ell+1$, a bound that grows with $\ell$. The next lemma replaces it by an absolute constant, by finding a point fixed by all of $G$.

Background — three facts about complete CAT(0) spaces $Y$. filled here (i) Variance inequality. If $b$ is the barycenter of a finitely supported probability measure $\mu$, then $\int d(z,y)^2\,d\mu(y)\ge\int d(b,y)^2\,d\mu(y)+d(z,b)^2$ for every $z$. (ii) Circumcenter. A bounded set $\Omega$ has a unique smallest enclosing closed ball; its center is the circumcenter of $\Omega$. If $\Omega\subset\bar B(p,\varrho)$ and $p\in\Omega$, the circumradius is at most $\varrho$, so the circumcenter is within $\varrho$ of $p$. (iii) Equivariance. Barycenters, circumcenters, midpoints and geodesics are defined by the metric alone. An isometry that preserves a measure or a set fixes its barycenter or circumcenter; in particular a group that has a bounded orbit fixes the circumcenter of that orbit (the Bruhat–Tits fixed point theorem), and the midpoint of two fixed points is fixed.
Background — Garland's local-to-global method and its metric version. filled here Let a two-dimensional simplicial complex be given and a map $y$ from its vertices to a space $Y$. The energy of $y$ is the mean of $d(y_{W_1},y_{W_2})^2$ over edges. Every edge $W_1W_2$ of a triangle is opposite to the third vertex $V$ of that triangle, and belongs to the link of $V$ (the graph formed by the edges opposite to $V$ in the triangles through $V$). Averaging over triangles and their vertices therefore writes the energy as an average over vertices $V$ of the energies of the links. Suppose that for every $V$ there is a point $z_V$ whose mean squared distance to the link vertices is at most $\kappa$ times the link energy, with a fixed $\kappa\lt1$. Moving every $y_V$ halfway toward $z_V$ multiplies the total energy by at most $\kappa$. Iteration gives a limit map of energy zero, i.e. a constant map.

For a real Hilbert space $Y$ and $z_V$ the mean of the link, $\kappa=1/(2\lambda_1)$ where $\lambda_1$ is the first positive eigenvalue of the normalized Laplacian of the link, and the condition is $\lambda_1\gt\tfrac12$. Garland [29] used this to prove vanishing of cohomology for groups acting on Bruhat–Tits buildings; Izeki and Nayatani [30] proved fixed-point theorems for isometric actions on CAT(0) spaces under a nonlinear version of the link condition. The paper's Lemma 17.2 uses the same scheme on a small complex (one triangle for each flag $0\subset L\subset P$), with one change: the map $y$ is required to send each vertex $V$ to a point fixed by its subgroup $K_V$, and each $z_V$ must also be fixed by $K_V$. The limit point is then fixed by every $K_V$.
Lemma 17.2. p. 63 paper Suppose $G$ acts isometrically on a complete CAT(0) space $Y$ in which the inequalities of Lemma 17.1 hold for all placements. There is an absolute constant $C_0$ such that, if $d(x,gx)\le1$ for all $g\in\bigcup_VK_V$, then $G$ fixes a point within $C_0/2$ of $x$. In particular $d(x,\pi x)\le C_0$.

The last sentence follows from the first: if $y_*$ is fixed by $G$ then $d(x,\pi x)\le d(x,y_*)+d(\pi y_*,\pi x)=2\,d(x,y_*)$.

Setup. The vertices of the complex are the subspaces $V$ of $\mathbb F_q^\ell$ of dimension $0$, $1$, $2$; there is one triangle $\{0,L,P\}$ for each flag $0\subset L\subset P$. Choose a flag uniformly at random and, within it, a vertex or an edge uniformly. A placement assigns to each $V$ a point $y_V\in Y$ fixed by $K_V$. The aim is to find, for each $V$, another point $z_V$ fixed by $K_V$ such that, for a uniform flag through $V$ with opposite vertices $W_1,W_2$ and $W$ uniform in $\{W_1,W_2\}$,

$$\mathbb E\,d(z_V,y_W)^2\ \le\ \tfrac34\,\mathbb E\,d(y_{W_1},y_{W_2})^2 .\tag{17.2}$$

The vertex $V=0$. The opposite edges are the pairs $(L,P)$ with $L\subset P$, i.e. the incidences of $\mathrm{PG}(\ell-1,q)$. Let $z_0$ be the barycenter of the points $y_L$ (uniform over lines $L$). Since $K_0\subset K_L$ for every $L$, $K_0$ fixes every $y_L$ and hence $z_0$. In Lemma 17.1(1), with point sites $y_L$ and line sites $y_P$, the barycenter is $z_0$ and $\mathbb E\,d(z_0,y_W)^2=(A+B)/2$, because the marginals of a uniform incidence are uniform on each side. The lemma gives $(A+B)/2\le e/[2(1-q^{-1/2})]$, and $1/[2(1-q^{-1/2})]\le1/1.8\lt0.556$ for $q\ge100$.

The vertex $V=L$. The opposite edges join $0$ to the planes $P\supset L$. Let $z_L$ be the barycenter of the $y_P$ with $P\supset L$; it is fixed by $K_L$ because $K_L\subset K_P$. The variance inequality with test point $y_0$ gives $\mathbb E_Pd(y_0,y_P)^2\ge\mathbb E_Pd(z_L,y_P)^2+d(y_0,z_L)^2$. The left side is the link energy and the right side is $2\,\mathbb E\,d(z_L,y_W)^2$. So (17.2) holds with coefficient $\tfrac12$.

The vertex $V=P$: where the difficulty is. The opposite edges join $0$ to the lines $L\subset P$; the link energy is $e_P=\mathbb E_{L\subset P}\,d(y_0,y_L)^2$. The barycenter construction fails here for a reason of symmetry, not of expansion: $K_P$ is the largest of the three groups, and the points $y_0$, $y_L$ are fixed only by the smaller groups $K_0$, $K_L$, so their barycenter need not be fixed by $K_P$. The paper replaces the link by a large affine incidence graph made of $K_P$-translates of $y_0$ and the $y_L$, whose point barycenter is moved by $K_P$ only a small distance.

Named partitions and their sites. Fix a basis of $P$ and a linear complement of $P$ in $\mathbb F_q^\ell$. In each fiber of $\mathcal I\to(\mathbb F_q^\ell/P)\otimes U$ this gives coordinates $(x_1,x_2)\in U^2$, and each value of $(x_1,x_2)$ names a class of $cN^m$ labels. Call this the coordinate name $\mathsf p:\mathcal I\to U^2$. A named partition is any other function $\mathsf p':\mathcal I\to U^2$, computed uniformly for all heights and inputs within the time bound of $G$, that takes each value $cN^m$ times in each fiber.

Gap fill — named partitions form the coset space $K_P/K_0$, and coarse partitions the coset space $K_P/K_L$. filled here Given $\mathsf p'$, define $g$ fiber by fiber: sort the labels with coordinate name $\nu$ and the labels with new name $\nu$, and map the first list to the second in order. Then $g$ preserves the fibers modulo $P$, is computable by sorting within the time bound, so $g\in K_P$, and $\mathsf p'\circ g=\mathsf p$, i.e. $\mathsf p'=\mathsf p\circ g^{-1}$. If also $\mathsf p'=\mathsf p\circ g'^{-1}$, then $\mathsf p\circ(g^{-1}g')=\mathsf p$. The elements of $K_P$ that preserve $\mathsf p$ are those that preserve the fibers of $\mathcal I\to\mathbb F_q^\ell\otimes U$, which is the group $K_0$. So $g'=g\kappa$ with $\kappa\in K_0$, and $g'y_0=g\,y_0$ because $K_0$ fixes $y_0$. The site $\xi(\mathsf p')=g\,y_0$ is therefore well defined, and $\xi(\mathsf p'\circ\gamma^{-1})=\gamma\,\xi(\mathsf p')$ for $\gamma\in K_P$.

For a line $L\subset P$, reduce the name modulo $L\otimes U$; this gives a coarser partition of each fiber into $N$ classes. For the coordinate name, the elements of $K_P$ that preserve the reduced name are those that preserve the fibers of $\mathcal I\to(\mathbb F_q^\ell/L)\otimes U$, which is $K_L$. By the same argument the coarse partition $\mathsf p\circ g^{-1}\bmod L\otimes U$ has the well-defined site $g\,y_L$. A named partition and its reduction modulo $L$ have sites $g\,y_0$ and $g\,y_L$ with the same $g$, hence at distance exactly $d(y_0,y_L)$.

An affine plane of partitions. Let $Q=q^R$ with $R\le m$. Use $R$ of the auxiliary $U$-coordinates $(\eta_1,\dots,\eta_R)$ of a label as the values of an $\mathbb F_q$-linear map $\mathsf u:\mathbb F_Q\to U$ on a fixed $\mathbb F_q$-basis of $\mathbb F_Q$. Each label thus carries its own linear map $\mathsf u$, and for a uniform label in a fiber, $\mathsf u$ is uniform and independent of $(x_1,x_2)$. For each $(\mathsf a,\mathsf b)\in\mathbb F_Q^2$ form the name

$$\mathsf p_{\mathsf a,\mathsf b}=\bigl(x_1+\mathsf u(\mathsf a),\ x_2+\mathsf u(\mathsf b)\bigr).$$

For fixed auxiliary coordinates this is a translation of $U^2$, so every value is taken $cN^m$ times in each fiber: $\mathsf p_{\mathsf a,\mathsf b}$ is a named partition, with a site $\xi_{\mathsf a,\mathsf b}$. Consider an affine line of $\mathbb F_Q^2$ with direction $(\lambda_1,\lambda_2)\in\mathbb F_q^2$, and let $L\subset P$ be the line spanned by the vector with coordinates $(\lambda_1,\lambda_2)$. Moving from $(\mathsf a,\mathsf b)$ to $(\mathsf a+\tau\lambda_1,\mathsf b+\tau\lambda_2)$, $\tau\in\mathbb F_Q$, changes the name by $(\lambda_1\mathsf u(\tau),\lambda_2\mathsf u(\tau))$, because $\mathsf u(\lambda\tau)=\lambda\,\mathsf u(\tau)$ for $\lambda\in\mathbb F_q$. This change lies in $L\otimes U$. So the name modulo $L\otimes U$ is constant along the affine line, and the affine line receives the site of that coarse partition. This is the reason why only directions defined over $\mathbb F_q$ are retained. The result is a placement of graph 2 of Lemma 17.1 in which every incidence in direction $L$ has length exactly $d(y_0,y_L)$; every point has one line in each of the $q+1$ directions, so its incidence energy is $e_P$. With $b_0$ the barycenter of the $Q^2$ point sites, Lemma 17.1(2) gives

$$(1-q^{-1/2})(A+B)\ \le\ e_P .$$

A law of large numbers. For $d_*$ independent uniform samples $\xi_1,\dots,\xi_{d_*}$ of point sites, form the successive geodesic mean: $M_1=\xi_1$, and $M_i$ is the point at fraction $1/i$ on the geodesic from $M_{i-1}$ to $\xi_i$. Then

$$\mathbb E\,d(M_{d_*},b_0)^2\le A/d_* .\tag{17.3}$$
Gap fill — proof of (17.3). filled here For the point $M$ at fraction $\theta$ on a geodesic $[p,p']$ of a CAT(0) space, $d(z,M)^2\le(1-\theta)d(z,p)^2+\theta d(z,p')^2-\theta(1-\theta)d(p,p')^2$. Apply this with $z=b_0$, $p=M_{i-1}$, $p'=\xi_i$, $\theta=1/i$, and take the expectation over $\xi_i$ with $M_{i-1}$ fixed. Write $\epsilon=d(b_0,M_{i-1})^2$. We have $\mathbb E\,d(b_0,\xi_i)^2=A$ and, by the variance inequality at $b_0$, $\mathbb E\,d(M_{i-1},\xi_i)^2\ge A+\epsilon$. Hence $$\begin{aligned}\mathbb E\,d(b_0,M_i)^2&\le\Bigl(1-\frac1i\Bigr)\epsilon+\frac Ai-\frac1i\Bigl(1-\frac1i\Bigr)(A+\epsilon)\\ &=\Bigl(1-\frac1i\Bigr)^2\epsilon+\frac{A}{i^2}.\end{aligned}$$ With $E_i=\mathbb E\,d(b_0,M_i)^2$ this gives $E_1\le A$ and, by induction, $E_i\le\frac{(i-1)^2}{i^2}\cdot\frac{A}{i-1}+\frac{A}{i^2}=\frac Ai$.

Independent tuples. A tuple of named partitions is independent if its joint name is uniform in every fiber, at every height and input. Choose a large absolute integer $d_*$ and set $m=R=4d_*$. For $4d_*$ independent uniform elements of $\mathbb F_Q$, the probability of an $\mathbb F_q$-linear dependence is at most $\sum_{j=0}^{R-1}q^{j-R}\lt1/(q-1)$ (the $(j+1)$st element lies in the span of the first $j$ with probability at most $q^j/q^R$). So with probability at least $\tfrac12$ the coordinates $\mathsf a_i,\mathsf b_i$ of $2d_*$ sampled affine points are linearly independent. The values of the uniform linear map $\mathsf u$ on linearly independent elements are jointly uniform, also conditionally on $(x_1,x_2)$; so the $2d_*$ partitions $\mathsf p_{\mathsf a_i,\mathsf b_i}$ are then independent.

Gap fill — transitivity of $K_P$ on independent tuples, and the constant $8A/d_*$ (the step on p. 64). filled here Transitivity. Let $(\mathsf p^{(1)},\dots,\mathsf p^{(2d_*)})$ and $(\mathsf q^{(1)},\dots,\mathsf q^{(2d_*)})$ be two independent tuples, each computable within the time bound. In each fiber every joint name in $(U^2)^{2d_*}$ is taken by the same number $cN^{m+2-4d_*}$ of labels under both tuples. Sort and match the labels with equal joint names. The resulting $g$ preserves fibers modulo $P$ and is computable by sorting, so $g\in K_P$, and $\mathsf q^{(i)}=\mathsf p^{(i)}\circ g^{-1}$ for all $i$ at once. By the equivariance of the site map, $\xi(\mathsf q^{(i)})=g\,\xi(\mathsf p^{(i)})$ for all $i$.

Consequence. The successive geodesic mean is defined by the metric, so $g$ maps the mean of the first $d_*$ sites of one tuple to that of the other, and likewise for the last $d_*$ sites. The distance $\Delta$ between the means of the two halves is therefore the same number for every independent $2d_*$-tuple.

The bound on $\Delta$. For $2d_*$ independent uniform affine points with half-means $M',M''$, the triangle inequality and (17.3) give $\mathbb E\,d(M',M'')^2\le2\,\mathbb E\,d(M',b_0)^2+2\,\mathbb E\,d(M'',b_0)^2\le4A/d_*$. The tuple is independent with probability at least $\tfrac12$, and on that event $d(M',M'')^2=\Delta^2$. Hence $\tfrac12\Delta^2\le4A/d_*$, i.e. $\Delta^2\le8A/d_*$.

A good $d_*$-tuple. In the same way, $\mathbb E\,d(M,b_0)^2\le A/d_*$ for the mean $M$ of $d_*$ uniform affine points, and the tuple is independent with probability at least $\tfrac12$; so some independent $d_*$-tuple of affine partitions has $d(M,b_0)^2\le2A/d_*$.

The barycenter $b_0$ is almost fixed by $K_P$. Fix $g\in K_P$. Let $\mathbf p$ be the independent $d_*$-tuple with mean $M$ just found; its translate $g\mathbf p$ has mean $gM$. The $2d_*$-tuple $(\mathbf p,g\mathbf p)$ need not be independent. The paper constructs a third $d_*$-tuple $\mathbf p''$ such that both $(\mathbf p,\mathbf p'')$ and $(g\mathbf p,\mathbf p'')$ are independent. In each fiber, form the bipartite multigraph whose left vertices are the joint names under $\mathbf p$, whose right vertices are the joint names under $g\mathbf p$, and which has one edge for each label, joining its two joint names. Each side has $N^{2d_*}$ vertices and every vertex has degree $cN^{m+2-2d_*}=cN^{2d_*+2}$, which is divisible by $N^{2d_*}$. Color the edges properly (§17.2), group the colors into $N^{2d_*}$ sets of equal size indexed by $U^{2d_*}$, and let the third name of a label be the index of the color set of its edge. At every vertex each third name occurs equally often, which is the independence of $(\mathbf p,\mathbf p'')$ (left vertices) and of $(g\mathbf p,\mathbf p'')$ (right vertices). The coloring algorithm computes $\mathbf p''$ within the time bound. By the previous box the mean $M''$ of $\mathbf p''$ satisfies $d(M,M'')=\Delta$ and $d(gM,M'')=\Delta$. Therefore

$$\begin{aligned}d(b_0,gb_0)&\le d(b_0,M)+d(M,M'')+d(M'',gM)+d(gM,gb_0)\\ &\le2\bigl(\sqrt{2A/d_*}+\sqrt{8A/d_*}\bigr)\le0.04\sqrt A\end{aligned}$$

for $d_*$ sufficiently large. The middle expression equals $6\sqrt2\,\sqrt{A/d_*}$, so $d_*\ge45000$ suffices; this makes $m=R=180000$ (this numerical value is computed here and is not stated in the paper).

The point $z_P$. The orbit $K_Pb_0$ lies in the ball of radius $0.04\sqrt A$ about $b_0$. Its circumcenter $z_P$ is fixed by $K_P$ and satisfies $d(z_P,b_0)\le0.04\sqrt A$. Give each side of the affine incidence graph mass $\tfrac12$. The root mean squared distance from this distribution to $b_0$ is $\sqrt{(A+B)/2}$, so by the triangle inequality for root mean squares the one to $z_P$ is at most $\sqrt{(A+B)/2}+0.04\sqrt A$. Every affine point site is $\gamma y_0$ for some $\gamma\in K_P$, and every affine line site of direction $L$ is $\gamma y_L$; since $z_P$ is fixed by $\gamma$, $d(z_P,\gamma y_0)=d(z_P,y_0)$ and $d(z_P,\gamma y_L)=d(z_P,y_L)$. The mean squared distance is therefore $\tfrac12d(z_P,y_0)^2+\tfrac12\mathbb E_{L\subset P}d(z_P,y_L)^2$, which is the left side of (17.2) at $P$. Using $\sqrt A\le\sqrt{A+B}$ and $(1-q^{-1/2})(A+B)\le e_P$,

$$\begin{aligned}\mathbb E\,d(z_P,y_W)^2&\le\Bigl(\tfrac1{\sqrt2}+0.04\Bigr)^2(A+B)\\ &\le\frac{(1/\sqrt2+0.04)^2}{1-q^{-1/2}}\,e_P\ \lt\ \tfrac34\,e_P ,\end{aligned}$$

since $(1/\sqrt2+0.04)^2\lt0.559$ and $1-q^{-1/2}\ge0.9$. This proves (17.2) at $P$.

Iteration on the flags. Initialize $y_V$ as the circumcenter of the orbit $K_Vx$. The orbit lies within distance $1$ of $x$ by hypothesis, so $y_V$ is fixed by $K_V$ and within $1$ of $x$. Let $e$ be the mean squared edge length (uniform flag, uniform edge); initially $e\le4$ because $d(y_V,y_W)\le2$. In one step, replace each $y_V$ by the midpoint $y_V'$ of $y_V$ and $z_V$, which is again fixed by $K_V$.

Gap fill — the two estimates of one step and the final constant. filled here Energy. In a CAT(0) space the distance between corresponding points of two geodesics is a convex function of the parameter. Apply this to the geodesic from $y_V$ to $z_V$ and the geodesic from $z_W$ to $y_W$ (in this order): at the midpoints, $d(y_V',y_W')\le\tfrac12d(y_V,z_W)+\tfrac12d(z_V,y_W)$, hence $d(y_V',y_W')^2\le\tfrac12\bigl(d(y_V,z_W)^2+d(z_V,y_W)^2\bigr)$. Average over a uniform flag and a uniform edge $\{V,W\}$ of it. The right side becomes the mean of $d(z_V,y_W)^2$ over a uniform flag and a uniform ordered pair of its vertices. A uniform vertex of a uniform flag is $0$, a uniform line, or a uniform plane with probability $\tfrac13$ each, and the edge opposite to a uniform vertex is a uniform edge; so averaging (17.2) over $V$ gives $e_{\mathrm{new}}\le\tfrac34e$.

Movement. $d(y_V,y_V')=\tfrac12d(y_V,z_V)\le\tfrac12\bigl(d(y_V,y_W)+d(y_W,z_V)\bigr)$ for a neighbor $W$. Taking root mean squares over the flag, $V$ and $W$, the first term has root mean square $\sqrt e$ and the second at most $\sqrt{3e/4}$. So the root mean squared movement is at most $(\sqrt e+\sqrt{3e/4})/2\lt0.94\sqrt e\le\sqrt e$.

Convergence. After $j$ steps $e\le4(3/4)^j$, so the movement of step $j+1$ has root mean square at most $2(\sqrt3/2)^j$, and these numbers sum to $2/(1-\sqrt{3/4})\lt14.93$. On the set of placements use the weighted root-mean-square product metric (weight of $V$ = probability that a uniform vertex of a uniform flag is $V$). The product is complete, the sequence of placements is Cauchy, and its limit has energy zero. Every flag contains the vertex $0$, so the graph on the vertices is connected and the limit is a constant placement $y_V=y_*$. The fixed-point set of $K_V$ is closed, so $y_*$ is fixed by every $K_V$, hence by $G$ (§17.2).

Distance to $x$. The root mean square over $V$ of $d(y_V^{\mathrm{initial}},y_*)$ is at most $14.93$. Hence at least one $V$ has $d(y_V^{\mathrm{initial}},y_*)\le14.93$, and $d(x,y_V^{\mathrm{initial}})\le1$. So $d(x,y_*)\le1+2/(1-\sqrt{3/4})\lt16$. Any $C_0\ge32$ satisfies the lemma; the paper takes $C_0=100$.
∎

17.4 Finite tests on permutation orbits

Lemma 17.2 is about an action on a metric space. The routing protocol of §18 has no such space; it has a finite address set and a table of proposed prices. This subsection defines a local test on such a table and proves that passing the test almost everywhere implies the conclusion of Lemma 17.2 for the mean payload price.

Definition (core generators, commodities, tests). p. 65 paper
  • Core generators: a finite symmetric subset of $\bigcup_VK_V$ that contains the factors of a fixed coordinate factorization of $\pi$ and their inverses. For each input they define an undirected graph on $\mathcal I$ of bounded degree, with a core link from each label to its image under each generator. Each $i$ has a path to $\pi(i)$ along the factors, of length at most a fixed rational number $a_{\mathrm p}$ independent of $h$ and of the input.
  • Test parameters: the core generators, a ball radius, a maximum number of sites, and a maximum local dimension.
  • Commodities: each payload wire (the one-bit wire from $i$ to $\pi(i)$) is a commodity with its own index; so is every nonzero $k$-tensor of permitted format on an ordered list of distinct sites inside a common test ball, in fixed coordinate bases. The index is global: two balls that contain the same tensor on the same ordered sites refer to one index. There are $O(|\mathcal I|)$ indices.
  • Price array: a nonnegative number for each commodity. The number assigned to the payload wire $i\to\pi(i)$ is written $x_{i,\pi(i)}$. A payload price is capped at $a_{\mathrm p}$; each other format has a uniform rational cap that suffices to create the tensor at one site and transport its legs in binary along core links of the ball.
  • Test on a ball: compare the array with an ordinary spectral price on the sites of the ball. The error is the largest of: the absolute differences between assigned and spectral prices of the commodities of the ball, and the positive excesses above $1$ of the spectral lengths of the core links inside the ball. The discrepancy is the minimum error over all ordinary prices, the collapsed price included. The center passes to tolerance $\rho$ if the discrepancy is at most $\rho$.

The minimum is attained: a price with bounded error gives every core link length at most $1$ plus the error; the core links connect the ball, so by the spanning-tree bound of §16.2 the scale is bounded, and prices of bounded scale form a compact set.

Lemma 17.3 (Finite detection). p. 65 paper For each $\ell$ there are finite test parameters and $\rho\gt0$ such that the following holds for all sufficiently large $h$ and every valid permutation input. If all but a fraction $\rho$ of the centers pass to tolerance $\rho$, the assigned payload prices satisfy $$|\mathcal I|^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal I}x_{i,\pi(i)}\ \le\ C_0+1 .$$ Here $C_0$ is the absolute constant of Lemma 17.2.

The number $\rho$, the radius and the format bounds are obtained by contradiction from an ultralimit. The proof gives no estimate for them, and they depend on $\ell$.

The sequence of counterexamples. Suppose the lemma fails for some $\ell$. Then for every choice of finite test parameters and of $\rho$ there are counterexamples at arbitrarily large $h$. Choose a sequence indexed by $\nu=1,2,\dots$ in which the sets of core generators increase and exhaust the countable set $\bigcup_VK_V$, the radii and format bounds tend to infinity, the tolerance and the permitted exceptional fraction tend to zero, $h_\nu\to\infty$, and the factorization of $\pi$ and the cap $a_{\mathrm p}$ are kept fixed. In each counterexample the mean payload price exceeds $C_0+1$. The aim is an isometric action of $G$ in which every hop displaces a point $x$ by at most $1$ and $\pi$ displaces it by at least $C_0+1$, contradicting Lemma 17.2.

Charts and marks. At each passing center $i$ take a complete CAT(0) spectral realization $X_i$ of a price that attains the test on the ball of $i$ (the chart at $i$); at a failing center take the one-point space. For $g\in G$ the mark of $g$ in the chart at $i$ is the site $g^{-1}i$ (the center itself if that site is not in the ball). Since $g^{-1}$ is a fixed word in elements of the $K_V$, for large $\nu$ these elements are core generators and the ball contains the whole path from $i$ to $g^{-1}i$; each link of the path has length at most $1$ plus the tolerance, so the mark is at a distance from the center that is bounded for each fixed $g$.

The limit space. For each $\nu$ form the product of the charts over all centers $i\in\mathcal I$ with the root-mean-square distance $\bigl(|\mathcal I|^{-1}\sum_id_i(\cdot,\cdot)^2\bigr)^{1/2}$, pointed at the vector of centers. Take a pointed ultralimit over $\nu$, and let $Y$ be the closed convex hull of the limits $\mathbf m_g$ of the vectors of marks, $g\in G$. Products with the root-mean-square distance and ultralimits of complete CAT(0) spaces are complete CAT(0) spaces (the midpoint inequality is preserved by averaging and by limits). Both inequalities of Lemma 17.1 hold in $Y$: they hold in each chart; in a product the barycenter is taken coordinatewise and $e$, $A$, $B$ are the averages of their coordinate values; in the ultralimit the barycenter is the limit of the barycenters (background box in §17.1); and a closed convex subset contains the barycenters of the distributions supported in it. Lemma 17.1 is stated for spectral spaces over $k$; this step transfers its two inequalities to $Y$ and not a spectral structure, which is what the hypothesis of Lemma 17.2 asks for ("in which the inequalities of Lemma 17.1 hold for all placements").

The action. For $u\in G$ the paper claims that $\mathbf m_g\mapsto\mathbf m_{ug}$ extends to an isometry of $Y$. A dense subset of $Y$ consists of the points given by finite expressions of geodesic interpolations of marks. Fix two such expressions. In the chart at $i$, the translated expressions involve the labels $(ug)^{-1}i=g^{-1}(u^{-1}i)$; these are the labels of the original expressions in the chart at $u^{-1}i$. So the comparison is between two realizations, in two different charts, of prices on the same finite list of labels. For large $\nu$ both balls contain the list, and both centers pass except for a fraction tending to zero (the map $i\mapsto u^{-1}i$ preserves the uniform measure). Any fixed tensor on the distinct labels of the list is a commodity with one global index, and both realizations price it within the tolerance of its assigned price; so the two realizations differ by at most twice the tolerance on that tensor once the format bounds include it. The paper concludes by a compactness argument that the distance between the two expressions in the chart at $i$ and in the chart at $u^{-1}i$ differ by a quantity that tends to zero uniformly: a violating sequence would have a subsequence with a fixed pattern of coincidences among the labels; the word lengths bound the diameters, hence the scales (spanning-tree bound); limiting prices on the list exist in both charts and agree on every tensor; and by Convention 3 of §16.2 the limiting distances between the expressions agree. On exceptional pairs the distances are bounded (word lengths in a passing chart, zero in a collapsed chart), so they contribute a vanishing amount to the mean square. Reindexing the average by $i\mapsto u^{-1}i$ gives $d(u\mathsf E_1,u\mathsf E_2)=d(\mathsf E_1,\mathsf E_2)$ in the limit for all pairs of expressions. The map therefore is well defined and isometric on a dense subset, extends to $Y$, has the inverse coming from $u^{-1}$, and satisfies the group law.

The contradiction. Let $x=\mathbf m_{\mathrm{id}}$, the limit of the vectors of centers. For $g\in K_V$, the hop $g^{-1}$ is eventually a core generator; in a passing chart the mark $g^{-1}i$ is at distance at most $1$ plus the tolerance from $i$, and in a failing chart at distance $0$; so $d(x,gx)\le1$. The mark of $\pi^{-1}$ in the chart at $i$ is the site $\pi(i)$. In a passing chart its distance to the center is the spectral price of the payload wire $i\to\pi(i)$, which agrees with the assigned price $x_{i,\pi(i)}$ up to the tolerance; in a failing chart the distance is $0$ and the assigned price is at most $a_{\mathrm p}$, and such charts have vanishing measure. So $d(x,\pi^{-1}x)$ is the ultralimit of the root mean square of the assigned payload prices, which is at least their arithmetic mean, which exceeds $C_0+1$. Since $d(x,\pi x)=d(\pi^{-1}x,x)$, this contradicts $d(x,\pi x)\le C_0$ from Lemma 17.2. ∎

Not verified — the uniform comparison of two charts (p. 66, "Translation of the marks"). not verified The walk above reproduces the paper's argument at the paper's level of detail. Two points were not reconstructed in full. (1) The step from "the two realizations agree within twice the tolerance on every fixed tensor" to "the distances between interpolated points agree up to $o(1)$" rests on Lemma 5.2 and on the continuity statement of §16.2, applied to limits of price data in which the list of sites may have coincidences; the paper handles this with the phrase "a fixed pattern of coincident labels" and no further detail. (2) Whether the realization "attaining the test" can be chosen so that the marks depend measurably or consistently on the center is not discussed; the argument only uses averages over centers, so no choice issue is visible, but this was not checked against [42].

18. Bounded-width permutation routing pp. 67–73

Role in the chain. This section receives Lemma 17.3 (§17), the strict spectral comparison of §3, and two constructions of the companion paper [42]. It delivers Proposition 18.1 (a permutation tensor is an exact sum of networks that move a total width of at most $C\,nw$ through hops on short address sets, with $C$ absolute) and then, in 18.2, the proof of Theorem 16.1. Theorem 16.1 is used by Lemma 16.2 (§16.1) and in Part IV (§26, §27).

18.1 From a failed price test to a routing identity

Fix $\ell$, the core generators and the test parameters of Lemma 17.3, and write $M=|\mathcal I|$, so $n\le M\lt2n$. Sending one bit along the designated path from $i$ to $\pi(i)$ uses up to $a_{\mathrm p}$ core links, and $a_{\mathrm p}$ grows with $\ell$. The proposition replaces this by a sum of networks in which the mean number of link uses per payload bit is bounded by an absolute constant.

Proposition 18.1. p. 67 paper Suppose $\omega_k\lt9/4$ and fix $H\ge1$. For all sufficiently large $h$, every valid set of tags and every $h\le w\le h^H$, the associated permutation tensor for $w$-bit payloads is an exact sum over $k$ of networks with the following properties.
  1. Each label has a bounded number of fixed slots per core generator. A used slot carries $O(w)$ bits to a neighbor along the generator or its inverse. Both ends belong to the same prescribed group of at most $2^{th+O(1)}$ labels. The sum of the carried widths in any summand is at most $Cnw$, where $C$ is independent of $\ell$ and $t$.
  2. At each label a fixed trivalent network of size $O(w(1+\log w)^{O(1)})$ implements the local maps. Its configuration consists of a bounded list of menu indices, widths and offsets.
  3. A summand is specified by $O(n\log(2w))$ bits. Given these bits and the tags, a deterministic controller computes the local configurations and matchings in $nh^{O(1)}$ word operations. The exponents and slot capacities are independent of $H$. The running time may have a multiplicative constant depending on $H$.

Here $t=(m+2)/(m+\ell)$ is the number of (17.1), and the "permutation tensor" for valid tags is the tensor of the $M$ bundles of $w$ parallel one-bit wires from $i$ to $\pi(i)$. The proof gives $C=2(C_0+4)$, which is $208$ for $C_0=100$.

Imported — the local replacement argument [42, Section 9]. imported The paper writes: "We use the local replacement argument of [42, Section 9]." What follows in the paper's proof is a description of that argument in the present setting; the paper does not state which statements of [42, Section 9] are quoted verbatim and which are re-proved.
Imported — strict spectral comparison [42, Lemma 2.2] (from §3). imported If two nonzero tensors on the same sites satisfy $\varphi(T)\lt\varphi(U)$ for every spectral point $\varphi$, there is a fixed integer $\mathsf C$ such that, for every $j\ge1$, $T^{\otimes j}$ is a restriction of the direct sum of $\mathsf C$ copies of $U^{\otimes j}$.
AHA. The proof never constructs a good routing directly. It keeps a table of prices for all outstanding requests. If the prices are locally consistent with a spectral price almost everywhere, Lemma 17.3 says that payload wires are already cheap on average. If they are inconsistent on some ball, the inconsistency means that no spectral point prices the demands on that ball as high as the table does; strict spectral comparison turns that statement into an exact identity that expresses the demands through cheaper supplies. Either the account is small or it can be lowered.

Vocabulary. A request for a commodity leaves that tensor to be supplied at specified legs. Its cap $a_i$ bounds the cost of supplying it directly: prepare the tensor at one site and send its legs along designated core paths, in binary encoding with unused coordinates padded by zero. By multilinearity of contraction, a tensor identity can be substituted for several requests at any stage.

Step 1: the identity on a ball. Let $x\in\prod_i[0,a_i]$ be a price array with positive discrepancy on a ball that is not a single site. Request one copy of each commodity of the ball with $x_i\gt0$; let $D$ be the tensor product of these demands and $L=\sum_{i:x_i\gt0}x_i$ its assigned price. ($L\gt0$, because the zero array passes the test with the collapsed price.) The available supplies $T_j$ are all commodities of the ball, at their assigned prices, and the one-bit core links of the ball, at price $1$. The paper claims: there are numbers $l_j$ strictly above the supply prices and a number $0\lt L_-\lt L$ such that every normalized spectral log price $\mathsf P$ (that is, $\mathsf P=\log_2\varphi$ for a spectral point $\varphi$) satisfies

$$\mathsf P(D)\ \lt\ L_-\,\max_j\frac{\mathsf P(T_j)}{l_j}.$$
Gap fill — why positive discrepancy gives this strict inequality. filled here The maximum ratio is bounded below. A spanning tree of one-bit core links restricts to the binary diagonal $\Delta$ on all sites of the ball (§16.2), and $\mathsf P(\Delta)=\log_22=1$ because $\Delta$ is a direct sum of two units. So $1\le\sum_{\text{tree}}\mathsf P(\text{link})\le\bigl(\max_j\mathsf P(T_j)/l_j\bigr)\sum_{\text{tree}}l_j$.

Contradiction if the inequality with $L$ fails. Suppose that for a sequence of arrays $l^{(\nu)}$ decreasing to the supply prices there are $\mathsf P_\nu$ with $\mathsf P_\nu(D)\ge L\max_j\mathsf P_\nu(T_j)/l^{(\nu)}_j$. Divide $\mathsf P_\nu$ by its maximum ratio. The result $\mathsf P_\nu'$ is a price whose scale is the reciprocal of that ratio, hence at most $\sum_{\text{tree}}l_j^{(\nu)}$, and it satisfies $\mathsf P_\nu'(T_j)\le l_j^{(\nu)}$ for all $j$ and $\mathsf P_\nu'(D)\ge L$. By compactness (§16.2) a subsequence converges to a price $\mathsf P^*$ with $\mathsf P^*(T_j)\le x_j$ for the commodities, $\mathsf P^*(\text{link})\le1$, and $\mathsf P^*(D)\ge L$. By additivity, $\mathsf P^*(D)=\sum_{i:x_i\gt0}\mathsf P^*(T_i)\le\sum_{i:x_i\gt0}x_i=L$. So equality holds for every commodity with $x_i\gt0$, and commodities with $x_j=0$ have $0\le\mathsf P^*(T_j)\le0$. The price $\mathsf P^*$ then matches every assigned price exactly and gives every core link length at most $1$: the discrepancy of the ball is zero, a contradiction. Hence for some fixed $l_j$ strictly above the supply prices, $\mathsf P(D)\lt L\max_j\mathsf P(T_j)/l_j$ for all $\mathsf P$. Applying the same compactness to the normalized prices $\mathsf P'$ (the set $\{\mathsf P':\mathsf P'(T_j)\le l_j\}$ is compact and $\mathsf P'(D)\lt L$ on it) gives $\sup\mathsf P'(D)\lt L$, and $L_-$ can be chosen strictly between this supremum and $L$.

Choose an integer $b_0$ and form the direct sum

$$U=\bigoplus_jT_j^{\otimes\lfloor b_0L_-/l_j\rfloor}$$

(this $U$ is a tensor; it is unrelated to the vector space $U=\mathbb F_q^r$ of §17.2).

Gap fill — $b_0$ large makes $U$ dominate $D^{\otimes b_0}$ at every spectral point. filled here Let $\epsilon\gt0$ be the margin with $\mathsf P(D)\le(L_--\epsilon)\,R$ for all $\mathsf P$, where $R=\max_j\mathsf P(T_j)/l_j\gt0$ is attained at $j^*$. A spectral point is additive on direct sums and multiplicative on products, so $\log_2\varphi(U)\ge\mathsf P(T_{j^*})\lfloor b_0L_-/l_{j^*}\rfloor\ge R\,(b_0L_--l_{j^*})$, while $\log_2\varphi(D^{\otimes b_0})=b_0\mathsf P(D)\le b_0(L_--\epsilon)R$. The first exceeds the second as soon as $b_0\epsilon\gt\max_jl_j$.

Strict spectral comparison now gives, for every $r\ge1$, an exact restriction of $D^{\otimes b_0r}$ from at most $K(b_0)$ direct copies of $U^{\otimes r}$. Here $K(b_0)$ is the integer $\mathsf C$ of the imported statement for the pair $T=D^{\otimes b_0}$, $U$; it depends on $b_0$ and not on $r$. Expand $U^{\otimes r}$ into its blocks, one for each ordered word $(j_1,\dots,j_r)$ of supply choices, and apply the restricting maps blockwise. A restriction of a direct sum is the ordinary sum of the restrictions of its blocks, so this yields an identity

$$D^{\otimes b}=\sum_{\text{copy},\ \text{word}}\ (\text{local maps})\Bigl(\bigotimes_{a=1}^rT_{j_a}^{\otimes\lfloor b_0L_-/l_{j_a}\rfloor}\Bigr),\qquad b=b_0r .$$

Each term supplies $b$ copies of every demand from supplies of total price at most $\sum_al_{j_a}\lfloor b_0L_-/l_{j_a}\rfloor\le bL_-$. With $s$ supply types, the index of a term (a copy and a word) takes at most $\lceil\log_2(K(b_0)s^r)\rceil$ bits. The ratio of this length to $b$ is at most $(\log_2K(b_0)+1)/(b_0r)+(\log_2s)/b_0$, which is made arbitrarily small by first increasing $b_0$ and then $r$.

Step 2: repeating the identity, and histograms. Use the identity $J$ times in parallel on $Jb$ copies of each demand. The expanded sum runs over ordered $J$-tuples of term indices. The paper makes only the histogram of the tuple (the number of occurrences of each index value) part of the outer sum; since the alphabet is fixed, a histogram is described by $O(\log(2J))$ bits. The sum over the ordered tuples with a given histogram is carried out inside one network: a root sums over the ordered words, sends each word along a spanning tree of core links of the ball, and enforces the histogram. The histogram determines how many copies of each supply are used; the word assigns them to the individual applications of the restricting maps. Each ordered word occurs once with its original coefficient in $k$. The index rate is chosen so low that transmitting the word costs at most $Jb(L-L_-)/2$. One application therefore retires demands of assigned price $JbL$ and introduces supplies and control of price at most $Jb(L_-+(L-L_-)/2)$: a saving of at least $Jb(L-L_-)/2$.

Step 3: a finite menu. The demands stay positively priced and the saving stays strict in a neighborhood of the array $x$. There are finitely many types of balls, because the field, the radius, the degrees and the formats are fixed. The set of price arrays on a ball type with discrepancy at least $\rho/2$ is compact, so finitely many replacements (a menu) cover it, with a uniform positive lower bound on the saving per unit of demand and on the prices of the selected demands. The menu comes from a compactness argument and is not given explicitly.

Step 4: an account that bounds the total width. Follow one summand of the expansion. Let $w\,z_i$ be its integer stock of outstanding requests for commodity $i$, and let $S$ be its installed core width divided by $Mw$. The summand is given the account $S+F(z)$, where

$$F(z)=\frac1M\max_{0\le x\le a}\sum_i\Bigl(z_ix_i-\tfrac12\mu x_i^2\Bigr),$$

the maximum is over arrays $x$ with $0\le x_i\le a_i$, and $\mu\gt0$ is a rational number with $\mu\sum_ia_i^2/(2M)\le1$ (possible because there are $O(M)$ commodities with uniformly bounded caps). The maximizing array $x(z)$ serves as the price array of the summand.

Gap fill — the four properties of $F$ that the proof uses. filled here $F(z)=\frac1M\sum_i\phi_i(z_i)$ with $\phi_i(\zeta)=\max_{0\le\xi\le a_i}(\zeta\xi-\tfrac12\mu\xi^2)$. For $\zeta\ge0$ the maximizer is $\xi=\min(a_i,\zeta/\mu)$, so $$\phi_i(\zeta)=\begin{cases}\zeta^2/(2\mu),&\zeta\le\mu a_i,\\ a_i\zeta-\tfrac12\mu a_i^2,&\zeta\ge\mu a_i,\end{cases}$$ $$\phi_i'(\zeta)=\min(a_i,\zeta/\mu)=x_i .$$ (a) Stock supports price: $z_i\ge\mu x_i$. (b) Smoothness: $\phi_i'$ is Lipschitz with constant $1/\mu$, so for $z+v\ge0$, $$F(z+v)-F(z)\le\frac1M\sum_ix_iv_i+\frac1{2\mu M}\sum_iv_i^2 .$$ (c) Lower bound: taking $x=a$ in the maximum, $F(z)\ge\frac1M\sum_ia_iz_i-\frac{\mu}{2M}\sum_ia_i^2\ge\frac1M\sum_ia_iz_i-1$. (d) Excess stock: for an integer $B\gt\max_i\mu a_i$ and $z_i\ge B$, $\phi_i$ is linear with slope $a_i$. Supplying $w\,v$ excess requests of commodity $i$ directly lowers $F$ by exactly $a_iv/M$ and raises $S$ by at most $a_iv/M$ (the cap $a_i$ bounds the installed width per request). So the account does not increase when every stock above $wB$ is supplied directly.

A round. Suppose the mean maximizing price of the $M$ payload commodities exceeds $C_0+2$. By Lemma 17.3 more than $\rho M$ balls have discrepancy greater than $\rho$. A ball meets a bounded number of other balls, so a greedy choice selects $\Omega(M)$ pairwise disjoint such balls. On each selected ball apply its menu replacement with $J=\lfloor\alpha w/b\rfloor$ for a small fixed rational $\alpha\gt0$; property (a) and the lower bound on selected demand prices guarantee that the stock is sufficient. New core links are charged to $S$, and the other supplies become new requests. In the linear term of (b) each ball contributes at most $-Jb(L-L_-)/(2Mw)$, the saving of Step 2 in normalized units; over $\Omega(M)$ balls and for large $w$ this is at most $-c\alpha$ for some $c\gt0$. All changes satisfy $|v_i|=O(\alpha)$ on $O(M)$ coordinates, so the quadratic term of (b) is $O(\alpha^2)$. For $\alpha$ small, each round lowers the account by a fixed positive amount in every resulting summand. After the round, stocks above the cap are supplied directly (property (d)).

Introducing the payloads. Start with no requests. Introduce the $w$ copies of each payload in equal increments on a fixed fine mesh (rounded to integers with exact total $w$). Before each increment, run rounds until the mean payload price is at most $C_0+2$.

Gap fill — the arithmetic that gives $C=2(C_0+4)$. filled here An increment raises $z_i$ by $\varepsilon$ for the $M$ payload commodities, with $1/\varepsilon$ increments in total. By (b) it raises the account by at most $\frac1M\sum_{\text{payloads}}x_i\varepsilon+\frac{1}{2\mu M}M\varepsilon^2\le(C_0+2)\varepsilon+\varepsilon^2/(2\mu)$. Over all increments this is at most $C_0+2+\varepsilon/(2\mu)\le C_0+3$ for a fine mesh. The account starts at $0$, is never negative, increases by at most $C_0+3$ in total, and decreases by a fixed positive amount in each round. So each summand undergoes a bounded number of rounds, and its final account is at most $C_0+3$. Supplying all remaining requests directly installs width at most $\sum_ia_i\,wz_i$, which in normalized units is $\frac1M\sum_ia_iz_i\le F(z)+1$ by (c). The total installed width is therefore at most $(S+F(z)+1)\,Mw\le(C_0+4)Mw\le2(C_0+4)\,nw$.

The constant $2(C_0+4)$ does not depend on $\ell$: the caps $a_i$, the menu, $\mu$, $\alpha$, $\rho$ and the number of rounds all depend on $\ell$, but they enter only through the number of slots and the size of the control, not through the bound on the total width.

Step 5: realizing the summands. The paper then argues the three listed properties. (1) A label lies in at most one selected ball per round; a direct supply that uses an edge originates within bounded distance of it; bounded degree, boundedly many commodity types per neighborhood and the stock cap $wB$ give a bounded number of slots of width $O(w)$ per generator, and the generator (an element of some $K_V$) prescribes the group of at most $2^{th+O(1)}$ labels that contains both ends. (2) At the sites of a commodity the requests are kept in a common order, retired from the front and appended at the back; the histogram fixes the list lengths; prefix counts of the ordered word assign supply positions; comparison sorting on addresses of $O(1+\log w)$ bits realizes the resulting permutations and partial matchings with $O(w(1+\log w)^{O(1)})$ trivalent gates at a site; each block map has fixed size over $k$. (3) The controller enumerates the bounded neighborhoods, computes prices from stocks, uses finite tables (a rational grid of price arrays with a valid menu entry for each cell; the discrepancy is $1$-Lipschitz in the array) to select the balls, and assigns slots. There are $O(n)$ histograms over the bounded number of rounds, each encoded uniquely in $O(\log(2w))$ bits, which gives $O(n\log(2w))$ selector bits; the work is $nh^{O(1)}$ word operations. ∎

Not verified — parts of the proof of Proposition 18.1 (pp. 67–70). not verified The following were reproduced from the paper without an independent derivation. (1) The correspondence between "price" and installed width for supplies other than core links: the account treats a new request for a commodity at its current price $x_j$, and the argument that this is consistent across overlapping balls is part of the imported local replacement argument [42, Section 9]. (2) The realization inside one network of the sum over ordered words with a prescribed histogram, at transmission cost at most $Jb(L-L_-)/2$ (p. 68): the regrouping of the sum is an identity, but the network that enforces a histogram and its cost were not reconstructed. (3) Step 5 as a whole (p. 69): the slot count, the size $O(w(1+\log w)^{O(1)})$ of the local networks and the controller's running time are stated in the paper in one paragraph each. (4) The role of the threshold $C_0+2$ where Lemma 17.3 has $C_0+1$: the paper mentions "a fixed slack in the test" for the grid of price arrays; the exact use of the extra unit was not traced. (5) The statement requires $w\le h^H$ and "sufficiently large $w$" is used for integer rounding; how the constants depend on $H$ is given only as in item 3 of the proposition.

18.2 From local transports to stable sorting

Write $S(h,w)$ for the least size of a trivalent network over $k$ for $\operatorname{Tag}(h,w)$, with $n=2^h$. (This $S$ replaces the normalized width $S$ of 18.1.) Proposition 18.1 bounds the transport in each summand, but the summands use different slots, and one fixed network must serve all of them. This subsection derives a recurrence for $S$ and solves it. Several letters are reused by the paper with new meanings in this subsection; each is defined where it appears.

Sharing a fixed pool of calls

Fix $\ell$, the factor $t=(m+2)/(m+\ell)$ and the protocol of Proposition 18.1. In each term (summand) the protocol has at most $Ln$ links of total width at most $Cnw$; $C$ is independent of $\ell$ and $L$ is not. Choose $0\lt Y\le1$ with $L\le C/Y$ and split each link into chunks of $w'=\lceil Yw\rceil$ bits, with the padding of a last chunk pinned to zero at both ends.

Gap fill — the chunk count. filled here A link of width $\beta$ gives $\lceil\beta/w'\rceil\le\beta/w'+1$ nonempty chunks. Summing over at most $Ln$ links of total width at most $Cnw$, and using $w'\ge Yw$ and $L\le C/Y$, the number of chunks in any term is at most $Cnw/w'+Ln\le Cn/Y+Cn/Y=m_*n$ with $m_*=2C/Y$.

A chunk is a transfer of $w'$ bits between two labels of one group of at most $2^{th+O(1)}$ labels. If the set of active chunks were fixed, the chunks of a group could be executed by one tagged permutation on that group. The set is not fixed; it depends on the term. The fixed network therefore contains a pool of modules and a mechanism that assigns active chunks to module positions.

Imported — assignment graphs [42, Lemma 10.2]. imported The paper states: such a graph connects at most $\mathsf cK$ candidates to $K$ slots. Every active subset of at most $K/2$ candidates can be assigned injectively to adjacent slots. The degrees are bounded in terms of $\mathsf c$, and the assignment takes $O_{\mathsf c}(K)$ deterministic word operations with the graph as fixed advice. (Here $K$ is a number of slots, unrelated to the field $K$ of Lemma 16.2 and to the groups $K_V$.)

The paper's construction, in order:

  1. Lists. There are $O(n)$ candidate slots, grouped by the short-address sets within which they communicate. Choose a dyadic capacity $U=2^{th+O(1)}$ at least twice the largest group (a new meaning of $U$). Pack whole groups greedily into lists of capacity $U$; every closed list is at least half full, so there are $O(n/U+1)$ lists.
  2. Batches. Fix a small dyadic fraction $K/U$. In each list divide the active chunks, in order, into batches of $K/2$, the last one possibly shorter, and reserve $K$ slots for each nonempty batch. Full batches reserve two slots per chunk, and each list has at most one short batch, so the total reservation is at most $2m_*n+O((n/U+1)K)\le3m_*n$; the last inequality holds for large $h$ because $U=o(n)$ and $K/U$ is chosen small after the packing constants are fixed.
  3. First assignment. For each list install enough candidate batches for all its chunks. One assignment graph places the source ends of a batch in $K$ slots and another places the target ends.
  4. Second assignment. Whole batches are assigned to a shared pool of $\lceil6m_*n/K\rceil$ physical modules, each with $K$ input and $K$ output positions. At most $3m_*n/K$ batches are active, which is at most half the pool, and the number of candidate batches is a bounded multiple of the pool size; so the assignment-graph property applies again, and the two lists of an active batch move together to its module. Counting idle modules, at most $\lceil6m_*n/K\rceil K\le7m_*n$ positions are installed for large $h$.
  5. Modules. Every module is one copy of $\operatorname{Tag}(h',w')$ with $h'=\log_2K=th+O(1)$. Unmatched positions are filled in order, unused bits are pinned to zero, and an idle module receives identity tags and zero payloads. All assignments are deterministic functions of the term and its tags. Because the assignment graphs have bounded degree, the switches cost $O(nw)$ in total.

Control. The remaining control data are tags, selectors and slot descriptions. The controller's computation is verified inside the network by guessing a time-stamped execution, checking each instruction, and sorting all memory transactions by address, time and access order with a comparison network, so that every read can be checked against the last write. Fixed initial memory and padding make the accepting execution unique for each legal selector, which keeps the sum exact. A comparison network also checks that the original tags are distinct. For fixed $H$ and $h\le w\le h^H$ the size of everything outside the shorter calls is

$$D_{\mathrm{control}}(h,w)=O_H\bigl(nw(\log(2h))^{O(1)}+nh^J\bigr),\qquad J\gt2 .\tag{18.1}$$

The first term pays for the local maps of Proposition 18.1(2); the second for $nh^{O(1)}$ word operations, their bit implementation and the memory check. Here $J$ is an exponent (a new meaning of $J$), and the exponents do not depend on $H$. The paper states these claims without further proof. With $7m_*n/K=7m_*n/2^{h'}$ modules the result is the recurrence

$$S(h,w)\ \le\ D_{\mathrm{control}}(h,w)+\frac{7m_*n}{2^{h'}}\,S(h',w'),\qquad h'=th+O(1),\quad w'=\lceil Yw\rceil .$$

Wide payloads

Background — Batcher's sorting networks. filled here A comparator network is a fixed sequence of compare-and-exchange operations on pairs of positions; the sequence does not depend on the data. A sequence is bitonic if it increases and then decreases, or is a cyclic shift of such a sequence. For a bitonic sequence of even length $\mathsf N$, comparing position $i$ with position $i+\mathsf N/2$ for all $i\lt\mathsf N/2$ produces two bitonic halves such that every element of the first is at most every element of the second (it suffices to check this for $0/1$ sequences, by the zero–one principle). Recursion sorts a bitonic sequence with $(\mathsf N/2)\log_2\mathsf N$ comparators. Sorting two halves recursively, reversing one, and merging gives a count $\mathsf B(\mathsf N)=2\mathsf B(\mathsf N/2)+(\mathsf N/2)\log_2\mathsf N$, hence $\mathsf B(\mathsf N)=\tfrac14\mathsf N\log_2\mathsf N\,(\log_2\mathsf N+1)$ for $\mathsf N$ a power of two (Batcher [8]; check: $\mathsf B(2)=1$, $\mathsf B(4)=6$). With $\mathsf N=n=2^h$ this is $O(nh^2)$ comparators. A comparator on records with $h$-bit keys and $w$-bit payloads is a Boolean circuit of $O(h+w)$ gates.

It suffices to prove part 2 of Theorem 16.1 for $0\lt\zeta\lt1$. The parameters are chosen in this order.

ParameterChoiceDepends on
$\ell$so large that $14C\,t^\zeta\lt1/4$$\zeta$, $C$, $m$
$Y$, $J$from the protocol for this $\ell$ ($L\le C/Y$; exponent in (18.1))$\ell$ (non-effective, through Lemma 17.3)
$\mathsf u$smooth nonincreasing $\mathsf u:[1,\infty)\to[\zeta,2]$, equal to $2$ on $[1,J]$, eventually equal to $\zeta$, with $b=-\mathsf u'$ satisfying $0\le b\lt1$ and $Y^{-b}\lt3/2$ everywhere$\zeta$, $J$, $Y$
$H$in the interior of the interval where $\mathsf u=\zeta$$\mathsf u$

(The paper writes $u$ for the function called $\mathsf u$ here.) The claim is that for $h\ge2$ and $h\le w\le h^H$, with a constant $D$ (a new meaning of $D$),

$$S(h,w)\ \le\ D\,n\,w\,h^{\mathsf u(a)},\qquad a=\log_hw .\tag{18.2}$$

Base range $a\le J$. Batcher's network puts the tagged sources in destination order with $O(nh^2)$ comparators of bit cost $O(h+w)=O(w)$. Comparing the sorted tags with the positions $0,\dots,n-1$ checks that the tags form a permutation, and comparing the payloads with the proposed outputs completes the verifier. The size is $O(nwh^2)=O(nwh^{\mathsf u(a)})$ because $\mathsf u=2$ on $[1,J]$.

Inductive range $a\gt J$. Induct on $h$; for large $h$, $h'\lt h$ and $w'\ge h'$. Write $a'=\log_{h'}w'$. The paper states, uniformly for $J\le a\le H$,

$$\begin{aligned}a'-a&=\frac{\log Y-a\log t+o(1)}{\log h},\\ \frac{w'(h')^{\mathsf u(a')}}{w\,h^{\mathsf u(a)}}&=(1+o(1))\,t^{\mathsf u(a)+a\,b(a)}\,Y^{1-b(a)} .\end{aligned}$$
Gap fill — the two formulas and the closing of the induction. filled here First formula. $w'=Yw(1+o(1))$ and $h'=th(1+o(1))$, so $a'=\dfrac{\log Y+a\log h+o(1)}{\log t+\log h+o(1)}$ and $a'-a=\dfrac{\log Y-a\log t+o(1)}{\log h+\log t+o(1)}$, which is the stated expression because $\log t$ is a constant.

Second formula. $(h')^{\mathsf u(a')}=(1+o(1))\,t^{\mathsf u(a')}h^{\mathsf u(a')}$ and, by Taylor expansion of $\mathsf u$ on the compact interval, $h^{\mathsf u(a')-\mathsf u(a)}=\exp\bigl(\log h\cdot(-b(a))(a'-a)+o(1)\bigr)=(1+o(1))\,Y^{-b(a)}t^{a\,b(a)}$. Multiplying by $w'/w=(1+o(1))Y$ gives the ratio $t^{\mathsf u(a)+ab(a)}Y^{1-b(a)}$.

The recursive share. By the induction hypothesis the recursive term of the recurrence is at most $\dfrac{7m_*n}{2^{h'}}\cdot D\,2^{h'}w'(h')^{\mathsf u(a')}=7m_*\,D\,n\,w'(h')^{\mathsf u(a')}$. With $7m_*=14C/Y$ and the ratio above this is $(1+o(1))\,14C\,t^{\mathsf u(a)+ab(a)}\,Y^{-b(a)}\cdot D\,n\,w\,h^{\mathsf u(a)}$. Since $t\lt1$, $\mathsf u\ge\zeta$ and $ab\ge0$, $t^{\mathsf u+ab}\le t^\zeta$. So the share is at most $(1+o(1))\cdot\tfrac14\cdot\tfrac32\lt\tfrac12$ of the claimed bound for large $h$.

The control share. For $a\ge J$, $nh^J\le nw$, and $(\log(2h))^{O(1)}=o(h^\zeta)$; so (18.1) is $o(nwh^{\mathsf u(a)})$ uniformly. The two shares together are below the claimed bound for $h\ge h_0$; enlarging $D$ covers $h\lt h_0$.

The factor $Y^{-b}$ is the reason for the gradual exponent $\mathsf u$. A call has width $w'\approx Yw$ with $Y\lt1$, so its width exponent $a'$ can be smaller than $a$, and near the threshold where the cheap exponent $\zeta$ starts the call may fall into the range of a larger exponent. A slowly varying $\mathsf u$ makes this loss a bounded factor $Y^{-b}\lt3/2$. The independence of $C$ from $\ell$ is what allows $14Ct^\zeta\lt1/4$: $t\to0$ as $\ell\to\infty$, while $Y$ and $L$ depend on $\ell$ but cancel from the product $7m_*\cdot Y=14C$.

Calls and inputs outside the range. If a call has $w'\gt(h')^H$, the payload is split evenly into $\lceil w'/\lfloor(h')^H\rfloor\rceil$ slices with the same tags; the number of slices is bounded, their width exponents are $H+o(1)$ (where $\mathsf u=\zeta$), and copying the tags costs $O(h')$ per slot and slice, which is within (18.1). For an input with $w\gt h^H$, split it into slices of maximum width $\lfloor h^H\rfloor$ in the same way; tag copying costs at most a constant times the data volume. At bounded heights the Batcher network costs $O_h(w)$. Let $A\gt1$ be a number such that $\mathsf u=\zeta$ on $[A,\infty)$. Then

$$S(h,w)=O_\zeta(2^h\,w\,h^\zeta)\qquad(w\ge h^A).\tag{18.3}$$

This is the bound of Theorem 16.1(2) for $\operatorname{Tag}$, with threshold exponent $A$ (called $a$ in the theorem). The threshold $A$ depends on $J$ and $Y$, hence on the non-effective data of Lemma 17.3.

From tagged permutations to stable sorting

paper For $2^s$ records of $v$ bits, a stable sort uses one $\operatorname{Tag}(s,v)$ and $O(2^s(s+v))$ additional gates: give each proposed output record a guessed position in the input list; use the tagged permutation to check that this assignment sends every output to an equal input; and check along the output list that the pairs (key, guessed input position) are strictly increasing in lexicographic order.

Gap fill — why these checks force the stably sorted output. filled here The tagged permutation check says that the guessed positions $\gamma_1,\dots,\gamma_{2^s}$ are distinct and that output $j$ equals input $\gamma_j$; in particular they have the same key. So $\gamma$ is a bijection from output positions to input positions that preserves keys, and it maps the outputs with key $\kappa$ bijectively onto the inputs with key $\kappa$. The order check says that keys do not decrease along the output and that, among outputs with equal key, $\gamma$ increases. An increasing bijection between two finite ordered sets is unique. So $\gamma$ is determined by the input, also when records are identical, and the output is the stable sort. Exactly one guess is accepted, so the network is exact.

The stable-sort tensor therefore inherits (18.3). This completes part 2 of Theorem 16.1.

Packing ordinary-width records

For records of width $O(h)$ the bound (18.3) does not apply, since it needs width at least $h^A$. The paper applies (18.3) at two scales in each pass of a radix sort, with two choices of $\zeta$ (the letters $a$, $B$, $s$, $P$ below have the meanings of this paragraph only):

  • $\zeta=\tfrac12$, with threshold exponent $a\gt1$;
  • $\zeta=1/(2a)$, with threshold exponent $B\gt1$.

Start with $O(n)$ records whose keys and payloads have $O(h)$ bits. The radix digit has $s=\lfloor h^{1/a}\rfloor$ bits. Run $O(h/s)$ stable passes, least significant digit first, the last digit padded with zeros.

One pass.

  1. Pad the list to blocks of $2^{2s}$ slots.
  2. Local sort. Stably sort each block by (dummy flag, digit), dummies last in their old order.
  3. Packs. Choose a power of two $P\in[h^{B-1},2h^{B-1})$. Extend each run of one real digit to a multiple of $P$ by dummies at its end. Then each pack of $P$ consecutive slots contains records of at most one real digit. A fixed allowance of $O((2^s+1)P)$ extra slots per block covers the padding of all runs (at most $2^s$ digit values, less than $P$ dummies each), keeps the old dummies and rounds the length to a multiple of $P$. Scans compute the run lengths and the destinations of all old records; a second local tagged permutation checks these destinations; new dummies are appended with guessed destinations that are required to increase strictly, so that exactly one filling is valid.
  4. Global sort. Scan each pack for its digit and stably sort the packs by digit, empty packs last. Records with equal digits keep their order inside a block and across blocks.
Input block in orderPacks of digit $0$Packs of digit $1$
$a_1\ b_0\ c_1\ d_1$$[b,\cdot]$$[a,c]\ [d,\cdot]$
$e_0\ f_1\ g_0\ h_0$$[e,g]\ [h,\cdot]$$[f,\cdot]$

The table reproduces the paper's Figure 6 (p. 72): one radix pass with two records per pack. A subscript is the digit being sorted and a dot is a dummy slot. Stable sorting of whole packs collects the middle column and then the right column, each from top to bottom; reading the real records gives $b,e,g,h,a,c,d,f$.

Gap fill — the cost of one pass and the exponent $p=2-1/(2a)$. filled here Local calls. A block has address length $2s+O(1)$. Its records are padded to width $\mathsf c\,h$ with a constant $\mathsf c\ge2^{a}+1$, which is at least $(2s+O(1))^a$ for large $h$ because $s^a\le h$. So the first bound applies with $\zeta=\tfrac12$: the cost per slot is $O(\mathsf ch\cdot(2s)^{1/2})=O(hs^{1/2})$, and over $O(n)$ slots it is $O(nhs^{1/2})\le O(nh^{1+1/(2a)})$.

Global call. There are $O(n/P)$ packs of $O(hP)$ bits, with address length $h+O(\log h)$ (the paper's figure). Since $hP\ge h^B$, constant-factor padding reaches the second threshold, and the bound with $\zeta=1/(2a)$ gives $O\bigl((n/P)\cdot hP\cdot h^{1/(2a)}\bigr)=O(nh^{1+1/(2a)})$.

All passes. There are $O(h/s)=O(h^{1-1/a})$ passes, so the total is $$O\bigl((h/s)\,n\,h^{1+1/(2a)}\bigr)=O\bigl(n\,h^{\,2-1/a+1/(2a)}\bigr)=O\bigl(n\,h^{\,2-1/(2a)}\bigr).$$ The two choices of $\zeta$ balance the local and the global cost: the local cost per record bit is $s^{1/2}=h^{1/(2a)}$, and the global exponent is chosen equal to it.

The list stays of size $O(n)$. One pass multiplies the length by $1+O(2^{-s}P)$ (allowance $(2^s+1)P$ per block of $2^{2s}$ slots) and adds $O(2^{2s}+2^sP)$ for the last block. Over $O(h/s)$ passes the product of the factors is $\exp\bigl(O(h\,2^{-s}P/s)\bigr)$, which is bounded because $P$ is polynomial in $h$ and $2^{-s}=2^{-\lfloor h^{1/a}\rfloor}$ decays faster than any power of $h$. The accumulated additive term is $2^{O(s)}h^{O(1)}=2^{o(h)}=o(2^h)$.

An observation that the paper does not state. Since $a\gt1$, the exponent satisfies $\tfrac32\lt p\lt2$. The paper asserts only $1\lt p\lt2$.

From the radix sort to $\operatorname{Tag}(h,h)$. paper Take the union of the $n$ source records and the $n$ proposed target records. Give each source its claimed destination and each target its prescribed position, and radix-sort the union by destination, sources before targets at equal destination. A scan over the real records, with a register of $O(h)$ bits, requires for each destination in order exactly one source followed by one target with equal payloads. This rejects repeated or missing tags and verifies the payloads. Thus $\operatorname{Tag}(h,h)$ has an exact trivalent network of size $O(nh^p)$ with

$$p=2-\frac1{2a},\qquad1\lt p\lt2 .$$

For width $ch$ the payload is split across a bounded number of tagged calls with the same tags. The reduction from stable sorting to tagged permutations then gives the ordinary-width statement for stable sorting. This completes the proof of Theorem 16.1 (p. 73). ∎

Not verified — items in 18.2 that depend on [42] or are stated without proof (pp. 70–71). not verified (1) The assignment graphs of [42, Lemma 10.2] are used as stated; their existence, their degree bound and the linear-time assignment were not checked. (2) The claim that the second assignment can move "the two lists of each active batch together" at total switch cost $O(nw)$ was not reconstructed beyond the counting given above. (3) The control bound (18.1), including the memory-checking construction and the statement that its exponents are independent of $H$, is taken from the paper. (4) The constants are non-effective: $Y$, $J$, the thresholds $A$, $a$, $B$ and therefore $p$ depend on the test parameters and on $\rho$ from Lemma 17.3, which come from an argument by contradiction with ultralimits, and on the finite menu of 18.1, which comes from compactness. The paper gives no numerical value for any of them.
