Part III — Concentrating arity on spheres Paper §19–22, pp. 73–86.

19. Concentrating the arity of a tensor network pp. 73–76

Role in the chain. Part III of the paper (§19–§22, pp. 73–86) proves one statement, Lemma 19.1 (Local concentration). It receives from §13 the fermionic sphere tensors, Proposition 13.1 (Fusion, p. 48) and Lemma 13.2 (Dimension of a level, p. 49); from §14 Corollary 14.4 (A common comparison tensor, p. 55); and from §3 the transport bound (3.1) and two results that the paper imports from [42]. The hypothesis $\omega_k \lt 9/4$ enters Part III at one place only: display (20.4) in §20.2, through Corollary 14.4. Lemma 19.2, Lemma 20.1 and the identities of §21 are algebra that holds without the hypothesis. Lemma 19.1 is applied in §25.1 (p. 96), once in every round, with $\ell=10$ and $W=C_1L$, inside the proof of Lemma 23.1 (Compression of a tensor power, p. 86); §27 applies that lemma repeatedly to prove Theorem 2.1 (Compression of tensor powers, p. 7). Section 19 itself states Lemma 19.1, fixes the multi-species sphere forms, proves the dimension bound (19.1), and proves Lemma 19.2, which §21.2 uses to carry an insertion from one cell to the next.

The paper states the aim on p. 73: gather the large tables of a tensor network at a few stations, without changing the tensor that the network represents, and keep the sum of squared arities bounded while doing so. The four sections of Part III are one proof. This section gives the statement, the plan, and the algebraic tools.

Definition (station, arity, cell, host, immobile). p. 73 paper A station is a tensor given by an arbitrary table on an ordered list of bits: a function $\{0,1\}^t\to K$, where $K$ is the coefficient field. Its arity $t$ counts its external bits and its ends of internal wires. The stations are grouped into cells. The cells are the vertices of a host graph of maximum degree $g$. Every wire runs inside one cell, or between two adjacent cells, or to the exterior. One host may carry several networks at once; a cell used by two networks counts twice. Lemma 19.1 counts occupied cells; by the previous sentence, a cell is counted once for each network that uses it. A station designated immobile keeps its external bits and its cell throughout the construction. Every external bit must belong to an immobile station.
Background — arity, contraction, and why merging stations costs arity. filled here A table network has stations joined by binary wires. For a fixed setting of the external bits, its value is the sum, over all settings of the internal wire bits, of the product of the table entries. This sum is the contraction of the network. Two stations of arities $t_1,t_2$ joined by $w$ wires can be replaced by one station whose table is the product of the two tables summed over the $w$ shared bits; the new arity is $t_1+t_2-2w$. The contraction is unchanged. A table of arity $t$ has $2^t$ entries, so the cost of using a station grows with its arity. Lemma 16.2 (Batched table evaluation, p. 59) charges a station of arity $t_v$ about $(1+t_v)^p$ per copy with an exponent $1\lt p\lt 2$ (see §16). Merging stations in this way adds their arities, up to the shared wires. The construction of Part III merges stations in a different way. Each station is attached to the rest of the network through one leg of a sphere form, and when two stations with legs of widths $w_1,w_2$ are merged, the leg of the merged station has width about $\sqrt{w_1^2+w_2^2}$ and not $w_1+w_2$. The price is that the one network is replaced by a sum of many networks.
Gap fill — the budget arithmetic of the paper's first paragraph. filled here The paper's example (p. 73) has $W/T$ stations of arity $T$. Three computations give its three numbers.
  1. The sum of squared arities is $(W/T)\cdot T^2=WT$.
  2. If $D$ stations at a time are merged with arities added, there are $W/(DT)$ stations of arity $DT$, and the sum of squares is $\frac{W}{DT}(DT)^2=WDT$. The budget is multiplied by $D$.
  3. If instead there are $N=O(W/(DT))$ stations whose squared arities still sum to $O(WT)$, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality gives $$\begin{aligned}\sum_{i=1}^N t_i&\le\Bigl(N\sum_i t_i^2\Bigr)^{1/2}\\&=\Bigl(O\bigl(\tfrac{W}{DT}\bigr)\cdot O(WT)\Bigr)^{1/2}=O\bigl(W/\sqrt D\bigr).\end{aligned}$$
The total arity at the large stations falls from $W$ to $O(W/\sqrt D)$. Lemma 19.1 asserts that case 3 can be reached.
Lemma 19.1 (Local concentration). p. 73 paper Fix a finite field $k$ with $\omega_k\lt 9/4$, a degree bound $g$, and an integer $\ell\ge 1$. For each sufficiently large fixed integer $D$, the following holds for all sufficiently large integers $T$. Use an extension $K/k$ containing a fixed finite extension, with $|K|$ at least a sufficiently large polynomial in $T$ and $[K:k]=O_D(\log T)$. The input tables may already be over $K$.

Suppose there are at most $W/(DT)$ occupied cells and at most $W/(DT)$ immobiles in total. In any one network each cell has at most $100D$ stations of total arity at most $100DT$, and over all stations of all networks $\sum_i t_i^2\le WT$. Then the tensor of each network is a sum over $K$ of networks with the same exposed bits. Every choice of one term per network, taken together, satisfies the following bounds.

  1. There are $O(W/(DT))$ frozen stations, including the holders of the original external bits in their original cells. Their maximum arity is $O(DT)$, their squared arities sum to $O(WT)$, and their arities sum to $O(W/\sqrt D)$.
  2. All other stations, called the core, have arity at most $D^{-\ell}T$ and total arity $O\bigl(W(1+\log D)^2\bigr)$.
  3. A common host replaces each original cell by $D^{O(1)}$ positions, with possible contacts only within and between neighboring cells. The induced host on the core positions has absolute bounded degree. No two frozen stations touch by a wire. For some $c=c(g,\ell,k)$ all active internal bundles can have width at least $\lceil T/D^{c}\rceil$, and the bounds in the first two conclusions continue to hold when every station is charged at least that arity.
  4. The outer summation uses $O_D(\log(T+1))$ control bits per occupied cell. For its computational bound, represent each input table by a label and its ports by ordered bundles, one per station pair, specified by lengths and endpoint slots. Bits agree in order along a bundle. Any local reordering is part of the supplied table label. Given a fixed coloring with $O_D(1)$ colors separating cells within a bounded distance depending on $D,g$, the validity of the indices, output layout, and tracking of exposed sublists can be determined in $O_D(1)$ rounds of communication on the old host. With input labels of at most $A$ bits, the work and communication per occupied cell are $O_D\bigl(A+(1+\log T)^{O(1)}\bigr)$ bit operations and bits. Each output table has a label of length $O_D(A+\log(T+1))$ in a dictionary fixed by the input dictionary and the parameters.
The implicit constants in the first three conclusions can depend on $k,g,\ell$, independently of $D,T,W$. A fixed change of the numerical input bound $100$ changes only these constants. (The statement begins on p. 73; the four conclusions are on p. 74.)

Notation in the statement. $\omega_k$ is the exponent of matrix multiplication over $k$: the infimum of the numbers $\tau$ such that two $n\times n$ matrices over $k$ can be multiplied with $O(n^\tau)$ arithmetic operations. A subscript on an $O$ names the parameters on which the implied constant may depend: $O_D(\cdot)$ may depend on $D$ (and on $k,g,\ell$), $O_g(\cdot)$ on $g$, and $O_{g,\ell}(\cdot)$ on $g$, $\ell$ and the characteristic. The paper uses "external bits" and "exposed bits" for the same thing, the bits of a network that are not summed. A label is a bit string that names the table of a station in a dictionary, a list of tables fixed for the network (§23, p. 86); the network description carries labels and not table entries.

How to read the statement. The order of the quantifiers is: first $k,g,\ell$; then $D$, large in terms of $k,g,\ell$; then $T$, large in terms of $D$. The number $W$ is not required to be large; it enters only through the four hypotheses. The constants of conclusions 1–3 do not depend on $D,T,W$. The thresholds are not effective as given: the threshold for $T$ depends on an internal block size $b$, which must exceed a bound $b_0$ that comes from a limit without a stated rate (Corollary 14.4), and the $O_D$ constants of conclusion 4 depend on $b$ and on constants $c_\tau$ imported from [42] (see §20.2). In the terms of Table 1 of §23 (p. 87), $T$ is the working arity scale and $D$ is the concentration parameter. The hypotheses say that the input looks like the example above after a coarse grouping: at most $W/(DT)$ cells, each holding at most $100D$ stations of total arity at most $100DT$, with squared arities summing to at most $WT$.

The conclusion does not bound the total arity below its input value. The hypotheses give at most $100W/T$ stations, so by Cauchy–Schwarz the input total is at most $\sqrt{(100W/T)\cdot WT}=10W$. The bound for the core alone, $O(W(1+\log D)^2)$, is larger than this. What the lemma delivers is a separation into two kinds of station. The frozen stations are few, $O(W/(DT))$, and carry total arity $O(W/\sqrt D)$. The core stations are small, each of arity at most $D^{-\ell}T$. Part IV then shortens the wires among the core stations (§24) before the next application of the lemma.

Two notations in the statement need a remark. A bundle is the ordered list of all parallel wires between one pair of stations. "A sum over $K$ of networks" means that the tensor $V$ of an input network is written as $V=\sum_{\beta}V_\beta$, where $\beta$ runs over the values of a string of control bits, and each $V_\beta$ is the tensor of a table network over $K$ on the same exposed bits (coefficients in $K$ are stored in the tables). The bounds 1–3 hold for every $\beta$. Conclusion 4 says that $\beta$ is short and that the shape of the network $V_\beta$ can be computed from $\beta$ by local work.

AHA. The construction represents a station of arity $t$ by a vector space $V_e$ of dimension about $2^{t}$, graded by an energy $e$ with $t\approx\text{const}\cdot\sqrt e$ (display (19.1) below). When two such objects collide, the Laurent coefficients of negative order pass through a space $V_{e'}$ with $e'\lt e_1+e_2$ (Proposition 13.1). Energy is therefore subadditive under merging, and energy is proportional to squared arity. This is the reason why the sum of squared arities, and not the sum of arities, is the quantity that the construction can hold fixed.

Plan of the proof (pp. 74–86). The paper gives the plan in two paragraphs on p. 74. In each occupied cell it uses two sphere forms. The first form joins neighboring cells along pairs of legs called seams, and represents each station by an insertion at a marked point. The second form stays in its cell and serves to realize the actual wires of the input network. The table shows where each step is carried out.

StepWhereWhat it doesNumbered output
Tools§19.1Multi-species sphere forms; two-site fusion maps; the seam identity.(19.1), Lemma 19.2
Planting§20Rewrites the network of each cell as two sphere forms with one insertion table per mark. Fixes all scales.(20.1)–(20.4), Lemma 20.1
Motion§21Moves every movable station until it merges into a collector. Bounds the energy at the collectors and the new thin wires.(21.1), (21.2)
Accounting§22Converts energies to arities, splits the sphere forms into small tables, builds the common host, counts the indices of the sum.(22.1); conclusions 1–4

19.1 Sphere forms and a transposition identity

This subsection (pp. 74–76) extends the construction of §13 from one species of fermion to $r$ species, records the dimension bound, restates fusion for two sites, defines a seam, and proves Lemma 19.2.

Background — exterior algebra, Wick's rule and Pfaffians. filled here Let $E$ be a vector space with an ordered basis $(\varepsilon_1,\varepsilon_2,\ldots)$. The exterior algebra $\Lambda(E)$ has the basis of wedges $\varepsilon_{i_1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\varepsilon_{i_d}$ with $i_1\lt\cdots\lt i_d$; the number $d$ is the length of the wedge. The product is associative and $\varepsilon_i\wedge\varepsilon_j=-\varepsilon_j\wedge\varepsilon_i$, $\varepsilon_i\wedge\varepsilon_i=0$. Two facts are used below. (i) $\Lambda(E_1\oplus E_2)\cong\Lambda(E_1)\otimes\Lambda(E_2)$: a wedge is a wedge of generators of $E_1$ followed by a wedge of generators of $E_2$. (ii) Covectors $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_d\in E^*$ define a linear functional on length-$d$ wedges by $v_1\wedge\cdots\wedge v_d\mapsto\det[\lambda_s(v_t)]$; if the $\lambda$ range over a spanning set of $E^*$, these functionals span the dual of the space of length-$d$ wedges.

Let $g_1,\ldots,g_{2n}$ be a list of symbols and let $K_{st}$ be numbers with $K_{ts}=-K_{st}$. The Pfaffian is $$\operatorname{Pf}(K)=\sum_{\text{perfect matchings}}\operatorname{sgn}\cdot\prod_{r=1}^{n}K_{s_rt_r},$$ where a matching is a partition of the list into pairs $(s_r,t_r)$ with $s_r\lt t_r$, and $\operatorname{sgn}$ is the sign of the permutation that sends $(1,\ldots,2n)$ to $(s_1,t_1,\ldots,s_n,t_n)$. For four symbols, $\operatorname{Pf}(K)=K_{12}K_{34}-K_{13}K_{24}+K_{14}K_{23}$; this is the four-point example of §13.1 (p. 47). Wick's rule is the name for a form defined by such a signed sum over pairings, with $K_{st}$ called the contraction of $g_s$ with $g_t$. Three properties are used repeatedly. (P1) The Pfaffian is linear in each symbol: if $g_s$ is replaced by a linear combination of symbols, row and column $s$ of $K$ change linearly. (P2) Reordering the list by a permutation multiplies the Pfaffian by the sign of the permutation. (P3) If the list is split into groups, the matchings can be sorted by the set of pairs that lie inside the groups; this is the step behind fusion.

Definition (species, mode, energy, the spaces $V^{(r)}_e$). p. 74 paper Take the exterior algebra on generators $\psi_{s,m}$ with species $1\le s\le r$ and mode $m\ge 0$. The generator $\psi_{s,m}$ has energy $m+\tfrac12$; the energy of a wedge is the sum of the energies of its generators. $V^{(r)}_e$ is the span of the wedges of energy $e\in\tfrac12\mathbb Z_{\ge 0}$. Its basis is the wedge basis, with generators in lexicographic order on $(s,m)$. Every basis vector has length congruent to $2e$ modulo $2$, because a wedge of length $d$ has energy $d/2$ plus an integer. The wedge of length zero is the vacuum; it spans $V^{(r)}_0$. For $r=1$ these are the spaces $V_h$ of §13.1.

Put $c_0=\pi/\sqrt3$ (the constant $c$ of Lemma 13.2). The paper proves on pp. 74–75:

$$\dim V^{(r)}_e\le\exp\bigl(c_0\sqrt{re}\bigr).\tag{19.1}$$
Gap fill — derivation of (19.1), and the partition count behind "width is proportional to the square root of energy". filled here For $q\gt 0$ let $Z(q)=\prod_{m\ge0}\bigl(1+e^{-q(m+1/2)}\bigr)$. Expanding the product, each wedge of one species contributes $e^{-q\cdot\text{energy}}$, so $Z(q)=\sum_e\dim V^{(1)}_e\,e^{-qe}$. By fact (i) above, the generating function for $r$ species is $Z(q)^r$. Every term of a sum of nonnegative terms is at most the sum, so $\dim V^{(r)}_e\le e^{qe}Z(q)^r$.

The function $f(t)=\ln(1+e^{-t})$ is convex. For a convex function the value at the midpoint of an interval is at most its mean over the interval. Apply this to the interval $[qm,q(m+1)]$, whose midpoint is $q(m+\tfrac12)$, and sum over $m$: $$\ln Z(q)=\sum_{m\ge0}f\bigl(q(m+\tfrac12)\bigr)\le\frac1q\int_0^\infty f(t)\,dt=\frac{\pi^2}{12q}.$$ The integral is $\sum_{j\ge1}(-1)^{j+1}/j^2=\pi^2/12$ (expand $\ln(1+x)$ at $x=e^{-t}$ and integrate term by term). Hence $\ln\dim V^{(r)}_e\le qe+r\pi^2/(12q)$. The right side is least at $q=\pi\sqrt{r/(12e)}$, where both terms equal $\pi\sqrt{re/12}$; their sum is $\pi\sqrt{re/3}=c_0\sqrt{re}$. For $e=0$ the space is spanned by the vacuum and the bound is $1\le1$.

The count behind this: doubling the energies turns a one-species wedge of energy $h$ into a partition of $2h$ into distinct odd parts $2m+1$. Lemma 13.2 states $\dim V_h\sim\exp(c_0\sqrt h)/(2^{5/2}3^{1/4}h^{3/4})$, so the upper bound is sharp in the exponent. For $r$ species that share energy $e$ equally, each has energy $e/r$ and the log-dimensions add to $r\cdot c_0\sqrt{e/r}=c_0\sqrt{re}$. In bits, the width of a leg (the base-two logarithm of its dimension) satisfies $$w\le\frac{c_0}{\ln2}\sqrt{re}\approx2.617\sqrt{re},\qquad\text{so}\qquad w^2\le\frac{c_0^2}{(\ln 2)^2}\,r\,e .$$ Squared width is linear in energy, with slope proportional to the number of species.

The paper encodes a leg by the binary index of its basis state and pads unused codes with zero weights (p. 75). A leg of dimension $d$ thus becomes a bundle of $\lceil\log_2 d\rceil$ bits.

Definition (the sphere form $C_r$). p. 75 paper Let $z_1,\ldots,z_n\in K$ be distinct points, called marks or sites; they are points of the projective line over $K$, which the paper calls the sphere. The sphere form $C_r$ is a multilinear form on a tuple of wedges, one at each site. List the generators of all the wedges in site order. $C_r$ is the Pfaffian of this list with the following contractions. Two generators at a common site, or of different species, have contraction zero. For one species, the contraction of mode $m$ at site $i$ with mode $n$ at site $j$, in that order, is $$[x^my^n]\,\frac{1}{z_j+y-z_i-x}=\frac{(-1)^n\binom{m+n}{m}}{(z_j-z_i)^{m+n+1}}.\tag{13.1}$$ The Pfaffian of the empty list is one, and an odd list gives zero. The definition is over the integers and includes characteristic two by reduction.
Gap fill — the closed form in (13.1), and a generating-series way to read the kernel. filled here With $\Delta=z_j-z_i$, $\;\frac{1}{\Delta+y-x}=\sum_{n'\ge0}\frac{(x-y)^{n'}}{\Delta^{n'+1}}$. The coefficient of $x^my^n$ in $(x-y)^{m+n}$ is $(-1)^n\binom{m+n}{m}$. This gives the right side of (13.1).

It is convenient to attach to a site at position $p$ with local coordinate $x$ the formal series $\Psi(p+x)=\sum_{m\ge0}x^m\psi_m$. Then (13.1) says: the contraction of $\Psi(P_1)$ with $\Psi(P_2)$, in that order, is the kernel $1/(P_2-P_1)$, and the contraction of two modes is the corresponding coefficient. The kernel is antisymmetric in $P_1,P_2$, as a Pfaffian requires. The proof of Lemma 19.2 uses this reading.

The paper lists the properties of $C_r$ that it needs (p. 75); each one is inherited from the one-species form of §13.1.

  • Site exchange. Exchanging two adjacent sites with wedges of energies $e,d$ multiplies the form by $(-1)^{(2e)(2d)}$. This is (P2): a block of length $\equiv 2e$ passes a block of length $\equiv 2d$. When all energies are fixed, every such sign is a scalar that a table can absorb.
  • Poles. As a function of one position, with the others fixed, each value of the form is a rational function with poles only at the other positions; it is bounded at infinity. Each contraction (13.1) is a negative power of a difference of positions.
  • Species factorization. Contractions between different species vanish. After the generators are sorted by species, the Pfaffian is the product of the one-species Pfaffians, up to the sign of the sorting.
Background — Laurent expansion at a collision, and the two-site fusion maps $I_{e'}$. filled here Put two adjacent sites at $z$ and $z+u$, with wedges of energies $e$ and $m$, and expand the form in powers of $u$ at $u=0$. Sort the matchings by the pairs that join the two colliding sites (P3). A pair of modes $(n_1,n_2)$ between them contracts to a multiple of $u^{-(n_1+n_2+1)}$, that is, $u$ to the power minus the energy of the pair. The generators that are not paired inside the cluster are paired with the other sites; their kernels are regular at $u=0$. Taylor expansion of such a kernel in the offset replaces a generator $\psi_{s,d}$ at offset $\theta$ (here $\theta=0$ for the first site, $\theta=u$ for the second; the paper writes $a$ for the offset) by $$\psi_{s,d}\longmapsto\sum_{f\ge d}\binom fd \theta^{f-d}\psi_{s,f},\tag{13.3}$$ which is the identity $\Psi(z+\theta+x)=\sum_f(\theta+x)^f\psi_f$ read coefficient by coefficient. Each unit of mode raised carries one factor of $u$. Collecting powers of $u$: the coefficient of $u^{e'-e-m}$ is the same form with the two sites replaced by one site at $z$ of energy $e'$, applied to the vector $I_{e'}(v_1,v_2)$, where $v_1,v_2$ are the two colliding wedges and $$I_{e'}:V^{(r)}_e\otimes V^{(r)}_m\longrightarrow V^{(r)}_{e'}$$ is computed at offsets $0,1$: sum over the partial pairings between the two wedges, contract the pairs by (13.1) at positions $0,1$, translate the unmatched generators by (13.3), wedge them in order, and keep the part of energy $e'$. Only $e'\ge0$ with $e'-e-m\in\mathbb Z$ occur. This is Proposition 13.1 for a two-site cluster, applied species by species; the paper states the extension on p. 75. The exponent $e'-e-m$ is the order of the fusion. The rule used throughout §21 is $$\text{energy after fusion}=\text{sum of the two energies}+\text{order}.$$ Example (§13.2, p. 48): two generators $\psi_0,\psi_0$ at offsets $0,1$ have total energy $1$. Their internal contraction gives the vacuum, energy $0$, at order $u^{-1}$. If they stay unmatched, the first nonzero translated wedge is $\psi_0\wedge\psi_1$, energy $2$, at order $u^{+1}$.
Definition (seam, sewing, insertion). pp. 74–75 paper To join two spheres, choose one site on each and contract the two legs by equality on the wedge basis at an energy $e$: the joined form is $\sum_{a}C(\ldots,a,\ldots)\,C'(\ldots,a,\ldots)$, where $a$ runs over the wedge basis of $V^{(r)}_e$. The pair of sites is a seam, and $e$ is the energy of the seam. The paper calls this operation sewing. The paper uses the word insertion without a displayed definition; from its use on pp. 74 and 76, an insertion at a mark is a station whose ports include the leg of a sphere form at that mark (from §20 on: one leg on each of two spheres), together with ordinary bits. The sphere form itself is one more tensor of the network; a network of spheres sewn along seams, with an insertion table at every other mark, contracts to a number for each setting of the exposed bits.
Background — why sewing uses a basis equality, and why that creates a problem. filled here In a tensor network a wire is the equality pairing $\sum_a\varepsilon_a\otimes\varepsilon_a$ on a chosen basis. A seam is a wire of dimension $\dim V^{(r)}_e$ between two sphere forms. Fusion is an operation on the sphere form of one side: when a moving insertion with state $x$ collides with the seam site, the near sphere sees the vector $I_{e'}(a,x)$ in place of $a$. To continue the motion on the other sphere, this operator, which acts on the near index, has to be rewritten as an operator on the far index. For a general basis pairing, the transpose of a fusion map need not be a fusion map. Lemma 19.2 shows that here it is one, up to an explicit substitution $S_j$ applied to the moving state.
Lemma 19.2 (Transposition at a seam). p. 75 paper Let $a,b$ be basis states at energies $e,e'$, and $x\in V^{(r)}_m$. There are parity-preserving maps $S_j:V^{(r)}_m\to V^{(r)}_j$ for $j\le m$, independent of $a,b$, and a nonzero scalar $\alpha=\alpha(e,e',m)$ such that $$[I_{e'}(a,x)]_b=\alpha\sum_{j\le m}[I_e(b,S_jx)]_a .\tag{19.2}$$ The coefficient is taken in the indicated wedge basis.

Here $[v]_b$ is the coefficient of the basis state $b$ in the vector $v$, and "parity-preserving" means that $S_j$ maps wedges of length $d$ to combinations of wedges of length $d$. On the left, $x$ fuses with $a$ and the result is read at $b$. On the right, a modified $x$ fuses with $b$ and the result is read at $a$. The roles of input level $e$ and output level $e'$ are exchanged.

Step 1: a chart at infinity. paper Adjoin a square root $i$ of $-1$ to $k$; this is part of the "fixed finite extension" in Lemma 19.1. (In characteristic two, $i=1$.) Put a site at infinity with local coordinate $y$, so that its position is $1/y$, and multiply the kernel by $i/y$ for each generator at that site before extracting coefficients in $y$. For a finite position $U$ listed first, $$\frac iy\cdot\frac{1}{\tfrac1y-U}=\frac{i}{1-Uy}=i\sum_{n\ge0}U^ny^n .$$ With $U=x$ this says: mode $m$ at $0$ and mode $n$ at $\infty$ have contraction $i\,\delta_{mn}$.

Step 2: the two-point form at $0$ and $\infty$. paper Take basis states of lengths $d$ and $d'$ at $0$ and $\infty$. Contractions inside a site vanish, so $d=d'$ and each generator at $0$ pairs with the generator of the same species and mode at $\infty$. The two states are equal and the matching is unique. Its permutation sign is that of $(1,\ldots,d,1',\ldots,d')\to(1,1',2,2',\ldots)$, which is $(-1)^{d(d-1)/2}$. The value is $$\pi_d:=(-1)^{d(d-1)/2}\,i^d=\begin{cases}1,&d\ \text{even},\\ i,&d\ \text{odd},\end{cases}$$ and all other coefficients vanish. (Check: $d=1,2,3,4$ give $i,\ 1,\ i,\ 1$.) So the two-point form is the equality pairing on the wedge basis, up to the factor $\pi_d$.

Step 3: the three-point form at $0,1,\infty$ is a matrix coefficient of fusion. paper Evaluate the form at sites $0,1,\infty$, in this order, on $a,x,b$. Sort the matchings by their pairs between sites $0$ and $1$; these are the internal pairs in the definition of $I$, at offsets $0,1$. Each remaining generator pairs with a generator at $\infty$. By Step 1 with $U=\theta+x$, a generator $\psi_{s,d}$ at offset $\theta\in\{0,1\}$ and mode $n$ at infinity contract to $$[x^dy^n]\,\frac{i}{1-(\theta+x)y}=i\binom nd \theta^{\,n-d}.$$ This is the translation (13.3) followed by the contraction $i\,\delta$ of Step 1. By (P1) the remaining Pfaffian is the two-point form of Step 2 evaluated on the wedge of the translated unmatched generators and on $b$. Summing over the internal pairings gives $$C_{0,1,\infty}(a,x,b)=\pi_{|b|}\,[I_{e'}(a,x)]_b ,$$ where $|b|$ is the length of $b$ and $e'$ its energy; Step 2 selects the component of energy $e'$.

Step 4: inversion of the coordinate. paper Put $V=1/U$. The identity $$\frac{-i}{U_1}\cdot\frac{-i}{U_2}\cdot\frac{1}{\tfrac1{U_2}-\tfrac1{U_1}}=\frac{1}{U_2-U_1}$$ says that the kernel in the coordinate $U$ equals the kernel in the coordinate $V$ when each argument receives the factor $-i/U$. In the series notation, $\Psi(U)=(-i/U)\,\Psi'(1/U)$, where $\Psi'$ has the standard kernel in $V$. All contractions between different sites are preserved by this rewriting, so the three-point value is unchanged. The three sites transform as follows.

  • Old $\infty$: its chart series is $(i/y)\Psi(1/y)=(i/y)(-iy)\Psi'(y)=\Psi'(y)$. It becomes an ordinary site at $V=0$ with the same modes and factor one. It carries $b$.
  • Old $0$: $\Psi(U)=(-i/U)\Psi'(1/U)=-\bigl[(i/U)\Psi'(1/U)\bigr]$, and the bracket is the chart series at $V=\infty$ with local coordinate $U$. It becomes a site at infinity with the same modes and one factor $-1$ per generator. It carries $(-1)^{|a|}a$.
  • Old $1$, local coordinate $X$: $\Psi(1+X)=\frac{-i}{1+X}\Psi'\bigl(1+Y\bigr)$ with $Y=\frac1{1+X}-1$. Reading the coefficient of $X^d$ gives the single-generator substitution $$\psi_{s,d}\longmapsto\sum_{f=0}^{d}\Bigl([X^d]\,\frac{-i}{1+X}\Bigl(\frac1{1+X}-1\Bigr)^{f}\Bigr)\psi_{s,f}.$$ The sum stops at $f=d$ because $\frac1{1+X}-1=-X/(1+X)$ is divisible by $X$. Let $S$ be the map induced on wedges (the exterior power of this substitution) and $S_j$ its component of energy $j$. Each generator keeps its species and lowers or keeps its mode, so $S$ preserves length and $S_jx=0$ unless $j\le m$.

Step 5: reverse the order and apply Step 3 again. paper After Step 4 the list order is still (site of $a$, site of $x$, site of $b$), but now $b$ is at $0$, $Sx$ is at $1$ and $a$ is at $\infty$. Reverse the order of the three blocks; by (P2) this costs a sign that depends only on the three lengths. Step 3, with $(b,Sx,a)$ in place of $(a,x,b)$, evaluates the result as $\pi_{|a|}\sum_j[I_e(b,S_jx)]_a$. Comparing with Step 3 for the original order proves (19.2), with $\alpha$ the ratio of the collected factors. The factors depend only on the parities of $|a|,|x|,|b|$, which are the parities of $2e,2m,2e'$.∎

Gap fill — the value of $\alpha$. filled here The paper states only that $\alpha$ is a nonzero scalar depending on $(e,e',m)$. Collecting the factors of Steps 3–5: reversing blocks of lengths $|a|,|x|,|b|$ costs $(-1)^{|a||x|+|a||b|+|x||b|}$, and the old site $0$ contributes $(-1)^{|a|}$. Hence $$\begin{aligned}\pi_{|b|}\,[I_{e'}(a,x)]_b&=\sigma\,\pi_{|a|}\sum_{j}[I_e(b,S_jx)]_a ,\\ \sigma&=(-1)^{|a|+|a||x|+|a||b|+|x||b|}.\end{aligned}$$ Both sides vanish unless $|a|+|x|+|b|$ is even (an odd list has Pfaffian zero). Checking the four parity patterns with even total gives $$\alpha=\sigma\,\frac{\pi_{|a|}}{\pi_{|b|}}=\begin{cases}1,&|x|\ \text{even (}m\in\mathbb Z\text{)},\\ i,&|x|\ \text{odd (}m\in\mathbb Z+\tfrac12\text{)}.\end{cases}$$ For example, for $(|a|,|x|,|b|)=(\text{even},\text{odd},\text{odd})$, $\sigma=-1$ and $\pi_{|a|}/\pi_{|b|}=1/i=-i$, so $\alpha=i$; for $(\text{odd},\text{odd},\text{even})$, $\sigma=+1$ and the ratio is $i$. The substitution of Step 4 also has a closed form: since $[X^{d-f}](1+X)^{-(f+1)}=(-1)^{d-f}\binom df$, $$\psi_{s,d}\longmapsto-i\,(-1)^d\sum_{f=0}^{d}\binom df\psi_{s,f}.$$ These two closed forms are this tutorial's computation; the paper does not state them, and nothing later depends on them beyond $\alpha\ne0$.

A second check, with $|x|$ even. Take one species, $a=1$ (vacuum), $b=x=\psi_0\wedge\psi_1$, so $e=0$ and $e'=m=2$. Left side: translating at offset $1$ gives $(\psi_0+\psi_1+\cdots)\wedge(\psi_1+2\psi_2+\cdots)$, whose component of energy $2$ is $\psi_0\wedge\psi_1$; the coefficient is $1$. Right side: the substitution gives $\psi_0\mapsto-i\psi_0$ and $\psi_1\mapsto i(\psi_0+\psi_1)$, so $Sx=(-i)(i)\,\psi_0\wedge\psi_1=\psi_0\wedge\psi_1$. Then $I_0(\psi_0\wedge\psi_1,\psi_0\wedge\psi_1)$ is the Pfaffian of the four generators at offsets $0,0,1,1$: by (13.1) with difference $1$, $K_{13}=1$, $K_{24}=-2$, $K_{14}=-1$, $K_{23}=1$, and $K_{12}=K_{34}=0$, so the value is $-K_{13}K_{24}+K_{14}K_{23}=2-1=1$. Hence $\alpha=1$, as the closed form predicts.

The paper's check (p. 76). paper For one species take $a=1$ (the vacuum, $e=0$) and $b=x=\psi_0$ ($e'=m=\tfrac12$). Left side: $I_{1/2}(1,\psi_0)$ translates $\psi_0$ at offset $1$ to $\sum_f\psi_f$ and keeps energy $\tfrac12$, which is $\psi_0$; the coefficient at $b$ is $1$. Right side: the substitution sends $\psi_0$ to $[X^0]\frac{-i}{1+X}\psi_0=-i\psi_0$, so $S_{1/2}x=-i\psi_0$. Then $I_0(\psi_0,-i\psi_0)$ contracts mode $0$ at offset $0$ with mode $0$ at offset $1$; by (13.1) the contraction is $1$, so the value is $-i$ at energy zero. With $\alpha=i$ the right side is $i\cdot(-i)=1$. This agrees with the left side and with the closed form above ($|x|$ odd).

What the lemma will do. Write a seam as $\sum_aC_{\rm near}(a)\,C_{\rm far}(a)$ with $a$ of energy $e$. A Laurent coefficient of a collision at the near seam site replaces $C_{\rm near}(a)$ by $C_{\rm near}(I_{e'}(a,x))=\sum_bC_{\rm near}(b)[I_{e'}(a,x)]_b$. By (19.2) the sum over $a$ becomes $\alpha\sum_j\sum_bC_{\rm near}(b)\,C_{\rm far}(I_e(b,S_jx))$: a seam of energy $e'$, with the modified state $S_jx$ now colliding with the seam site on the far sphere. §21.2 carries this out.

20. Planting the wiring at marked points pp. 76–80

Role in the chain. This section receives the input networks of Lemma 19.1 and the sphere forms of §19.1. It uses Lemma 13.2 (p. 49) for the size of an energy level, Corollary 14.4 (p. 55) for one tensor comparison, the transport bound (3.1), and two imported results, [42, Lemma 2.2] and [42, Section 2]. It delivers an exact rewriting of the part of each network that lies in one cell: two sphere forms $A$ and $B$, and one insertion table at every mark, with explicit bounds on the energy at each mark. §21 starts its motion from this configuration, and §22 uses the storage bounds of §20.2 for the insertions that the motion never touches. The one use of the hypothesis $\omega_k\lt 9/4$ in Part III is display (20.4).

The problem is stated on p. 76. A station of the input may have wires to many other stations. To move the station as one insertion, all its wire ends must be encoded in a single sphere leg. The sphere form then has to test that the bits at the two ends of every wire agree. A sphere form is a fixed Pfaffian; it does not perform this test by itself. The construction makes it do so by placing additional insertions, at auxiliary marks, whose joint state is chosen so that the form with these insertions equals the required test. The test is implemented separately in each occupied cell of each network.

Definition (the sewn sphere $A$ and the private sphere $B$). p. 76 paper Each occupied cell has two sphere forms. The form $A$ carries the station legs and the seams to the neighboring cells. Extra marks on $A$ supply a joint state that tests the wiring. The private form $B$ realizes this joint state through a separate insertion table at each extra mark. After planting, each insertion has one leg on each sphere, together with any exposed or control bits. At the original stations and at the seams the $B$ leg is the vacuum. Only $A$ is sewn to neighboring cells. A mark at $z$ on $B$ corresponds to the mark at $z^P$ on $A$, where $P$ is a power of the characteristic of $k$.

Figure 7 (p. 76), in words. Two horizontal bands are drawn one above the other. The upper band is labelled "Sewn sphere $A$, $R$ species"; a line labelled "seam" leaves it at each end. The lower band is labelled "Private sphere $B$, $R/b$ species". Between the bands are two boxes. The box "original station" has a solid leg up to $A$ and a dotted leg down to $B$ labelled "vacuum". The box "auxiliary insertion" has solid legs to both bands; the upper attachment point is labelled $z^P$ and the lower one $z$.

Background — the Frobenius map, and where Part III needs positive characteristic. filled here Let $K$ be a field of characteristic $p\gt 0$ and $P=p^{\nu}$. For $0\lt j\lt p$ the binomial coefficient $\binom pj$ is divisible by $p$, so $(x+y)^p=x^p+y^p$ in $K$; by iteration $(x+y)^P=x^P+y^P$. The map $z\mapsto z^P$ is therefore a field homomorphism $K\to K$. It is injective, as every field homomorphism is. If $K$ is finite, an injective self-map is bijective: $z\mapsto z^P$ permutes $K$, and every element of $K$ has exactly one $P$-th root in $K$. Part III uses this in three places.
  1. $(z+t)^P-z^P=t^P$: "moving $z$ by $t$ moves $z^P$ by $t^P$" (p. 77), and $z^P-z_0^P=(z-z_0)^P$ in §21.1 (p. 80). A function with a pole of order $i$ in the $A$-coordinate has a pole of order $Pi$ in the $B$-coordinate.
  2. "Taking $P$th roots permutes that field" (p. 79): the $B$-positions of the marks stay in one fixed finite field when $P$ varies.
  3. "The map $z\mapsto z^P$ permutes $K$" (p. 82): a regular point for interpolation on $B$ corresponds to a regular point on $A$.
Uses 1 and 2 need characteristic $p$; over a field of characteristic zero no map $z\mapsto z^P$ with $P\gt 1$ is additive. The finiteness of $K$ is used for the bijectivity in 2 and 3 and for the bit counts of field elements in Lemma 19.1(4). These are the reasons why Lemma 19.1 is stated over a finite field $k$ and not over an arbitrary field; the passage from an arbitrary field to a finite one is Lemma 2.5 (Finite specialization, p. 9).

The exchange rate between the two spheres. paper By the fusion rule of §19.1, a collision on $A$ of order $i$ changes the $A$-energy by $i$, and a collision on $B$ of order $v$ changes the $B$-energy by $v$. If both are produced by the same motion of $z$, they appear at order $Pi+v$ in $z-z_0$, by use 1 above. Dividing by $P$: in units of the Laurent order on $A$, the energy of a pair of legs is $$e_A+P^{-1}e_B .$$ The paper calls this the weighted energy (from §21 on). A given budget of weighted energy allows $P$ times as much energy on $B$ as on $A$. To keep the $B$ leg narrow in spite of this, $B$ is given fewer species.

The scales. paper Fix $g,\ell$ as in Lemma 19.1. The paper sets (p. 77):

SymbolDefinitionMeaning
$F$the first power of the characteristic that is at least $D^{1000(\ell+1)}$ratio of squared widths, $A$ to $B$, at equal weighted energy
$a$$\lceil F^3\rceil$arity $T$ is spread over about $T/a$ species
$M$a power of $D$, fixed in §22number of chunks
$b$a sufficiently large power of the characteristic, $b\gt F$block size: one $B$ species serves $b$ species of $A$
$R$the largest multiple of $bM$ that is at most $T/a$number of species on $A$
$R'$$R/b$number of species on $B$
$P$$b/F$exponent of the map $z\mapsto z^P$; exchange rate
$\kappa$$P^{-1}$weight of $B$-energy
$H$$Ra^2$unit of energy: weighted energy $H$ gives $A$-width $O(T)$
$$R'=R/b,\qquad P=b/F,\qquad \kappa=P^{-1},\qquad H=Ra^2 .\tag{20.1}$$

For sufficiently large $T$, $T/(2a)\le R\le T/a$ (the multiples of $bM$ are spaced by $bM\le T/(2a)$). $P$ is a power of the characteristic because $b$ and $F$ are. Several letters are reused by the paper and are kept here: in §19–§22, $H$ is the energy unit $Ra^2$ and not a price; $a$ and $b$ are these integers and no longer the basis states of Lemma 19.2 (except in §21.2, where the paper returns to $a,b$ for basis states); $F$ is an integer and not a field; $D$ is the concentration parameter and not a scale. Since $F$ is an integer, the ceiling in $a=\lceil F^3\rceil$ has no effect.

If $e_A+\kappa e_B\le h$, the widths of the two legs in bits are, up to rounding,

$$w_A=O\bigl(T\sqrt{h/H}\bigr),\qquad w_B=O\bigl(T\sqrt{h/(FH)}\bigr).\tag{20.2}$$
Gap fill — derivation of (20.2). filled here From $e_A+e_B/P\le h$: $e_A\le h$ and $e_B\le Ph$. By (19.1), $w_A\le\frac{c_0}{\ln2}\sqrt{Rh}$ and $w_B\le\frac{c_0}{\ln2}\sqrt{R'Ph}$. Now $R'P=(R/b)(b/F)=R/F$, so $w_B\le\frac{c_0}{\ln 2}\sqrt{Rh/F}$. Since $H=Ra^2$ and $Ra\le T$, $$\sqrt{Rh}=\sqrt{R^2a^2\,h/H}=Ra\sqrt{h/H}\le T\sqrt{h/H}.$$ At $h=H$ the $A$ leg has $O(T)$ bits. At the same weighted energy the $B$ leg is narrower by the factor $\sqrt F\ge D^{500(\ell+1)}$. This factor is the reason for the two different species counts: the $B$ leg is what a station leaves behind when it crosses a seam (§21.2).

20.1 The wiring form for one species

Packets. paper Combine the wires between each pair of distinct stations into one ordered bundle; permutations of the endpoints are absorbed into the station tables, and self-loops are contracted inside their table first. Split each internal bundle into packets of $R$ bits, padding the last packet with zeros. Species $s$ will carry bit $s$ of every packet. In this way a station's wire ends are distributed over the $R$ species, and every species sees the same small combinatorial problem.

Gap fill — the counts of bits per species. filled here A station can have bundles to the other stations of its cell (fewer than $100D$) and to the stations of the at most $g$ neighboring cells (at most $100gD$). It meets $O_g(D)$ bundles. A bundle of $\lambda$ bits gives $\lceil\lambda/R\rceil\le\lambda/R+1$ packets. Summing over the bundles of a station of arity $t_i$, one species carries at most $t_i/R+O_g(D)$ bits there. Between a cell and one neighbor there are at most $(100D)^2$ bundles of total width at most $100DT$, so the packets across that interface give one species at most $100DT/R+10^4D^2$ bits. With $R\ge T/(2a)$ and $a\ge D$ this is $O_g(Da)$. These are the paper's numbers $t_i/R+O_g(D)$ and $O_g(DT/R+D^2)=O_g(Da)$.

Energies. paper At station $i$, choose distinct wedge basis states of one species at an integer energy $h_i$ to encode the possible bit strings of that species. At seam $j$, choose an integer energy $q_j$ in the same way for the bits that cross the interface; the two cells use the same bit order and the same codes there. The paper states that one may take

$$h_i=O_g\bigl(a^2(1+t_i/T)^2\bigr),\qquad q_j=O_g(D^2a^2).$$
Gap fill — why these energies suffice. filled here To encode $\beta$ bits one needs $\dim V_h\ge2^{\beta}$. The upper bound (19.1) does not give this; the asymptotic formula of Lemma 13.2 does: $\ln\dim V_h=c_0\sqrt h-O(\log h)$ as $h\to\infty$ through integers, so $\dim V_h\ge2^\beta$ for some integer $h=O((1+\beta)^2)$. With $\beta=t_i/R+O_g(D)\le2at_i/T+O_g(D)$ and $a\ge D$ this gives $h_i=O_g(a^2(1+t_i/T)^2)$. With $\beta=O_g(Da)$ it gives $q_j=O_g(D^2a^2)$. Integer energies are chosen so that every code is a wedge of even length (the length is congruent to $2h$ modulo $2$). In all $R$ species together, station $i$ has $A$-energy $Rh_i=O_g\bigl((1+t_i/T)^2H\bigr)$ and seam $j$ has energy $Rq_j=O_g(D^2H)$. These two numbers are the inputs to the accounting of §21.3.
Definition (the wiring form $L$ and its zero extension). p. 77 paper $L$ is the multilinear form on the station levels $V_{h_i}$ and the seam levels $V_{q_j}$ of one species, given by its coefficients on basis tuples. A basis tuple has coefficient one if all codes are valid and both ends of every bit agree; for a wire to another cell, the station end is compared with the seam end. Every other coefficient is zero. Put $Q=\sum_jq_j$. Extend $L$ by zero to all other tuples of nonnegative half-integer seam energies of sum at most $Q$; the station energies stay fixed. (This $L$ is not the integer $L=\lceil2P\mu\rceil$ of §21.)

The extension is needed because a seam of all $R$ species will be sewn at a fixed total energy $Rq_j$, which does not fix the energy of each species. The extended form is a linear functional on the direct sum, over all seam energy tuples $(q_j')$ with $\sum_jq'_j\le Q$, of the spaces $\bigotimes_iV_{h_i}\otimes\bigotimes_jV_{q'_j}$; it is zero on every summand except the prescribed one.

Lemma 20.1 (Auxiliary marks of bounded weight). p. 77 paper Inserting a joint state at polynomially many extra marks on a single-species sphere realizes the extended form $L$. At each extra mark the state uses only the vacuum and $\psi_0$. In every word in its support the number of occupied marks is even and at most $O_g(Da)$.

Explicitly: there are $m$ extra marks, with $m$ bounded by a polynomial in $D$ and $a$, and a vector $\omega$ in the $m$-fold tensor power of the two-dimensional space spanned by the vacuum and $\psi_0$, such that the one-species sphere form on the original and the extra marks, with $\omega$ in the extra arguments, equals the extended $L$. A word is a basis vector of that tensor power, that is, a subset $S$ of the extra marks at which $\psi_0$ is placed ("occupied marks"); the others receive the vacuum. The paper's proof is on pp. 77–78.

Step 1: lengths. paper A one-species wedge of length $d$ has energy at least $\tfrac12+\tfrac32+\cdots+(d-\tfrac12)=d^2/2$. At station $i$ the length is therefore at most $\sqrt{2h_i}=O_g(a(1+t_i/T))$. A cell has at most $100D$ stations of total arity at most $100DT$, so the lengths at the stations sum to $O_g(Da)$. At the at most $g$ seams, the lengths $d_j$ satisfy $\sum_jd_j\le\sqrt{g\sum_jd_j^2}\le\sqrt{2gQ}=O_g(Da)$ by Cauchy–Schwarz and $Q=O_g(D^2a^2)$. Let $d_{\max}=O_g(Da)$ bound the total length of every input tuple. List all pairs $(v,n)$ of an original mark $v$ and a mode $n$ that can occur in the input spaces; the list has polynomial size in $D,a$. Let $E$ be the vector space with basis $\varepsilon_{(v,n)}$.

Step 2: choose the marks. paper By (13.1), $\psi_0$ at a new point $z$, listed after the original marks, contracts with mode $n$ at $z_v$ to $(z-z_v)^{-n-1}$. So a new mark defines the covector $\lambda_z\in E^*$ with coordinates $(z-z_v)^{-n-1}$. The rational functions $z\mapsto(z-z_v)^{-n-1}$ have distinct principal parts and are linearly independent in every characteristic (uniqueness of partial fractions). Choose points one at a time: if the covectors chosen so far do not span $E^*$, some nonzero $c\in E$ is annihilated by all of them; the nonzero rational function $\sum c_{(v,n)}(z-z_v)^{-n-1}$ has finitely many zeros, so it is nonzero at some unused point of the algebraic closure, and that point enlarges the span. After at most $\dim E$ points the covectors span $E^*$. All points lie in a finite extension of $k$. The paper adds vacuum marks if necessary so that there are at least three auxiliary marks.

Step 3: tests without internal pairs. paper For a subset $S$ of the new marks let $\tau_S$ be the functional on the input spaces obtained by inserting $\psi_0$ at the marks of $S$ and the vacuum at the others. Let $\tau^\circ_S$ be the part of the Pfaffian in which every auxiliary generator pairs with an original generator. Sorting the matchings by their exact set $\Pi$ of pairs between two auxiliary generators (property (P3) of §19.1) gives $$\tau_S=\sum_{\Pi}\pm\Bigl(\prod_{(z,z')\in\Pi}\frac1{z'-z}\Bigr)\,\tau^\circ_{S\setminus\cup\Pi},$$ where the sign depends only on the positions of the pairs within $S$, because the auxiliary generators are last in the list. The term $\Pi=\emptyset$ is $\tau^\circ_S$. By induction on $|S|$, each $\tau^\circ_S$ is a linear combination of functionals $\tau_{S'}$ with $S'\subseteq S$ and $|S'|\equiv|S|$ modulo $2$.

Step 4: triangularity in the length. paper Identify a basis tuple of total length $d$ with the wedge in $\Lambda^d(E)$ of all its generators in list order; distinct tuples, also from different seam energies, give distinct basis wedges. Let $|S|=d$. On a tuple of length less than $d$, $\tau^\circ_S$ vanishes: there are not enough original generators. On a tuple of length exactly $d$, every original generator is paired with an auxiliary one, and the signed sum over these bijections is, up to the common sign $(-1)^{d(d-1)/2}$, the determinant $\det[\lambda_{z_s}(g_t)]$, that is, the functional $\lambda_{z_1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\lambda_{z_d}$ on $\Lambda^d(E)$. Now let $x$ be a nonzero vector in the even part of the direct sum of the input spaces, and let $d$ be the greatest length that occurs in $x$. Its length-$d$ component is a nonzero element of $\Lambda^d(E)$. The covectors $\lambda_z$ span $E^*$, so by fact (ii) of §19.1 some product of $d$ of them is nonzero on that component. The corresponding $\tau^\circ_S$ vanishes on the shorter components of $x$, so $\tau^\circ_S(x)\ne0$. The functionals $\tau^\circ_S$ with $|S|$ even therefore have no common zero in the even part except $0$, and so they span its dual.

Step 5: conclusion. paper By Steps 3 and 4, the restriction of the extended $L$ to the even part is a combination $\sum_Sc_S\tau_S$ with $|S|$ even and $|S|\le d_{\max}=O_g(Da)$. On the odd part every such $\tau_S$ vanishes, because the list has odd length. The extended $L$ also vanishes there: it is supported on the prescribed integer energies, where all lengths are even. So $L=\sum_Sc_S\tau_S$ on the whole space, and $\omega=\sum_Sc_S\,(\psi_0\text{ on }S,\ \text{vacuum elsewhere})$ is the required joint state.∎

Not verified — "at least three auxiliary marks". not verified The proof of Lemma 20.1 (p. 78) adds vacua "to obtain at least three auxiliary marks". The paper does not say where the number three is needed. At least two marks are needed in §20.2 (the diagonal argument for $M_u\gt0$, and Theorem 14.1 assumes at least two sites). This tutorial did not find a step that needs a third mark.

From one species to $R$ species. paper Use one copy of $\omega$ in each of the $R$ species; the joint auxiliary state is $\omega^{\otimes R}$. The insertion table at station $i$ accepts only states that are at level $h_i$ in every species, decodes the packets, checks that the padded bits are zero, and returns the entry of the original table, with its exposed bits. Seam $j$ is sewn by basis equality at total energy $Rq_j$. The paper then argues (p. 78) that this network contracts to the original one.

Gap fill — why the total seam energy fixes the energy of every species. filled here A wedge basis state of $R$ species is a product of one-species basis states, so every term of the contraction has a definite energy $q^{(s)}_j$ of species $s$ at seam $j$, with $\sum_sq^{(s)}_j=Rq_j$. Summing over the seams of the cell, $\sum_s\sum_jq_j^{(s)}=RQ$. By species factorization (§19.1), the $A$-form with $\omega^{\otimes R}$ inserted is, up to sign, the product over $s$ of the one-species forms with $\omega$ inserted. If $\sum_jq^{(s)}_j\le Q$, the factor for species $s$ is the extended $L$, which is zero unless $(q^{(s)}_j)_j=(q_j)_j$. So in a nonzero term every species has either the prescribed tuple, of sum exactly $Q$, or a tuple of sum greater than $Q$. An average of $R$ numbers, each at least $Q$, equals $Q$ only if every number equals $Q$. Every species has the prescribed tuple, and each factor is the unextended $L$: the product tests the equality of bit $s$ of every packet, for all $s$, which is the equality of the bits along every wire.

Signs. paper To factor the Pfaffian by species, the original generators and the auxiliary generators are each sorted by species. The auxiliary sorting sign depends only on the auxiliary word and is absorbed into the joint state. Moving the auxiliary generators of one species next to the original generators of that species costs no sign, because each species has an even number of auxiliary generators (Lemma 20.1). The sorting sign of the original generators depends only on their lengths; in a nonzero term all original wedges have even length in every species (integer energies), so this sign is one. The paper also places one vacuum insertion in each cell, the designated collector of §21.

20.2 Realizing the joint auxiliary state

Lemma 20.1 prescribes one tensor, $\omega^{\otimes R}$, shared by all $m$ auxiliary marks of a cell. The motion of §21 moves one insertion at a time, so it needs a separate table at each mark. A tensor with $m$ legs is produced from separate tables at its legs when it is a restriction of another tensor, in the sense of §3: one linear map at each site. The paper obtains $\omega^{\otimes R}$ as a sum of restrictions of sphere forms on the private sphere $B$. In this subsection $m$ is the number of auxiliary marks; it is polynomial in $D$ for fixed $\ell$ and characteristic, because $a=F^3$ and $F$ is less than the characteristic times $D^{1000(\ell+1)}$.

Definition (blocks, types, the tensors $J_\tau$). pp. 78–79 paper Partition the $R$ species of $A$ into $R'=R/b$ consecutive blocks of $b$ species. One $B$ species will serve one block. A block has type $\tau=(d_i)_{i=1}^m$ if $d_i$ of its modes $\psi_0$ are occupied at auxiliary mark $i$. Projecting the block tensor $\omega^{\otimes b}$ onto the local subspaces with these occupation numbers gives a tensor $J_\tau$, including the sign for reordering within the block. Its $i$th leg has dimension $\binom b{d_i}$. For a fixed ordered word of block types, the joint tensor is the product of the $J_\tau$ up to a known sign. Only the types with $J_\tau\ne0$ are retained.

So $\omega^{\otimes b}=\sum_\tau J_\tau$, and $\omega^{\otimes R}$ is the sum, over all words $(\tau_1,\ldots,\tau_{R'})$ of types, of $\pm J_{\tau_1}\otimes\cdots\otimes J_{\tau_{R'}}$. Let $H_2$ be the binary entropy function in bits. Round each $H_2(d_i/b)$ up to a strictly larger point $u_i$ of a fixed positive grid of mesh $1/m$. The paper states:

$$\log_2\binom b{d_i}\le bu_i,\qquad\max_iu_i\le2,\qquad\sum_iu_i=O_{g,\ell}(Da\log D).\tag{20.3}$$
Gap fill — proof of (20.3). filled here First, $\binom b{d}\le2^{bH_2(d/b)}$ is the standard entropy bound for a binomial coefficient, and $H_2(d_i/b)\lt u_i$. Second, $H_2\le1$ and the rounding adds at most $1/m\le1$. Third, put $p_i=d_i/b$ and $s=\sum_ip_i$ (the paper's letter; not a species index). By Lemma 20.1 each species has at most $O_g(Da)$ occupied marks, so $\sum_id_i\le b\cdot O_g(Da)$ and $s=O_g(Da)$. $H_2$ is concave, so $\sum_iH_2(p_i)\le mH_2(s/m)$. For $0\lt x\le1$, $H_2(x)\le x\log_2(e/x)$, because $(1-x)\ln\frac1{1-x}\le x$. Hence $$\sum_iH_2(p_i)\le s\log_2\frac{em}{s}.$$ The right side increases with $s$ on $(0,m]$ and $m$ is polynomial in $D$, so it is $O_{g,\ell}(Da\log D)$. Rounding adds at most $m\cdot\frac1m=1$ to the sum. Each $u_i$ is at least $1/m$, which is used below.

The comparison tensor. paper At the $B$ marks that correspond to the auxiliary marks, let $S_\tau$ be the direct sum of all nonzero single-species sphere forms with energy at most $C(bu_i)^2$ at site $i$, where $C$ is an absolute constant (not a sphere form). The paper asserts: for sufficiently large $b$ there is a constant $c_\tau$ such that for every $d\ge1$ a product of local linear maps gives

$$\bigoplus_{s=1}^{c_\tau}S_\tau^{\otimes d}\longrightarrow J_\tau^{\otimes d}.\tag{20.4}$$

In words: $J_\tau^{\otimes d}$ is a restriction of $c_\tau$ copies of $S_\tau^{\otimes d}$, and $c_\tau$ does not depend on $d$. (Here $d$ is a multiplicity and $s$ a copy index.) The independence from $d$ lets one selector index serve every occurrence of the type. The paragraph after (20.4) on p. 79 gives the argument. It rests on three results proved elsewhere, which the next three boxes quote. The proof walk of (20.4) follows them.

Imported — [42, Lemma 2.2] (strict spectral comparison), as quoted in §3.2. imported If two nonzero tensors $T,U$ on the same sites satisfy $\varphi(T)\lt\varphi(U)$ for every spectral point $\varphi$, there is a fixed integer $C$ such that, for every $j\ge1$, $T^{\otimes j}$ is a restriction of the direct sum of $C$ copies of $U^{\otimes j}$.
Imported — [42, Section 2] (field extension), as quoted in §3.2. imported Under a field extension, the possible joint values of spectral points on tensors defined over the original field are preserved.

Recalled from §14 — Corollary 14.4 (A common comparison tensor), p. 55. paper Suppose $\omega_F\lt9/4$ for an algebraically closed field $F$ (in this corollary $F$ is a field, and $a_i$, $k$, $H$ have the meanings of §14). Fix the $k$ marked positions and positive caps $a_1,\ldots,a_k$. Let $A_N$ be the direct sum of all nonzero $C(\mathbf h;\mathbf z)$ with $w_N(h_i)\le a_i$ at every leg, where $w_N(h)=c_0\sqrt h/N$. Then $H_{\mathbf x}(A_N)/N=\inf_{o\in X}\sum_ia_i\,d(o,x_i)+o(1)$, uniformly over placements $\mathbf x$ of bounded scale in complete CAT(0) spectral spaces $X$ over $F$. The $i$th leg of $A_N$ has log dimension at most $Na_i+O_{k,\mathbf a}(\log(2N))$. Here $H_{\mathbf x}$ is the price at the placement $\mathbf x$, in natural logarithms, and $d$ is the wire distance of §3.

Step 1: an upper bound for the price of $J_\tau$. paper Work over the algebraic closure of $k$, where the exponent is still below $9/4$, and use natural logarithms. Take any spectral point, normalized to scale one, and realize its sites $x_1,\ldots,x_m$ in a complete CAT(0) spectral space (§3.2). A nonzero tensor with all legs at one point $o$ has price zero. Moving leg $i$, of dimension at most $2^{bu_i}$ by (20.3), from $o$ to $x_i$ costs at most $d(o,x_i)\cdot bu_i\ln2$ by the transport bound (3.1). With $M_u=\inf_o\sum_iu_i\,d(o,x_i)$, the price of $J_\tau$ is at most $b(\ln2)M_u$.

Step 2: $M_u$ is bounded below. paper The diagonal of size two on all $m$ sites is the tensor $e_1^{\otimes m}+e_2^{\otimes m}$, which shares one bit among all sites ($\Delta_S(2)$ in §3.1). It is a direct sum of two units, so every spectral point gives it the value $2$, and its price is $\ln2$ at scale one. The same transport bound applied to it gives $\ln2\le\sum_id(o,x_i)\ln2$ for every $o$, so $\inf_o\sum_id(o,x_i)\ge1$ and $M_u\ge\min_iu_i\ge1/m\gt0$. The sites of a normalized price cannot all coincide.

Step 3: a lower bound for the price of $S_\tau$. paper $S_\tau$ is the tensor $A_N$ of Corollary 14.4 with $N=b$ and caps $a_i=c_0\sqrt C\,u_i$: the condition $c_0\sqrt{h_i}/b\le c_0\sqrt Cu_i$ is $h_i\le C(bu_i)^2$. Its price is $b\bigl(c_0\sqrt C\,M_u+o(1)\bigr)$, uniformly in the normalized price. Choose $C$ with $c_0\sqrt C\gt2\ln2$. Then $$\text{price}(S_\tau)-\text{price}(J_\tau)\ge b\bigl((c_0\sqrt C-\ln2)M_u-o(1)\bigr)\ge b\bigl(\tfrac{\ln2}{m}-o(1)\bigr),$$ which is positive once $b$ is large.

Step 4: one threshold for $b$. paper The $o(1)$ of Corollary 14.4 depends on the caps and on the marked positions. For fixed $D$ there are finitely many grid vectors $(u_i)$ and finitely many choices of the $A$ positions, all in a fixed finite extension $k_0$ of $k$. The $B$ positions are the $P$-th roots of the $A$ positions, and $P=b/F$ changes with $b$; but $z\mapsto z^P$ permutes $k_0$, so the $B$ positions also lie in $k_0$ and range over a finite set. One threshold $b_0$ therefore serves all cases: for every power $b\ge b_0$ of the characteristic, $S_\tau$ has strictly larger value than $J_\tau$ at every spectral point over the algebraic closure.

Step 5: from prices to a restriction. paper By the field-extension result, the strict inequality holds at every spectral point over $k_0$. By [42, Lemma 2.2] there is an integer $c_\tau$ with $J_\tau^{\otimes d}$ a restriction of $c_\tau$ copies of $S_\tau^{\otimes d}$ for every $d\ge1$, over $k_0$. The linear maps extend to the field $K$ of the input tables by extension of scalars.∎

Gap fill — remarks on the proof of (20.4). filled here (a) The numerical condition holds for $C=1$: $c_0=\pi/\sqrt3\approx1.814$ and $2\ln2\approx1.386$. Step 3 as reconstructed here needs only $c_0\sqrt C\gt\ln2$; the margin is then $(c_0\sqrt C-\ln2)M_u\ge(c_0\sqrt C-\ln2)/m$. The paper asks for $c_0\sqrt C\gt2\ln2$ and does not say what the additional factor $2$ is for. (b) The constants are not effective as given. The threshold $b_0$ comes from the $o(1)$ of Corollary 14.4, for which the paper states no rate, and $c_\tau$ comes from [42, Lemma 2.2], for which this paper quotes no bound. (c) This is the only step of Part III that uses $\omega_k\lt9/4$. Without the hypothesis, the transport bound still gives the upper bound of Step 1 for both tensors, but nothing forces $S_\tau$ to be as expensive as its transport bound, and the restriction (20.4) is not available by this route.
Not verified — the inputs to (20.4). not verified This tutorial checked that the paragraph after (20.4) follows from the three quoted results: the arithmetic of Steps 1–3, the identification of $S_\tau$ with $A_N$, and the finiteness argument of Step 4. It did not check [42, Lemma 2.2] or [42, Section 2], which are outside this paper; the proof of Corollary 14.4 belongs to §14. One point is left implicit by the paper: the tensor $J_\tau$ and the list of types depend on $b$, while Corollary 14.4 is a limit for fixed caps and positions. Steps 1–4 use $J_\tau$ only through the dimension bound (20.3), and the caps range over the finitely many grid vectors, so in this reconstruction the threshold $b_0$ does not depend on the type. The paper does not state this.

Assembling the auxiliary insertions. paper Fix a type word. For each type of multiplicity $d\gt0$ in the word, expand the restriction (20.4): choose one copy index $s$ and one $B$-energy tuple for each of the $d$ factors of $S_\tau^{\otimes d}$. Assign the factors to the private species in the original block order. The product of the chosen single-species forms is the $R'$-species sphere form $B$, restricted to the chosen energies in each species, up to the sign for permuting the homogeneous lists. The linear map at site $i$ in (20.4) now is a table that couples the $A$ leg and the $B$ leg at mark $i$; it may act jointly on all blocks of a given type at that mark. Summing over the choices and over the type words produces $\omega^{\otimes R}$. The $B$ legs at the original stations and at the seams remain the vacuum.

Definition (private selector, histogram). pp. 79–80 paper A private selector records the ordered type word, the $B$ energies of each block, and one index $s$ per occurring type. A single table checks its validity, including zero padding of unused slots, and supplies the permutation sign. The selector is a string of bits on ordinary wires inside the network; every auxiliary insertion of the cell reads it, and the network sums over it. Split the blocks into $M$ equal consecutive chunks. A histogram of a chunk records, for each pair (type, $B$-energy tuple), the number of blocks of the chunk with that pair. The histograms are part of the index of the outer sum of Lemma 19.1. A histogram records multiplicities; the selector records the order.

The reason for the two levels is stated on p. 80: the selector is summed inside the network, but the layout of each term of the outer sum must know the energy and the width at each mark. The histograms fix the energy and the code length of each leg in each chunk. A table checks the histograms against the selector, and each valid selector occurs in exactly one outer term. The alphabet of pairs is fixed independently of $T$ and each count is at most $R\le T$, so the histograms cost $O_{D,b,M}(\log(T+1))$ bits per cell.

Gap fill — the three cost estimates of "Control and storage" (pp. 79–80). filled here

Selector length. A block needs its type ($m$ numbers $d_i\le b$) and its doubled $B$ energies ($m$ integers of size $O(b^2)$): $O(m\log(2b))$ bits. There are $R/b\le T/(ab)$ blocks, so the selector has $O\bigl(T\,m\log(2b)/(ab)\bigr)$ bits plus $O_{D,b}(1)$ bits for the copy indices. Since $m$ does not depend on $b$, this is below $\epsilon T$ for any fixed $\epsilon\gt0$ when first $b$ and then $T$ is large.

Energy. On $A$, each occupied auxiliary mode $\psi_{s,0}$ has energy $\tfrac12$; each species has $O_g(Da)$ of them, so the auxiliary $A$-energy of a cell is $O_g(RDa)$. On one $B$ species, the caps sum to $\sum_iC(bu_i)^2\le2Cb^2\sum_iu_i=O_{g,\ell}(b^2Da\log D)$ by (20.3). Multiply by $R'=R/b$ species and by $\kappa=F/b$:

$$\sum_i\bigl(e_{A,i}+\kappa e_{B,i}\bigr)=O_{g,\ell}\bigl(RDa+FRDa\log D\bigr)\le DH .$$

The last step needs $O_{g,\ell}(1+F\log D)\le a$, since $DH=DRa^2$. It holds for large $D$ because $a=F^3$. This is where the exponent $3$ in $a=\lceil F^3\rceil$ is used.

Storage. With the block's type selected, the $A$ leg at mark $i$ has $\log_2\binom b{d_i}\le bu_i$ bits, and the $B$ leg has at most $\frac{c_0}{\ln2}\sqrt{C(bu_i)^2}=O(bu_i)$ bits by (19.1). Over the $R/b$ blocks, one auxiliary insertion needs $O(R)=O(T/a)$ bits for its two legs, because $u_i\le2$. Summed over the marks: $\frac Rb\sum_iO(bu_i+1)=O\bigl(R\sum_iu_i+Rm/b\bigr)=O_{g,\ell}(RDa\log D)=O_{g,\ell}(DT\log D)$, using $b\ge m$ and $Ra\le T$.

The order of the choices. paper $M$ is chosen for the chunk layout (in §22), then $\epsilon$ for the routing of the selector (in §21.3), and finally $b$ for the comparison (20.4) and the selector length; all three are fixed before $T$ grows.

The configuration after planting. In each occupied cell of each network, in every term of the outer sum and for every value of the selector, the marks and their weighted energies $e_A+\kappa e_B$ are as follows.
