21. Moving insertions through a sewn surface pp. 80–83

Role in the chain. This section receives the planted configuration of §20, the fusion rule (Proposition 13.1, p. 48), Lemma 19.2, and the scales (20.1) and widths (20.2). It combines the insertion tables at a small number of marks. It delivers, for every term of the outer sum: each original movable station has been merged into a collector; the collector energies sum to $O_g(WH/T)$; no cell ever holds more than $O_g(D^2H)$ of insertion energy; there are $O(W/(DT))$ collectors; and the new thin wires have total width $O(D^{-2\ell}T)$ per cell. §22 converts these energy bounds into the arity bounds of Lemma 19.1. The section has no numbered statement; its numbered displays are (21.1) and (21.2).

The paper names the two tools on p. 80. Both are outcomes of a Laurent expansion in the position of one moving insertion. At an insertion mark, a coefficient fuses the moving table with the table already there. At a seam, a sufficiently negative coefficient transfers the moving table to the neighboring cell and lowers the energy of the seam. The decrease of the seam energy bounds the number of crossings and the amount of energy that a cell can receive.

Definition (collector, mover, weighted energy, reserve). p. 80 paper In each cell, the marks of the original immobile stations and one additional vacuum mark are designated collectors. They stay in their cell. The original movable stations are processed in turn, skipping those already absorbed; the station in process is the mover. The moving table keeps its ordinary bit ports. If its current legs on $A$ and $B$ have energies $m_A,m_B$, put $$m=m_A+m_B/P,\qquad\mu=m+H,\qquad L=\lceil2P\mu\rceil .$$ $P$ is the exchange rate of (20.1) and $m$ is the weighted energy of the mover. The reserve $H$ stays with a move from its start to its end, while $m$ is updated after every fusion or crossing. (This integer $L$ is not the wiring form of §20.1, and $m$ is not the number of auxiliary marks of §20.2.)

21.1 Reconstructing an evaluation from collisions

The function of one variable. paper Fix the term of the outer sum and the value of the private selectors; the term determines all energies, so the identity below is the same for all allowed selector values. Hold all other positions and insertion tables fixed and let the mover have $B$-coordinate $z$ and $A$-coordinate $z^P$. Each entry of the contraction of the cell is a rational function of $z$. Its poles are at the other marks of the two spheres, and it is bounded at infinity (§19.1). Near a mark with $B$-coordinate $z_0$ write $s=z-z_0$. The coordinate difference on $A$ is $z^P-z_0^P=s^P$ (Frobenius, §20). An $A$-fusion of order $i$ and a $B$-fusion of order $v$ therefore contribute to order $j=Pi+v$ in $s$. At an insertion of weighted energy $m_0$ the merged weighted energy is

$$m_{\rm new}=m_0+m+j/P .\tag{21.1}$$
Gap fill — (21.1) and the pole bound. filled here Let the stationary insertion have energies $(m_{0,A},m_{0,B})$. By the fusion rule of §19.1, the merged $A$-energy is $m_{0,A}+m_A+i$ and the merged $B$-energy is $m_{0,B}+m_B+v$. The weighted sum is $m_0+m+i+v/P=m_0+m+j/P$. Both merged energies are nonnegative, so $i\ge-(m_{0,A}+m_A)$ and $v\ge-(m_{0,B}+m_B)$. Hence $j\ge-P(m_0+m)$: the pole at this mark has order at most $P(m_0+m)$. For a given $j$ the pairs $(i,v)$ with $Pi+v=j$ and these lower bounds are finite in number.
Background — the degree of a divisor on the projective line. filled here A nonzero rational function $f=p/q$ in one variable, with $p,q$ coprime polynomials, has $\deg p$ zeros and $\deg q$ poles in the algebraic closure, counted with multiplicity, and at infinity a zero of order $\deg q-\deg p$ if this is positive, or a pole of order $\deg p-\deg q$. The number of zeros equals the number of poles when infinity is included. Consequently: if $f$ has no pole at infinity and has more zeros at finite points than its total pole order, then $f=0$.

Which coefficients are used. paper To reconstruct the value of the contraction at the current position of the mover, the paper uses the following Laurent coefficients at the other marks of the cell:

  • at every insertion, its entire principal part (all negative orders);
  • at the designated vacuum collector, also the orders $0,\ldots,gL$, even after this collector has absorbed a table;
  • at each seam, only the orders $j\le-L$ within its pole bound.

Claim (p. 81). paper On the space of rational functions with the prescribed pole bounds, evaluation at the mover's position is a linear combination of the listed coefficients.

paper Let $f$ be a function in this space on which all listed coefficients vanish. At every insertion its principal part is zero, so it has no pole there. At each of the at most $g$ seams the orders $\le-L$ are zero, so the pole has order at most $L-1$. The total pole order is at most $g(L-1)$. At the designated collector the orders up to $gL$ are zero, so $f$ has a zero of order at least $gL+1$. It has no pole at infinity. Since $gL+1\gt g(L-1)$, the divisor count gives $f=0$.

filled here The space $\Phi$ of functions with the prescribed pole bounds is finite-dimensional. The listed coefficients are linear functionals on $\Phi$ with common kernel $\{0\}$, so they span the dual space $\Phi^*$. Evaluation at the mover's position, which is not a mark, is an element of $\Phi^*$. The coefficients of the resulting combination depend only on the positions and the pole bounds. The combination is applied to each entry separately, so it does not depend on the tables.∎

AHA. A rational function is determined by any set of Laurent coefficients that leaves no room for a nonzero function, and the construction chooses the set. It reads $gL+1$ Taylor coefficients at one vacuum mark. In exchange, at a seam it needs only the coefficients of order $\le-L$. Every crossing then has order at most $-L$, and by (21.2) below it removes at least $\mu\ge H$ from the energy of the seam. A seam energy starts at $O_g(D^2H)$ and never increases, so each seam allows $O_g(D^2)$ crossings.

What a coefficient at an insertion is. paper A coefficient of order $j$ at an insertion is a sum over the compatible pairs $(i,v)$. For each pair, apply the two fusion maps, $I$ on $A$ at order $i$ and $I$ on $B$ at order $v$, and contract them with the moving table and the stationary table, including the sign of their chosen order. The result is one new insertion at the stationary mark, whose ordinary ports are those of both tables. Each choice of mark, order and pair is one branch of the outer sum. A collision with a collector ends the move. At any other insertion only $j\lt0$ is used, so by (21.1) the combined table continues as the mover with at most the sum of the two weighted energies. Such a step can absorb an auxiliary insertion, an original station that awaits its turn, or a private insertion left by an earlier crossing.

21.2 Crossing a seam

Suppose the selected coefficient has order $j\le-L$ at a seam of energy $e$. Only $A$ is sewn there; on $B$ the corresponding mark carries the vacuum, so the $B$-expansion has Taylor orders $v\ge0$ only. For each such $v$ with $i=(j-v)/P$ an integer in the fusion range, the new $A$-energy at the seam is

$$e'=e+m_A+i\le e+m_A-2\mu\le e-\mu .\tag{21.2}$$
Gap fill — (21.2). filled here The equality is the fusion rule on $A$: the seam leg of energy $e$ and the mover's $A$-leg of energy $m_A$ fuse at order $i$. Since $v\ge0$, $i\le j/P\le-L/P\le-2\mu$, because $L\ge2P\mu$. This is the first inequality. The second is $m_A\le m\le\mu$. Terms with $e'\lt0$ vanish. So one crossing lowers the seam energy by at least $\mu=m+H\ge H$.

The $B$ side: a residue. paper With $i$ fixed, the paper interpolates the Taylor coefficient of order $v$ on $B$ by evaluations at fresh regular positions. Each resulting term has one private insertion of $B$-energy $m_B$ left behind in the near cell, with the vacuum on $A$. Its index is carried to the moving table by an ordinary equality wire. This private insertion is the residue of the crossing.

Gap fill — what the interpolation does. filled here By linearity fix basis states $x_A,x_B$ of the mover's two legs. The entry is then a product $G_A(z^P)\,G_B(z)$ of a value of the $A$-form and a value of the $B$-form. Its coefficient of order $j$ in $s$ is $\sum_{Pi+v=j}[u^i]G_A\cdot[s^v]G_B$, where $u=s^P$. The Taylor coefficient $[s^v]G_B$ is a linear functional on a finite-dimensional space of rational functions, so it is a combination $\sum_\zeta c_\zeta\,G_B(\zeta)$ of values at regular points $\zeta$ (see the end of this subsection). The value $G_B(\zeta)$ is the $B$-form with the state $x_B$ inserted at the new mark $\zeta$. So the $B$-leg of the mover has been placed at a fixed point of the near cell. The table of this residue is the identity between the basis state $x_B$ and its binary code; the code travels on a wire to the moving table, which keeps the port. By (20.2) with $h=O_g(D^2H)$, the wire has $O_g(DT/\sqrt F+1)$ bits. The weighted energy of the residue is $m_B/P$.

The $A$ side: Lemma 19.2. paper Fix the mover's old $A$-state $x$ by linearity. Let $a,b$ range over the wedge basis states of energies $e,e'$ (here $a,b$ are basis states again, as in Lemma 19.2). Write $C_{\rm near},C_{\rm far}$ for the two $A$-sphere forms as functions of the state at their seam site, with all other arguments suppressed. Lemma 19.2 gives

$$\begin{aligned} &\sum_bC_{\rm near}(b)\sum_a[I_{e'}(a,x)]_b\,C_{\rm far}(a)\\ &\quad=\alpha\sum_{t\le m_A}\sum_bC_{\rm near}(b)\sum_a[I_e(b,S_tx)]_a\,C_{\rm far}(a). \end{aligned}$$

The left side is the coefficient of order $i$ of the collision of $x$ with the seam site on the near sphere: the seam contraction $\sum_aC_{\rm near}(a)C_{\rm far}(a)$ with $a$ replaced by $I_{e'}(a,x)$ on the near side. On the right, the inner sum $\sum_a[I_e(b,S_tx)]_aC_{\rm far}(a)=C_{\rm far}(I_e(b,S_tx))$ is the Laurent coefficient of order $e-e'-t$ for an insertion $S_tx$, of energy $t$, that approaches the seam site of the far sphere, where the state is $b$. This order is an integer, because $e'-e-m_A=i$ and $m_A-t$ are integers for nonzero terms. The paper interpolates this coefficient by regular evaluations on the far sphere, with the vacuum at the corresponding $B$-position. The sum over $b$ sews the two spheres at the new energy $e'$. The new moving table is on the far side with energies $(t,0)$, $t\le m_A$; it keeps its ordinary ports and the wire to the residue. All reordering signs depend only on the specified energies, and the identity stays valid when $x$ is coupled to the private index or to a spectator.

Figure 8 (p. 82), in words. Two boxes, "Near cell" and "Far cell", each contain the label "sewn sphere $A$". A solid line joins a point on the edge of each box and is labelled "seam energy $e'$". Below, the near cell contains a box "private residue $(0,m_B)$" and the far cell a box "moving table $(t,0)$"; a dashed line labelled "$B$-state index" joins them. The caption states $e'\le e-\mu$ and $t\le m_A$.

Energy ledger of one crossing. Before: mover $m=m_A+m_B/P$, seam $e$. After: mover $t\le m_A$, residue $m_B/P$, seam $e'\le e-\mu$. The insertion energy does not increase, and the seam loses at least $\mu$.

Gap fill — interpolation over the finite field $K$. filled here Let $\Phi$ be the space of rational functions that are bounded at infinity and have poles of order at most $n_w$ at prescribed points $w$, and let $n=\sum_wn_w$. Multiplication by $\prod_w(z-z_w)^{n_w}$ embeds $\Phi$ into the polynomials of degree at most $n$. A nonzero polynomial of degree at most $n$ has at most $n$ roots, so evaluation at more than $n$ regular points of $K$ is injective on $\Phi$. The evaluations therefore span $\Phi^*$, and every Laurent or Taylor coefficient is a combination of them. This needs more than $n$ unoccupied points in $K$. §21.3 bounds $n$ by $O_D(T)$ and the number of occupied positions per cell by a polynomial in $D$; this is the reason for the condition "$|K|$ at least a sufficiently large polynomial in $T$" in Lemma 19.1. The paper notes that these bounds can be obtained first over the algebraic closure and then used to choose $K$. Because $z\mapsto z^P$ permutes $K$, a regular point on one sphere corresponds to a regular point of $K$ on the other. The paper fixes an ordering of $K$ and stores, for each allowed set of regular points, one reconstruction matrix in the table dictionary; the local control only names the selected point and checks that it is available.

The limit on crossings. paper A move is allowed at most $U=D^5$ crossings. When the limit is reached, the current mark of the mover becomes a collector. Each nonterminal fusion between two successive crossings removes one stationary insertion, and a completed move never releases a new mover, so the procedure terminates in every term.

21.3 Accounting for motion

Three kinds of bound are proved on pp. 82–83: on the energy stored in the final insertions, on the energy and the number of crossings in each cell, and on the wires created by crossings.

Initial energy. paper An original station of arity $t_i$ has initial weighted energy $O((1+t_i/T)^2H)$ (§20.1). The auxiliaries contribute at most $DH$ per occupied cell (§20.2). One reserve $H$ is allocated to each original movable station. Over all cells of all networks the total is

$$\begin{aligned}&O\Bigl(H\Bigl(D\,\#\{\text{occupied cells}\}+\#\{i\}+T^{-2}\sum_it_i^2\Bigr)\Bigr)\\&\quad=O(WH/T).\end{aligned}$$
Gap fill — the three terms. filled here Use $(1+t_i/T)^2\le2+2t_i^2/T^2$. The hypotheses of Lemma 19.1 give: at most $W/(DT)$ occupied cells, so $D\cdot\#\{\text{cells}\}\le W/T$; at most $100D$ stations per occupied cell, so $\#\{i\}\le100W/T$; and $\sum_it_i^2\le WT$, so $T^{-2}\sum t_i^2\le W/T$. In one cell, the stations have $\sum_i(1+t_i/T)^2\le200D+2(100D)^2$, because $\sum_it_i\le100DT$. With the auxiliaries and at most $100D$ reserves, the initial charge of a cell is $O(D^2H)$.

A potential that does not increase. paper Count energy at collectors with weight $1/(1+3g)$, and energy at all other insertions, and the reserve of the current move, with weight one. The paper asserts that this sum cannot increase at any of the three operations or when a move expires.

Gap fill — the four cases. filled here
  1. Continuing fusion ($j\lt0$ at a non-collector). By (21.1), $m_{\rm new}\lt m_0+m$. If the absorbed insertion was an original station that awaited its turn, its reserve is discarded.
  2. Crossing. The mover's energy $m$ is replaced by $t$ on the far side and $m_B/P$ at the residue, and $t+m_B/P\le m_A+m_B/P=m$. The reserve travels with the mover.
  3. Final fusion at a collector. The order is at most $gL$ (at the designated collector; negative at the other collectors). By (21.1) the collector gains at most $$\begin{aligned}m+\frac{gL}{P}&\le m+\frac{g(2P\mu+1)}{P}\\&\le m+3g\mu\le(1+3g)\mu ,\end{aligned}$$ using $L\le2P\mu+1$, $P\ge1$ and $\mu\ge H\ge1$. With weight $1/(1+3g)$ the gain counts at most $\mu$. The mover's energy $m$ and its reserve $H$, together $\mu$, leave the sum.
  4. Expiry. The mover's mark becomes a collector: its weight drops from one to $1/(1+3g)$ and its reserve is discarded.
Hence the total insertion energy in every resulting term is at most $(1+3g)$ times the initial total: it is $O_g(WH/T)$. In particular the collector energies sum to $O_g(WH/T)$.

The bound in each cell. paper A crossing brings into the far cell at most $t+H\le\mu$ of charge (energy plus reserve). By (21.2) the seam loses at least this much. A cell has at most $g$ seams, each of initial energy $O_g(D^2H)$ (§20.1), and a seam energy never increases. The charge that ever enters a cell is therefore $O_g(D^2H)$; with the initial charge $O(D^2H)$ and the factor $1+3g$ of case 3, the insertion energy of a cell is $O_g(D^2H)$ at every step. In particular every collector has energy $O_g(D^2H)$.

Counts. paper Each crossing lowers a seam energy by at least $H$, so a cell has $O_g(D^2)$ crossing incidences during the whole construction. New stationary residues arise only at these incidences, so a cell has $O_g(D^2)$ residues. A move expires only after $U=D^5$ crossings, so the number of expired moves over all cells is at most the total number of crossings divided by $U$, which is $O_g(D^2)\cdot\frac{W}{DT}\cdot D^{-5}$. With the at most $W/(DT)$ immobiles and one designated collector in each of the at most $W/(DT)$ occupied cells, the total number of collectors is $O(W/(DT))$.

The pole bound. paper By the pole bound after (21.1), the total pole order at the marks of a cell is at most $P$ times a sum of weighted energies. The cell bound $O_g(D^2H)$, the polynomial number of marks, the bound $H=Ra^2\le aT$, and the fact that $P$ is fixed give a total pole bound $O_D(T)$. The same holds for $gL$, since $L=\lceil2P\mu\rceil$ and $\mu=O_g(D^2H)$.

Routing the wires created by crossings. paper Each crossing creates one ordinary wire of $O_g(DT/\sqrt F+1)$ bits between the residue and the mover. One endpoint follows the mover. The other stays at the residue until another insertion absorbs the residue, and then travels with that insertion. Wires run only inside a cell or between adjacent cells, so a wire whose endpoints have moved apart is a chain of segments along the path of the moving endpoint. The paper counts: a move visits at most $U+1$ cells, counting repetitions; in each visit it collects at most $O_g(D^2)$ residue endpoints, and it creates at most $U$ of its own; hence it carries $O_g((U+1)D^2)$ endpoints along any one hop. The paper then states the bound $O_g((U+1)D^6)$ on local wire segments per cell, "using the bound on incident crossings and including terminations", and calls it looser. filled here The $O_g(D^2)$ crossing incidences of a cell, each with $O_g((U+1)D^2)$ endpoints, give $O_g((U+1)D^4)$ segments across the seams of the cell; this part of the count is this tutorial's.

Gap fill — the total width of the motion wires. filled here With $U=D^5$, a cell has $O_g(D^{11})$ segments, each of $O_g(DT/\sqrt F+1)$ bits. The total is $O_g(D^{12}T/\sqrt F)+O_D(1)$. Since $F\ge D^{1000(\ell+1)}$, $\sqrt F\ge D^{500(\ell+1)}$, and $D^{12-500(\ell+1)}\le D^{-2\ell-2}$. For large $D$ the constant $O_g(1)$ is at most $D$, so the total is $O(D^{-2\ell-1}T)+O_D(1)$ per cell, as the paper states. This estimate needs $\sqrt F\ge D^{2\ell+14}$; the definition of $F$ gives $\sqrt F\ge D^{500(\ell+1)}$.

Selector wires. paper The private-selector wires start at the marks that read them and at the stations that check the selectors and evaluate the sphere forms. Their endpoints follow the same paths. A cell starts with a number of such ports that is polynomial in $D$ and in the number $M$ of chunks, so the number of incident selector segments and copies is polynomial in $D$ and $M$; it does not depend on $b$ or on the selector rate $\epsilon$. After $M$ is fixed, $\epsilon$ is chosen so small that the total selector width, including the equality copies, is at most $D^{-2\ell}T$ per cell.

What §21 hands to §22.

Not verified — the segment count $O_g((U+1)D^6)$. not verified The paper (p. 83) derives the per-hop count $O_g((U+1)D^2)$ and then states the bound $O_g((U+1)D^6)$ on local wire segments per cell "using the bound on incident crossings and including terminations". This tutorial reconstructed the factor $D^4$ for segments across seams but did not reconstruct the paper's count of the segments that end inside a cell. The conclusion is not sensitive to the exponent: any fixed power of $D$ is absorbed by $\sqrt F\ge D^{500(\ell+1)}$.

22. Accounting for the new arities pp. 83–86

Role in the chain. This section receives the energy and wire bounds of §21.3, the storage bounds of §20.2, and the width formulas (19.1) and (20.2). It converts energies into arities, cuts each sphere form into tables of small arity, builds the common host, and counts the indices of the outer sum. It delivers the four conclusions of Lemma 19.1 and so completes the proof that began in §19. The lemma is then used in §25.1 (p. 96). The section has no numbered statement; its numbered display is (22.1). Its last paragraph is on p. 86.

What is left to do. After the motion, each collector has two homogeneous sphere legs and may also hold external bits or thin wires. Two objects are still too large for the core. First, a sphere form of a cell is one tensor that is connected to every insertion of the cell; its arity is the sum of all leg widths in the cell. Second, a seam can still have energy of order $D^2H$, that is, width of order $DT$ by (20.2), and conclusion 2 of Lemma 19.1 allows a core station only arity $D^{-\ell}T$. The paper divides the species into groups so that each group meets a small table, even when one group receives the entire energy of a seam.

Definition (chunks and chunk forms). pp. 83–84 paper Choose $M$, a power of $D$ specified below. $R$ and $R'$ are divisible by $M$ by (20.1). Divide the species of each sphere into $M$ consecutive equal chunks, preserving whole blocks in the private construction. Expand each remaining homogeneous leg as a direct sum over its tuple of chunk energies, using the prescribed tuple at each untouched auxiliary insertion and the same tuple on both sides of a seam. Every basis state occurs once in this expansion. For fixed chunk energies, the sphere form factors into the chunk forms, one on the species of each chunk. Reordering the generators contributes a single sign determined by the parity of each chunk at each site.
Gap fill — why the leg splits and the form factors. filled here By fact (i) of §19.1, the exterior algebra on the generators of $r$ species is the tensor product of the exterior algebras of the $M$ chunks, and energy is additive. So $$V^{(r)}_e=\bigoplus_{e_1+\cdots+e_M=e}\ \bigotimes_{j=1}^{M}V^{(r/M)}_{e_j},$$ and each wedge basis state lies in exactly one summand. The tuple $(e_j)$ at each leg becomes part of the index of the outer sum ("the final chunk splittings"). A seam is an equality of basis states, so both of its sides have the same tuple. Contractions between different species vanish (§19.1); once the generators are sorted by chunk, the Pfaffian is the product of the Pfaffians of the chunks. The sorting sign depends only on the lengths, modulo two, of the chunk parts at each site, and these are fixed by the chunk energies. Since $R$ is a multiple of $bM$, a chunk of $A$ has $R/M$ species, which is $R/(bM)$ whole blocks, and the matching chunk of $B$ has $R'/M$ species.

Consider a generic leg of energy $e$ on $r$ species, with energy $e_j$ in chunk $j$, $\sum_je_j=e$. The paper proves that the total coded width satisfies

$$\sum_{j=1}^{M}\Bigl\lceil\frac{c_0}{\ln2}\sqrt{(r/M)\,e_j}\Bigr\rceil\le\frac{c_0}{\ln2}\sqrt{re}+M .\tag{22.1}$$
Gap fill — proof of (22.1). filled here By (19.1), chunk $j$ of the leg has at most $\frac{c_0}{\ln2}\sqrt{(r/M)e_j}$ bits before rounding. Each ceiling adds less than one, in total less than $M$. By Cauchy–Schwarz, $\sum_{j=1}^M\sqrt{e_j}\le\sqrt{M\sum_je_j}=\sqrt{Me}$, so $\sum_j\sqrt{(r/M)e_j}\le\sqrt{r/M}\cdot\sqrt{Me}=\sqrt{re}$. The paper adds that a layout can use the integer width $\lceil4\sqrt{(r/M)e_j}\rceil$ instead; since $c_0/\ln2\approx2.617\lt4$ this is a valid width, the same bound holds with coefficient four, and $4\sqrt{(r/M)e_j}=\sqrt{8(r/M)(2e_j)}$ is the square root of an integer, which an integer computation can round.
AHA. Cutting the species into $M$ chunks divides the width that any one table sees by $\sqrt M$: a chunk has $r/M$ species, so even if it receives the whole energy $e$ its width is at most $\frac{c_0}{\ln 2}\sqrt{re/M}$. The sum of the widths over the chunks does not grow by a matching factor; by (22.1) it grows by an additive $M$ only, because the chunks share one energy. This is the square-root law (19.1) used a second time.

Auxiliary legs. paper An untouched auxiliary leg does not use the generic bound. It uses the actual product subspace named by its selector. Within a chunk, the number of bits is read from the counts of block types and block energies in the histogram, since a block of type $(d_i)_i$ with rounded entropy bounds $u_i$ needs at most $O(bu_i)$ bits on leg $i$ (§20.2). Under a fixed selector these codes inject into the corresponding tensor product of homogeneous spaces. The table of the chunk form decodes them and gives weight zero to unused words.

The chunk tables are small. paper One arbitrary table implements the form at each cell and chunk on $A$, and separately on $B$. The paper bounds the total arity of such a table by $D^{c_1}T/\sqrt M+O_D(1)$, before the selector wires are attached, with $c_1$ independent of $M$, $b$ and the selector rate. $M$ is then taken to be a power of $D$ so large that this is below $D^{-\ell}T/10$.

Gap fill — the arity of one chunk table. filled here A cell has a number of legs that is polynomial in $D$: collectors, at most $g$ seams, the $m$ auxiliary marks, and $O_g(D^2)$ residues. For a generic port on $A$ in one chunk, (22.1) with a single term and $e\le O_g(D^2H)$ gives $$\begin{aligned}\frac{c_0}{\ln2}\sqrt{\frac RM\,e}+1&=O_g\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{R\,D^2Ra^2}{M}}\Bigr)+1\\&=O_g\Bigl(\frac{DRa}{\sqrt M}\Bigr)+1\le O_g\Bigl(\frac{DT}{\sqrt M}\Bigr)+1 .\end{aligned}$$ On $B$ the same computation with $R'$ species and energy at most $P\cdot O_g(D^2H)$ gives the smaller bound $O_g(DT/\sqrt{FM})+1$. An auxiliary leg has $R/(bM)$ blocks in the chunk and $O(bu_i)\le O(b)$ bits per block, hence $O(R/M+1)\le O(T/(aM))+1$ bits. The sum over the polynomially many legs is at most $D^{c_1}T/\sqrt M+O_D(1)$. The exponent $c_1$ depends on $g,\ell$ and the characteristic (through $m$), and not on $M$. If $M\ge400D^{2(c_1+\ell)}$, the first term is at most $D^{-\ell}T/20$, and the term $O_D(1)$ is at most $D^{-\ell}T/20$ for large $T$.

The order of the choices. paper The paper fixes the parameters in this order, all before $T$ grows (pp. 80 and 84).

OrderParameterChosen so that
1$D$, then $F$ and $a=F^3$the estimates "for sufficiently large $D$" hold (auxiliary energy at most $DH$; wire widths)
2$M$, a power of $D$each chunk table has arity below $D^{-\ell}T/10$
3the selector rate $\epsilon$selector wires, with copies, are at most $D^{-2\ell}T$ per cell (§21.3, with this $M$)
4the block size $b$(20.4) holds and the selector is shorter than $\epsilon T$
5$T$all rounding terms $O_D(1)$, $O(M)$ are negligible; $R\ge T/(2a)$

Frozen stations: conclusion 1

The collectors become the frozen stations. The paper's argument (p. 84) has three charges for the arity of a collector: its two sphere legs, its external bits, and the additional thin wires.

Sphere legs. paper At a collector of weighted energy $e$, the two sphere legs have total width $O(T\sqrt{e/H})+O(M)$, by (20.2) and (22.1). §21.3 gives $\sum e=O(WH/T)$ over all collectors and $e=O(D^2H)$ at each one. filled here Hence $$\sum_{\rm collectors}\bigl(T\sqrt{e/H}\bigr)^2=\frac{T^2}{H}\sum e=O(WT),\qquad\max\,T\sqrt{e/H}=O(DT).$$ The unit $H$ cancels: the bounds depend only on the ratios $e/H$.

External bits. paper Every immobile station keeps its own external ports. Its external width is at most its input arity, so the squares of the external widths sum to at most $\sum_it_i^2\le WT$, and each is at most $100DT$.

Thin wires. paper All additional wires from motion and selectors together have total width $O(D^{-2\ell}T)$ per cell (§21.3). Even if all of them end at the same collector, their squared width is $O(D^{-4\ell}T^2)$ per cell. filled here Over at most $W/(DT)$ cells this is $O(WT\,D^{-4\ell-1})$.

Rounding. paper The additive terms $O(M)$ come from a number of chunks that is constant once $D$ is fixed. filled here Their squares sum to $O(M^2)\cdot O(W/(DT))$, which is at most $WT$ once $T^2\ge M^2$.

Combination. paper By $(x+y+z)^2\le3(x^2+y^2+z^2)$ the squared arities of the frozen stations sum to $O(WT)$, and the maximum arity is $O(DT)$. There are $O(W/(DT))$ collectors (§21.3), and the immobile stations are among them, in their original cells. Cauchy–Schwarz gives $$\sum_{\rm frozen}t_i\le\Bigl(O\Bigl(\frac W{DT}\Bigr)\,O(WT)\Bigr)^{1/2}=O\bigl(W/\sqrt D\bigr).$$ This is conclusion 1 of Lemma 19.1.∎

Core stations: conclusions 2 and 3

The core consists of the chunk tables, the untouched auxiliary insertions, the residues, the tables that check selectors, the identity tables on thin wires, and scalar tables that hold coefficients. The paper first bounds the total arity before the host degree is controlled (pp. 84–85).

ContributionWidth per cellSource
untouched auxiliary insertions, both legs, all chunks$O(DT(1+\log D))$storage bound of §20.2
seam ports of the chunk tables, at most $g$ seams$O_g(DT)$(22.1) summed over chunks, $e=O_g(D^2H)$
$O_g(D^2)$ residual private legseach $O_g(DT/\sqrt F)+O(M)$; together $O(T)$(20.2) and (22.1)
legs paired with collectorsat most the global frozen arity $O(W/\sqrt D)$conclusion 1
motion wires, selector wires, scalar tables$O(T)$§21.3

Total arity before the host is built. filled here Multiply the per-cell widths by the number of occupied cells, at most $W/(DT)$: the auxiliaries give $O(W(1+\log D))$, the seams $O_g(W)$, the residues and the wires $O(W/D)$; the legs paired with collectors add at most the frozen total $O(W/\sqrt D)$. Each width is counted at both of its ends, which doubles the constant. paper The core arity at this stage is $O(W(1+\log D))$.

Individual arities. paper An untouched auxiliary has arity $O(T/a)$, a residue $O(DT/\sqrt F)$, and the chunk tables and routing wires are below $D^{-\ell}T$ by a fixed margin. filled here Since $a=F^3$ and $F\ge D^{1000(\ell+1)}$, $T/a\le D^{-3000(\ell+1)}T$ and $DT/\sqrt F\le D^{1-500(\ell+1)}T$; with their $O$-constants both are below $D^{-\ell}T$ for large $D$.

The common host. paper Reserve slots in each cell for each kind of table and bundle that can occur simultaneously in a single term; $D^{O(1)}$ slots per cell suffice. The block size $b$ changes table entries and selector lengths, but the same slots serve every block type. Scalar factors are combined in their cell. Initially all connections between slots in the same cell or in neighboring cells are allowed. A direct connection between two collectors is split by a core identity table on the thin wire that runs between them, so no two frozen stations touch by a wire.

Bounded degree. paper For each core station, attach a fixed balanced binary tree with one leaf per possible bundle. The root receives the ordered ports of the table, and each internal vertex separates its list of bits into the sublists for its two children. The equality tables at the tree vertices pass each bit along a unique path of length $O(1+\log D)$, and no internal arity exceeds twice the arity of the original station. filled here A core station can have bundles to $D^{O(1)}$ slots, so its tree has $D^{O(1)}$ leaves and depth $O(1+\log D)$. Every tree vertex has at most three neighbors, so the host induced on the core positions has degree bounded by an absolute constant. Each bit of a core station is now also counted at $O(1+\log D)$ identity tables. This multiplies the total core arity by $O(1+\log D)$ and gives the bound $O(W(1+\log D)^2)$ of conclusion 2; the square of the logarithm in the statement of Lemma 19.1 has this origin. The margin $1/10$ in the choice of $M$ keeps twice the arity of a chunk table, plus its selector wires, below $D^{-\ell}T$.

Minimum bundle width. paper Take $c$ larger than the exponents in these counts by a margin that depends on $\ell$. Padding each nonempty bundle to $\lceil T/D^c\rceil$ bits, or charging each station at least this arity, then uses $O(D^{-2\ell}T)$ extra bits per cell. filled here A cell has $D^{O(1)}$ bundles and stations, so the extra bits number $D^{O(1)}(T/D^c+1)$. paper The padded bits are pinned to zero, and a unique zero padding preserves the tensor. The padding also absorbs any bounded number per cell of weight-only tables. This proves conclusions 1–3 of Lemma 19.1.∎

The network of one term, in words. In each occupied cell there are $2M$ chunk tables, $M$ for the sphere $A$ and $M$ for $B$. Each chunk table has one port for each mark of the cell. The chunk tables of $A$ in adjacent cells are joined by the seam bundles, chunk by chunk. Each mark carries an insertion table: a collector (frozen), an untouched auxiliary insertion, or a residue (both core). Thin wires carry residue indices and selector bits. Every core station reaches its neighbors through its binary tree of identity tables.

Indices of the outer sum: conclusion 4

Conclusion 4 concerns the description of the terms and not the tensor identity. The paper argues it in prose on pp. 85–86. The walk below lists its assertions in order.

The index of a term. paper A bundle is encoded by its endpoint slots, its length, and its position in the ordered ports at its ends. No address of an individual exterior bit is needed, because a bundle is carried as an ordered list. The branching data at one occupied cell are: the initial chunk histograms (§20.2); the motion and fusion choices, namely positions, energies, interpolation points and orders (§21); and the final chunk splittings (this section). For fixed $D$ the number of entries is bounded, including the size of the alphabet of the histograms. Each integer ranges over $T^{O_D(1)}$, and a field point uses $O_D(\log T)$ bits because $[K:k]=O_D(\log T)$. This gives $O_D(\log(T+1))$ control bits per occupied cell. The private selector bits are wires inside the term and are not part of the index. Unused entries are set to zero, which makes the padding of an index unique.

The schedule. paper Use the supplied coloring of the $(4D^5+20)$-th power of the host; greedy coloring gives one with $O_D(1)$ colors for fixed $g$. Motions are started successively by color and station slot. Concurrent motions affect disjoint neighborhoods, since each has at most $D^5$ crossings. A complete padded schedule has length bounded in terms of $D,g$ only. filled here The power graph has maximum degree at most $g^{4D^5+21}$, so the greedy algorithm uses at most that many colors plus one. A move with at most $D^5$ crossings stays within host distance $D^5$ of its starting cell.

Labels. paper In each step the cells may send their local descriptions to their neighbors. A merger concatenates the descriptions of its two input tables and the operation parameters. A crossing adds the named interpolation, the fusion, and the new endpoints of its thin wire. The history of any resulting table draws on a number of these records that is bounded in terms of $D$. A label of length $O_D(A+\log(T+1))$ therefore specifies each entry as a function of its ports through the fixed dictionary. The dictionary may use arbitrary interpolation weights, restriction maps and internal contractions; generating labels requires no evaluation of entries.

No inspection of selector bits. paper Counts in a chunk histogram fix the energies and coded widths. Range and total checks admit every realizable histogram; the selector check, which is a table inside the network, gives zero whenever an admitted histogram is unrealizable. Interpolation may use a fixed ordered collection of all regular field points, with weights indexed by the local pole bounds. The scheduler only checks avoidance of marks, the ranges of the indices, and the rules of fusion and motion. Zero coefficients may be retained.

Cost. paper The checks and length estimates use arithmetic on integers or field coordinates of $O_D(\log(T+1))$ bits, including division with remainder and rounded square roots (the integer widths after (22.1)). The scheduler copies old labels as strings in fixed padded slots. At each cell the number of records and of operations on them is bounded independently of $T$. This gives $O_D\bigl(A+(1+\log T)^{O(1)}\bigr)$ bit operations and bits communicated per occupied cell, and proves conclusion 4.∎

Not verified — conclusion 4 of Lemma 19.1. not verified The paper (pp. 85–86) describes the scheduler and the label dictionary in prose and does not give them as an algorithm with a stated data format. This tutorial checked the count of control bits and the existence of the coloring. It did not verify the bound $O_D(A+(1+\log T)^{O(1)})$ on bit operations, the label length $O_D(A+\log(T+1))$, or the claim that the history of each table uses a bounded number of records. It also did not determine why the coloring radius is $4D^5+20$: two moves of at most $D^5$ crossings whose starting cells are more than about $2D^5$ apart already visit disjoint cells, and the paper does not say what the additional margin covers (moved wire endpoints are a candidate).

Forward. In §25.1 the paper applies Lemma 19.1 with $\ell=10$ and $W=C_1L$, where $L$ is the total arity budget of a round (not the integer $L$ of §21). The outputs quoted there are: frozen charges of total $Z=O(L/\sqrt D)$, square sum $O(LT)$ and maximum $O(DT)$; core stations of arity at most $D^{-10}T$ and total arity $O(L(\log D)^{O(1)})$; and internal bundles of width at least $T/D^{O(1)}$. The square-sum bound is the hypothesis of the next application of the lemma, which is why the paper tracks $\sum t_i^2$ and not only $\sum t_i$.

23. Reducing the total arity of a tensor power pp. 86–88

Role in the chain. Part IV of the paper (§23–§27, pp. 86–107) proves Theorem 2.1 (Compression of tensor powers, p. 7), the statement that §2 combines with Theorem 2.2 to contradict $\omega_k\lt 9/4$. Part IV receives Lemma 19.1 (Local concentration, p. 73; §19), Theorem 16.1 (Conditional stable sorting, p. 59) and Lemma 16.2 (Batched table evaluation, p. 59; §16). Section 23 states Lemma 23.1 (Compression of a tensor power), defines the objects on which its proof operates, and constructs the starting configuration. The proof of Lemma 23.1 continues through §24 (one wire reduction), §25 (one round, and the iteration of rounds) and ends on p. 106 in §26.2. Lemma 23.1 is used $J$ times in §27 to prove Theorem 2.1.

The paper describes the task of Part IV on p. 5: the expansion produced by the tensor identities "has many summands with different wirings. A single network must describe the possible wirings, route the records, look up the entries and sum the contributions. Counting all four operations gives the logarithmic saving in Theorem 2.1 and completes the contradiction." Sections 23–25 bound each summand. Section 26 puts all summands on one network. Section 27 does the final count.

Definition (bit tensor, network, trivalent, size, volume; table network, bundle, label). pp. 7, 86 paper A bit tensor of arity $u$ over a field is a function $\{0,1\}^u\to$ field. In a tensor network each vertex carries such a table. A wire pairs two argument positions; each unpaired position is a named exterior bit. For a fixed setting of the exterior bits, multiply the selected entries of all vertices and sum over all settings of the wires. The resulting function of the exterior bits is the tensor represented by the network. A network is trivalent if every vertex has arity at most three. For a trivalent network, size counts the vertices and the exterior bits, and volume counts the vertices and all wires including the exterior ones (p. 7).

For unrestricted arity the paper uses the following terms (p. 86). A table network over a field $K$ has bit wires and a table $\{0,1\}^s\to K$ at each station of arity $s$. Its size is the number of stations plus the sum of their arities, including external legs. Parallel bits between two stations form an ordered bundle; the width of a bundle is its number of bits. A label names the table of a station in a dictionary fixed for the network: a rule that assigns a table to each label.

Background — why the three size measures agree up to a constant for trivalent networks. filled here Let a trivalent network have $N$ vertices, $E$ internal wires and $e$ exterior bits. Each internal wire uses two argument positions and each exterior bit one, so $2E+e\le 3N$.
  • Size is $N+e$. Volume is $N+E+e\le N+\tfrac12(3N-e)+e\le\tfrac52(N+e)$. Hence size $\le$ volume $\le\tfrac52\,$size.
  • The table-network size is $N+(2E+e)$. It is at least $N+e$ and at most $4N$. Hence trivalent size $\le$ table-network size $\le 4\,$(trivalent size).
A bound on any one of the three quantities therefore gives a bound on the others with an absolute constant. Theorem 2.2 is stated for volume and Theorem 2.1 for size; §27 starts the macros from the table-network size $s_0\le 4m$.

Why the total arity must fall

Lemma 19.1 turns a network into a sum of networks, each with a few large stations (called frozen) and many small stations (the core). The paper observes (p. 86) that this is not sufficient: the cost of the final evaluation also depends on the total arity. If $u_v$ is the arity of station $v$, the batched evaluation of Lemma 16.2 costs, per copy of the tensor, at most a constant times the number of stations plus

$$\sum_{v:\,u_v\gt 0}u_v^{\,p}\ \le\ \Bigl(\max_vu_v\Bigr)^{p-1}\sum_vu_v .$$

Here $1\lt p\lt 2$ is the exponent of Theorem 16.1(1). The inequality is $u_v^p=u_v^{p-1}u_v\le(\max u)^{p-1}u_v$, summed over $v$. The right side is a product of two factors: a power less than one of the largest arity, and the total arity. The paper's plan is to let the largest arity grow by a factor $h^{(1+\delta)a}$ if the total arity falls by a factor $h^{(1-\delta)a}$. The product then changes by $h^{(1+\delta)a(p-1)-(1-\delta)a}$, and this exponent is negative when $\delta$ is small, because $p-1\lt 1$.

The quantity that controls the trade is the sum of squared arities. Lemma 19.1 takes a bound on this sum as its hypothesis and returns a bound of the same order. The construction keeps the sum of squares within a budget in every round, so that Lemma 19.1 can be applied again.

Lemma 23.1 (Compression of a tensor power). p. 86 paper Let $k$ be a finite field with $\omega_k\lt 9/4$. Let $h=\lceil\log_2n\rceil$, $0\lt\delta\le 1/10$, and $0\lt\beta\le b\le 1/2$. For every sufficiently small fixed $a\gt 0$ depending only on $k,\delta,\beta$, and all sufficiently large $n$, the following holds. Work over an extension $K/k$ of degree $O(\log h)$ large enough for Lemma 19.1 at the scales used below. Suppose that an input table network has maximum arity $h^b$ or less, $(\log h)^{O(1)}$-bit table labels, size at most $m$ with $n\le m\le n^{1.1}$, and $e\le m\,h^{-2a}$ external bits. Some $d$ with $1\le d=n^{o(1)}$ admits a table network over $K$ for its $d$th tensor power, with all $de$ external bits separately accessible, such that $$\text{size}\le m\,d\,h^{-(1-\delta)a},\qquad\text{maximum arity}\le h^{\,b+(1+\delta)a}.$$ The output labels again have $(\log h)^{O(1)}$ bits. The cutoff for $n$ may depend on the fixed exponent in the input label-length bound.

The first sentence of the box (the hypothesis on $k$) is stated by the paper for the whole of Part IV on p. 86, not inside the lemma. The paper calls one application of Lemma 23.1 a macro. The output of a macro satisfies the hypotheses of the next macro with $b$ replaced by $b+(1+\delta)a$, as long as this exponent is at most $1/2$ and the exterior-bit condition holds; §27 arranges both.

Gap fill — the effect of one macro on the lookup bound. filled here The paper states (p. 87): before the macro the lookup bound is $O(m\,h^{b(p-1)})$ per copy, and after it the bound acquires the factor $h^{-a\{(1-\delta)-(1+\delta)(p-1)\}}$. The computation: by the display above with $\max u\le h^b$ and total size $m$, the bound before is $O(m\,h^{b(p-1)})$. After the macro, the size per copy is at most $m\,h^{-(1-\delta)a}$ and the largest arity is at most $h^{b+(1+\delta)a}$, so the bound is $$O\bigl(m\,h^{-(1-\delta)a}\cdot h^{(b+(1+\delta)a)(p-1)}\bigr)=O\bigl(m\,h^{b(p-1)}\bigr)\cdot h^{-a\{(1-\delta)-(1+\delta)(p-1)\}}.$$ The brace is positive if and only if $\delta\lt(2-p)/p$. For $p\ge 2$ it is negative for every $\delta\gt 0$.

The structure of the proof of Lemma 23.1

The macro replaces a power of the input tensor by a sum of products. The paper's vocabulary for this expansion is used in all of §23–§26.

Definition (summand, block, host, occurrence, grouping step, round, beat). p. 87 paper
  • A summand is one term of the expansion. It is a product of factors called blocks. Different blocks have disjoint lists of external bits.
  • In one summand, its $N_b$ blocks use the same simple graph of possible adjacencies, the host. The vertices of the host are positions; $H$ is their number. The host may depend on the summand.
  • An occurrence is a pair (host position, block). It can be empty. Every actual internal wire joins stations in the same block. A host edge can carry bundles of different widths in different blocks. The local port order at each end of a wire records which bits correspond.
  • A grouping step relabels the blocks and groups them $r$ at a time, with $r$ a power of two. In each group the old blocks are multiplied and transformed together, and the group becomes one new block.
  • A round has three phases: it concentrates the squared arities (Lemma 19.1), then applies a fixed number of wire reductions to the core, then restores bounded degree of the host. A wire reduction is called a beat; a beat consists of several contractions (subrounds) followed by a reset of the core degree (§24).
The number of rounds grows with $h$. Once the parameter $D$ is fixed, all repetition counts within one round are constants.

Table 1 of the paper (p. 87) lists the quantities. It is reproduced here with one added column that says where each quantity is fixed.

QuantityMeaning within one summand (paper)Where fixed (this tutorial)
$H,\ N_b$positions of the common host and blocks using itchange at every grouping step, (23.1)
$r$number of blocks combined at each grouping step§25.2
$S,\ T_0$initial blocks and original copies per block, with $d=ST_0$$T_0$ in §23.1; $S$ in §25.2
$L,\ T$total arity budget and working arity scale(23.3) at the start; (25.3) after each round
$D$concentration parameter, fixed for all rounds§25.1
$X,\ Z$width between core stations and total frozen charge§24
$\Delta,\ \nabla$degree bounds for the core host and the full host§24
$B,\ u$maximum packing load and unit of internal width§24, §25.1

The paper's caption adds: the budgets $L$, $X$ and $Z$ each cover all blocks of the summand.

The proof of Lemma 23.1 has four stages. The bounds of stages 1–3 hold for each summand separately.

  1. Initialization (§23.1). The input network is given bounded degree by binary trees. $T_0\approx h^b$ copies are stacked so that every station has arity between $T_0/2$ and $T_0$. $S$ such stacks are the initial blocks. The invariants (23.2) hold with $L=L_0$ and $T=T_0$.
  2. One round (§25.1, using Lemma 19.1 once and Lemma 24.1 a constant number of times). If (23.2) holds with $(L,T)$ at the start of a round, it holds with $(L/G,\ qT)$ at the end, where $G$ and $q\le G^{1+\delta/3}$ are constants.
  3. Iteration (§25.2). After $j\approx a\log h/\log G$ rounds the total charged arity is $O(md\,h^{-a}\log h)$ and the arity scale is $O(h^{b+(1+\delta/3)a})$.
  4. Assembly (§26). The sum over all summands is represented by one fixed network of size at most $md\,h^{-(1-\delta)a}$ whose large tables are dictionary gates of arity at most $h^{b+(1+\delta)a}$.
