23.1 The common graph and its initial loads pp. 87–88

Grouping and the host. At a grouping step the new positions may be indexed by an old position, a dyadic subinterval of the $r$ block indices of a group, and a bounded number of other choices. A dyadic interval of $\{0,\dots,r-1\}$ is a set of indices that share a binary prefix; there are $2r-1$ of them. The paper allows padding of the host subject to

$$H'\le c\,r\,H,\qquad N_b'=N_b/r,\tag{23.1}$$

where $c$ is fixed throughout all repetitions of that step and may depend on the design of the round. Consequently the number $HN_b$ of allocated occurrences grows by at most the factor $c$ at each grouping step. This is used in §25.2, display (25.4).

The invariants of a round. At the beginning of each round the paper requires: every live occurrence holds one station (the paper does not define live; the tutorial reads it as: not empty); the host has degree bounded by an absolute constant; external bits are fixed at specified ports. Let $T$ be a dyadic width (a power of two) and $L$ a total arity budget. The charged arity of a live station of actual arity $s_v$ is $z_v=\max(s_v,\theta T)$, where $\theta\gt 0$ is a small fixed dyadic constant chosen in §25.1. The invariants are

$$\sum_vz_v\le L,\qquad\sum_vz_v^2\le LT,\qquad\max_vz_v\le DT/2.\tag{23.2}$$

All three sums and the maximum run over all stations of all blocks of the summand. The paper gives the purpose of each bound (p. 88):

  • the first pays for the surviving bits;
  • the second is the input to the next local concentration (it is the hypothesis $\sum t_i^2\le WT$ of Lemma 19.1);
  • the third lets several stations be packed into one cell of capacity $DT$ without overflow;
  • the floor $\theta T$ bounds the number of stations, and so of table descriptions, that can enter a pack of a given charge.

Every station of arity zero is a scalar. It is removed from the network and its value is kept as a factor of the summand.

Gap fill — what the pair $(L,T)$ measures. filled here If all charged arities were equal to one value $z$, the first two bounds of (23.2) would read $Nz\le L$ and $Nz^2\le LT$ for $N$ stations, and they hold with equality for $z=T$, $N=L/T$. So $T$ is the typical arity and $L/T$ the typical number of stations. In general the two bounds give $\sum z_v^2/\sum z_v\le T$ when the first is tight: $T$ bounds the arity-weighted mean arity. A later configuration with budget $L/G$ and scale $qT$ has square budget $(L/G)(qT)=(q/G)\,LT$. If $q$ is only slightly larger than $G$, the square budget is almost unchanged while the total arity has fallen by $G$ and the typical arity has risen by $q$.

First step: bounded degree by binary trees. A station of arity $s$ may be joined to $s$ different stations, so the input graph can have degree $h^b$. The paper replaces each station of positive arity $s$ as follows. The $s$ ports of the station leave it as one ordered bundle to the root of a balanced binary tree. At each split an identity tensor divides one ordered bundle into two bundles whose lengths differ by at most one. At each leaf there is an identity tensor on one bit. The original wires and external bits are attached at the leaves. An input wire that was a loop at one station becomes a connection between two leaves of the same tree.

Background — a balanced binary tree of equality tensors. filled here The identity (or equality) tensor that splits a bundle of $t$ bits is the $0/1$ table on $t+\lceil t/2\rceil+\lfloor t/2\rfloor=2t$ bits which is $1$ exactly when the first $t$ bits equal the concatenation of the other two groups. Three properties are used.
  1. The tensor is unchanged. In the sum over wire values, each identity tensor is zero unless its two sides agree. For given values at the leaves there is exactly one assignment of the tree wires with nonzero weight, and its weight is the original table entry. The network with trees represents the same tensor as before.
  2. Degree and arity ratio. The original station now has one neighbour (the root). A split vertex has three neighbours, a leaf two. A vertex that splits a $t$-bit bundle has arity $2t$ and its children have arity about $t$; the paper records that the arities at the two ends of a tree edge have bounded ratio. A leaf-to-leaf connection has width one at both ends.
  3. Cost. A tree on $s$ ports has $\lceil\log_2s\rceil+1$ levels, and each level has total arity at most $2s$. With $s\le h^b\le h^{1/2}$ there are $O(\log(2+h))$ levels. Summing over stations, the new network has total arity $O(m\log(2+h))$. The new tables are identities described by their lengths, so their labels are short.

Second step: stacking $T_0$ copies. Choose a dyadic $T_0\in[4h^b,8h^b]$ and take $T_0$ copies of the tree network constructed in the first step. At a station of arity $s$ (every arity is now at most $2h^b\le T_0/2$) let $v$ be the largest power of two with $v\le T_0/s$. Divide the $T_0$ copies into consecutive intervals of length $v$. Each interval becomes one position of the host, and the station at that position is the tensor product of the $v$ corresponding copies of the table.

Gap fill — the arithmetic of stacking. filled here
  • Arity. The product of $v$ copies has arity $sv$. From $v\le T_0/s\lt 2v$ follows $sv\in(T_0/2,\,T_0]$.
  • Number of positions. A station of arity $s$ gives $T_0/v\lt 2s$ intervals. Summing over the stations of the tree network, the stack has $H_0=O(m\log(2+h))$ positions.
  • Bounded degree. If two stations of arities $s,s'$ are adjacent, then $s/s'$ is bounded (property 2 of the previous box, and width one at both ends of a leaf-to-leaf edge). Then $v/v'$ is bounded, and an interval of length $v$ at one end meets at most $\max(1,v/v')$ intervals at the other end. The stack therefore has bounded degree.
  • Bit order. A bundle between two interval stations is ordered first by the old bundle order and then by the copy index. Both ends use this order.
  • Labels. The table at an interval is specified by the old table label, the interval length and a bounded number of ordered port lengths.
The stacking is the reason for the factor $T_0$ in $d$: one position of the host now serves $v$ copies, and every station has arity of order $T_0$ whatever its original arity was.

Third step: $S$ blocks. Take $S$ blocks, each a copy of this stack, and pad $H_0$ to the order $m\log(2+h)$. The represented tensor is the $d$th power of the input tensor with $d=T_0S$, up to the scalar stations removed earlier; the $d$th power of the product of those scalars is kept as a factor of the final tensor. The starting parameters are

$$d=T_0S,\qquad T=T_0,\qquad L=L_0=C\,m\,T_0\,S\log(2+h).\tag{23.3}$$
Gap fill — the invariants (23.2) hold at the start. filled here One copy of the tree network has total arity $O(m\log(2+h))$. Stacking multiplies the total by $T_0$ and taking $S$ blocks by $S$, so the total arity is at most $L_0$ for a sufficiently large absolute $C$. Every station has arity $s_v\in(T_0/2,T_0]$; with $\theta\le 1/2$ the charged arity is $z_v=s_v$. Then $\sum z_v\le L_0$; $\ \sum z_v^2\le T_0\sum z_v\le L_0T_0$; and $\max z_v\le T_0\le DT_0/2$ when $D\ge 2$. These are the three bounds of (23.2) with $L=L_0$, $T=T_0$. The host has bounded degree and every occurrence holds one station.

The $ed$ exterior bits keep their individual names throughout the construction. The number $S$ of blocks and the group size $r$ are chosen in §25.2, after the design of one round is fixed.

24. Reducing the width between unfrozen stations pp. 88–96

Role in the chain. This section proves Lemma 24.1 (Reduction of core width): one fixed sequence of steps, called a beat, halves the total width of the wires between the small stations of a summand, up to an additive error, and returns a host whose small stations have degree at most three. It receives the division of a summand into frozen stations and core stations from Lemma 19.1 (as set up in §25.1), the wide-record bound of Theorem 16.1(2) (§16) for a permutation network, and from §3 the geometry of prices together with two results that the paper imports from [42]. It delivers three things: Lemma 24.1, applied $J=\lceil 2\epsilon\log D\rceil$ times in every round in §25.1; the dyadic partition estimate (24.1), reused in §25.1 to form the cells of Lemma 19.1; and Lemma 24.2 (A fixed choice of widths), used inside the beat. The section is part of the proof of Lemma 23.1 (§23).

Notation in force (from §23): a summand is a product of $N_b$ blocks; all blocks of a summand share a host graph with $H$ positions; an occurrence is a pair (position, block); a grouping step merges the blocks $r$ at a time ($r$ a power of two), so that $N_b$ becomes $N_b/r$ and $H$ becomes at most $crH$. The field $k$ is finite with $\omega_k\lt 9/4$, and logarithms are binary. Several letters of this section have other meanings elsewhere: here $\rho$ is the contraction factor of a subround, $\Delta$ a degree bound, $u$ a unit of width, $P$ a length ratio, $b$ the width of the wiring identity, and $A,B,C,D,M,N'$ are, in §24.1 only, the names of six parties.

Definition (core, frozen, tether, the widths $X$ and $Z$, loads, unit, degree bounds). pp. 88–89 paper After the concentration step of a round, some stations are frozen for the rest of the round; the others form the core. Each core occurrence carries one tensor. Each frozen occurrence holds a list of tensors and the charge assigned to each when it was frozen. All exterior legs are on frozen tensors. Host adjacencies between frozen stations are discarded, since they carry no wires.
  • $X$ is the sum, over all blocks, of the widths of the core–core wires, each wire counted once.
  • $Z$ is the total frozen charge.
  • A tether is a core–frozen wire. The tethers have total width at most $Z$.
  • For packing, the load of a core occurrence is its arity; the load of a frozen occurrence is the sum of its charges times a fixed dyadic factor in $(0,1]$. $B$ is an upper bound on one load. $\mathcal M$ is the sum of all loads; $\mathcal M\le 2X+2Z$.
  • Each internal width is a multiple of an integer unit $u\le B$.
  • $\Delta\ge 3$ bounds the core degree of the host (core neighbours of a core position), and $\nabla\ge\Delta$ bounds its full degree.
Gap fill — $\mathcal M\le 2X+2Z$, and why $\nabla$ can be much larger than $\Delta$. filled here The arity of a core station is the sum of the widths of its core–core wires and of its tethers. Summed over the core, each core–core wire is counted at both ends, which gives $2X$, and the tethers give at most $Z$. The frozen loads sum to at most $Z$ because the dyadic factor is at most one. Hence $\mathcal M\le 2X+Z+Z$. The tether bound holds because a tether ends at a frozen station whose arity is at most its charge.

The host records possible contacts across all blocks. A core station can be tethered to different frozen stations in different blocks, so a core position of core degree three can have many potential tether neighbours in the common host. The estimates of Lemma 24.1 are uniform in this number.

Lemma 24.1 (Reduction of core width). p. 89 paper Fix a bound on the incoming core degree and assume the hypothesis of Theorem 16.1. There is a fixed sequence of grouping and contraction steps, called a beat, with the following bounds in each summand, for slack $\sigma\gt 0$: $$X_{\mathrm{out}}\le\tfrac12X+C(Z+\sigma),\qquad\Delta_{\mathrm{out}}\le 3,\qquad Z_{\mathrm{out}}=Z.$$ The identity retains all exterior legs separately. The maximum load, the reciprocal bundle unit and $\log(2\nabla)$ increase by at most fixed factors. These factors and $C$ are independent of the incoming full degree.

At a step with $H$ host positions and $N_b$ blocks, a power of two, choose dyadic $r\mid N_b$. Grouping leaves $N_b/r$ blocks and uses at most $crH$ positions. It suffices to take $r\ge c_*$, $c_*HN_bB/r\le\sigma$ at each step and a starting unit divisible by the product of the subdivision factors. The position and slack constants $c,c_*$ may depend on the degree bounds at the step in question.

The "hypothesis of Theorem 16.1" is $\omega_k\lt 9/4$. The slack $\sigma$ is a free parameter: the error term $C\sigma$ pays for partially filled packs, and the condition $c_*HN_bB/r\le\sigma$ says that one load $B$ per position and group, times a constant, is within the slack. In §25.1 the paper takes $\sigma=L/\sqrt D$.

Plan of the proof. A beat consists of $t$ wire-saving subrounds followed by one reset.

  • A subround (§24.2) replaces the core width $X$ by at most $\rho X+C(Z+\sigma)$ with an absolute $\rho\lt 1$. It increases the core degree from $\Delta$ to at most $C_2\Delta^{C_2}$. It uses the wiring identity of §24.1.
  • The reset (§24.3) returns the core degree to three. It multiplies the core width by a constant $\kappa$ that depends on the degree reached.
  • §24.4 chooses $t$ with $\kappa\rho^t\le\tfrac12$ and resolves the mutual dependence between $t$, $\kappa$ and the degree.

24.1 A wiring identity pp. 89–90

Six parties are placed in a triangular array (Figure 9, p. 90): the first row is $C,M,A$; the second row is $N',D$; the third row is $B$. The columns are $(C,N',B)$, $(M,D)$ and $(A)$. A pair of parties is permitted if it lies in one row or in one column. The permitted pairs are the four row pairs $CM$, $CA$, $MA$, $N'D$ and the four column pairs $CN'$, $CB$, $N'B$, $MD$; write $E_{\mathrm{perm}}$ for this set of eight pairs. An $s$-bit wire between two parties is the equality tensor on two $s$-bit indices. The two demanded wires are $AB$ and $CD$; neither pair is permitted.

What Figure 9 shows. Three panels show the same six parties. In the first, "Demanded wires", dashed lines join $C$ to $D$ and $A$ to $B$. In the second, "Row partition", three boxes enclose the rows $\{C,M,A\}$, $\{N',D\}$, $\{B\}$. In the third, "Column partition", three boxes enclose the columns $\{C,N',B\}$, $\{M,D\}$, $\{A\}$. The caption states the use: an equality wire between $C$ and $M$, for example, disappears when the rows are contracted and survives when the columns are contracted; every permitted wire disappears in one of the two layouts.

The goal of the subsection is an identity that writes the product of the two demanded wires as a sum of networks that use permitted pairs only, with total permitted width strictly less than twice the demanded width. The comparison value is the direct route: $AB$ through $C$ and $CD$ through $M$ uses four permitted wires for two demanded wires, a ratio of exactly two.

Background — prices and wire distances, as used in this subsection. filled here The definitions are from §3 (pp. 10–11). Fix a finite set of sites (here the six parties). A tensor has one vector space at each site. A tensor $T$ is a restriction of $U$ if one linear map at each site takes $U$ to $T$. A spectral point $\varphi$ assigns to each tensor a number in $\mathbb R_{\ge 0}$, is monotone under restriction, sends $0$ to $0$ and the unit tensor to $1$, and satisfies $\varphi(T\oplus U)=\varphi(T)+\varphi(U)$ and $\varphi(T\otimes U)=\varphi(T)\varphi(U)$. In Parts II–IV the (ordinary) price is $\log_2\varphi$. The wire distance $d(x,y)$ between two sites is the price of a one-bit wire between them, so an $s$-bit wire has $\varphi=2^{s\,d(x,y)}$. The function $d$ satisfies the triangle inequality. The scale is the price of one bit shared by all sites; for $\log_2\varphi$ it equals $1$, because that tensor is a direct sum of two unit tensors and $\varphi$ is additive. A scale-one ordinary price over $k$ is such a function $\log_2\varphi$ on tensors over $k$: the price axioms (5.1) of p. 15 at scale one, with trivial valuation, state that the exponential of the price is monotone, multiplicative and additive. The paper states (p. 16 after the range bound (5.2), and p. 60) that the prices of a fixed scale on a fixed finite list of sites form a compact set in the topology of pointwise convergence, because the axioms are closed conditions.
Imported — [42, Theorem 4.3] (with [42, Sections 2 and 2.3, Proposition 3.3]). imported As the paper states it in §3.2 (p. 11): every spectral space embeds, with all its prices, in a complete $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ spectral space. In the extension every pair $a,b$ has a unique midpoint $\mu$, and every point $o$ satisfies $$d(o,\mu)^2\le\tfrac12d(o,a)^2+\tfrac12d(o,b)^2-\tfrac14d(a,b)^2.$$ Sites at wire distance zero are identified. (The paper writes $m$ for the midpoint; this tutorial writes $\mu$ because $m$ is a distance below.)
Imported — [42, Lemma 2.2] (strict spectral comparison). imported As the paper states it in §3.2 (p. 11): if two nonzero tensors on the same sites satisfy $\varphi(T)\lt\varphi(U)$ for every spectral point $\varphi$, there is a fixed integer $C$ such that, for every $j\ge 1$, $T^{\otimes j}$ is a restriction of the direct sum of $C$ copies of $U^{\otimes j}$.

The strict inequality. For a scale-one ordinary price over $k$ on the six parties, let $m$ be the greatest wire distance on a permitted pair. The paper asserts a constant $\lambda_*\lt 2$, uniform over all such prices, with

$$d(A,B)+d(C,D)\le 2\lambda_*\,m.$$
Gap fill — the derivation of $\lambda_*\lt 2$. filled here
  1. $m\ge 1/5$. The graph on the six parties with the eight permitted pairs as edges is connected; a spanning tree has five edges, for example $CM,CA,CN',CB,MD$. Put a one-bit wire on each tree edge and, at each party, the local map that keeps only the assignments in which all incident bits agree. The result is one bit shared by all six parties, whose price is the scale, $1$. It is a restriction of the tensor product of the five wires, whose price is the sum of five distances, each at most $m$. Monotonicity gives $1\le 5m$.
  2. Both demanded distances are at most $2m$. $d(A,B)\le d(A,C)+d(C,B)\le 2m$, since $AC$ is a row pair and $CB$ a column pair. $d(C,D)\le d(C,M)+d(M,D)\le 2m$, and also $d(C,D)\le d(C,N')+d(N',D)\le 2m$.
  3. If $d(C,D)=2m$, then $M$ and $N'$ coincide in the extension. Equality forces $d(C,M)=d(M,D)=m$ and $d(C,N')=d(N',D)=m$. Let $\mu$ be the midpoint of $C,D$ in the $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ extension. The imported inequality with $o=M$ gives $d(M,\mu)^2\le\tfrac12m^2+\tfrac12m^2-\tfrac14(2m)^2=0$. So $M=\mu$, and likewise $N'=\mu$.
  4. If also $d(A,B)=2m$, then $C=M$. Equality forces $d(A,C)=d(C,B)=m$, so by the argument of step 3, $C$ is the midpoint of $A,B$. The pair $AM$ is permitted, and $N'B$ is permitted with $N'=M$; hence $d(A,M)\le m$ and $d(M,B)\le m$, and $M$ is also the midpoint of $A,B$. Midpoints are unique, so $d(C,M)=0$.
  5. Contradiction. Step 3 gave $d(C,M)=m\ge 1/5\gt 0$. So no scale-one price has $d(A,B)=d(C,D)=2m$, and with step 2, $d(A,B)+d(C,D)\lt 4m$ for every such price.
  6. Uniformity. The ratio $f=(d(A,B)+d(C,D))/(2m)$ is a continuous function on the compact set of scale-one prices, because each distance is the value of the price at one fixed tensor and $m\ge 1/5$. A continuous function on a compact set attains its maximum. The maximum is the value of $f$ at some price, hence less than $2$. Call it $\lambda_*$.
The constant $\lambda_*$ is not effective: step 6 gives no numerical bound. Steps 3 and 4 apply the imported embedding theorem to every element of the compact set; each element is itself a scale-one price, and the paper states in §3.2 that identifying sites at wire distance zero turns a price on finitely many sites into a spectral space.

The finite identity. Choose $\lambda_*\lt\lambda_0\lt 2$. The paper claims: for fixed $\eta'\gt 0$ and arbitrarily large $b$, the two demands of width $b$ (the tensor product of a $b$-bit wire $AB$ and a $b$-bit wire $CD$) are an ordinary sum of at most $2^{\eta'b}$ networks, each of which has wires only on permitted pairs, of total width at most $2\lambda_0b$, and a local tensor at each party.

Gap fill — from the price inequality to an exact identity. filled here
  1. Supply and demand. Choose $h_0$ so large that $w_s:=\lfloor 2\lambda_0h_0\rfloor\gt 2\lambda_*h_0$. The demand $T$ is the product of the two demanded wires of width $h_0$. The supply $U$ is the direct sum, over the eight permitted pairs, of a single wire of width $w_s$ on that pair.
  2. Strict comparison at every spectral point. $\varphi(T)=2^{h_0(d(A,B)+d(C,D))}\le 2^{2\lambda_*h_0m}$. By additivity, $\varphi(U)=\sum_{e\in E_{\mathrm{perm}}}2^{w_s\,d(e)}\ge 2^{w_sm}$. Since $w_s\gt 2\lambda_*h_0$ and $m\gt 0$, $\varphi(T)\lt\varphi(U)$.
  3. Restriction. By [42, Lemma 2.2] there is $C_{\mathrm{cmp}}$ such that for every $r\ge 1$, $T^{\otimes r}$ is a restriction of $C_{\mathrm{cmp}}$ direct copies of $U^{\otimes r}$. Now $T^{\otimes r}$ is the pair of demands of width $b=h_0r$. Distributing the tensor power over the direct sum, $U^{\otimes r}$ is the direct sum over sequences $(e_1,\dots,e_r)\in E_{\mathrm{perm}}^r$ of the products of $r$ wires of width $w_s$; each such product is a set of permitted wires of total width $r\,w_s\le 2\lambda_0b$.
  4. From a restriction of a direct sum to a sum of networks. A linear map from a direct sum of spaces is a row of maps, one per block. Applying the restriction maps to the direct sum therefore gives the ordinary sum, over the blocks, of the restricted blocks. Each term is a network: the permitted wires of that block, with the block's restriction map at each party as the local tensor (its ports are the party's ends of the permitted wires and its ends of the demanded legs).
  5. Number of terms. There are $C_{\mathrm{cmp}}\cdot 8^r$ terms; an index takes $r\log_2|E_{\mathrm{perm}}|+O_{h_0}(1)=3r+O_{h_0}(1)$ bits. This is at most $\eta'b=\eta'h_0r$ when $h_0\gt 3/\eta'$ and $r$ is large. Hence the order of choice: $h_0$, then $r$.
The identity is an equality of tensors over $k$; the same tables give the same identity over every extension $K/k$.

The finite identity has $2^{\eta'b}$ terms, a number that grows with the width. A subround applies the identity to wires that carry many copies of a bit, and it needs a number of terms that does not grow. Lemma 24.2 provides this.

Lemma 24.2 (A fixed choice of widths). p. 90 paper There are constants $b\in\mathbb N$ and $\lambda\lt 2$ such that for every $z\ge 1$ the two width-$bz$ demands are the sum of a bounded number of networks on the same six parties. Each uses at most $2\lambda b$ bundles of $z$ bits on permitted edges. Its local tensors are specified by $z$, the party and the index of the summand. The index set and choices of widths (in units of $z$) can be fixed independently of $z$.

Step 1: the $z$th power. Raise the finite identity (width $b$, at most $2^{\eta'b}$ terms) to its $z$th tensor power. The two demands now have width $bz$. A term is indexed by a sequence of $z$ selectors, each of at most $\lceil\eta'b\rceil$ bits. The number of terms, up to $2^{\eta'bz}$, grows with $z$.

Step 2: group the sequences by rounded widths. For a sequence of selectors and a permitted edge, add the widths that the $z$ chosen terms put on that edge, and round the total up to a multiple of $z$. Group the sequences by the vector of the eight rounded totals, measured in units of $z$. Each entry of the vector is an integer between $0$ and the largest width of a single term on an edge, so the vector belongs to a fixed finite set that does not depend on $z$. Empty groups are allowed. Rounding costs at most one unit per permitted edge.

Step 3: one network per group. Within a group, the selector sequence becomes an internal variable. Choose a spanning tree of the permitted graph (five edges) and send the full sequence on each tree edge; this uses at most $\lceil\eta'b\rceil$ additional units of $z$ bits per tree edge. The local table at a party checks that the sequences on all its incident tree edges are identical and valid, that the sequence belongs to the group, and that the padding positions carry zeros. It then uses the sequence to cut the bits of each incident permitted edge into the $z$ slices prescribed by the selectors, and evaluates the product of the $z$ local tensors of the finite identity.

Step 4: exactness. Because the tree is connected, a nonzero contribution requires the same sequence at all six parties, and for a given sequence there is exactly one assignment of the tree wires. Summing over the tree wires therefore gives the sum of the terms of that group, each once. Summing over the groups gives the $z$th power of the finite identity.

Step 5: the width. In units of $z$ the total width of a network is at most $2\lambda_0b+|E_{\mathrm{perm}}|+5\lceil\eta'b\rceil$. Choose $\eta'$ sufficiently small and then $b$ sufficiently large to bound this by $2\lambda b$ with $\lambda\lt 2$.

∎
Gap fill — the arithmetic of Step 5 and the order of the constants. filled here With $|E_{\mathrm{perm}}|=8$ and $\lceil\eta'b\rceil\le\eta'b+1$, the total is at most $(2\lambda_0+5\eta')b+13$. Fix $\lambda$ with $\lambda_0\lt\lambda\lt 2$ and take $\eta'=(\lambda-\lambda_0)/5$. Then the total is at most $(\lambda_0+\lambda)b+13\le 2\lambda b$ as soon as $b\ge 13/(\lambda-\lambda_0)$. The finite identity exists for arbitrarily large $b=h_0r$, so such a $b$ is available. The order of choice is $$\lambda_*\ \to\ \lambda_0\ \to\ \lambda\ \to\ \eta'\ \to\ h_0\ \to\ r\ \to\ b=h_0r.$$ All of these are absolute constants once $k$ is fixed, and none is effective, because $\lambda_*$ and the constant $C_{\mathrm{cmp}}$ of the imported comparison are not. The number of networks is at most the number of vectors, $(\lfloor 2\lambda_0b\rfloor+1)^8$.

24.2 Subrounds pp. 91–92

In a subround, the occurrences at each position are grouped into packs (a pack is the tensor product of its members, kept as one station). Two outgoing units of width from two different packs of the same colony play the roles of the demanded wires $AB$ and $CD$ of §24.1. The subround must produce many such pairs and must bound the number of packs that one contraction merges.

Balanced partitions (p. 91). Within each group of $r$ block indices, use the dyadic intervals of the indices in their common order. Give every host edge $e$ a propagation distance

$$s_e=64\lceil\log_2(2x_e)\rceil,\qquad x_e=\begin{cases}\Delta&\text{on core edges},\\ \nabla&\text{on tethers}.\end{cases}$$

Let $2\le A\le r$ be dyadic. At each position, mark every interval whose load (the sum of the loads of its occurrences at that position) is greater than $AB$. A marked interval must be split. Marks propagate: a mark passes to the parent interval at the same position, and, for each incident host edge $e$, to the ancestor $s_e$ levels higher at the other end of $e$, whenever that ancestor exists. The maximal unmarked intervals are the leaves of the resulting binary partitions; the paper calls them colonies.

Gap fill — three properties of colonies. filled here
  • Load at most $AB$. A colony is unmarked, so its load is at most $AB$.
  • Length at least $A$. One occurrence has load at most $B$, so an interval of load greater than $AB$ has more than $A$ indices and, being dyadic, at least $2A$. A propagated mark sits on a longer interval than its source. So every marked interval has length at least $2A$, and its children, among them every colony other than a full group, have length at least $A$. A full group has length $r\ge A$.
  • Bounded length ratio across an edge. Let colonies $I$ at one end of $e$ and $I'$ at the other end intersect, with $|I|\lt|I'|$; then $I\subset I'$ as index sets. The parent of $I$ is marked and has length $2|I|$. Its ancestor $s_e$ levels higher, of length $2^{s_e+1}|I|$, is marked at the other end if it exists. If $|I'|\ge 2^{s_e+1}|I|$, then $I'$ contains that marked interval, so $I'$ equals it or is one of its ancestors and is marked, a contradiction. If the ancestor does not exist, the group itself is shorter than $2^{s_e+1}|I|$. In both cases $|I'|\le 2^{s_e}|I|$.

The paper states that a common permutation of the indices inside each group gives a total colony count

$$Q\le C_1\Bigl(\frac{HN_b}{r}+\frac{\mathcal M}{AB}\Bigr)\tag{24.1}$$

with $C_1$ absolute. The first term is one colony per position and group, which cannot be avoided; the second is the number of colonies needed to hold the total load $\mathcal M$ at capacity $AB$.

Gap fill — proof of (24.1), following p. 91. filled here A binary partition has one more leaf than marked internal nodes. So $Q$ equals the number $HN_b/r$ of (position, group) pairs plus the number of marks. It suffices to find a permutation with $O(\mathcal M/(AB))$ marks.
  1. The random permutation. Identify the $r$ indices of a group with the field $\mathbb F_r$, written in a binary basis, and multiply all indices by one uniformly random nonzero element $g$. Two distinct indices $i,i'$ fall in the same interval of depth $j$ exactly when the first $j$ coordinates of $g(i-i')$ vanish. The element $g(i-i')$ is uniform among the $r-1$ nonzero elements, and $r2^{-j}-1$ of them have that property. The probability is $(r2^{-j}-1)/(r-1)\le 2^{-j}$.
  2. Initial marks. At one position and group, let $x_i\le 1/A$ be the loads divided by $AB$ and $x=\sum_ix_i$. An initially marked interval $I$ has $\xi_I=\sum_{i\in I}x_i\gt 1$, hence $$\sum_{i\ne i'\in I}x_ix_{i'}=\xi_I^2-\sum_{i\in I}x_i^2\ge\xi_I^2-\frac{\xi_I}{A}\ge\xi_I\bigl(\xi_I-\tfrac12\bigr)\gt\tfrac12.$$ The number of initial marks at depth $j$ is therefore at most twice the sum of the cross products of pairs that share a depth-$j$ interval. By step 1 its expectation is at most $2\sum_{i\ne i'}x_ix_{i'}2^{-j}\le 2x^22^{-j}$.
  3. How far a mark travels. By the choice of $s_e$, $2^{-s_e/16}\le(2x_e)^{-4}$. A vertex has at most $\Delta$ core edges and at most $\nabla$ tethers, so $\sum_{e\ni v}2^{-s_e/16}\le\Delta(2\Delta)^{-4}+\nabla(2\nabla)^{-4}\le\tfrac12$. Give a path $\pi$ the weight $2^{-\sum_{e\in\pi}s_e/16}$. The weights of all paths of a given number of edges from one position sum to at most the corresponding power of $\tfrac12$, so the weights of all paths from one position sum to at most $2$. A path of total propagation distance at most $s$ has weight at least $2^{-s/16}$. Hence at most $2\cdot 2^{s/16}$ positions are reachable with total propagation distance at most $s$.
  4. Marks at upward displacement $s$. A propagated mark is determined by its source mark, the position it reaches and its total upward displacement $s$ (steps to a parent count one level, steps across $e$ count $s_e$ levels). At one reachable position, the marks that come from depth-$j$ sources at displacement $s$ are intervals of depth $j-s$ in one group; there are at most $2^{j-s}$ such intervals, and at most as many as there are sources. The expected number, summed over $j\ge s$, is at most $$\sum_{j\ge s}\min\bigl\{2^{j-s},\ 2x^22^{-j}\bigr\}=O\bigl(x\,2^{-s/2}\bigr).$$ (The two terms are equal where $2^j=\sqrt2\,x\,2^{s/2}$; on each side of this value the sum is geometric.)
  5. Summation. For one source position and group, the expected number of marks at displacement $s$ is at most $2\cdot 2^{s/16}\cdot O(x2^{-s/2})$, and $\sum_{s\ge 0}2^{s/16-s/2}$ converges. So one source contributes $O(x)$ marks in expectation. The sum of $x$ over all positions and groups is $\mathcal M/(AB)$.
  6. Derandomization. Some multiplier has a number of marks at most the expectation. The paper chooses a multiplier that minimizes the actual count; §26.2 computes it by trying all $r-1$ multipliers.
The same multiplier is used at all positions, because a block index is shared by all positions of the block.

A wire-saving subround (pp. 91–92). The construction has six steps.

Step 1: colours and packs. Orient all core edges. Colour each block thick or thin by one affine functional over $\mathbb F_2$ on its within-group index; every position of a block has the colour of the block. At each core colony, all thick tensors form one pack. The thin tensors are packed in order: a thin pack is closed when its load reaches $B$. There are $O(A)$ thin packs per colony, each of load at most $2B$. At a frozen colony the lists are concatenated. Loops created by later mergers are contracted into the local table. Put

$$P=2^{64\lceil\log_2(2\Delta)\rceil},\qquad K_1=\Delta P.$$

Step 2: the thick and thin outgoing widths are nearly balanced. The paper states that pairwise independence of the colours bounds the expected absolute imbalance between thick and thin outgoing widths in a colony by $(B\sum_ia_i)^{1/2}$, where $a_i$ is the outgoing core width of its $i$th occurrence, and that summing gives a colouring of total imbalance at most $\sqrt{BQX}$.

Gap fill — the imbalance bound. filled here For a uniformly random affine functional, the colours of two distinct indices are independent uniform bits. Write $\varepsilon_i=\pm1$ for thick or thin. In one colony the imbalance is $\sum_i\varepsilon_ia_i$. Its mean is zero and its variance is $\sum_ia_i^2$, since the cross terms vanish by pairwise independence. Each $a_i$ is at most the arity of the occurrence, hence at most $B$, so the variance is at most $B\sum_ia_i$, and the expected absolute value is at most $(B\sum_ia_i)^{1/2}$ by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. Summing over the $Q$ colonies and applying Cauchy–Schwarz again, $$\sum_{\text{colonies}}\Bigl(B\sum_ia_i\Bigr)^{1/2}\le\Bigl(Q\cdot B\sum_{\text{all}}a_i\Bigr)^{1/2}=\sqrt{BQX},$$ because each core wire is outgoing at exactly one end. Some functional achieves at most the expected total.

Step 3: match whole thin packs to thick destinations. In a source colony, group the thick outgoing units by their destination pack; these groups are the bins. A thick wire stays in a thick block, and each destination colony has one thick pack; by the length-ratio property a source colony meets at most $P$ colonies at each of at most $\Delta$ neighbours, so there are at most $K_1$ bins. Treat the outgoing units of each thin pack as one item and place the entire item into any bin with enough remaining capacity. The paper defines $I$ as the resulting one-sided matched width (the matched thin width, equal to the matched thick width) and states

$$I\ge\tfrac12X-\tfrac12\sqrt{BQX}-2BK_1Q,$$

and then, with $A$ a sufficiently large dyadic multiple of $1+K_1$, $\ I\ge X/4-O(Z+\sigma)$ with an absolute implicit constant.

Gap fill — the two bounds on $I$. filled here
  • One colony. Let $\tau_{\mathrm{k}}$ and $\tau_{\mathrm{n}}$ be the thick and thin outgoing widths of the colony. An item has width at most the load of its pack, less than $2B$. If every item is placed, the matched width is $\tau_{\mathrm{n}}$. If some item fails, every bin has remaining capacity less than $2B$ at that moment, so the unmatched thick width is less than $2BK_1$. In both cases the matched width is at least $\min(\tau_{\mathrm{k}},\tau_{\mathrm{n}})-2BK_1=\tfrac12(\tau_{\mathrm{k}}+\tau_{\mathrm{n}})-\tfrac12|\tau_{\mathrm{k}}-\tau_{\mathrm{n}}|-2BK_1$.
  • All colonies. The widths $\tau_{\mathrm{k}}+\tau_{\mathrm{n}}$ sum to $X$, the imbalances to at most $\sqrt{BQX}$ (Step 2), and there are $Q$ colonies. This is the first display.
  • Elimination of $Q$. By (24.1) and the slack condition $c_*HN_bB/r\le\sigma$ with $c_*\ge A$: $\ BQ\le C_1(HN_bB/r+\mathcal M/A)\le C_1(\sigma+2X+2Z)/A\le 2C_1Y_0/A$, where $Y_0=X+Z+\sigma$. Then $\tfrac12\sqrt{BQX}\le\tfrac12\sqrt{2C_1/A}\,\sqrt{XY_0}\le\tfrac12\sqrt{2C_1/A}\,\bigl(X+\tfrac12(Z+\sigma)\bigr)$ and $2BK_1Q\le 4C_1K_1Y_0/A$. With $A\ge 32C_1(1+K_1)$ the coefficient of $X$ in each of the two error terms is at most $\tfrac18$, and the remaining parts are at most $\tfrac18(Z+\sigma)$ each. Hence $I\ge\tfrac12X-\tfrac18X-\tfrac18X-\tfrac14(Z+\sigma)$.
The condition $c_*\ge A$ is the lower bound on $r$ and on the slack that Lemma 24.1 states for this step.

Step 4: apply the wiring identity to each matched pair. Within each bin, pair the matched thin units with distinct thick units. Use Lemma 24.2 with $b_*=b$ and $z=u/b_*$, an integer by the divisibility hypothesis on the unit, so that one unit of width $u$ is one demand of width $b_*z$. In each pair the thick wire plays $AB$, with $A$ at its source, and the thin wire plays $CD$, with $C$ at its source. The parties are packs: $A$ is the thick pack and $C$ a thin pack of the same source colony; $B$ is the thick pack of the destination colony of the thick wire; $D$ is a thin pack (a wire stays in one block and the colour belongs to the block). Fresh auxiliary parties $M,N'$ are introduced for each pair. Across all pairs the two demanded widths total $2I$, and in each product summand the replacement wires have total width at most $2\lambda I$.

Step 5: contract the cheaper layout. In one summand, add over all pairs the widths that survive when the rows $\{C,M,A\}$, $\{N',D\}$, $\{B\}$ are each contracted to one station; do the same for the columns $\{C,N',B\}$, $\{M,D\}$, $\{A\}$. The paper performs the cheaper of the two contractions, the same one in every pair, and obtains

$$X'\le X-(2-\lambda)I\le\rho X+C(Z+\sigma),\qquad\rho=1-(2-\lambda)/4\lt 1.$$
Gap fill — why the cheaper layout keeps at most half. filled here A wire inside a contracted part becomes internal to one station and no longer counts toward $X$. Under the row contraction the surviving replacement wires are those on the column pairs $CN',CB,N'B,MD$. Under the column contraction they are those on the row pairs $CM,CA,MA,N'D$. Each of the eight permitted pairs belongs to exactly one of the two lists. So the two surviving totals add up to the whole replacement width, at most $2\lambda I$, and the smaller one is at most $\lambda I$. The matched demanded wires, of total width $2I$, are removed, and the unmatched core wires, of total width $X-2I$, are unchanged. Hence $X'\le(X-2I)+\lambda I$. Further identifications of parties (one pack plays several roles in several pairs) can only make more wires internal. Substituting $I\ge X/4-O(Z+\sigma)$ gives $X'\le X-(2-\lambda)X/4+O(Z+\sigma)$. Since $0\lt 2-\lambda\le 2$, $\ \tfrac12\le\rho\lt 1$.
AHA. The saving of a subround comes from two facts. A geometric one: no price geometry can make both demanded pairs as far apart as two permitted steps, so the replacement costs strictly less than twice the demand ($\lambda\lt 2$). A combinatorial one: each permitted pair lies in a row or in a column but not both, so one of the two contractions removes at least half of the replacement. Together: demand $2I$ is replaced by at most $\lambda I\lt 2I$.

Step 6: loads, unit, degree, positions. The paper explains that matching each thin item as a whole controls the growth. The merged packs form stars: in its source role a thin pack joins its own thick pack (row layout) or its one chosen thick destination (column layout); in its destination role it joins only an auxiliary party. One thick centre absorbs $O((1+K_1)A)$ thin packs. Each auxiliary party is absorbed into a demand endpoint. The paper states the consequences:

  • a dyadic load multiplier $g$, polynomial in $\Delta$, bounds the new core loads and the scaled loads of the frozen lists;
  • the new unit is $u/b_*$, because the replacement wires are bundles of $z=u/b_*$ bits;
  • pack slots are reserved at every potential dyadic interval; possible core contacts run between packs of one colony or of intersecting neighbouring intervals with length ratio at most $P$; a fixed power of this graph gives a common core host of degree at most $C_2\Delta^{C_2}$; the full degree is at most $C_2\nabla^{C_2}$, with $C_2$ absolute;
  • there are fewer than $2r$ potential intervals per position, which gives the bound $crH$ on the number of new positions; tethers stay attached to their frozen tensors.
Not verified — the constants of Step 6. not verified The paper asserts on p. 92 the load multiplier "polynomial in $\Delta$", the degree bounds $C_2\Delta^{C_2}$ and $C_2\nabla^{C_2}$ with an absolute $C_2$, and the position bound $crH$. The tutorial reconstructed the reasons given (stars of bounded size; contacts only between intersecting intervals of length ratio at most $P$; a bounded-diameter neighbourhood closed by taking a fixed graph power) but did not verify the exponents, in particular that one constant $C_2$ serves for both the core degree and the full degree and that the charge of each auxiliary party to a demand endpoint is "a constant multiple of the matched width". §24.4 uses the recurrence $\Delta'\le C_2\Delta^{C_2}$ only through the fact that the logarithm of the degree is multiplied by a constant per subround.

24.3 Resetting the core degree pp. 93–95

After $t$ subrounds the core degree $\Delta$ is large. The reset replaces the core by a host of degree three. Throughout this subsection $H,N_b,\Delta,\nabla$ are the parameters immediately before the reset, and $P=2^{64\lceil\log_2(2\Delta)\rceil}$, $K_1=\Delta P$.

Definition (rotor). p. 93 paper Fix a trivalent network of size $c_0Yw_0$ that realizes all permutations of $Y$ rows of width $w_0$ by fixing its tags, where $4K_1\le Y$ and $c_0\ge 1$. This network is the rotor.
Background — why a permutation network is needed, and the Beneš network. filled here In a single tensor network, connecting outputs to inputs by a known permutation costs nothing: one draws the wires. Here the wiring differs from block to block, while the host, the graph of possible adjacencies, is common to all blocks and must have degree three. The reset therefore needs a fixed graph of bounded degree along which every permutation of $Y$ rows can be routed, with the tables at its vertices chosen per block.

The tagged permutation tensor of §16, $\operatorname{Tag}(y,w_0)$ with $Y=2^y$, has inputs $(j_i,x_i)$ and outputs $x'_j$ (the paper writes $y_j$), where $j_i$ is the $y$-bit destination (tag) of the $w_0$-bit record $x_i$; it is the indicator that the $j_i$ are distinct and $x'_{j_i}=x_i$ for all $i$. If the tags are pinned to a permutation $\pi$, the tensor becomes the product of the equality wires $x'_{\pi(i)}=x_i$. A trivalent network for $\operatorname{Tag}(y,w_0)$ with pinned tags is thus a fixed graph that routes the rows by $\pi$; its vertex tables depend on $\pi$, its graph does not.

The classical example is the Beneš network: for $Y=2^y$ rows it has $2y-1$ columns of $Y/2$ two-by-two switches, and every permutation is obtained by some setting of the switches. Each row bit passes $2y-1$ switches, so the size is of order $Y\,y\,w_0$, that is $c_0=\Theta(y)$. Theorem 16.1(2) gives, under $\omega_k\lt 9/4$ and for wide rows $w_0\ge y^{a_\zeta}$, size $O_\zeta(Yw_0y^{\zeta})$, that is $c_0=O_\zeta(y^\zeta)$ for every $\zeta\gt 0$. §24.4 shows where the smaller $c_0$ is used.

What Figure 10 shows (p. 93). Two boxes labelled "good base" face each other. Each contains a column of input terminals on its outer side, a box labelled "rotor" in the middle, and a column of output terminals on its inner side. Old tensors and their tethers stay at the input terminals. A thick path runs from one input terminal through the rotor to one output terminal, crosses a "reserved link" to an output terminal of the other good base, and continues through that rotor. A dashed path leaves another input terminal and reaches an input terminal in a box labelled "leaf of a bad base", with the annotation "via contact trees". The figure thus shows the two kinds of route of the reset.

Bases and capacities (pp. 93–94). Choose a dyadic $F\ge Y^2w_0$, and require $c_*\ge F$ in this step. Let $\mu_v$ be the mean load at position $v$ over the $N_b$ blocks, and define the smoothed mean

$$\nu_v=\max\Bigl\{\frac{FB}{r},\ \max_{\pi:\,y\to v}\mu_y\,2^{-\sum_{e\in\pi}s_e/2}\Bigr\},$$

where $\pi$ runs over host paths from a position $y$ to $v$, including the path of length zero. (In this formula $y$ is a position; in the definition of the rotor and in §24.4 the same letter is the exponent in $Y=2^y$.) At position $v$ the block indices are partitioned into intervals, called bases, of the largest dyadic length $s_v\le FB/\nu_v$; the paper notes $F\le s_v\le r$. For each pair of intersecting bases at neighbouring core positions $v,w$ a capacity

$$\overline y_{vw}\min(s_v,s_w)+\frac{FB}{K_1}$$

is reserved, where $\overline y_{vw}$ is the mean core width of that contact per block. Base lengths and capacities are fixed from the means, before the blocks are permuted, so that the output terminals for a given pair of base positions occupy the same host slots in every group.

Gap fill — the five properties of bases stated on pp. 93–94. filled here
  1. $\sum_v\nu_v\le HFB/r+C\sum_v\mu_v$. Bound the inner maximum by the sum over all paths. Exchanging the order of summation gives $\sum_y\mu_y$ times the total weight $\sum_\pi 2^{-\sum s_e/2}$ of the paths that start at $y$. This is at most the total weight with exponent $s_e/16$, which is at most $2$ by step 3 of the proof of (24.1).
  2. $F\le s_v\le r$. $\nu_v\ge FB/r$ gives $FB/\nu_v\le r$. Every $\mu_y\le B$, and $r\ge c_*\ge F$, so $\nu_v\le B$ and $FB/\nu_v\ge F$.
  3. Neighbouring base depths differ by at most $s_e/2$. Extending a path by the edge $e=vw$ shows $\nu_w\ge\nu_v2^{-s_e/2}$ and conversely. So $FB/\nu_v$ and $FB/\nu_w$ differ by a factor at most $2^{s_e/2}$, and since $s_e/2$ is an integer, so do their dyadic roundings.
  4. The number of bases is $O((\mathcal M+\sigma)/(FB))$. Position $v$ has $N_b/s_v\le 2N_b\nu_v/(FB)$ bases. By item 1 the total is at most $2HN_b/r+2C\,N_b\sum_v\mu_v/(FB)$. Here $N_b\sum_v\mu_v=\mathcal M$, and the slack condition $c_*HN_bB/r\le\sigma$ with $c_*\ge F$ gives $HN_b/r\le\sigma/(FB)$.
  5. The reserved capacity at a base is at most $2FB$. A base at $v$ of length $s_v$ meets $\max(1,s_v/s_w)$ bases at $w$, and the first terms of these reservations sum to at most $\overline y_{vw}s_v$. Summing over the core neighbours $w$ gives at most $s_v\mu_v\le s_v\nu_v\le FB$. There are at most $\Delta\cdot 2^{s_e/2}\le K_1$ reservations at a base, and each second term is $FB/K_1$.

Mixing (p. 94). All block indices are permuted by one invertible binary matrix (the indices are vectors over $\mathbb F_2$). Within the bases, every dyadic subinterval of load above $2FB$, possibly a base itself, is marked, and marks propagate with the same edge distances as in §24.2. The leaves have load at most $2FB$ and length at least $F$, and they satisfy the length-ratio bounds. A base is bad if it splits or if one of its core reservations overflows; otherwise it is good. The paper states: for any $\delta_*\gt 0$, choosing $F$ sufficiently large in terms of $\Delta,Y,\delta_*$ gives a permutation with

$$\sum_{\text{bad bases}}\mathrm{load}\le\delta_*(\mathcal M+\sigma).\tag{24.2}$$
Gap fill — the proof of (24.2), with the steps of p. 94 separated. filled here The proof is a second-moment estimate for a uniformly random invertible matrix. An interval at depth $j$ below the bases at position $v$ is the set of blocks whose permuted index has a given prefix of length $\log_2n_v+j$, where $n_v=N_b/s_v$ is the number of bases at $v$.
  1. Normalization. Divide the loads at $v$ by $FB$: $x_i\le 1/F$. Their sum is $N_b\mu_v/(FB)\le N_b\nu_v/(FB)\le n_v$. Let $a_J$ be the normalized load of interval $J$ at depth $j$ and $\overline a$ the mean of the $a_J$; then $\overline a\le 2^{-j}$. An interval is overloaded if $a_J\gt 2$.
  2. Two estimators. Put $V_j=\sum_J(a_J-\overline a)^2$ and $U_j=\sum_Ja_J^2-\sum_ix_i^2$. Each bounds the number of overloaded intervals and half their total normalized load. For $V_j$: if $a_J\gt 2$ then $a_J-\overline a\ge a_J/2\ge 1$, so $(a_J-\overline a)^2\ge\max(1,a_J/2)$. For $U_j$: the contribution of $J$ is $a_J^2-\sum_{i\in J}x_i^2=\sum_{i\ne i'\in J}x_ix_{i'}$, which is nonnegative; it is at least $a_J(a_J-1/F)$ because $x_i\le 1/F$, and for $a_J\gt 2$ this is at least $\max(1,a_J/2)$.
  3. Expectations. For a uniformly random invertible matrix, two distinct indices have a common prefix of a given length with probability at most the reciprocal of the number of intervals of that length (their difference is mapped to a uniformly random nonzero vector). With $n_v2^j$ intervals, $$\mathbb E\sum_Ja_J^2\le\sum_ix_i^2+\frac{(\sum_ix_i)^2}{n_v2^j}.$$ Since $\sum_J\overline a^{\,2}=(\sum_ix_i)^2/(n_v2^j)$, this gives $\mathbb EV_j\le\sum_ix_i^2\le\frac1F\sum_ix_i\le n_v/F$, and $\mathbb EU_j\le(\sum_ix_i)^2/(n_v2^j)\le n_v2^{-j}$.
  4. How many bases one mark can split. A mark at depth $j$ below the bases at some position that propagates along a path $\pi$ rises by $\sum_{e\in\pi}s_e$ levels, while the base depth changes by at most $\tfrac12\sum s_e$ (property 3 of the previous box). It is still inside a base at the end of $\pi$ only if $\sum s_e\le 2j$. By dyadic nesting it then lies in one base there. By step 3 of the proof of (24.1), at most $2\cdot 2^{2j/16}=2\cdot 2^{j/8}$ positions are reachable. So one mark at depth $j$ splits at most $2\cdot 2^{j/8}$ bases.
  5. The charge for splits. A split base of load at most $2FB$ costs at most $2FB$. A base of load above $2FB$ is itself an overloaded interval at depth $0$, and its load is at most $2FB$ times its contribution to the depth-zero estimator. At depth $j$ use $V_j$ if $2^j\le F$ and $U_j$ otherwise, with weight $4\cdot 2^{\lceil j/8\rceil}$: item 4 gives at most $2\cdot 2^{j/8}$ split bases per overloaded interval, each costing at most $2FB$. The total load of split bases is then at most a constant times $FB$ times the weighted sum, for every matrix. The expected weighted sum at $v$ is at most a constant times $$n_v\sum_{j\ge 0}2^{j/8}\min\{F^{-1},2^{-j}\}=O\bigl(n_vF^{-7/8}\bigr):$$ for $2^j\le F$ the terms $2^{j/8}/F$ sum to $O(F^{1/8}/F)$, and for $2^j\gt F$ the terms $2^{-7j/8}$ sum to $O(F^{-7/8})$. Summing over $v$ with $\sum_vn_v=O((\mathcal M+\sigma)/(FB))$ gives an expected split load $O(F^{-7/8}(\mathcal M+\sigma))$.
  6. The charge for overflows. For a core contact $vw$, apply the estimator $V$ to the contact widths divided by $FB$, on the intersections of the bases at $v$ and $w$. The mean of an intersection is $\overline y_{vw}\min(s_v,s_w)/(FB)$, and an overflow exceeds it by more than $1/K_1$, so it contributes at least $1/K_1^2$ to $V$. As in item 3, $\mathbb EV\le\sum_i\chi_i^2\le\frac1F\sum_i\chi_i=X_{vw}/(F^2B)$, where $\chi_i\le 1/F$ are the normalized widths of the contact in the blocks $i$ and $X_{vw}$ is the total width of the contact. Summing over core edges gives $X/(F^2B)$, so the expected number of overflows is at most $K_1^2X/(F^2B)$. Each overflow affects at most two bases, and each affected base not already charged costs at most $2FB$: expected load $O(K_1^2X/F)$.
  7. Conclusion. Let $\Phi$ be the sum of the weighted interval estimators and $K_1^2$ times the contact variances. The bad load is at most $O(FB\,\Phi)$ for every matrix, and $FB\,\mathbb E\Phi=O(F^{-7/8}(\mathcal M+\sigma)+K_1^2X/F)$. Since $X\le\mathcal M/2$ and $K_1$ depends only on $\Delta$, the right side is at most $\delta_*(\mathcal M+\sigma)$ when $F$ is large in terms of $\Delta$ and $\delta_*$. A matrix with $\Phi$ at most its mean satisfies (24.2).
The estimate does not involve $\nabla$: tethers enter only through the propagation distances, and the path count of step 3 of (24.1) is uniform in $\nabla$. The matrix is found in §26.2 by computing the collision scores.

The routes (pp. 94–95). After mixing, the blocks are grouped in consecutive runs of $r$. Every core leaf receives $Y$ input terminals. Its old tensors are assigned whole, one after another, to a currently least-loaded terminal; each terminal receives load at most $2FB/Y+B\le 3FB/Y$. Tethers stay at the terminal of their tensor, with their other ends in the concatenated frozen list.

Between good bases. In every good core base the rotor connects the $Y$ input terminals to $Y$ output terminals. A reservation of capacity $c$ receives $\lceil cY/(8FB)\rceil$ pairs of dedicated output terminals, one terminal of each pair in each of the two bases, joined by a direct link. This uses at most $Y/4+K_1\le Y$ output terminals per base. The units of a good–good contact are divided evenly among its pairs, so an output terminal has load at most $8FB/Y+u\le 9FB/Y$.

At a good base let $m_{ij}$ be the number of units routed from input $i$ to output $j$. The paper bounds the row and column sums of the matrix $(\lceil m_{ij}/w_0\rceil)$ by

$$\frac{9FB}{Yuw_0}+Y\le\frac{10FB}{Yuw_0},$$

brings all row and column sums to the largest one by adding edges, and decomposes the resulting regular bipartite multigraph into perfect matchings by Hall's theorem. Each matching configures one layer of the rotor, with every bit edge of the rotor replaced by a bundle of width $u$. Unused inputs and the corresponding outputs are pinned to zero. The table at a rotor vertex is the tensor product of its layer tables. Balanced binary trees at each rotor row collect its $w_0$ ports, at $O(\log(2w_0))$ hops per unit. Including the direct links, the paper counts a cost $O((c_0+\log(2w_0))FB)$ per good base.

Background — decomposing a bipartite multigraph into perfect matchings (König, Hall). filled here Let $N=(n_{ij})$ be a $Y\times Y$ matrix of nonnegative integers with all row and column sums at most $\Lambda$. Claim: there are $\Lambda$ permutation matrices whose sum dominates $N$ entrywise.
  1. Padding. If some row sum is less than $\Lambda$, then some column sum is too, because the row sums and the column sums have the same total. Add one to the entry in that row and column. Repeat until all row and column sums equal $\Lambda$. The matrix is now the adjacency matrix of a $\Lambda$-regular bipartite multigraph on rows and columns.
  2. Hall's condition. For a set $S$ of rows, the $\Lambda|S|$ edges leaving $S$ end in the neighbour set $N(S)$, which absorbs at most $\Lambda|N(S)|$ edges. So $|N(S)|\ge|S|$, and Hall's theorem gives a perfect matching.
  3. Induction. Removing a perfect matching leaves a $(\Lambda-1)$-regular multigraph. After $\Lambda$ steps the graph is a union of $\Lambda$ perfect matchings. This is König's edge-colouring theorem for bipartite multigraphs.
Applied with $n_{ij}=\lceil m_{ij}/w_0\rceil$: the pair $(i,j)$ lies in at least $n_{ij}$ matchings, each layer carries $w_0$ units along it, and $n_{ij}w_0\ge m_{ij}$. The number of layers is $\Lambda\le 10FB/(Yuw_0)$.
Gap fill — the numerical claims of the routing paragraph. filled here
  • Terminal load $3FB/Y$. A leaf has load at most $2FB$, so a least-loaded terminal has at most $2FB/Y$ before it receives a tensor of load at most $B$. And $B\le FB/Y$ because $F\ge Y^2w_0\ge Y$.
  • Output terminals. The reservations at a base sum to at most $2FB$ and number at most $K_1$, so $\sum\lceil cY/(8FB)\rceil\le 2FB\cdot Y/(8FB)+K_1=Y/4+K_1$, and $K_1\le Y/4$.
  • Output load. A good base has no overflow, so a contact of reservation $c$ has width at most $c$; divided among at least $cY/(8FB)$ pairs and rounded up to a unit, each pair carries at most $8FB/Y+u$, and $u\le B\le FB/Y$.
  • Line sums. A row or column of $(\lceil m_{ij}/w_0\rceil)$ has $Y$ entries, and $\sum_jm_{ij}$ is at most the terminal load divided by $u$. So a line sum is at most $9FB/(Yuw_0)+Y$. The inequality $Y\le FB/(Yuw_0)$ is $F\ge Y^2w_0\,u/B$, which follows from $F\ge Y^2w_0$ and $u\le B$. This is the reason for the lower bound on $F$.
  • Cost per good base. One layer of the rotor has $c_0Yw_0$ vertices with edges of width $u$, so $\Lambda$ layers have total width $O(c_0Yw_0u\Lambda)=O(c_0FB)$. Every unit passes two row trees of depth $O(\log(2w_0))$, and the units of a base have total width $O(FB)$; the direct links carry at most $2FB$.
  • Load at a rotor vertex. A vertex has at most three edges of width $u$ per layer, so its load is at most $3u\Lambda=O(FB/(Yw_0))$, within the bound $O(FB)$ stated by the paper.

With a bad endpoint. A core contact with a bad endpoint uses balanced binary contact trees attached to the input terminals at both ends. The possible neighbouring leaf intervals have length ratios at most $P$, and each leaf has $Y$ terminals, so the trees have depth $O(\log(2\Delta Y))$. Ordered lists are routed through the trees by equality tensors; the assigned lists at both ends determine the transmitted bits uniquely. The total width of the wires that take this route is at most the load of the bad bases, at most $\delta_*(\mathcal M+\sigma)$ by (24.2). The paper chooses $\delta_*$ small enough to pay the depth, that is $\delta_*\cdot O(\log(2\Delta Y))\le 1$, and concludes, for an absolute $A_0$,

$$X'\le A_0\bigl(1+c_0+\log(2w_0)\bigr)(X+Z+\sigma).$$

The count includes every core link in the rotors, collecting trees, direct links and contact trees. It follows from the cost per good base times the number $O((\mathcal M+\sigma)/(FB))$ of bases, plus the bad routes, with $\mathcal M\le 2X+2Z$.

State after the reset (p. 95). The paper records: the unit remains $u$; the load at every input terminal, output terminal, tree vertex and rotor vertex is $O(FB)$, and the scaled list load of a frozen leaf is at most $2FB$; the core has degree at most three (a rotor vertex has degree three; an input terminal has one edge to a row tree and contact-tree connections; an output terminal has a row-tree connection and a direct link; binary trees have degree three); the common host allows a tether from every input terminal at every possible intersecting core leaf of the prescribed length ratio, so the full degree is bounded by a fixed power of $2\nabla$, with constants depending on this reset; reserving the hardware at all possible interval slots costs at most $crH$ positions.

Not verified — the host after the reset. not verified The tutorial did not re-derive the last two assertions of p. 95: that the full degree after the reset is at most a fixed power of $2\nabla$, and that all hardware fits in $crH$ positions with $c$ depending only on the degree bounds. It also did not check the statement of the degree at an input terminal in detail: the text gives an input terminal "rotor-row (via one edge to a row tree) and contact-tree connections", and the count to at most three core neighbours depends on how the contact trees for the several neighbours of a leaf are attached, which the paper does not spell out.

24.4 Paying for the reset pp. 95–96

Write $\kappa=A_0(1+c_0+\log(2w_0))$ for the width multiplier of the reset, and let $t$ be the least positive integer with $\kappa\rho^t\le\tfrac12$, where $\rho\lt 1$ is the factor of one subround.

Step 1: the width recurrence. After $t$ subrounds the core width is at most $\rho^tX+C(Z+\sigma)(1+\rho+\dots+\rho^{t-1})\le\rho^tX+C(Z+\sigma)/(1-\rho)$. The reset multiplies the sum of this width and $Z+\sigma$ by $\kappa$: $$X_{\mathrm{out}}\le\kappa\rho^tX+\kappa\Bigl(\frac{C}{1-\rho}+1\Bigr)(Z+\sigma)\le\tfrac12X+\widetilde C(Z+\sigma).$$ Here $C$ is the constant of the subround estimate and $\widetilde C=\kappa(C/(1-\rho)+1)$ is the constant called $C$ in the statement of Lemma 24.1. The frozen charges are unchanged, so $Z_{\mathrm{out}}=Z$, and the reset gives $\Delta_{\mathrm{out}}\le 3$. This is the conclusion of Lemma 24.1, provided a rotor with $Y\ge 4K_1$ exists for the degree reached after $t$ subrounds.

Step 2: the circular dependence. The rotor size $Y$ must be at least $4K_1$, and $K_1=\Delta P$ is computed from the core degree $\Delta$ after $t$ subrounds. The number $t$ depends on $\kappa$, and $\kappa$ depends on the rotor through $c_0$ and $w_0$. The paper resolves this with the bound $$\log_2(4K_1)\le C^{\,t+1}\le C''\bigl(2+c_0+\log(2w_0)\bigr)^{C'},$$ where $C,C',C''$ are new constants, unrelated to the $C$ of Step 1; they depend only on the incoming core degree and the subround constants (derivation in the next box).

Step 3: the rotor from Theorem 16.1(2). For $Y=2^y$, Theorem 16.1(2) gives, for each $\zeta\gt 0$, a rotor with $w_0=\lceil y^{a_\zeta}\rceil$ and $c_0=O_\zeta(y^\zeta)$. Fix $\zeta\lt 1/(2C')$. Then $c_0+\log(2w_0)=O_\zeta(y^\zeta)$, and the right side of the display is $O_\zeta(y^{\zeta C'})=O_\zeta(y^{1/2})$, which is at most $y$ for large $y$. So $\log_2(4K_1)\le y$, that is $4K_1\le Y$: this rotor covers the required core degree.

Step 4: what depends on what. The number $t$ of subrounds, the rotor, $F$ and $\delta_*$ depend only on the incoming core-degree bound and on the conditional sorting construction. They bound the growth of the maximum load. The full degree $\nabla$ affects only the tether propagation distances and the number of host positions. All subrounds together divide the unit $u$ by $b_*^{\,t}$. The degree bounds of the subrounds, followed by the reset, multiply $\log(2\nabla)$ by at most a fixed factor. This completes the proof of Lemma 24.1.

∎
Gap fill — the two inequalities of Step 2, and the role of the conditional rotor. filled here
  • First inequality. One subround maps $\Delta$ to at most $C_2\Delta^{C_2}$, so $\log_2(2\Delta)$ is multiplied by at most $C_3=C_2+\log_2(2C_2)$. After $t$ subrounds from an incoming degree $\Delta_0$, $\log_2(2\Delta)\le C_3^{\,t}\log_2(2\Delta_0)$. From $K_1=\Delta\cdot 2^{64\lceil\log_2(2\Delta)\rceil}$, $\ \log_2(4K_1)\le 2+\log_2\Delta+64\log_2(2\Delta)+64\le 131\log_2(2\Delta)$. Hence $\log_2(4K_1)\le C^{\,t+1}$ with $C=\max\{C_3,\ 131\log_2(2\Delta_0)\}$.
  • Second inequality. By minimality of $t$, either $t=1$ or $\kappa\rho^{t-1}\gt\tfrac12$; in both cases $t\le 1+\log(2\kappa)/\log(1/\rho)$. Then $C^{\,t+1}\le C^2(2\kappa)^{C'}$ with $C'=\log C/\log(1/\rho)$. Substituting $\kappa=A_0(1+c_0+\log(2w_0))$ gives the stated form with $C''=C^2(2A_0)^{C'}$. The exponent $C'$ does not depend on the rotor.
  • Why this derivation needs a rotor with small $c_0$. (Observation of this tutorial.) With the constant $C=\max\{C_3,131\log_2(2\Delta_0)\}$ of the first item, $C\ge 131$. Since $\rho\ge\tfrac12$ (Step 5 of §24.2), $C'=\log C/\log(1/\rho)\ge\log_2C\gt 7$. For a Beneš rotor, $c_0=\Theta(y)$, and the right side of the display is of order $y^{C'}$, which exceeds $y$ for large $y$; the inequality "at most $y$" does not follow from this bound. With $c_0=O_\zeta(y^\zeta)$ and $\zeta\lt 1/(2C')$ it does. The tutorial did not examine whether a different argument could close the dependence with a Beneš rotor. In the paper's argument this is the place where Lemma 24.1 uses the hypothesis of Theorem 16.1, through its part (2).

Summary of the beat. The following table collects the effect of each component on the quantities that §25.1 tracks.

ComponentCore width $X$Core degreeUnitGrouping steps
one subround (§24.2)$\le\rho X+C(Z+\sigma)$, $\ \rho=1-(2-\lambda)/4$$\Delta\to C_2\Delta^{C_2}$$u\to u/b_*$one
the reset (§24.3)$\le\kappa(X+Z+\sigma)$$\to 3$unchangedone
