The count of grouping steps in the last column is this tutorial's reading: the paper says that the beat is "a fixed sequence of grouping and contraction steps" and bounds the positions of each step by $crH$, and it describes one grouping for the colonies of a subround and one for the reset.

25. The arity budgets after one round pp. 96–101

Role in the chain. This section has no numbered statement. It is the middle of the proof of Lemma 23.1. It receives the invariants (23.2) and the starting configuration (23.3) from §23.1, Lemma 19.1 (Local concentration) from §19, and Lemma 24.1 (Reduction of core width) from §24. Subsection 25.1 constructs one round and proves that the invariants (23.2) with budgets $(L,T)$ imply the same invariants with budgets $(L/G,\ qT)$; the numbered displays are (25.1)–(25.3). Subsection 25.2 iterates the round $j$ times, chooses the group size $r$ and the number $S$ of blocks, and establishes the terminal bounds (25.4)–(25.6). §26.2 uses (25.5) and (25.6) to bound the size of one fixed network for the sum of all summands.

Notation in force: $k$ is a finite field with $\omega_k\lt 9/4$; $h=\lceil\log_2n\rceil$; logarithms are binary; the input network of the macro has size at most $m$, arity at most $h^b$ and $e\le mh^{-2a}$ external bits; $d=T_0S$ is the number of copies. A summand is a product of $N_b$ blocks on a common host graph with $H$ positions; an occurrence is a pair (position, block); the charged arity of a station of arity $s_v$ is $z_v=\max(s_v,\theta T)$. The invariants at the start of a round are

$$\sum_vz_v\le L,\qquad\sum_vz_v^2\le LT,\qquad\max_vz_v\le DT/2.\tag{23.2}$$

In this section $J$ is the number of beats in one round and $j$ is the number of rounds in one macro. In §27 the letter $J$ counts macros.

Lemma 19.1 (Local concentration), recalled from §19 in the form used here. pp. 73–74 paper Fix $k$ with $\omega_k\lt 9/4$, a degree bound $g$ for a host graph of cells, and an integer $\ell\ge 1$. For each sufficiently large fixed integer $D$ and all sufficiently large integers $T$, over an extension $K/k$ with $|K|$ at least a sufficiently large polynomial in $T$ and $[K:k]=O_D(\log T)$: suppose there are at most $W/(DT)$ occupied cells and at most $W/(DT)$ immobile stations in total; in any one network each cell has at most $100D$ stations of total arity at most $100DT$; all external bits belong to immobile stations; and over all stations of all networks $\sum_it_i^2\le WT$. Then the tensor of each network is a sum over $K$ of networks with the same exposed bits, and every choice of one term per network satisfies:
  1. there are $O(W/(DT))$ frozen stations, including the holders of the original external bits; their maximum arity is $O(DT)$, their squared arities sum to $O(WT)$, and their arities sum to $O(W/\sqrt D)$;
  2. all other stations, the core, have arity at most $D^{-\ell}T$ and total arity $O(W(1+\log D)^2)$;
  3. a common host replaces each cell by $D^{O(1)}$ positions, with possible contacts only within and between neighbouring cells; the induced host on the core positions has absolute bounded degree; no two frozen stations touch by a wire; for some $c=c(g,\ell,k)$ all active internal bundles can have width at least $\lceil T/D^c\rceil$, and conclusions 1–2 continue to hold when every station is charged at least that arity;
  4. the outer summation uses $O_D(\log(T+1))$ control bits per occupied cell; with input labels of at most $A$ bits, the work and communication per occupied cell are $O_D(A+(1+\log T)^{O(1)})$ bit operations and bits, and each output table has a label of length $O_D(A+\log(T+1))$.
The implicit constants in conclusions 1–3 depend on $k,g,\ell$ and not on $D,T,W$.

The paper's introduction to the section (p. 96) states what remains after concentration. The frozen stations have total arity $O(L/\sqrt D)$, which is below the budget $L$ by the factor $\sqrt D$. The core stations are small individually, but their total arity is $O(L(\log D)^{O(1)})$, which can exceed $L$. Repeated beats reduce the width of the wires inside the core until the total charge fits the smaller budget $L/G$. To start the next round, the construction must also bound the squared charges and give the full host a degree bounded by an absolute constant.

25.1 From concentration to bounded degree pp. 96–99

The constants of a round and their order. The paper fixes them as follows.

  1. Lemma 19.1 is applied with arity exponent $\ell=10$. Its conclusion 3 gives an absolute bound on the core degree. This bound is the "incoming core degree" of Lemma 24.1 and therefore fixes all constants of a beat: the number $t$ of subrounds, the rotor, $F$, $\delta_*$, and the three per-beat growth factors (of the maximum load, of the reciprocal bundle unit, and of $\log(2\nabla)$, where $\nabla$ bounds the full host degree).
  2. $\epsilon\in(0,1/100]$ is chosen so small that over $$J=\lceil 2\epsilon\log D\rceil$$ beats, the products of these three per-beat factors are each at most $D^{0.03}$ when $D$ is sufficiently large.
  3. $D$ is then taken sufficiently large in terms of $k,\delta,\epsilon$; the paper increases it several times in the course of the section.
Gap fill — why a suitable $\epsilon$ exists. filled here Let $c_1\ge 1$ be the largest of the three per-beat factors. By Lemma 24.1 it does not depend on $D$. Over $J\le 2\epsilon\log_2D+1$ beats the product is at most $c_1^{J}\le c_1\cdot D^{2\epsilon\log_2c_1}$. If $2\epsilon\log_2c_1\le 0.02$, this is at most $c_1D^{0.02}\le D^{0.03}$ as soon as $D^{0.01}\ge c_1$. The independence of the per-beat factors from $D$ is what makes the choice of $\epsilon$ possible before $D$.

Two requirements on the budgets. The round requires

$$ed\le L/D,$$

and, for each grouping in the round,

$$H\,N_b\,T/r\le c_D\,L\tag{25.1}$$

with a sufficiently small constant $c_D\gt 0$, where $H,N_b$ are the current host counts and $L,T$ the budgets at the start of the round. The left side of (25.1) is the total charge that would be present if every group at every position held one station of arity $T$; the condition says that this "one partially filled pack per position and group" overhead is a small fraction of $L$. Both requirements are met in §25.2 by the choice of $r$ and by the hypothesis $e\le mh^{-2a}$.

Phase 1: concentrating the squared arities. Group the blocks $r$ at a time. Within a group the $r$ block indices have dyadic intervals. The paper applies the dyadic partition estimate (24.1) of §24.2 to the charged arities, with maximum load $DT/2$ and capacity $DT$: at each position the $r$ indices are partitioned into dyadic intervals whose total charge is at most $DT$, and intersecting intervals at adjacent positions have lengths within an absolute ratio. Each interval is one cell for Lemma 19.1; the stations of the blocks in the interval, at that position, are the stations of the cell. All candidate intervals are positions of a new host; two intervals at adjacent old positions are joined when they intersect and satisfy the length-ratio bound. Dyadic nesting bounds the degree of this host by an absolute constant.

Inside each occupied interval the stations are prepared as follows.

  • Every station of charged arity greater than $T$ is retained.
  • The other stations are packed in order: a pack is closed when its total charged arity reaches $T$. The table of a pack is the product of its members with internal indices contracted.
  • The external bits of the interval, if any, are moved to one additional identity station that keeps their order and is joined to the original incidences by duplicate ports. These exterior identities are the immobile stations.
Gap fill — the hypotheses of Lemma 19.1 hold with $W=C_1L$. filled here The paper states that the bounds are "precisely the occupation, immobility, and square bounds of Lemma 19.1". The verification, item by item:
  1. Number of cells. Estimate (24.1) with $B=DT/2$ and $A=2$ gives at most $C_1(HN_b/r+\mathcal M/(DT))$ intervals, where $\mathcal M=\sum_vz_v\le L$. If $c_D\le 1/D$, then (25.1) gives $HN_b/r\le L/(DT)$. The number of intervals is $O(L/(DT))$.
  2. Stations per cell. An interval has charge at most $DT$. A retained station has charge greater than $T$, so there are at most $D$ of them. A closed pack has charge at least $T$, and one pack may be unfinished, so there are at most $D+1$ packs. With the exterior identity the count is at most $2D+2\le 100D$.
  3. Arity per cell. A pack has charged arity less than $2T$ (it was below $T$ before its last member, and that member has charge at most $T$). The stations and packs of an interval have total arity at most $DT$. The exterior identity on $f_i$ external bits has arity $2f_i\le 2DT$. The total is at most $3DT\le 100DT$.
  4. Immobile stations. There is at most one per occupied interval, so $O(L/(DT))$ in total.
  5. Squares. The retained stations contribute at most $\sum_vz_v^2\le LT$. The packs contribute at most $2T\cdot\sum(\text{arity})\le 2TL$. The identities contribute $\sum_i(2f_i)^2=4\sum_if_i^2\le 4\max_if_i\sum_if_i\le 4\,DT\cdot ed\le 4LT$, where the last step is the requirement $ed\le L/D$. The sum is at most $7LT$.
All five hold with $W=C_1L$ for an absolute $C_1\ge 7$ that also covers the constants in items 1 and 4. The third invariant $\max z_v\le DT/2$ is what allows capacity $DT$ with $A=2$ in item 1. The requirement $ed\le L/D$ is used only in item 5.

Result of the expansion. Apply Lemma 19.1. Write $Z$ for the total charged arity of the frozen stations and $X$ for the total width of the internal wires between core stations, each wire counted once. In every summand:

  • the frozen charges have total $Z=O(L/\sqrt D)$, square sum $O(LT)$ and maximum $O(DT)$;
  • each core station has arity at most $D^{-10}T$, and the core has total arity $O(L(\log D)^{O(1)})$, so $X=O(L(\log D)^{O(1)})$;
  • every positive internal bundle has width at least $T/D^{O(1)}$;
  • the constants are uniform in $D$.

Units and loads for packing. Lemma 24.1 needs every internal width to be a multiple of an integer unit $u$, and it needs $u$ to be divisible by the product of the subdivision factors of all later steps (each subround divides the unit by the constant $b_*$ of Lemma 24.2). The paper takes $u$ below the minimum guaranteed internal width, with $u\ge T/D^{O(1)}$ and $u$ a multiple of the required product; this is possible for fixed $D$ once $T$ is large. Each positive internal bundle is padded to the next multiple of $u$ with bits pinned to zero.

Gap fill — the cost of padding and the scaled frozen loads. filled here A bundle of width $w\ge u$ is padded to width less than $w+u\le 2w$. The arity of each station at most doubles and its square at most quadruples. Each frozen charge is raised to the larger of its old value and its padded arity, so it at most doubles; $Z$ and the frozen square sum keep their orders $O(L/\sqrt D)$ and $O(LT)$. The paper then chooses a dyadic load bound $B$ of order $D^{-10}T$ that covers every core station. A frozen station has charge up to $O(DT)$, which exceeds $B$ by a factor of order $D^{11}$. For the packing arguments of §24 only, each frozen charge is multiplied by one fixed dyadic factor of order $B/(DT)$, so that the scaled charge is at most $B$. This factor is the "fixed dyadic factor in $(0,1]$" in the definition of the load of a frozen occurrence (§24). It changes no width and no entry of (23.2). Finally $u\le B$ is arranged by taking $u$ smaller if necessary.

Phase 2: removing the core wires. Apply Lemma 24.1 $J$ times, each time with slack $\sigma=L/\sqrt D$. Each application gives $X_{\mathrm{out}}\le\tfrac12X+C(Z+\sigma)$, keeps $Z$, and returns a core of degree at most three.

Gap fill — the width after $J$ beats, and the slack condition. filled here Let $X_i$ be the core width after $i$ beats. From $X_{i+1}\le\tfrac12X_i+C(Z+\sigma)$, $$X_J\le 2^{-J}X_0+C(Z+\sigma)\sum_{i\lt J}2^{-i}\lt 2^{-J}X_0+2C(Z+\sigma).$$ Here $2^{-J}\le 2^{-2\epsilon\log_2D}=D^{-2\epsilon}$, $X_0=O(L(\log D)^{O(1)})$, and $Z+\sigma=O(L/\sqrt D)$. Since $\epsilon\le 1/100$, $D^{-1/2}\le D^{-2\epsilon}$. Therefore $$X_{\mathrm{end}}\le 2^{-J}X_{\mathrm{start}}+O(Z+\sigma)=O\bigl(L\,D^{-2\epsilon}(\log D)^{O(1)}\bigr),$$ the display on p. 97.

Lemma 24.1 requires $c_*HN_bB'/r\le\sigma$ at each of its grouping steps, where $B'$ is the load bound at that step. The paper notes that $B'$ is a fixed multiple of $T$ depending only on $D$, say $B'\le g_DT$. Then (25.1) gives $c_*HN_bB'/r\le c_*g_D\,c_D\,L$, which is at most $L/\sqrt D=\sigma$ when $c_D\le 1/(c_*g_D\sqrt D)$. This is the meaning of "$c_D$ sufficiently small": a positive constant that depends on $D$ only.

After the $J$ beats the paper records the following state (p. 97). The verification of each item uses only the three products bounded by $D^{0.03}$.

  • Frozen stations keep their charges. One occurrence can now hold a list of frozen stations. Each list has $D^{O(1)}$ members: its total scaled load is at most the final load bound $D^{0.03}B$, and each member has scaled load at least (floor $T/D^{O(1)}$) $\times$ (multiplier of order $B/(DT)$) $=B/D^{O(1)}$.
  • Each core station has arity at most $D^{0.03}\cdot O(D^{-10}T)\le D^{-9}T$.
  • The bundle unit is at least $D^{-0.03}\,T/D^{O(1)}=T/D^{O(1)}$.
  • The core degree is at most three.
  • The full host degree $\nabla$ satisfies $\log(2\nabla)\le D^{0.03}\cdot O(\log D)\le D^{0.04}$. The factor $O(\log D)$ is the logarithm of the initial full degree, which is polynomial in $D$ by conclusion 3 of Lemma 19.1.

Phase 3: restoring bounded degree. The next round needs a host of absolute bounded degree with one station per occurrence. Two features of the current state violate this. A frozen position holds a list of up to $D^{O(1)}$ stations. And a core station, although it has at most three core neighbours, may be joined by a tether (a core-to-frozen wire) to different frozen stations in different blocks, so its number of possible neighbours in the common host can be as large as $\nabla$. The paper routes the tethers through three stages of trees (Figure 11, p. 98).

  1. Split into units. At a frozen position a slot is reserved for each member of its list. From each frozen station a binary tree splits its active tether units, in order, into separate outlets, one per unit. There are $D^{O(1)}$ outlets, because the frozen arity is $O(DT)$ and the unit is at least $T/D^{O(1)}$. These trees have depth $O(\log D)$.
  2. Select destinations. From each outlet a binary tree has one leaf for each potential core endpoint of that outlet.
  3. Collect at endpoints. At each core endpoint a binary tree has one leaf for each potential incoming outlet. Corresponding leaves of the trees of stages 2 and 3 are linked.

An internal branching table is the identity on the concatenated strings of its active children. Inactive branches carry no station. The trees and slots are fixed on the common host; each block uses them with its own tether assignment. The trees of stages 2 and 3 have depth $O(\log(2\nabla)+\log D)=O(D^{0.04})$. The host grows by a factor depending only on $D$. A core endpoint keeps at most three core contacts and receives one tree contact, so the overall degree is at most four.

What Figure 11 shows. One frozen station on the left is joined by a thick line to a branch vertex under the heading "Split into units". Two lines leave it to two outlet vertices. Under "Select destinations", each outlet has a solid route to one of two collecting vertices and a dashed route that crosses to the other one. Under "Collect at endpoints", each collecting vertex is joined to one core station. The solid routes are the ones active in the block shown; the dashed routes exist in the host so that, in a different block, either outlet can reach the other endpoint. The caption states the three width regimes: the splitting tree may carry the full frozen arity for $O(\log D)$ levels; in the middle each active branch carries a single unit; at the right the width is bounded by the tether width at the receiving core station.

Gap fill — the two budgets of the tether trees. filled here
  • Linear budget. The tethers have total width at most $Z$. Each tether bit travels along one route of $O(\log D)+O(D^{0.04})$ tree vertices and adds a bounded amount to the arity of each. The extra arity is $O(Z\,D^{0.04})=O(L\,D^{-1/2+0.04})=O(L\,D^{-0.46})$.
  • Square budget, splitting trees. On one level of the splitting tree of a frozen station of arity $t_f$, the bundles are disjoint sets of its tether bits, of widths $w_1,w_2,\dots$ with $\sum w_i\le t_f$. A vertex that splits a bundle of width $w_i$ has arity $2w_i$. The squares on that level sum to $\sum(2w_i)^2\le 4(\sum w_i)^2\le 4t_f^2$. Summing over the frozen stations gives $4\sum_ft_f^2=O(LT)$ per level, and over $O(\log D)$ levels $O(LT\log D)$.
  • Square budget, all other vertices. Every other new vertex carries one unit or all tether bits of one core station, so its arity is at most $2D^{-9}T$. A set of stations with arities at most $y$ and total arity $Y_{\mathrm{tot}}$ has square sum at most $y\,Y_{\mathrm{tot}}$. For these vertices that is $2D^{-9}T\cdot O(L\,D^{-0.46})$. The old core stations have total arity at most $2X_{\mathrm{end}}+Z$ and arity at most $D^{-9}T$ each, so their square sum is at most $D^{-9}T\,(2X_{\mathrm{end}}+Z)$. Both are below $LT$.

The state at the end of a round. Collecting the frozen stations ($Z$), the old core ($2X_{\mathrm{end}}+Z$) and the trees ($O(ZD^{0.04})$), the charged arities $z_v$ at the end of the round satisfy

$$\begin{aligned}\sum_vz_v&=O\bigl(L\,D^{-2\epsilon}(\log D)^{O(1)}\bigr),\\ \sum_vz_v^2&=O\bigl(L\,T\,(\log D)^{O(1)}\bigr),\qquad\max_vz_v=O(DT).\end{aligned}\tag{25.2}$$

The constants and the powers of $\log D$ are independent of $D$. All charges respect a positive floor of order $T/D^{O(1)}$: the frozen stations keep the floor from conclusion 3 of Lemma 19.1, and every other remaining station meets a positive internal bundle of width at least $T/D^{O(1)}$.

The restart. Set $G=2^{\lfloor\epsilon\log D\rfloor}$ and choose a dyadic $q$ with $G^{1+\delta/4}\le q\le G^{1+\delta/3}$. The next round starts with

$$L'=L/G,\qquad T'=qT.\tag{25.3}$$
Gap fill — the invariants (23.2) hold with $(L',T')$. filled here Note $D^{\epsilon}/2\lt G\le D^{\epsilon}$. Let $C$ and $c$ be the constant and the exponent of $\log D$ in (25.2).
  1. A dyadic $q$ exists. The interval $[G^{1+\delta/4},G^{1+\delta/3}]$ has ratio $G^{\delta/12}$, which is at least $2$ when $G\ge 2^{12/\delta}$, that is, when $D$ is large in terms of $\delta,\epsilon$.
  2. First bound. $\sum z_v\le C\,L\,D^{-2\epsilon}(\log D)^c\le L/G$ holds when $C\,D^{-\epsilon}(\log D)^c\le 1$, because $1/G\ge D^{-\epsilon}$.
  3. Second bound. $L'T'=(q/G)\,LT\ge G^{\delta/4}\,LT$. The bound $\sum z_v^2\le C\,LT(\log D)^c\le L'T'$ holds when $(D^{\epsilon}/2)^{\delta/4}\ge C(\log D)^c$. A fixed positive power of $D$ exceeds a fixed power of $\log D$ for large $D$.
  4. Third bound. $\max z_v\le C\,DT\le D\,qT/2=DT'/2$ holds when $q\ge 2C$.
  5. The floor. The new charged arity is $\max(s_v,\theta T')$. Choose the dyadic constant $\theta$ so small that $\theta\,qT$ is at most the floor $T/D^{O(1)}$ already respected by all charges. Then replacing each charge by $\max(s_v,\theta T')$ does not increase it beyond the value used in items 2–4. The paper notes that no estimate used in choosing $G,q,D$ needs a lower bound on $\theta$, so this choice is not circular.
  6. The host. Its degree is at most four, each occurrence holds one station, and the external bits sit at the exterior identities.
Each condition is of the form "$D$ sufficiently large in terms of $k,\delta,\epsilon$". No explicit value of $D$ is available: the constants of Lemma 19.1 and of the beat come from compactness arguments in Parts I–III and in §24.1.
AHA. One round divides the total-arity budget $L$ by $G$ and multiplies the arity scale $T$ by $q\le G^{1+\delta/3}$. The square budget $LT$ grows only by $q/G\le G^{\delta/3}$. A reduction of the total arity by a factor $x$ therefore costs an increase of the largest arity by about $x^{1+\delta/3}$, for any $x$ that is a power of $G$. The final lookup charges the largest arity only to the power $p-1\lt 1$ (§27), so the exchange lowers the cost.

25.2 Iteration and the description of the final tables pp. 99–101

Fix the round just constructed: the constants $D$, $G$, $q$, and its number $s$ of grouping steps. To reduce the total charge by a factor $h^a$, the paper takes

$$j=\Bigl\lceil\frac{a\log h}{\log G}\Bigr\rceil,\qquad L_i=L_0G^{-i},\qquad T_i=T_0q^i\qquad(0\le i\le j),$$

where $L_i,T_i$ are the budgets after $i$ completed rounds. From the definition of $j$, $\ h^a\le G^j\lt G\,h^a$. Two parameters remain free: the group size $r$ and the initial number $S$ of blocks. A large $r$ makes the partially occupied packs inexpensive (requirement (25.1)). A large $S$ supplies enough blocks for all $sj$ grouping steps and leaves many blocks at the end, over which the fixed cost of the host is divided in §26.2.

Packing slack. By (23.1), a grouping step divides $N_b$ by $r$ and multiplies $H$ by at most $cr$, so the number $HN_b$ of allocated occurrences grows by at most $c$. The fixed expansions of the host inside a round are included in $c$. Starting from $H_0N_b=O(m\log(2+h))\cdot S$, at every stage

$$H\,N_b\le C^{\,j+1}\,m\,S\log h,\tag{25.4}$$

where $C$ depends only on the design of one round. With $L_0=C_0mT_0S\log(2+h)$ from (23.3), the ratio of (25.1) during the round that starts from $(L_i,T_i)$ satisfies

$$\frac{H\,N_b\,T_i}{r\,L_i}\le\frac{C_1\,C^{\,j+1}(qG)^i}{r}.$$

The paper chooses $r$ as the least power of two with $r\ge C_2(CqG)^{j+1}$, where the constant $C_2$ of the round design is large enough that the ratio is at most $c_D$ in every round and $r$ satisfies the lower bounds $r\ge c_*$ of Lemma 24.1 at every step.

Gap fill — the ratio, and the size of $r$. filled here Substituting (25.4), $T_i=T_0q^i$ and $L_i=L_0G^{-i}$: $$\frac{HN_bT_i}{rL_i}\le\frac{C^{j+1}mS\log h\cdot T_0q^i\,G^i}{r\,C_0\,m\,T_0\,S\log(2+h)}\le\frac{C^{j+1}(qG)^i}{C_0\,r}.$$ With $r\ge C_2(CqG)^{j+1}$ and $i\le j$ this is at most $1/(C_0C_2)$, which is at most $c_D$ for $C_2$ large.

Size of $r$: $j+1\le a\log h/\log G+2$, so $$\log_2r\le 1+\log_2C_2+\Bigl(\frac{a\log h}{\log G}+2\Bigr)\log_2(CqG)=a\,\frac{\log(CqG)}{\log G}\,\log_2h+O_D(1).$$ The ratio $\log(CqG)/\log G$ depends only on the round design. Hence $r=h^{O_D(a)+o(1)}$, and $r$ does not depend on $S$. The same computation gives $C^{j+1}=h^{O_D(a)+o(1)}$.

Exterior width. The $ed$ exposed bits are present in every round, and the round requires $ed\le L_i/D$. For $i\le j$,

$$\frac{ed}{L_i}=\frac{e\,G^i}{C_0\,m\log(2+h)}=O_D\Bigl(\frac{h^{-a}}{\log h}\Bigr).$$
Gap fill — this is the first use of the hypothesis $e\le mh^{-2a}$. filled here With $d=T_0S$ and $L_i=C_0mT_0S\log(2+h)\,G^{-i}$, the factors $T_0S$ cancel and the first equality follows. Then $e/m\le h^{-2a}$ and $G^i\le G^j\lt G\,h^a$ give $ed/L_i\lt G\,h^{-a}/(C_0\log(2+h))$. Since $G$ depends only on $D$, this is $O_D(h^{-a}/\log h)$, and it is at most $1/D$ for large $h$. Of the factor $h^{-2a}$ in the hypothesis, one factor $h^{-a}$ is cancelled because $L_i$ falls by up to $G^j\lt Gh^a$ during the macro; the remaining factor $h^{-a}$ tends to zero and is therefore below $1/(DG)$ for large $h$.

Copies. Let $R$ be the least power of two with $R\ge h^M$, for a fixed $M\gt 0$ chosen in §26.2. Take $S=R\,r^{sj}$. Each of the $sj$ grouping steps divides the number of blocks by $r$, so exactly $R$ blocks remain after $j$ rounds. Each initial block contains $T_0$ copies, so $d=T_0S$.

Gap fill — $\log d=O((\log h)^2)$. filled here $\log_2d=\log_2T_0+\log_2R+sj\log_2r$. The first two terms are $O(\log h)$. In the third, $s$ is a constant of the round design, $j=O(a\log h/\log G)$ and $\log_2r=O_D(a\log h)$. Hence $\log_2d=O_D((\log h)^2)$. Since $h=\lceil\log_2n\rceil$, this is $O((\log\log n)^2)=o(\log n)$, so $d=n^{o(1)}$. This is the bound $\log_2r_j=O((\log h)^2)$ on the copy factor of one macro that §27 quotes.

Terminal budgets. From $h^a\le G^j\lt Gh^a$, $q\le G^{1+\delta/3}$, $T_0=\Theta(h^b)$, (23.3) and (25.4):

$$\begin{aligned}L_j&=O\bigl(m\,d\,\log h\cdot h^{-a}\bigr),\qquad T_j=O\bigl(h^{\,b+(1+\delta/3)a}\bigr),\\ H\,N_b&\le m\,S\,h^{O_D(a)+o(1)}.\end{aligned}\tag{25.5}$$
Gap fill — the three bounds of (25.5). filled here
  • $L_j=L_0G^{-j}\le C\,mT_0S\log(2+h)\,h^{-a}=O(md\log h\cdot h^{-a})$, using $G^j\ge h^a$ and $d=T_0S$.
  • $T_j=T_0q^j\le 8h^b\,G^{(1+\delta/3)j}\lt 8h^b\,(G\,h^a)^{1+\delta/3}=O_D\bigl(h^{b+(1+\delta/3)a}\bigr)$. The constant depends on $D$ through $G^{1+\delta/3}$.
  • $HN_b\le C^{j+1}mS\log h=mS\,h^{O_D(a)+o(1)}$ by (25.4) and the previous box. The paper states that the coefficient in $O_D(a)$ is independent of $M,a,b,\beta$.
The exponent $(1+\delta/3)a$ of $T_j$ is below the exponent $(1+\delta)a$ allowed by Lemma 23.1. The difference $2\delta a/3$ absorbs the constant depending on $D$ and the additional fields of a dictionary call in §26.2.

One working field. The scales $T_i$ range from $T_0\ge 4h^\beta$ to a fixed power of $h$. The paper chooses one extension $K/k$ whose degree is divisible by the degree of the fixed extension required by Lemma 19.1 and large enough for the cardinality requirement at the largest scale $T_j$. Its degree is $O(\log h)$, which is $O(\log T_i)$ for every $i$ because $\log T_i\ge\beta\log h$.

Descriptions of tables. Each occurrence carries a label for its table, the widths of its bundles, and their positions in its port order. An incident host edge is named by its place in the local neighbour list, which costs a number of bits bounded in terms of $D$. The paper bounds the total length of these local data per occurrence by

$$C_3^{\,j+1}(\log h)^{O(1)}=h^{O_D(a)+o(1)}.\tag{25.6}$$

The argument is a recursion on label length. At initialization a stacked station is specified by its original table label, its stack length and the locations of its ordered sublists, in $(\log h)^{O(1)}$ bits. Within a round, two facts bound the number of old labels that enter one new label.

  • Concentration. A colony of capacity $DT_i$ contains at most $D/\theta$ live entering stations, since each is charged at least $\theta T_i$. By conclusion 4 of Lemma 19.1, each output description uses a bounded number of old labels, discrete choices and field elements; the additional description length is $O_D(\log h)$.
  • Subrounds and resets. The load per leaf is at most a fixed multiple (depending on $D$) of $T_i$, and the number of leaves contributing to one resulting pack is bounded in terms of $D$. Each live core station contributes at least one internal unit of width $u\ge T_i/\mathrm{poly}(D)$, and each frozen station has a charged load with a lower bound of the same kind. Scalar stations are withdrawn when they form. Hence a contributing pack has a bounded number of old labels and of units, and the lists of unit pairings, rotor layers and ordered sublists at a port have a bounded number of entries, each of $O_D(\log h+\log r)$ bits. The same holds for the routing on the tether trees.

So if $A$ bounds the label length before one step, the bound after the step is $C_4A+C_4(\log h)^{C_5}$. The multiplicative constant $C_4$ counts old labels; it does not depend on the field degree or on the length of the input labels. Over the $sj$ steps the bound is multiplied by $C_4^{sj}=C_3^{\,j}$ with $C_3=C_4^{\,s}$, which gives (25.6); the second expression follows as for $C^{j+1}$ above. Global host addresses are used only for the control of a whole position and are not appended to table descriptions.

Gap fill — why the floor $\theta T$ is needed for (25.6). filled here Without a lower bound on the charge of a station, a cell of capacity $DT_i$ could contain an unbounded number of stations of arbitrarily small arity. Each has its own label, and the label of a pack lists the labels of its members. The floor in $z_v=\max(s_v,\theta T)$ bounds the number of members by $D/\theta$, so one step multiplies label length by a constant. A constant factor per step, over $sj=O(\log h)$ steps, is a factor $h^{O_D(a)}$: small when $a$ is small. This is one of the reasons why Lemma 23.1 holds only for small $a$.
Not verified — the constants behind (25.4) and (25.6). not verified Displays (25.4) and (25.6) on pp. 99–100 rest on two assertions that the tutorial reports and did not re-derive. First, that every step of a round (the concentration of Lemma 19.1, each subround, each reset, the tether trees) enlarges the host by a factor at most $cr$ with $c$ depending only on the round design; the parts of this claim that belong to §24 are marked there. Second, that one step multiplies the label length by a constant $C_4$ that depends only on $D$: the paper's two bullet points give the reason (a bounded number of old labels per new label), but the bound on the number of contributing leaves and units per pack is stated, not computed. The paper also states without computation that the coefficient in the exponent $O_D(a)$ of (25.5) is independent of $M,a,b,\beta$; §26.2 needs this independence when it chooses $a$.

The tensor represented by the sum. After $j$ rounds each term of the expansion consists of $R$ surviving blocks and the scalar factors withdrawn along its history. The paper records why the sum of all terms is the $d$th power of the input tensor on its $ed$ named exterior bits (p. 100): regrouping permutes the exterior lists along with their stations and preserves each product entry by entry; each local tensor identity replaces a product by a sum with the same entries on the named ports; colourings, packings and routings follow fixed tie-breaking rules and so do not index the expansion. Only the choices in local tensor identities index the terms. The internal wires of the final $R$ blocks are summed when the blocks are contracted.

One dictionary. For the evaluation the paper uses a single dictionary. A key records the type, stage, description and arity of a table, followed by its incident bits; length fields or fixed padding make the encoding unique. The input dictionary and the rules of the construction assign a value in $K$ to each valid key; values at unused keys are arbitrary. A key for a withdrawn scalar has no incident bits. By the maximum-arity invariant and (25.6), a longest key has length

$$O_D(T_j)+h^{O_D(a)+o(1)}.$$

A derived table description can be longer than the $(\log h)^{O(1)}$ bits that Lemma 23.1 allows for a label. The paper resolves this in §26: the description is placed in the argument of a dictionary call, and the label of the installed table names only the key format and length. The description then counts toward the arity of the lookup (which Lemma 23.1 bounds by $h^{b+(1+\delta)a}$), and the label stays short for the next macro.

26. Realizing the expansion on a fixed network pp. 101–106

Role in the chain. Sections 23–25 bound each summand of the expansion separately: after $j$ rounds a summand consists of $R$ blocks with total charged arity $L_j$ and arity scale $T_j$ (display (25.5)), and each table has a description of length $h^{O_D(a)+o(1)}$ (display (25.6)). Different summands have different wires and different tables. This section represents the sum of all summands by one network that does not depend on the summand. Subsection 26.1 proves Lemma 26.1 (Realizing a transcript), a general statement about distributed checkable computations; it uses the wide-record bound of Theorem 16.1(2) (§16) and a construction imported from [42]. Subsection 26.2 describes the rounds as such a computation, bounds its cost in display (26.2), and completes the proof of Lemma 23.1 on p. 106. Lemma 23.1 is then used in §27.

Notation in force: $k$ is a finite field with $\omega_k\lt 9/4$, $K\supseteq k$ the working extension of degree $O(\log h)$, $h=\lceil\log_2n\rceil$, and logarithms are binary. The input network of the macro has size at most $m$ (with $n\le m\le n^{1.1}$), arity at most $h^b$, and $e\le mh^{-2a}$ external bits. From §25.2: $T_0\in[4h^b,8h^b]$ copies per initial block, $S=Rr^{sj}$ initial blocks, $d=T_0S$ copies in total, $R\ge h^M$ blocks at the end, $H$ host positions and $N_b$ blocks at the current stage.

AHA. A tensor network already contains a summation: the sum over the values of its internal wires. If the choice of summand is written on internal wires, and $0/1$ gates check that the written data are a valid encoding with exactly one encoding per summand, then the network's own contraction performs the sum over summands. The remaining task is to make the checking gates cheap.

26.1 From a transcript to a tensor pp. 101–103

Definition (processors, records, messages, transcript tensor, charge). p. 101 paper Fix $P\le n^{O(1)}$ numbered processors. Each processor has binary advice of at most $h^c$ bits and a fixed ordered list of exterior ports. Its local record includes guesses and at most $h^c$ incoming and outgoing messages. A message consists of an $O(h)$-bit key identifying source, destination and port, together with a bit string. Communication is valid if every used key occurs exactly once at each end with the same string.

One fixed finite bit-RAM program checks the local record against the advice and the exterior bits. The program may emit strings of length at most $U$ as arguments to a common dictionary $W$ with values in $K$. Its decisions depend only on the strings, not on the values of $W$. A transcript is the collection of all local records. It is accepted if every local check passes and communication is valid. For fixed exterior bits, the transcript tensor is the sum, over accepted transcripts, of the product of all values returned by $W$. The record format, including length fields and padding, is fixed; all size-dependent program data enter through the advice.

The charge at a processor counts its deterministic running time, advice, exterior bits, guesses, messages with headers, and emitted strings, with at least one unit per record or item. The charge is determined by the record. A local address or time uses $(\log h)^{O(1)}$ bits; bit arithmetic on words of that length adds at most a factor $(\log h)^{O(1)}$ to the bounds.

Lemma 26.1 (Realizing a transcript). pp. 101–102 paper Suppose the charge of each local record is at most $V_{\max}=2^{(\log h)^{O(1)}}$. Let $V\le n^{O(1)}$ bound the total charge of every accepted transcript and the volume of the fixed advice and exterior interface, including one unit per processor. If $U\le h$ and the wide-record bound of Theorem 16.1 holds, then for every fixed $\tau\gt 0$ the transcript tensor has a fixed network of size at most $$h^{\tau}\,V+P\,h^{C_0}.$$ The gates are trivalent or are dictionary tables of arity $O(1+U+\log h)$. Their labels use $(\log h)^{O(1)}$ bits. The constant $C_0$ depends only on $\tau,c$, the polynomial bounds in $n$ and the wide-record estimate. The exponent in $V_{\max}$ occurs only in factors $(\log h)^{O(1)}$ before absorption.

The bound has two terms with different roles. The term $h^\tau V$ is nearly linear in the total charge: work that is proportional to the data costs only the factor $h^\tau$. The term $Ph^{C_0}$ is a fixed overhead per processor with a possibly large exponent. Subsection 26.2 makes the second term small by having few processors relative to the data.

Background — a checked computation with guesses as a tensor network. filled here A Boolean circuit becomes a tensor network when each gate is replaced by its $0/1$ truth table (value $1$ exactly when the output wire equals the gate function of the input wires) and each fan-out by an equality tensor. For fixed inputs there is exactly one assignment of the internal wires with weight $1$; all others have weight $0$. If the output is pinned to "accept", the contraction is $1$ on accepted inputs and $0$ otherwise.

Now leave some input wires unpaired with the exterior and sum over them: these are guesses. The contraction counts the guesses that the circuit accepts. If, in addition, one-bit or table gates with values in $K$ are attached to some wires, the contraction is the sum over accepted guesses of the product of those values. This is the form of the transcript tensor: guesses are the records, the $0/1$ gates are the checks, and the $K$-valued gates are the dictionary calls. Exactness requires that each accepted transcript corresponds to exactly one assignment of all internal wires. Every step of the proof below includes a uniqueness clause for this reason. The $0/1$ tables are tensors over the prime field, hence over $k$ and over $K$.

Imported — [42, Section 16.2]. imported The paper states (p. 102) that the check of a memory trace used in the proof is "the trace construction of OpenAI [42, Section 16.2]". As the paper uses it: a guessed execution of a RAM program is verified by (i) time-ordered checks of instructions, registers, counter, addresses and halt flag, and (ii) a check of memory consistency in which the reads, writes and initial contents are sorted by address and execution order, and a scan of each address verifies that a read returns the most recent value, starting at zero for unused cells. The tutorial does not reproduce [42, Section 16.2].
Background — sorting by a comparison network. filled here A comparison network on $N$ records is a fixed sequence of compare-exchange operations on fixed pairs of positions. The positions do not depend on the data, so the network is a fixed circuit. Batcher's network sorts $N$ records with $O(N\log^2N)$ comparators (the paper cites it in Proposition 2.3 and uses the count $O(t\log^2(2t))$ here). A comparator on words of $\omega$ bits is $O(\omega)$ Boolean gates. To use an unstable sorting network where the order of equal keys matters, one appends a distinguishing field (here the execution time) to the key. The cost per record is the factor $\log^2N$ times the word length. This is affordable when $\log N$ is $(\log h)^{O(1)}$, as inside one slot below, and not affordable when $\log N$ is of order $h$, as for the global matching of messages.

Step 1: allocate space by record length (p. 102). The network is fixed before the transcript is known, so it cannot give each processor exactly the space its record needs. The paper reserves slots in dyadic size classes. For each dyadic capacity $t$, from $1$ to the least power of two at least $\min(V,V_{\max})$, it reserves $N_t=\min(P,\lfloor 2V/t\rfloor)$ slots. A processor with charge $s$ uses the least $t\ge s$. The network guesses, for each slot, a processor identifier and a record; it checks the identifier range and the charge, requires the occupied slots to form an increasing prefix in identifier order (within each capacity class, in this tutorial's reading), and pins all unused entries and padding. The two capacity bounds are

$$\sum_tt\,N_t=O\bigl(V(1+\log V_{\max})\bigr),\qquad\sum_tN_t=O\bigl(P(1+\log V_{\max})\bigr).\tag{26.1}$$

Step 2: check the computation and its dictionary arguments (p. 102). In a slot of capacity $t$ the network guesses the deterministic execution of the checking program for at most $t$ steps and verifies it by the trace construction of [42, Section 16.2]: time-ordered checks of the processor state, and a memory check by sorting the accesses. A comparison network on $O(t)$ accesses uses $O(t\log^2(2t))$ comparisons of $(\log h)^{O(1)}$-bit words. Long inputs, advice, messages and emitted strings occupy addressed arrays and are processed in pieces. The local cost is $t(\log h)^{O(1)}$ plus $h^{O(1)}$ per slot for long identifiers and framing.

The dictionary calls are installed as follows. Within the slot, the emitted strings are grouped by least dyadic padded length $u$ (an empty string counts as length one). The slot reserves $\lfloor 2t/u\rfloor$ seats in that class and fills them in order of issuance. The checker writes each emitted bit at the address given by its length class, ordinal and bit offset; the same memory-trace test compares these arrays with the inputs of the installed seats, including active flags, actual lengths and zero padding. Each address has $O(1+\log t+\log h)$ bits. A seat returns the value of $W$ on its string when active and returns $1$ when inactive. Its table is named by the padded length. The arity of a seat is $O(1+U+\log h)$, and the total table size per slot is $O(t\log(2+U)\log(2+h))$.

Step 3: match the messages (pp. 102–103). Fix $w=\lceil h^{C_1}\rceil$ with $C_1\gt 1$ chosen below. Each message is split into $w$-bit chunks; the last chunk is padded, and an empty message gets one chunk. A chunk record contains an active flag, the key, the direction (send or receive), the total length, the chunk number and the payload. For each processor a fixed sender with a key in a reserved namespace supplies that processor's advice and exterior bits with their component lengths; an occupied slot must receive the whole supply for its claimed identifier, even when the supply is empty.

Local checks compare each outgoing source and incoming destination with the slot identifier and verify the splitting, piece order, lengths and padding. Then all chunk records of all slots are sorted by active flag, key, chunk number and direction. In the active part every key and chunk number must occur as one send and one receive with equal length and payload; a scan of adjacent records checks this. A duplicate message on one side produces a collision at chunk zero. In particular each private supply is matched to exactly one slot, so every processor occurs exactly once among the slots.

The sort is the stable-sort tensor of Theorem 16.1(2). For a given input the stable-sort relation has exactly one output, so the sorted list can be an internal (summed) set of wires without creating multiplicity. Its $0/1$ tensor over $k$ is the same relation over $K$.

Step 4: count (p. 103). A slot of capacity $t$ reserves $O(t/w+h^c+1)$ chunk records. The $O(h)$-bit header is copied once per chunk, so constructing the records costs $(t+h^{O(1)})(\log h)^{O(1)}$. A fixed sender is charged for its actual supply and one partial chunk. By (26.1) the reserved bit volume, including padding at message boundaries, is

$$V(\log h)^{O(1)}+P\,h^{O(1)}.$$

The list is padded to a power of two. Its length is polynomial in $n$, so the logarithm of its length is $O(h)$. Theorem 16.1(2) with tolerance $\tau/3$ gives an exponent $a_{\tau/3}$; a fixed $C_1$ large enough makes $w$ at least the required power of the logarithm of the list length, and then sorting costs at most $O(h^{\tau/3})$ times the volume. The scan after the sort has linear size. By (26.1) the local computations and the dictionary tables have the same volume bound. The powers of $\log h$ are absorbed into $h^\tau$ and the per-processor costs into $Ph^{C_0}$.

Step 5: exactness. The checking and sorting gates are trivalent over $k$. Each dictionary gate is named by its length class, in $(\log h)^{O(1)}$ bits. For each accepted transcript, the slot capacities, identifiers, order, traces, calls and chunks are uniquely determined; conversely the local and communication checks recover an accepted transcript from any assignment of nonzero weight. Contracting the network therefore gives the weighted sum over accepted transcripts, exactly, over $K$.

∎
Gap fill — the slot counts and (26.1). filled here
  • $N_t$ slots suffice. In an accepted transcript the processors of class $t\ge 2$ have charge greater than $t/2$, and those of class $1$ have charge $1$. The total charge is at most $V$, so class $t$ contains fewer than $2V/t$ processors when $t\ge 2$ and at most $V$ when $t=1$; in both cases at most $\lfloor 2V/t\rfloor$. It also contains at most $P$.
  • First bound. $tN_t\le 2V$ for each class, and the number of classes is at most $2+\log_2\min(V,V_{\max})\le 2+\log_2V_{\max}$.
  • Second bound. $N_t\le P$ for each class.
  • Why dyadic classes. Reserving the maximum $V_{\max}$ for each of $P$ processors would cost $PV_{\max}$. The dyadic scheme costs $O(V\log V_{\max})$ in capacity, which is $V(\log h)^{O(1)}$ because $\log V_{\max}=(\log h)^{O(1)}$. The bound $V_{\max}$ enters the proof in two places, each time as a factor $(\log h)^{O(1)}$: the number of capacity classes here, and the factor $\log^2(2t)$ of the comparison network inside a slot of capacity $t\le 2V_{\max}$ in Step 2. This is the content of the last sentence of the lemma.
  • Why the increasing prefix. Without an ordering rule, permuting the processors among the slots of one class would give several internal assignments for the same transcript, and the contraction would count the transcript several times. The rule "occupied slots form an increasing prefix in identifier order" leaves one assignment.
Gap fill — why messages are cut into chunks of $h^{C_1}$ bits. filled here Matching sends with receives is a global operation on a list of $N=n^{O(1)}$ records, so $\log_2N=O(h)$. A comparison network would cost a factor $\log^2N=O(h^2)$ per record bit, which exceeds the allowed factor $h^\tau$. Theorem 16.1(2) states: for every $\zeta\gt 0$ there is $a_\zeta\gt 1$ such that stable sorting of $N=2^{h'}$ records of $w\ge h'^{\,a_\zeta}$ bits has an exact trivalent network of size $O_\zeta(N\,w\,h'^{\,\zeta})$. The cost per record bit is then $h'^{\,\zeta}$, but only for wide records. With $h'=O(h)$ and $\zeta=\tau/3$, the choice $w=\lceil h^{C_1}\rceil$ with $C_1\gt a_{\tau/3}$ makes the records wide enough for large $h$. The key, flag, direction, length and chunk number add $O(h)$ bits to a chunk of $w\ge h$ bits, a constant factor. The price of chunking is padding: at most $w=h^{O(1)}$ bits per message, and a slot has at most $h^c$ messages. That is the term $h^{O(1)}$ per slot, which enters $Ph^{C_0}$ through the second bound of (26.1).
Gap fill — why $U\le h$ and why inactive seats return $1$. filled here A dictionary gate is the only non-trivalent gate. Its arity $O(1+U+\log h)$ is the quantity that Lemma 23.1 must bound by $h^{b+(1+\delta)a}$ for the next macro, so the lemma keeps $U$ explicit; the hypothesis $U\le h$ keeps addresses and length fields within $O(\log h)$ bits. The number of calls issued by a record is not known in advance, so seats are reserved by the same dyadic rule as slots: strings of padded length $u$ have length greater than $u/2$ (or $u=1$), and their total length is at most the charge $t$, so there are at most $\lfloor 2t/u\rfloor$ of them. An unused seat must not change the product of dictionary values; returning $1$ achieves this.

26.2 The transcript of the rounds pp. 103–106

The paper now describes the construction of §23–§25 as a transcript. Initially there are $S$ blocks of $T_0$ copies. At every stage of the $j$ rounds there are $N_b\ge R$ blocks on a host with $H$ allocated positions. Each position gets one processor at each stage, including the terminal stage, and each step gets $O(H)$ additional processors for balanced aggregation trees. An occurrence is one position in one block.

The cost analysis separates three kinds of data, with three different unit costs. The paper states the principle in the introduction of the section (p. 101): "All blocks at one host position use the same address. The bits carried on a wire vary independently across blocks."

One address for all blocks (p. 103). A position holds the ordered list of its host neighbours and an array of occurrence records in block order. Its advice specifies the parent and child naming conventions, integer parameters, the presentation of the field $K$, and the initial convention for ordering exterior ports; at an initial position it also gives the input table label. Exterior bits enter through fixed ports in stack order.

In one step every host degree is bounded in terms of the round design, so a ball of radius $O_D(1+\log r)$ contains $r^{O_D(1)}$ positions. Positions exchange neighbour lists to that radius, remove repetitions by identifier, and order the names. After this, each possible child and contact is located by an index in a local list; the cost is $h^{O(1)}$ common operations per position. A message to a contact can then carry the entire array of occurrence records, for all blocks, under one $O(h)$-bit header, and inside the array only local indices are needed. There are $h^{O(1)}$ phases and declarations per position. By (25.6) the handling of the arrays costs

$$N_b\,r^{O_D(1)}(\log h)^{O(1)}h^{O_D(a)}=N_b\,h^{O_D(a)+o(1)}$$

per position. The paper states that the coefficients in $O_D(a)$ are independent of $a,b,\beta$.

Decisions shared by the host (pp. 103–104). Several choices in a round are common to all blocks and are made by minimizing a score that is a sum of local contributions: the multiplier of the colony count (24.1), the affine colouring, the layout of a subround, the mixing matrix of a reset. Each position computes its local contributions to the scores of all candidates. The integer arrays are added along a balanced tree, and the first minimizing candidate is broadcast. At height $i$ of the tree a sum of entries that initially had $z$ bits has at most $z+i$ bits, while the number of tree vertices falls by a factor of order $2^i$; since $\sum_{i\ge 0}2^{-i}(z+i)^{O(1)}=O((1+z)^{O(1)})$, the aggregation costs no more than the leaves, up to a constant. At the root the paper allows $2^{(\log h)^{O(1)}}$ additional operations to choose the minimizer. A greedy colouring of a host (needed by conclusion 4 of Lemma 19.1) is checked locally: in fixed identifier order, each colour must be the least one absent from the lower-numbered neighbours; induction on identifiers shows that exactly one assignment passes the check.

Checking the changes of host (pp. 104–105). The paper lists how each operation of a round obtains its contacts from the neighbour lists and its common parameters from additive scores.

  1. Colony packing (§24.2). Try the multipliers of the $r$ slots, propagate the overloaded marks to ancestors and neighbours, and sum the candidate colony counts. A propagation moves upward by at least one dyadic level, so the radius is $O_D(1+\log r)$. Relative interval endpoints, loads and dyadically scaled charges have short encodings. Summing local scores also chooses the affine colouring for the whole-item pairing.
  2. Local concentration (§25.1). The colony interval and its old contacts give the possible neighbours of each sphere (the sphere forms of §19, one family per cell, are the objects on which the proof of Lemma 19.1 operates). In the checked colour order, the movement and interpolation choices of Lemma 19.1 specify the new tables, withdrawn scalars and ordered bundles. The description bounds of Lemma 19.1 (its conclusion 4) pay for the choices and labels. The port orders identify the exposed sublists.
  3. Wire-saving subround (§24.2). The source colony and bin determine the seats of its matched units. One summand choice is recorded for each actual gadget. The surviving replacement widths under each of the two contractions are summed, and the same minimizer is taken for all blocks. Bounded neighbourhoods of the colonies determine the new contacts, port orders and table labels.
  4. Reset (§24.3). Each position computes its mean load over the blocks. The smoothed mean has floor $FB/r$, and a propagated contribution decays by at least a factor of two per edge; since the starting load is at most $B$, a neighbourhood of radius $O(1+\log r)$ determines the bases and their capacities. The common mixing matrix is chosen as described in the next box. Positions permute their block arrays accordingly. Bounded length ratios of intersecting leaves and the bounded number of units per leaf allow the terminal assignments, rotor settings and new labels to be determined from neighbouring records.
  5. Tether trees (§25.1). The final splitting trees have bounded size, depending on $D$, per possible station and contact. Adjacent positions determine the active sublists. An edge between new children is supported by a bounded path between their parents, so every neighbourhood that is collected is local to its current step.

Messages are declared in a fixed order of phases, ports and blocks. Each successive array and host is enforced from the preceding data and the local summand choices. Each term of a local identity, including a term of weight zero, has one prescribed encoding, so the intermediate arrays introduce no further multiplicities.

Gap fill — choosing the mixing matrix with integer arithmetic. filled here In §24.3 the existence of a good invertible matrix over $\mathbb F_2$ is shown by averaging an estimator $\Phi$. The fixed network must compute a matrix that is at least as good as the average. The paper's method (pp. 104–105):
  1. Index the blocks by $i\in\mathbb F_2^{\ell}$, $\ell=\log_2N_b$. Let $x_i$ be the normalized load at a position, or the normalized width at an edge, in block $i$. Two indices $i,i'$ have the same output prefix of length $s$ under a matrix $A$ exactly when $z=i+i'$ satisfies $(Az)_{1:s}=0$. So the part of the collision estimator at prefix length $s$ that depends on $A$ is a multiple of $$\sum_{z\ne 0}\mathbf 1_{(Az)_{1:s}=0}\ \sum_ix_ix_{i+z}.$$ The diagonal and centering terms do not depend on $A$ and are omitted.
  2. The autocorrelations $c(z)=\sum_ix_ix_{i+z}$ are computed for all $z$ at once by the Walsh transform $\hat x(\xi)=\sum_i(-1)^{\langle\xi,i\rangle}x_i$. Indeed $\sum_z(-1)^{\langle\xi,z\rangle}c(z)=\sum_{i,i'}(-1)^{\langle\xi,i+i'\rangle}x_ix_{i'}=\hat x(\xi)^2$, so $c$ is the inverse transform of $\hat x^{\,2}$. The fast transform uses $O(N_b\log N_b)$ additions.
  3. The $x_i$ have denominators $FB$ times a fixed dyadic load denominator, and the weights of the estimator are integers after fixed scaling. After clearing denominators, all arithmetic is on integers of $(\log h)^{O(1)}$ bits. The paper notes that the choice therefore "uses integer arithmetic in every characteristic": the scores are computed by Boolean circuits, not in the field $K$.
  4. The coefficients are aggregated over positions by difference $z$ and prefix length. At the root all invertible matrices in dimension $\ell$ are enumerated. Since $N_b\le S$ and $\log_2S=O((\log h)^2)$, there are at most $2^{\ell^2}=2^{O((\log h)^4)}$ matrices, within the allowance $2^{(\log h)^{O(1)}}$ for the root. The first minimizer has score at most the mean, which is the bound (24.2).

Independent bits and tensor entries (p. 105). The exterior bits are transferred in the order specified by the splits and concatenations. Source and destination share one processor header for all pieces sent along one contact; array positions and bit offsets have $(\log h)^{O(1)}$ bits. A station that collects exterior bits is frozen during the following steps, and when blocks are reordered its lists are permuted with them. Each of the $ed$ exterior bits travels only along its specified route, at most once per transfer, and there are $O(\log h)$ steps, so the exterior payload costs $ed\,(\log h)^{O(1)}$.

At the terminal stage the network guesses one bit per internal wire at its designated endpoint and sends it to the other endpoint in contact and bundle order. A table call consists of the station description followed by its ordered port bits. By (25.5) the terminal payload costs $L_j(\log h)^{O(1)}$. A scalar withdrawn earlier issues its call at withdrawal, with only its short description. The scalar stations of the input are fixed: if their product is $c$, one nullary table of value $c^{\,d}$ supplies their contribution; naming this element of $K$ costs $O(\log h)$ bits.

The paper then states the correctness of the whole: for fixed summand choices, the free payload guesses range exactly over the assignments of the terminal wires, and the product of the call values is the contraction weight of that assignment. Summing over these assignments and then over summands gives the $d$th tensor power of the input on its ordered $ed$ exterior bits.

Size of the fixed network (pp. 105–106). By (25.5), $HN_b\le mS\,h^{O_D(a)+o(1)}$ at each stage. There are $O(\log h)$ steps and $N_b\ge R$. The number of processors and the total charge satisfy

$$\begin{aligned}P&\le m\,S\,h^{O_D(a)+o(1)}/R,\\ V&\le P\,h^{C_2}+m\,S\,h^{O_D(a)+o(1)}+(L_j+ed)\,h^{o(1)}+2^{(\log h)^{O(1)}}.\end{aligned}\tag{26.2}$$

The paper explains the four terms of $V$: the first pays for the common work per position, the second for the short records per occurrence, the third for the independent wire bits, the fourth for the computations at the roots. Every local record has charge at most $2^{(\log h)^{O(1)}}$, because $N_b\le S$, $\log S=O((\log h)^2)$, and one occurrence has polynomially many bits in $h$; so the hypothesis on $V_{\max}$ in Lemma 26.1 holds. The paper states that $C_2$ and the exponent bounding the number of declarations can be fixed independently of the exponent $M$ in $R=h^{M+o(1)}$; the dependence on $M$ is confined to factors polynomial in $\log S$ and to the additive root cost.

The final choice of constants and the end of the proof of Lemma 23.1 (p. 106). The longest dictionary call has $O_D(T_j)+h^{O_D(a)+o(1)}$ bits. The paper chooses $a$ so small that $Ca+2a\lt\beta$ for each coefficient $C$ in the short-work and label bounds, and $b+(1+\delta)a\lt 1$ for all allowed $b$. It applies Lemma 26.1 with $\tau=a\delta/3$ and then takes $M$ large. The costs per $md$, before the factor $h^\tau$, of the three charges of (26.2) that do not involve $P$ are

$$\frac{m\,S\,h^{Ca+o(1)}}{md}=h^{-b+Ca+o(1)},\qquad\frac{ed}{md}\le h^{-2a},\qquad\frac{L_j}{md}=O(h^{-a}\log h).$$

The paper concludes that the fixed network has size at most $md\,h^{-a+\tau+o(1)}\le md\,h^{-(1-\delta)a}$ for large $n$, that its maximum table arity, including activity and length fields, is at most $h^{b+(1+\delta)a}$, and that a dictionary table keeps its short length-class label. This completes the proof of Lemma 23.1.

Gap fill — the five cost classes and the arithmetic of the last step. filled here Lemma 26.1 gives size at most $h^\tau V+Ph^{C_0}$. Divide each term of (26.2) by $md=m\,T_0S$, with $T_0\ge 4h^b$, $b\ge\beta$, and $Ca+2a\lt\beta$.
Cost classTermPer $md$Why it is at most $h^{-a+o(1)}$

The largest of the five is the terminal wire bits, $O(h^{-a+o(1)})$. Multiplying by $h^{\tau}$ with $\tau=a\delta/3$: $$\text{size}\le md\,h^{-a+a\delta/3+o(1)}\le md\,h^{-(1-\delta)a}$$ for large $n$, since the exponents differ by $2a\delta/3\gt 0$.

Arity. By (25.5), $T_j=O_D(h^{b+(1+\delta/3)a})$. The description part of a call has $h^{Ca+o(1)}$ bits with $Ca\lt\beta\le b$, which is of smaller order. So the call length is $U=O_D(h^{b+(1+\delta/3)a})$, and the gate arity $O(1+U+\log h)$ is at most $h^{b+(1+\delta)a}$ for large $h$; the margin is again $h^{2a\delta/3}$. The hypothesis $U\le h$ of Lemma 26.1 holds because $b+(1+\delta)a\lt 1$.

Where the smallness of $a$ is used. Two conditions: $Ca+2a\lt\beta$ in the first two rows of the table, and $b+(1+\delta)a\lt 1$ for $U\le h$. The threshold is uniform in $b\in[\beta,1/2]$ because the first condition involves only $\beta$ and the second holds for all $b\le 1/2$ once $(1+\delta)a\lt 1/2$. The coefficients $C$ depend on $D$, and $D$ depends on $k,\delta$ (through $\epsilon$), which matches the statement of Lemma 23.1 that the threshold for $a$ depends only on $k,\delta,\beta$.

Not verified — the per-operation bounds behind (26.2). not verified Display (26.2) on p. 105 depends on assertions of pp. 103–105 that the tutorial reports and did not re-derive in detail:
  • that every decision of the rounds (items 1–5 above) is computed by one fixed bit-RAM program from data within host distance $O_D(1+\log r)$, with $h^{O(1)}$ common operations per position and $N_bh^{O_D(a)+o(1)}$ operations for the block arrays;
  • that the exponent $C_2$ and the number of declarations per position are independent of $M$;
  • that the coefficients hidden in $O_D(a)$ are independent of $a,b,\beta$ (the choice "$Ca+2a\lt\beta$" needs this);
  • that each summand of the expansion has exactly one accepted transcript for each assignment of its terminal wires (the paper asserts this through its statements about prescribed encodings and fixed tie-breaking rules).
The paper gives these as statements with short justifications. A complete verification would require the full specification of the checking program, which the paper does not write out. The arithmetic that follows from (26.2) is verified in the box above.

27. Trivalent compression and the exponent pp. 106–107

Role in the chain. This section receives three results: Lemma 23.1 (Compression of a tensor power, p. 86; one application is called a macro; its proof occupies §23–§26), Lemma 16.2 (Batched table evaluation, p. 59; §16), and the sorting exponent $p$ of Theorem 16.1(1) (p. 59). It delivers Lemma 27.1 (Descent of a trivalent network), which removes the field extension, and the proof of Theorem 2.1 (Compression of tensor powers), which §2 states on p. 7 without proof. Proposition 2.3 (p. 8) applies Theorem 2.1 a fixed number of times to a sorting network for list distinctness and contradicts Theorem 2.2 (p. 7). That contradiction is the paper's argument for $\omega_k\ge 9/4$ over every finite field $k$. Lemma 2.5 (p. 9) transfers the bound to every field, and the upper bound of prior work then gives Theorem 1.1.

Throughout, $k$ is a finite field with $\omega_k\lt 9/4$ (the hypothesis that the paper refutes), $n$ is a large integer, $h=\lceil\log_2 n\rceil$, and logarithms are binary. A table network over a field $K$ has stations joined by wires that each carry one bit; a station of arity $s$ carries a table $\{0,1\}^s\to K$; the network represents the tensor obtained by multiplying the selected table entries and summing over all values of the internal wires. Its size is the number of stations plus the sum of their arities. A network is trivalent when every arity is at most three. For a trivalent network the size in Theorem 2.1 counts the vertices and the exterior bits; this count and the table-network size differ by a factor of at most $4$. These definitions and the factor are given in full in §23.

The section has three parts: the three statements it combines, Lemma 27.1 with its proof, and the proof of Theorem 2.1. The proof of Theorem 2.1 is exponent bookkeeping. It is written out below with every parameter, the constraint on it, and the order of choice.

The three statements that are combined

Theorem 2.1 (Compression of tensor powers), recalled from §2. p. 7 paper Suppose $\omega_k\lt 9/4$. For every $\eta\gt 0$ there is a $\gamma\gt 0$ such that the following holds for all sufficiently large $n$. Write $h=\lceil\log_2 n\rceil$. If a trivalent $k$-network of size $m\le n^{1.05}$ with $e\ge n$ exterior bits represents $V$, then for some integer $d\ge 1$ its power $V^{\otimes d}$ has a trivalent $k$-network with $de$ distinct exterior bits and size at most $$d\bigl(m\,h^{-\gamma}+e\,h^{\eta}\bigr).\tag{2.1}$$ Here $d\le n^{o(1)}$ uniformly in the input network for fixed $k,\eta$.
Lemma 23.1 (Compression of a tensor power), recalled from §23. p. 86 paper Let $0\lt\delta\le 1/10$ and $0\lt\beta\le b\le 1/2$. For every sufficiently small fixed $a\gt 0$ depending only on $k,\delta,\beta$, and all sufficiently large $n$: work over an extension $K/k$ of degree $O(\log h)$. Suppose an input table network has maximum arity at most $h^{b}$, table labels of $(\log h)^{O(1)}$ bits, size at most $m$ with $n\le m\le n^{1.1}$, and $e\le m\,h^{-2a}$ external bits. Then some $d$ with $1\le d=n^{o(1)}$ admits a table network over $K$ for its $d$th tensor power, with all $de$ external bits separately accessible, such that $$\text{size}\le m\,d\,h^{-(1-\delta)a},\qquad \text{maximum arity}\le h^{\,b+(1+\delta)a},$$ and the output labels again have $(\log h)^{O(1)}$ bits.
Lemma 16.2 (Batched table evaluation), recalled from §16. p. 59 paper Assume the ordinary-width stable-sort bound of Theorem 16.1 over $k$, with exponent $p\gt 1$. Let a network of binary wires and $K$-valued tables represent a tensor $T$, where $K/k$ is any field extension. (In this statement $T$ is a tensor; elsewhere in Part IV, $T$ is the arity scale.) If $t_v\le u$ is the arity of station $v$ and $s=\sum_v(1+t_v)$, then $T^{\otimes 2^u}$ has an exact trivalent $K$-network of size $$O\bigl(2^u\,s\,(1+u)^{p-1}\bigr).$$ The constant is independent of $K$ and of the entries in the tables.
Background — table lookup by sorting, in the form used here. filled here A trivalent network cannot hold a table of arity $t\gt 3$ at one vertex. The proof of Lemma 16.2 (p. 59) evaluates $2^t$ copies of one table $f:\{0,1\}^t\to K$ together. The steps are as follows.
  1. The $2^t$ query addresses $x_1,\dots,x_{2^t}$ are the exposed legs. The required value is $\prod_i f(x_i)=\prod_z f(z)^{c_z}$, where $c_z$ is the number of queries with address $z$.
  2. For each address $z$ there is one dictionary row. The network guesses $c_z$ in $t+1$ bits. On count bit $j$ ($0\le j\le t$) it places a one-bit tensor with the two entries $(1,\ f(z)^{2^j})$. The product of these tensors over the bits of $c_z$ is $f(z)^{c_z}$.
  3. Each query also gets a row, with zeros in the count field. The $2^{t+1}$ rows (queries and dictionary rows) are sorted by address and then by row type, so that the dictionary row of an address follows all queries to that address. A counter starts at zero and increments on every query row; on a dictionary row it must agree with the guessed count, and it then resets to zero. This forces every guessed count to be the true multiplicity. For fixed exposed addresses there is exactly one consistent assignment of all internal wires, so the contraction equals $\prod_i f(x_i)$.
  4. The rows have $O(t+1)$ bits. By Theorem 16.1(1), the sort has size $O(2^t(1+t)^p)$; the counter has size $O(2^t(1+t))$.
A network with stations $v$ of arities $t_v\le u$ is copied $2^u$ times; the copies of station $v$ are grouped into $2^{u-t_v}$ batches of $2^{t_v}$. The total size is at most a constant times $$2^u\sum_v(1+t_v)^p\ \le\ 2^u(1+u)^{p-1}\sum_v(1+t_v).$$ The cost per unit of arity is therefore $(1+u)^{p-1}$, where $u$ is the largest arity. This is the only place in Part IV where the inequality $p\lt 2$ of Theorem 16.1(1) is used. The other uses of Theorem 16.1 in Part IV (the rotor in §24.3 and the message sort in §26.1) use part (2), the wide-record bound.

Removing the field extension: Lemma 27.1

The local tensor identities of Part III need a field $K\supseteq k$ with at least a fixed power of $T$ elements, where $T$ is the arity scale. The networks produced by the macros therefore have tables with values in $K$, although the tensor $V^{\otimes d}$ that they represent has entries in $k$. Theorem 2.1 asks for a network over $k$. Lemma 27.1 converts one into the other at a cost of a factor $[K:k]^2$.

Lemma 27.1 (Descent of a trivalent network). p. 106 paper Let $K/k$ have finite degree $D$. If a trivalent $K$-network of size $s$ represents a tensor with entries in $k$, the same tensor has a trivalent $k$-network of size $O(sD^2)$ with the same exterior positions.

In this lemma $D$ denotes the degree $[K:k]$. In §25 the same letter denotes the concentration parameter. The two are unrelated.

Background — multiplication in $K$ as a $D\times D$ matrix over $k$. filled here Choose a $k$-basis $\varepsilon_1=1,\varepsilon_2,\dots,\varepsilon_D$ of $K$. For $\lambda\in K$ define the matrix $M_\lambda\in k^{D\times D}$ by $\lambda\,\varepsilon_j=\sum_i (M_\lambda)_{ij}\,\varepsilon_i$. Three facts follow from the definition.
  1. $M_{\lambda\mu}=M_\lambda M_\mu$, because multiplication by $\lambda\mu$ is the composition of the two multiplications.
  2. The first column of $M_\lambda$ is the coordinate vector of $\lambda\cdot\varepsilon_1=\lambda$. Hence $(M_\lambda)_{11}=\pi(\lambda)$, where $\pi:K\to k$ sends an element to its first coordinate.
  3. The map $\pi$ is $k$-linear, and $\pi(c)=c$ for $c\in k$, because $c=c\,\varepsilon_1$.
The map $\pi$ is not multiplicative. The proof below applies it once, to a complete product, and uses matrix products for everything before that.

Step 1: a chain through all stations. Number the stations $v_1,\dots,v_N$ in an arbitrary order; $N\le s$, because the size $s$ counts the stations. The order need not be related to the wiring. Give station $v_t$ two additional ports: an incoming index $i_{t-1}$ and an outgoing index $i_t$, each in $\{1,\dots,D\}$. Join the outgoing port of $v_t$ to the incoming port of $v_{t+1}$. Fix an assignment $x$ of all original wires and exterior bits. Under $x$, station $v_t$ selects a table entry $\lambda_t\in K$. The enlarged table at $v_t$ returns the entry $(M_{\lambda_t})_{i_t\,i_{t-1}}\in k$. Pin $i_0=1$ and $i_N=1$.

Step 2: the chain computes one coordinate of the product. For the fixed $x$, summing over $i_1,\dots,i_{N-1}$ is a product of matrices: $$\sum_{i_1,\dots,i_{N-1}}\ \prod_{t=1}^{N}(M_{\lambda_t})_{i_t i_{t-1}}=\bigl(M_{\lambda_N}\cdots M_{\lambda_1}\bigr)_{11}=\bigl(M_{\lambda_1\cdots\lambda_N}\bigr)_{11}=\pi(\lambda_1\cdots\lambda_N).$$ The second equality is fact 1 of the background box, and the third is fact 2.

Step 3: linearity moves $\pi$ outside the sum over wires. The entry of the original tensor at given exterior bits is $\sum_x\prod_t\lambda_t(x)$, where $x$ runs over the internal wires. The new network gives $\sum_x\pi\bigl(\prod_t\lambda_t(x)\bigr)=\pi\bigl(\sum_x\prod_t\lambda_t(x)\bigr)$ by fact 3. The argument of $\pi$ is the original entry, which lies in $k$ by hypothesis, so $\pi$ returns it unchanged. The new network has entries in $k$ and represents the same tensor on the same exterior positions.

Step 4: the enlarged tables are made trivalent. Encode each index in $\lceil\log_2 D\rceil$ bits; codes that name no basis element get weight zero. A trivalent station has at most three original ports, so the enlarged table has at most $r=3+2\lceil\log_2 D\rceil$ bits. The paper states that every table on $r$ bits has a trivalent realization of size $O(2^r)$ (construction in the next box). Since $2^{\lceil\log_2 D\rceil}\lt 2D$, this is $2^r\lt 8\,(2D)^2=32D^2$ per original station, and $O(sD^2)$ in total.

∎
Gap fill — an arbitrary table on $r$ bits as a trivalent network of size $O(2^r)$. filled here Let $g:\{0,1\}^r\to k$ and let $x_1,\dots,x_r$ be the input bits. The paper's construction (p. 106) is a binary prefix tree. Written out:
  1. For each prefix $w\in\{0,1\}^j$, $0\le j\le r$, there is a wire carrying the indicator $I_w=[x_1\cdots x_j=w]$. The root indicator $I_\varnothing$ is pinned to $1$.
  2. For a prefix $w$ of length $j\lt r$, two gates with $0/1$ tables compute $I_{w0}=I_w\wedge\neg x_{j+1}$ and $I_{w1}=I_w\wedge x_{j+1}$. Each gate has two inputs and one output, so its arity is three. An equality tensor of arity three copies $I_w$ for the two gates.
  3. The $2^j$ nodes of depth $j$ need $2^{j+1}$ copies of $x_{j+1}$. A binary tree of arity-three equality tensors makes them with $O(2^j)$ vertices.
  4. At each leaf $w\in\{0,1\}^r$ a one-bit tensor with entries $(1,\ g(w))$ is attached to $I_w$: it contributes $1$ when $I_w=0$ and $g(w)$ when $I_w=1$.
For a fixed input $x$ every copy wire and indicator wire has exactly one value consistent with the $0/1$ gates. The sum over internal wires therefore has one nonzero term. Exactly one leaf has $I_w=1$, namely $w=x$, so the term equals $g(x)$. Level $j$ costs $O(2^j)$ vertices and the leaves cost $2^r$, so the size is $O\bigl(\sum_{j\le r}2^j\bigr)=O(2^r)$. The construction is valid in every characteristic and also when some $g(w)=0$.

Proof of Theorem 2.1: the parameters

The proof (pp. 106–107) runs $J$ macros, then one batched lookup, then one descent. Each macro multiplies the size per copy by $h^{-(1-\delta)a}$ and multiplies the bound on the largest arity by $h^{(1+\delta)a}$. The lookup costs a factor $(\text{largest arity})^{p-1}$. The proof stops the macros at a point where the accumulated saving still exceeds this cost.

Several letters in this section have other meanings elsewhere in Part IV. In §27, $J$ is the number of macros and $j$ indexes them (in §25, $J$ is the number of beats per round and $j$ the number of rounds per macro); $\rho=p-1$ (in §24.2, $\rho$ is a contraction factor); $\Delta$ is the net exponent saved (in §24, $\Delta$ is a degree bound); $q=h^{-(1-\delta)a}$ (in §25, $q$ multiplies the arity scale); $u$ is the final largest arity (in §24, $u$ is the unit of bundle width); $A$ is the total exponent $Ja$.

Definition (the parameters of the proof and their order of choice). pp. 106–107 paper
OrderSymbolMeaningConstraint when chosen
0$k,\ \eta$the finite field; the exterior-bit allowance of Theorem 2.1given; $\omega_k\lt 9/4$, $\eta\gt 0$
1$p,\ \rho=p-1$exponent of the ordinary-width sort, Theorem 16.1(1)$1\lt p\lt 2$, so $0\lt\rho\lt 1$; supplied by the hypothesis
2$\delta$loss parameter of Lemma 23.1$0\lt\delta\le 1/10$ and $1-\delta\gt\rho(1+\delta)$
3$A$total size exponent saved by all macros, before the loss $\delta$"sufficiently small" (made explicit below)
4$\beta$exponent of the starting arity bound $h^\beta$"sufficiently small" after $A$
5$b_*=\beta+(1+\delta)A$exponent of the final arity bound $h^{b_*}$derived; must satisfy (27.1)
5$\Delta=(1-\delta)A-\rho b_*$net exponent saved on the original sizederived; must satisfy (27.1)
6$J$, $a=A/J$number of macros; exponent of one macro$a$ below the threshold of Lemma 23.1 for the chosen $k,\delta,\beta$, uniformly for $\beta\le b\le 1/2$; and $2a\lt\eta/2$
