The paper requires $$b_*\lt\tfrac12,\qquad \rho\,b_*\lt\tfrac12\eta,\qquad \Delta=(1-\delta)A-\rho\,b_*\gt 0.\tag{27.1}$$
Gap fill — the constraints (27.1) can be met exactly when $p\lt 2$. filled here The paper says "take $A\gt 0$ sufficiently small, and then $\beta\gt 0$ sufficiently small". The explicit conditions are as follows. Put $$c_\delta=(1-\delta)-\rho(1+\delta)=(1-\rho)-\delta(1+\rho).$$ (The symbol $c_\delta$ is notation of this tutorial.) Substituting $b_*=\beta+(1+\delta)A$ gives $$\Delta=(1-\delta)A-\rho\beta-\rho(1+\delta)A=c_\delta A-\rho\beta.$$
  1. Necessity of $p\lt 2$. If $\rho\ge 1$, then $c_\delta\le(1-\delta)-(1+\delta)=-2\delta\lt 0$ for every $\delta\gt 0$. Then $\Delta=c_\delta A-\rho\beta\lt 0$ for all $A,\beta\gt 0$, and the third condition of (27.1) fails for every choice.
  2. Choice of $\delta$. If $\rho\lt 1$, the condition $c_\delta\gt 0$ is $\delta\lt(1-\rho)/(1+\rho)=(2-p)/p$. Take $\delta=\min\{1/10,\ (1-\rho)/(2(1+\rho))\}$; then $c_\delta\ge(1-\rho)/2\gt 0$.
  3. Choice of $A$. The first two conditions of (27.1) need $(1+\delta)A\lt\min\{1/2,\ \eta/(2\rho)\}$. Take $A$ with $(1+\delta)A=\tfrac12\min\{1/2,\ \eta/(2\rho)\}$.
  4. Choice of $\beta$. The three conditions become $$\beta\lt\min\Bigl\{\frac{c_\delta A}{\rho},\ \ \frac12-(1+\delta)A,\ \ \frac{\eta}{2\rho}-(1+\delta)A\Bigr\}.$$ Take $\beta=\tfrac12\min\{c_\delta A/\rho,\ 1/4,\ \eta/(4\rho)\}$. Then $b_*\le\tfrac18+\tfrac14\lt\tfrac12$, $\ \rho b_*\le\tfrac{\eta}{8}+\tfrac{\eta}{4}\lt\tfrac{\eta}{2}$, and $\Delta\ge c_\delta A/2\gt 0$.
So the three conditions hold simultaneously for suitable $\delta,A,\beta$ if and only if $\rho\lt 1$. The reason is the trade made by one macro: it lowers the size exponent by $(1-\delta)a$ and raises the arity exponent by $(1+\delta)a$, and the lookup converts arity exponent into size exponent at the rate $\rho$. The macro is profitable when $(1-\delta)\gt\rho(1+\delta)$.

Illustrative numbers. The paper gives no value of $p$; the exponent comes from Part II and is not effective. Suppose, for illustration only, $p=3/2$ and $\eta=1/10$. Then $\rho=0.5$. Take $\delta=0.1$: $c_\delta=0.9-0.55=0.35$. Take $A=0.05$: $(1+\delta)A=0.055\lt\min\{0.5,\,0.1\}$. Take $\beta=0.02\lt\min\{0.035,\ 0.445,\ 0.045\}$. Then $b_*=0.075$, $\rho b_*=0.0375\lt 0.05$, $\Delta=0.045-0.0375=0.0075$, $\gamma=0.00375$. The condition $2a\lt\eta/2$ needs $a\lt 0.025$, so $J\ge 3$ (and $J$ larger if the threshold of Lemma 23.1 requires it). With $J=3$: $2a+\rho b_*\approx 0.0333+0.0375=0.0708\lt\eta$.

Why $J$ is separate from $A$. Lemma 23.1 holds only for $a$ below a threshold that depends on $k,\delta,\beta$; the paper does not make the threshold explicit (it comes from the conditions $Ca+2a\lt\beta$ at the end of §26.2, where $C$ depends on the non-effective concentration parameter). The constraints (27.1) involve only the product $A=Ja$. Dividing $A$ into $J$ equal macros makes $a$ as small as required without changing $b_*$ or $\Delta$. This is why $\beta$ is chosen before $J$.

Proof of Theorem 2.1: the steps

Step 1: one field for all macros (p. 107). The scales $T$ that occur in the $J$ macros lie between a constant times $h^\beta$ and a fixed power of $h$. The local identities (Lemma 19.1) require a fixed finite extension of $k$ and a field of cardinality at least a fixed power of $T$. Choose $K\supseteq k$ with $[K:k]$ divisible by the degrees of the required fixed extensions and large enough for the largest such power. Because $k$ is finite, $|K|=|k|^{[K:k]}$, so $[K:k]=O(\log h)$ suffices; and $\log T\ge\beta\log h-O(1)$ at every scale, so $[K:k]=O(\log T)$ as Lemma 19.1 requires.

Step 2: the induction over macros. Let $s_j$ be the size (stations plus total arity) after $j$ macros, $d_j$ the cumulative number of copies, and $e_j=d_je$ the number of exterior bits. The network after $j$ macros represents $V^{\otimes d_j}$.

Start. $d_0=1$. The given network is trivalent of size $m$, so it has at most $m$ vertices of arity at most $3$; hence $s_0\le 4m$. Its arity is at most $3\le h^{\beta}$ for large $n$. The trivalent $k$-tables form a fixed finite dictionary, so labels have $O(1)$ bits.

Step $j\lt J$. Lemma 23.1 needs an input with at most $m\,h^{-2a}$ exterior bits. The current network may have fewer than $h^{2a}$ units of size per exterior bit. The paper therefore applies the lemma with the size allowance $$M_j=\max\bigl\{s_j,\ \lceil e_jh^{2a}\rceil\bigr\}$$ in the role of the lemma's "$m$". A network of size $s_j$ is also a network of size at most $M_j$. The hypotheses of Lemma 23.1 hold with $m:=M_j$, $e:=e_j$, $b:=\beta+(1+\delta)aj$:

  • $e_j\le M_jh^{-2a}$ by the definition of $M_j$;
  • $M_j\ge e_jh^{2a}\ge e\ge n$;
  • $M_j\le n^{1.1}$ (next box);
  • the arity bound is $h^{\beta+(1+\delta)aj}$ with $\beta\le\beta+(1+\delta)aj\le b_*\lt 1/2$, by the first condition of (27.1);
  • labels have $(\log h)^{O(1)}$ bits, by the conclusion of the previous macro.

If this macro makes $r_j$ copies, the lemma gives $d_{j+1}=r_jd_j$, arity bound $h^{\beta+(1+\delta)a(j+1)}$, and $$s_{j+1}\le r_j\,M_j\,q,\qquad q=h^{-(1-\delta)a}.$$ The construction in §25.2 has $\log_2r_j=O((\log h)^2)$, uniformly in the input network.

Step 3: the recurrence per exterior bit. Put $x_j=s_j/e_j$. Dividing the last display by $e_{j+1}=r_je_j$ cancels the replication factor: $$x_{j+1}\le q\,\frac{M_j}{e_j}=q\max\Bigl\{x_j,\ \frac{\lceil e_jh^{2a}\rceil}{e_j}\Bigr\}\le q\max\bigl\{x_j,\ 2h^{2a}\bigr\},$$ using $\lceil y\rceil\le 2y$ for $y\ge 1$. Iterating (next box), $$x_J\le\max\bigl\{q^Jx_0,\ 2q\,h^{2a}\bigr\}.$$

Step 4: batched lookup. Let $u$ be the largest arity after $J$ macros; $u\le h^{\beta+(1+\delta)aJ}=h^{b_*}$. Lemma 16.2 with $s=s_J$ gives an exact trivalent $K$-network for the $2^u$th power, of size $O\bigl(2^us_J(1+u)^{\rho}\bigr)$. The represented tensor is $V^{\otimes d}$ with $$d=2^u\,d_J,\qquad \log_2d\le h^{b_*}+O\bigl(J(\log h)^2\bigr)=o(h).$$ The last equality uses $b_*\lt 1$. Hence $d=n^{o(1)}$, uniformly in the input network for fixed $k,\eta$. Each of the $de$ exterior positions remains exterior. The size per exterior bit is now at most $O((1+u)^{\rho})\,x_J=O(h^{\rho b_*})\,x_J$.

Step 5: descent. $V^{\otimes d}$ has entries in $k$. Lemma 27.1 with degree $[K:k]=O(\log h)$ gives a trivalent $k$-network on the same exterior positions, larger by a factor $O([K:k]^2)=O((\log h)^2)=h^{o(1)}$.

Step 6: combination. With $q^J=h^{-(1-\delta)aJ}=h^{-(1-\delta)A}$, $x_0=s_0/e\le 4m/e$ and $q\le 1$, Steps 3–5 give $$\frac{\text{trivalent size over }k}{de}\ \le\ h^{\rho b_*+o(1)}\max\Bigl\{h^{-(1-\delta)A}\,\frac{4m}{e},\ 2h^{2a}\Bigr\}\ \le\ O\Bigl(\frac me\Bigr)h^{-\Delta+o(1)}+h^{2a+\rho b_*+o(1)},$$ because $-(1-\delta)A+\rho b_*=-\Delta$. This is the display on p. 107. Choose $\gamma=\Delta/2$. Since $\Delta\gt\gamma$, the factor $O(1)\,h^{-\Delta+o(1)}$ is at most $h^{-\gamma}$ for large $n$. Since $2a\lt\eta/2$ and $\rho b_*\lt\eta/2$, the exponent $2a+\rho b_*$ is strictly less than $\eta$, so the second term is at most $h^{\eta}$ for large $n$. Multiplying by $de$ gives the bound $d(mh^{-\gamma}+eh^{\eta})$ of (2.1).

∎
Gap fill — the allowance stays in the range $n\le M_j\le n^{1.1}$. filled here The paper states that "the original bound $s_0,e_0=O(n^{1.05})$, multiplied by a fixed number of these copy factors and fixed powers of $h$, gives $M_j\le n^{1.05+o(1)}\le n^{1.1}$". The derivation: $q\le 1$ gives $s_{j+1}\le r_jM_j$. Also $\lceil e_{j+1}h^{2a}\rceil=\lceil r_je_jh^{2a}\rceil\le r_j\lceil e_jh^{2a}\rceil\le r_jM_j$. Hence $M_{j+1}\le r_jM_j$ and, by induction, $M_j\le d_jM_0$. In a trivalent network the size counts the exterior bits, so $e\le m\le n^{1.05}$, and $M_0\le\max\{4m,\ 2e\,h^{2a}\}\le 4n^{1.05}h^{2a}$. Finally $\log_2d_j=\sum_{i\lt j}\log_2r_i=O(J(\log h)^2)=o(h)$, so $d_j=n^{o(1)}$. Therefore $M_j\le n^{1.05+o(1)}\le n^{1.1}$ for large $n$. The lower bound $M_j\ge n$ was shown in Step 2 from $e\ge n$. This is the place where the hypotheses $m\le n^{1.05}$ and $e\ge n$ of Theorem 2.1 are used.
Gap fill — solving the recurrence $x_{j+1}\le q\max\{x_j,2h^{2a}\}$. filled here Claim: $x_j\le\max\{q^jx_0,\ 2qh^{2a}\}$ for $1\le j\le J$. For $j=1$ the recurrence gives $x_1\le\max\{qx_0,\ 2qh^{2a}\}$. If the claim holds for $j$, then $$x_{j+1}\le q\max\bigl\{q^jx_0,\ 2qh^{2a},\ 2h^{2a}\bigr\}=\max\bigl\{q^{j+1}x_0,\ 2qh^{2a}\bigr\},$$ because $q\lt 1$ makes $2qh^{2a}\lt 2h^{2a}$. The paper's sentence "every contribution arising from an exterior-bit allowance is bounded by the most recent such allowance" is this computation: an allowance introduced at an early step is multiplied by further factors $q\lt 1$ and is then smaller than the allowance of the last step.
Gap fill — the origin of the two terms of (2.1). filled here

batched lookupfactor $O((1+u)^{\rho})$, $u\le h^{b_*}$$+\rho\,b_*$Lemma 16.2, exponent $p=1+\rho$
descent to $k$factor $O((\log h)^2)$$o(1)$Lemma 27.1, $[K:k]=O(\log h)$
  • The term $d\,m\,h^{-\gamma}$. It is the first branch: the original size per exterior bit $x_0=O(m/e)$ multiplied by $h^{-(1-\delta)A}\cdot h^{\rho b_*}\cdot h^{o(1)}=h^{-\Delta+o(1)}$, and then by $de$.
  • The term $d\,e\,h^{\eta}$. It is the second branch. A macro cannot bring the size below about $h^{2a}$ per exterior bit, because Lemma 23.1 requires $e\le m\,h^{-2a}$. Inside the macro this hypothesis is used twice: in §25.2 it gives $ed\le L_i/D$, the width allowance for the stations that hold the exterior bits, and in §26.2 it bounds the routing cost of the exterior bits by $ed\le md\,h^{-2a}$. The floor $2qh^{2a}$, multiplied by the lookup factor $h^{\rho b_*}$ and by $h^{o(1)}$, is at most $h^{\eta}$ because $2a\lt\eta/2$ and $\rho b_*\lt\eta/2$.
The two halves of $\eta$ are thus assigned to two different costs: $\eta/2$ to the macro's exterior-bit floor and $\eta/2$ to the lookup.
AHA. The proof never evaluates a large table until the end. Each macro exchanges total arity for largest arity at a rate close to one for one in the exponent, and the final lookup pays only the power $p-1\lt 1$ of the largest arity. With a sorting bound of the form $O(nh^2)$, that is $p=2$ and $\rho=1$, the gap $\Delta=-2\delta A-\beta$ is negative, and the same construction saves nothing.

Result of the check of the bookkeeping. With the conditions made explicit above, every inequality on p. 107 follows from (27.1), $2a\lt\eta/2$, Lemma 23.1, Lemma 16.2 and Lemma 27.1. The tutorial found no step of §27 that fails to close as written. The proof is conditional on Lemma 23.1, whose proof (§23–§26) contains items that this tutorial marks as not verified. The constants are not effective: $p$ comes from Part II, the threshold for $a$ comes from the concentration parameter of §25, and both depend on compactness arguments.

How the theorem closes the argument

The final paragraph of the paper's text (p. 107) connects Theorem 2.1 to the lower bound. The tensor $D_w$ is the $0/1$ tensor of list distinctness: its input is a list of $2^w$ words of $2w$ bits, and its value is $1$ exactly when the words are pairwise distinct. It has $n_w=2w\,2^w$ exterior bits. Theorem 2.2 (p. 7) states that there is $\sigma\gt 0$, depending only on $k$, with $\operatorname{vol}_k(D_w^{\otimes t})\gt t\,n_w\,w^{\sigma}$ for all large $w$ and all $1\le t\le 2^w$; here $\operatorname{vol}_k$ is the minimum trivalent volume over $k$, which agrees with trivalent size up to an absolute factor (§23).

Proposition 2.3 (p. 8) chooses $0\lt\eta\lt\sigma$, takes $\gamma$ from Theorem 2.1, and fixes an integer greater than $2/\gamma$; the paper calls this integer $J$ as well, and it is a different number from the $J$ of this section. Write it $J'$. The starting network is a Batcher sorting network followed by adjacent comparisons, of size $O(n_ww^2)$; its size per exterior bit is $x_0=O(w^2)$. With $n=n_w$ and $h=w+O(\log w)$, each application of (2.1) gives

$$x_{j+1}\le h^{-\gamma}x_j+h^{\eta}.$$

After $J'$ applications, $x_{J'}\le h^{-J'\gamma}x_0+h^{\eta}\sum_{r\ge 0}h^{-r\gamma}=O(w^{2-J'\gamma})+O(w^{\eta})=O(w^{\eta})$, because $J'\gamma\gt 2$. The number of lists is $t_{J'}=n^{o(1)}\le 2^w$. The resulting network for $D_w^{\otimes t_{J'}}$ has volume $O(t_{J'}n_ww^{\eta})$, which is less than $t_{J'}n_ww^{\sigma}$ for large $w$. This contradicts Theorem 2.2. The paper concludes that $\omega_k\lt 9/4$ is false for every finite field $k$.

The independence of the $de$ exterior positions in Theorem 2.1 is used here: the exterior positions of $D_w^{\otimes t}$ are the entries of $t$ separately specified lists, and Theorem 2.2 is uniform in $t$. The uniformity is needed because the replication factor $d$ is chosen by the compression and not by the user of the theorem.

AHA moments, in one list

  1. §1.3 — The exponent is a ratio of two geometric quantities: the perimeter of a triangle of wires divided by the rate of a label shared by its three corners.
  2. §1.3 — Flat geometry gives $2$. A tripod gives $6/(8/3)=9/4$. The difference is curvature.
  3. §2.3 — The factor $2$ in $\lceil n^{9/4}/2\rceil$ is the dual-number product rule: one gate makes at most two new bilinear products.
  4. §2.3 — One bilinear identity is a finite system of polynomial equations, so it reduces to a finite field.
  5. §2.2 — The lower bound is proved through a different problem. A faster product would test list distinctness too cheaply; compression is applied to its own output $J\gt2/\gamma$ times.
  6. §5.2 — Price extension is a duality: the obvious lower bound obtained by closing legs is sharp, also for a whole family at once.
  7. §6.2 — The parameter is a nonarchimedean variable. The Gauss lift and the star inequality turn tensor algebra into potential theory on the tree of disks.
  8. §7 — Either nothing moves, or $\omega\ge9/4$: motion gives a tree, four tips, and $\underline D\le3/2$.
  9. §8.2 — Rectangular multiplication at inner dimension $N^{1/4}$ supplies contact at infinity; a strong minimum principle carries it to equal arms, $3-\frac13=\frac83$.
  10. §11.3 — The same tripod inequality with the arithmetic–harmonic mean inequality re-proves the upper bound and fixes every wire at $3/4$.
  11. §12 — Synchronization: under $\omega\lt9/4$ the optimal site cannot depend on the parameter.
  12. §14.1 — Free fermions: collisions are fusion; width is $(\pi/\sqrt3)\sqrt{\text{energy}}$, so squared width is linear in energy and optimal widths are distances from a barycenter.
  13. §16 and §27 — Sorting costs $h^p$ per record with $p\lt2$, below the square that collisions control; this strict inequality is what lets the exponents of §27 close.

Checks that passed

  • Every numbered theorem, lemma, proposition and corollary of the preprint has a statement box on this page, and the page given in each box agrees with the page index built from paper.pdf.
  • Every equation number used on this page exists in the preprint; 18 of them in §5–14 were compared line by line with the PDF text.
  • All mathematics on the page renders without a MathJax error; all internal links resolve; the page has no horizontal overflow at a width of 390 px.
  • Widget 2 gives $D_*=8/3$ and ratio $9/4$ at arms $(1,1,1)$; $D_*=1$ and perimeter $9/4$ at $(3/8,3/8,3/8)$; ratio $2$ at $(1,1,0)$; and $D_*(1,1,19)=20.51282=19+\frac32+\frac1{78}$.

Derivations written and checked for this tutorial

These are the boxes marked filled here. Each was derived independently of the preprint’s wording and agrees with the preprint’s stated result. The principal ones in §1–14:

  • §1.3: $T_n$ as a product of three wires, and the perimeter inequality $r\ge n^{\text{perimeter}/D}$.
  • §2: the dual-number expansion in Lemma 2.4; the solution of the recurrence in Proposition 2.3; the span argument for geometric border rank.
  • §3.1: linearity of the diagonal price in $\log q$; the triangle inequality; the transport bound (3.1).
  • §4.1: the signed-sum identity for the second distinctness test; the exponent arithmetic of §4.3.
  • §5: the direct-sum rule from the sum bound (Gibbs variational principle); the midpoint estimate (5.6); the completion of squares in (5.5).
  • §6: unimodularity of the binomial matrix; the scalar model of the star inequality.
  • §7: convexity of the displacement $R(A,\cdot)$; the distance computations on the collision arms; the harmonicity table; the limit that gives $\underline D\le3/2$.
  • §8: every line from $\underline D\le3/2$ to $D(1,1,1)=8/3$ and $r\ge n^{9/4}$, including $D_*(1,1,b)=b+\frac32+\frac1{4b+2}$.
  • §9: the identities $u_j+u_i\beta^*_{ik}=1$ and $u_jd_i+u_id_j=D_*$ and the inequality that gives $\Delta\ge0$.
  • §11: the bounds (11.1); the derivative of the logarithmic terms in (11.3); the arithmetic–harmonic step in (11.6).
  • §13–14: the kernel formula (13.1); the saddle-point bound $d_h\le e^{c\sqrt h}$; the completion of squares (14.6); the telescoping step $(N+1)B_{N+1}-NB_N\ge\frac N{N+1}Q_N$.

Not verified

  • The companion preprints [42] and [43]. Not read. Imported from them: the CAT(0) extension of spectral spaces with joint convexity of prices, ultralimits of prices, Zariski-closedness of price sublevel sets, strict spectral comparison, ports, the tagged-permutation tensor and its descent, the product theorem, the graph entropy bound, and the exact batch lower bound for the signed counting tensor. Theorem 2.2 depends on these in both of its cases.
  • The prior upper-bound papers [41], [39], [5]. Not read. Imported from them in §9: shared-leg separation and the concavity and three-block inequalities for $F_{ik}$.
  • The Lean development mentioned in the repository README. Not opened.
  • Steps reproduced without independent derivation, each marked in place: the truncation argument in Lemma 8.2; the axis limit (11.4) and the domination estimates for (11.5); and the items in the red boxes of §15–27.
  • The preprint as a whole. This tutorial follows the argument and checks local steps. It did not check that the 111 pages fit together without a gap, and it is not a substitute for refereeing.

Differences between the PDF text and the LaTeX source

The text layer of paper.pdf drops overlines and underlines. Two places matter. In Lemma 2.4 the rank bound is $\operatorname{rank}_{\overline F}(T_s)\le2q$, over the algebraic closure, and the proofs of Lemma 2.5 and Corollary 1.2 work over $\overline F$ accordingly. In §7.4 the slope is $\underline D$, with an underline. This page follows the LaTeX source in both places.

Sections 15–27: what the checks cover

Sections 15–27 were drafted by three subagents, each working from the LaTeX source of its part under one written specification. The main session did not read their text. The following is known.

  • Checked by script, for all three parts: every numbered statement of §15–27 has a box, and its page agrees with the page index; every equation number exists in the preprint; the mathematics renders; the links resolve.
  • Part II (§15–18), subagent report: finished and self-checked. It reports that the proofs of Lemma 17.3 and Proposition 18.1 are sketches with open items, that the constants of Part II are non-effective, and that the numbers $d_*\ge45000$, $C=208$ and $p\gt3/2$ are its own computations and not statements of the preprint.
  • Part III (§19–22), subagent report: finished and self-checked. It reports four open items: the phrase “at least three auxiliary marks” in §20.1; the two imports from [42] behind (20.4); the segment count $O((U+1)D^6)$ in §21.3, where its own count gives $D^4$ for seam crossings only; and conclusion 4 of Lemma 19.1. It reports that the explicit value of the scalar in Lemma 19.2 is its own computation.
  • Part IV (§23–27): the subagent stopped at a usage limit during its own review and sent no final report. Its text is included as it stood. Treat every derivation in §23–27, and the parameter table of §27 in particular, as unreviewed.

The page as a whole has 17 red “not verified” boxes, 21 imported-result boxes and 136 “filled here” boxes.
